CURÉ Secrétaire du Syndicat des Maraîchers de la région parisienne Available from: A LA LIBRAIRIE AGRICOLE DE LA MAISON RUSTIQUE 26, RUE JACOB, 26 ET CHEZ L'AUTEUR, 72, ROUTE DE CHATILLON, A MALAKOFF (SEINE) 1900 DEuESdS GOOgle -. -.----------- -- " --. -- D - . - The 1900 Exhibition, like its predecessors, will be an expressive portrayal of the great economic struggle that exists between all civilized peoples, in terms of culture, commerce, the arts and sciences. From the point of view of land production, it will show the improvements made in different crops, in different climates, the intensity given to these crops by the use of chemical fertilizers, the rotation in crops imposed by this new scientific method, the selection of plants, etc. The aim of this "fin de siècle" exhibition is not only to showcase current production, but above all to illustrate the progress made during the 19th century in all areas covered by the exhibition. Horticulture, that important branch of agriculture, has made a giant leap forward in this century, and so it will have a considerable place in this exhibition, with all its different specialties widely represented. Today, when everything is becoming more specialized than ever, each of the classes within it will be keen to highlight the progress it has made, both culturally and commercially. This is the most practical way of creating new outlets for French products, which can compete advantageously with all foreign countries. Class 44 of Group VIII (vegetable growing) will play a major role in this economic demonstration, showing the many visitors from all over the world all the products it can supply to the trade, both as early crops and seasonal plants. Parisian market gardening has reached a degree of intensity that is unequalled anywhere. As Reclus put it in his Géographie Universelle: "It's the article of agricultural Paris". It will be represented at all competitions, where amateurs will be able to judge the selection and beauty of the produce. This important industry has made such great strides from every point of view in the course of this century, that we have decided to retrace its history through the centuries: the first courtiers of Paris, the locations they occupied, the plants they cultivated and how long they have been in existence; the new ones with the times of their introduction; the types of cultivation, the progress made in cultivation methods, fertilizers and their use. The tools used at each period, as well as the equipment, the transformations made in this equipment, the innovations. Trade in different eras and its successive advances, the causes of these advances, the value of current production. Packaging of vegetables for sale at markets, progress made in this area, means of transporting produce to bales, etc. The professors' way of life, and the well-being they have derived from the various social and economic transformations. We will also trace the origins of most of the corporation's members, their social status during the century, and the role played by some of them in society, socially and politically speaking. We will also describe their successive moves, as the big city expanded, the causes of these moves; the value of the land they have 98 occupied since the end of the last century, as well as its rental value. In a word, we'll follow them in their perambulations, their types of cultivation, their innovations, the progress they've made in every respect, whether in their crops, their equipment, or their social status. We'll look at the first market gardeners in Paris, worthy of the name, around the Châtelet and Halles Centrales, those at the end of the last century, around the Bastille, the lower suburbs around Charles V's enclosure, and the lower Champs-Elysées, le Gros-Caillou, the Ecole Militaire district and the Faubourg Saint-Marceau to Maisons-Alfort, Créteil, Bobigny, Gennevilliers, Bagneux and Vitry, where they were found in greater numbers in 1900. Diuired p (OOgle - == -. - HISTORICAL CONSIDERATIONS of a general nature OEtEEdS GOOgle DEEEed9 GOOgle .- ITS FOUNDATION - ITS FIRST GARDENS It is essential, despite the fact that this is beyond the scope of our book, to devote the first few chapters to saying a few words about the general history, character and customs of the oldest of the peoples who have occupied our territory. The importance of the successive social transformations that have taken place since the land of France was first occupied will only become clearer with time. It is generally agreed that the Celts or Gaels, whose Latin name Galli was later changed to "Gauls", had descended from the high plateaus of Central Asia, as had the peoples who followed them in Europe. Their people lived in a state bordering on barbarism. There were only two orders among them: the priests or druids, and the chiefs (Brenn). They were all ploughmen and had no towns, only large open villages, which later enabled Caesar to easily take control of their country. Their government was a federative republic, and the General Assemblies met in the Chartrain country. As long as there was agreement between the various tribes, they enjoyed their provinces peacefully; but when discord broke out, the Romans took advantage of the situation to conquer these immense and rich lands; this conquest took place some 56 years before our era. The seven small islands (1) that today form only two in the heart of Paris, the Cité and the ile Saint-Louis, were first occupied by the Senones, a people who had settled along the banks of the Seine. They were later joined by an immigration of Belgians (Volhes). This mixture gave rise to the Parisii. The Ile de la Cité, which today houses the Palais de Justice, the Tribunal de Commerce, the Préfecture de Police, the Hôtel-Dieu and Notre-Dame, became their main fort, Louhtès, which the Romans translated as Lucotecia and then Lutecia, hence the French name Lutèce. This island was then covered with swamps and scrub. The Parisii built their round houses here, made of wood and earth, covered with reeds and without chimneys. The Parisii, like other Gauls, were pagans; they had three temples close to their village: the first, which the Romans later dedicated to Ceres, the Isis of the Egyptians, located roughly where the church of Saint-Germain-des-Prés now stands; the second to Mathra or Mercury, in the vicinity of the Pantheon, on Mount Lucotitius; and the third to Heus or Mars, in a wood located on a mountain that later bore its name: Mons Martis, from which Montmartre is derived. Mistletoe, a parasite that grows on almost all trees - poplars, willows, lindens, etc., but rarely on oaks - was venerated among them. The most famous ceremony in the cult of these peoples was the mistletoe ceremony. On the sixth day of the first moon of the year, priests would go around the country, shouting "au gui, l'an neuf", especially the mistletoe of the oak. The Pontiff, followed by a large procession, arrived at the foot of the tree, preferably an oak, burned a cake, poured drops of water onto a lawn, offered bread and water as a sacrifice and distributed them to those present. He then climbed (1) L'Ile Louviers, three islets that formed Ile Saint-Louis, Ile Notre-Dame, Ile du Passeur aux Vaches and Ile des Treilles (terre-plein du Pont-Neuf). 206 on the tree, cut off the mistletoe with a golden serpette, threw it on a tablecloth and ended the ceremony with the immolation of two white bulls. It was a kind of gift received as a present from heaven, a remedy against a great number of illnesses, but all these alleged qualities have disappeared with superstition. Our ancestors still venerated other plants, notably verbena, which also cured a great many illnesses, but since then, like mistletoe, it has lost much of its prestige (1). (1) Duhamel du Monceau. Delized s/ GO0 The Parisii cleared the land they found most fertile and easiest to cultivate. They concentrated their efforts on the left bank of the Seine, a lush, green valley. They built a wooden bridge over the river, so that they could send their herds to graze in the large wild meadows that existed in the surrounding area. Gardens were more likely to be found in the valley, close to the dwellings. The first Celts had to live on hunting and fishing, and on the products that the land spontaneously yielded in its wild state. However, there is no doubt that they brought with them seeds of plants from their homelands and the regions they crossed, and sowed them. The generations that followed after they took possession of the land multiplied these plants as they cleared it. The Gauls, and among them the people we're talking about here, the Parisii, must therefore be credited with a certain degree of agricultural civilization as early as the earliest historians can tell us. The traditions of ancient peoples," says De Candolle, "attribute the first steps in agriculture and the introduction of useful plants to some divinity, or to some emperor, Pharaoh or Inca. But the observation of cultivation trials among savages shows that things happened differently. In general, in the progress that led to civilization, the beginnings were weak, obscure and limited. There are reasons for this to be the case in agricultural and horticultural beginnings. Between the practice of harvesting fruits, vegetables, seeds or roots in the countryside, and the regular cultivation of these plants, there are several steps that sometimes took centuries to climb from one to the other. Wars, hunts and rivalries between the powerful have often halted attempts at cultivation. If some great personage orders the cultivation of a new plant, and institutes some ceremony to demonstrate its usefulness, it is surely because obscure, unknown cultivators have spoken of it before, and experiments already carried out have been successful. To make these attempts, to renew them and make them succeed, one must first have within one's reach such and such a plant, offering advantages that men seek. Even the most uncivilized people are familiar with plants indigenous to their own countries. Other conditions are also essential for certain plants: a climate that is not too rigorous; for others, droughts that are not too prolonged. Some degree of security, of fixity; finally, for these primitive peoples who had no taste for lucre, the need to live when hunting, fishing and the products of indigenous plants were not sufficient to meet their needs. Civilization has had the unfortunate side of making men egoists, giving them a taste for lucre and inciting them to exploit each other to pile up more than they can consume for some, when next to them, others don't have their sufficiency. If men had remained in their primitive state, would they be happier? It's a serious problem that mankind has been grappling with for centuries, but which we still haven't been able to resolve. Great revolutionary movements have taken place to achieve this result. First of all, Christianity, whose aim was the emancipation of mankind, and which the egoism of the then506 sants later turned towards a diametrically opposed goal. Finally, the Revolution of 1789, whose ideal was to make all men equal before the law, and to put into practice its great motto: Liberté, Égalité, Fraternité, inscribed on the frontispiece of our public monuments. But the further we move away from this great era, the more we seem to be moving towards the opposite goal. According to some authors, the spontaneous vegetation of our regions was characterized by oak, brambles and thorns. But under the influence of successive human migrations, our fertile land of France soon saw the acclimatization of root crops such as turnips and carrots, and, as herbs, cabbage, wild chicory, etc., as well as a number of southern plants. Many of these plants have since been considered to be spontaneously part of the French flora. With this approach, the Parisians cultivating the virgin lands on the left bank of the Seine were not long without a flourishing crop, which gave them a certain ease of living and a greater sense of well-being than other settlers, less well shared for the fertility of the soil. The right bank of Lutetia was nothing but an immense swamp, which these first settlers didn't even bother with, as they had enough good land to cultivate to support themselves, and were not yet familiar with trade (1). Several centuries passed in this simple, pastoral way of life. There's no doubt that these men lived happily, freely and free from all bondage. But there's a kind of civilization that consists in going to peoples who don't ask you for anything and don't even know you exist. You take over their country and dictate laws to them because they are weak. It was by suffering this injustice, like many other peoples after them, that the Gauls first became acquainted with civilization. (1) Delamare, Traité de Police. 99 "The common character of the Gallic race," says Strabo, "is that they are irritable and war-mad, quick to fight, otherwise simple, and without malignity. If you irritate them, they march together straight to the enemy, and attack him head-on, without inquiring about anything else. So, by rusc, you can easily get to Lout; you can draw them into battle whenever you want, wherever you want, whatever your motives, they're always ready. if they had no other weapon than their strength and audacity. However, by persuasion, they can be persuaded to do useful things; they are susceptible to culture and literary instruction. Strengthened by their height and numbers, they gather easily in large crowds, simple and spontaneous as they are, willingly taking up the cause of the oppressed. Wouldn't you think, when reading this quotation, that you were reading about the character of today's French, albeit with the veneer acquired through the experience of twenty centuries of civilization? The genius of these Gaels is first and foremost nothing other than movement, attack and conquest; it was through war that ancient nations mixed and drew closer together; as trade did not exist, there was no peaceful relationship between peoples. Peoples of war and noise, they run the world with swords in their hands, less, it seems, from greed than from a vague and vain desire to see, to know, to act; breaking, destroying, for want of being able to produce again. These are the children of the nascent world. The sky itself did not frighten them; they threw arrows at it when it thundered. If the waves of the sea broke against them, they did not refuse to fight, and marched towards them sword in hand. Their point of honor was never to back down. No nation was better off for its life. Their greatest pleasure, after fighting, was to surround the stranger, and make him tell tales of distant lands. These barbarians were insatiably curious, terrible talkers, tireless, and it was a business in their assemblies to keep the speaker talking in the midst of interruptions. Their banquets, moreover, did not end without a fight. They liked to associate, in large hordes, camping in villages in the middle of wide-open plains, bonding easily with strangers, familiar with strangers, talkers, laughers, orators mixing with everyone and everything. They were neither brutal nor drunkards - these vices belonged more to the Germans. In a word, our ancestors had all the qualities and all the vices of rapid sympathy. However, these cheerful companions were not to be trusted. They were not serious about words. They promised, then laughed, and all was said (1). This people would perhaps have rolled on for a long time in the ebb and flow of barbarism, if a new element, coming from outside, hadn't brought them a principle of stability, a social idea. UNDER ROMAN DOMINATION At that time, the Romans were ahead of all other peoples in all aspects of progress: civil and political organization, instruction, education, luxury, and so on. This was the real reason for the success of the Roman conquest over these proud warriors, who had made Rome tremble just a few centuries earlier. Caesar pounced on the Gauls as if they were prey for his avarice; he waged a war of brigandage. Slave traders followed the army, and Caesar sold the population to them body and soul. The Gauls lost both their homeland and their freedom, the most cherished possession in the world. Lutetia was attacked by Labienus fifty-six years BC. The first attack was on the marshes of the right bank, the Butte-des-Moulins, which Caesar described and called perpetua palus, perpetual marsh. He was forced to lift the siege. A second time by the same general, by the Seine. The Parisii set fire to their village, broke the bridges (they had also built a wooden bridge across the river to connect with the right bank of their island) and presented battle to the Romans. Labienus countered their resistance with cunning, pretending to retreat; then he divided his army into three corps and, by this stratagem, enveloped that of his enemies. 0 THE GARDENERS OF PARIS The Parisii were one of the clans that put up the greatest resistance to the conquerors; nevertheless, they suffered the same fate as all the other Gauls. Under Roman rule, Lutetia became the capital of Gaul. Julien pourvu proconsul, governor general of the Gauls, chose it as his home in 355. This city, he says, "is on a small island, entered from two sides by wooden bridges. The river rarely overflows, and winter is not too harsh. They grow very good vines, even fig trees, which they cover in winter with wheat straw (1). These few words from the proconsul on the products of the Parisii indicate that they already had a sophisticated culture. First, they had meadows on the river banks, then further afield they cultivated cereals: wheat, barley and oats. Mount Lucotitius was covered with vines. According to Julian, they certainly had gardens on their island, since there were artfully cultivated fig trees, other trees, roots, native and imported vegetables that must also have been well grown. But as this people knew nothing of trade, all these products were consumed by the producers themselves. The Gauls had none of the indifference to property ownership generally found among barbarian peoples. Having only two orders, the druids and the chiefs, they had divided their domains, honors and immunities among themselves, so that the people barely counted, tyrannized by the great and cultivating their lands. The masters had all the rights of the master over his slaves, over the ploughmen of their possessions. The Latin domination did not introduce any appreciable change in this order of things; the men of the land were already in slavery; they remained slaves; only, the yoke was heavier. This is how far this ancient and formidable race had come 1) Delamare, Traité de Police. D9 LES CULTIVATEURS PARISII SOUS LA DOMINATION ROMAIXE 13 des Gaulois, who had made other peoples, their neighbors, tremble, after having been among the great nations before them, because the two privileged classes, after having reduced to slavery the third, the most numerous, had found themselves isolated and without strength at the time of the invasion. Property not being as solidly established as it was among the Romans, the latter, following the example of all conquerors, easily dispossessed the chiefs; the ploughmen merely changed masters; but the latter were much more rapacious and harsh than the former. They had four methods of exploiting rural property: 1° the owner used a workshop of slaves, governed by an auctor; 2° they had slaves cultivate the land in perpetuity; 3° they gave land to farmers, who worked it as they saw fit; 4° they entrusted it to a slave who had other slaves under his command. All these men were attached to the land, and were sold along with the property, like the beasts of burden that shared their labor. The Roman republics had created smallholdings, the only way to give men true freedom. This is what our great Revolution of 1789 set out to do once again, with some success. Unfortunately, its conquests may not last much longer, for barely a century later, we are witnessing the reconstitution of large-scale property in all its forms. The Roman emperors destroyed small-scale property and founded large-scale property, which gave rise to the most odious slavery that men can endure. These were the advances made by the Romans, especially in Gaul. However, they left social organization and general administration intact; thanks to this concession, the qualities peculiar to their race, combined with their love of agriculture, were later to make this people one of the nations leading the way in civilization and progress. D08 However, the Gallic chiefs did not meekly submit to the yoke of their new masters; the Roman occupation was a long period of wars and revolts. All the insults and vexations they received were passed on to the slaves, colonists or dependent farmers, whose condition became more and more akin to slavery. It was to them that the owners paid back all the outrages and exactions inflicted on them by superior agents. Overburdened by taxation and growing poverty, their numbers were dwindling by the day. The Gauls were becoming depopulated, and lands once so fertile were turning into marshes and ponds. Roman tyranny weighed so heavily on these unfortunate farmers that almost an entire race perished (1). Did the Parisii farmers suffer the same fate? There's little doubt about it, but as they were more numerous, and closer to the powerful of the day, they were better able to voice their grievances, and thus manage to improve their lot to some extent. Men passed on and succumbed, but the organization that the victors had brought with them remained, and all too slowly, alas! brought greater well-being to subsequent generations. LES JARDINIERS DE PARIS (1) Lactantius (On the death of persecutors) The time of the founding of gardens can be traced back to the earliest days of man's existence, since one of the oldest stories in the world tells us that Adam and Eve were born in a garden. La Quintinie rightly claims that gardening was man's first profession. Before the use of animals to plough the land, centuries passed during which men ploughed it with their hands, using crude tools. These primitive men lived on fruit and vegetables; it was only later that they ventured to train animals to use them, then to kill them to eat their flesh, at first with disgust (1). Garden cultivation was passed on to the Greeks by the Phoenicians and Egyptians, but these peoples only grew vegetables and fruit suitable for human consumption. From the esteem in which they held these foods and the gardens that produced them, the ancients deified Aesculapius as the first to regulate the use of these products in medicine, and made him the father of the goddess Salus, who is none other than health, to whom the Romans had built a temple. They even went so far as to give divine honors and a religious cult to certain vegetables, through the services they rendered in people's diets. These included cabbage, onions, etc. (2). (1) Delamare Traité de Police. (2) Louis Noisette. The Persians seem to be the creators of pleasure gardens. The Greeks never mastered the art of enhancing the beauty of gardens through enlightened cultivation, contenting themselves with harvesting fruit and vegetables. The Romans, more conquerors than farmers, neglected the cultivation of flowers in the first centuries of their power. Their cultivation consisted of planting a few vegetables entrusted to the care of the mother of the family, who was then, as now, responsible for providing food for the family. Later, the Roman emperors brought with them a taste for beautiful gardens, and horticulture in these remote times and in these countries already took on a certain development (1). The Romans called the garden heredium (inheritance), and the word hortus was applied, in a broader sense, to the tenant farm. Gardening was divided into four types: vegetable, fruit, cork and medicinal, in much the same way as today, with the addition of ornamental plant beds to the cork garden (2). The use of terraces dates back to the time of the first patriarchs. Semiramis, queen of the Assyrians, had such a great taste for gardens that she had one made right up to her palace in Babylon, and with such art that it was counted as one of the Seven Wonders of the World. This taste was passed on to the Romans, and when Julian had his palace built on the site of the Cluny museum, he added one to the immense gardens that surrounded it, on the vault of the Thermes (or baths) hall. We'll come back to these gardens later. The taste for terraces is still very pronounced among southerners. In Algeria, in the beautiful villas surrounding Algiers, and on the steep slopes of the Sahel, we have seen terraces planted with admirable taste, a credit to (1) Louis Noisette. (21 Olivier de Serres. the gardeners who planted them. It's worth adding that all the houses have their own terraces, which contributes greatly to the fashion for gardens. All these terraces are very well maintained, whitewashed every year; so when you see Algiers from the open sea, you first see a white dot, which grows as you approach until you can make out the houses. These white terraces, which serve as roofs for the houses, give the town a very special appearance and character. The Arabs have nicknamed it Algiers-la-Blanche. When you leave Marseille by sea too, you can see the old town with its dirty black roofs gradually fading and disappearing. Twenty-four hours later, when you see Algiers-la-Blanche as the crow flies, the contrast in your mind's eye is all in favor of the latter. With the decline of the Roman Empire, people were so enslaved that the taste for beautiful gardens no longer existed; all that remained, as centuries before, was the cultivation of vegetables for human consumption. GARDENS, THEIR ORIGIN IN PARIS We mentioned in the previous chapter that Julian, proconsul of Gaul and later named emperor by the armies he commanded, had his palace built on the left bank of the Seine, not far from Lutetia. This palace was surrounded by immense gardens, extending on one side to where the church of Saint-Germain-des-Prés was later built, and on the other to the top of the nearby mountain (Mont Lucotitius), which later took the name MontagneSainte-Geneviève. After having these constructions built, he had the waters of Arcueil brought to him via an aqueduct, some remains of which can still be seen in this old commune. These waters were used for bathing, for the use of the palace and certainly also for the upkeep of the gardens. By this time, the Roman Empire was in all its splendor, and Julian undoubtedly sent from Rome practitioners skilled in the art of gardening to lay out and cultivate these beautiful gardens. These men were our first masters. With Lutetia now the capital of the Gauls, Julian, the Roman emperor, established his residence there, as did some of his successors, and the city continued to grow in size and population. Trade, which had already existed for several centuries among the Romans, passed to the Gauls. The grain trade, presumably one of the first, began under the consuls (1). (1) Delamare Traité de Police. At the time of Julian's arrival, Gaul had been a Roman province for at least three centuries, and there were undoubtedly a few small gardeners established in the more marshy parts of the islands, so that when he arrived he was able to say that they cultivated fig trees with a certain art, and that they were farmers for the most part. The Consuls had their palace on the Ile de la Cité, roughly where the Palais de Justice stands today, with a large garden occupying its entire western tip. This palace was inhabited by some of our kings, and the garden long kept the name of Jardin Royal, as can easily be seen in the old maps of Paris. It was only under Henri IV, when the Pont-Neuf was built, that this part of the island changed its appearance. The old writers say nothing of this former garden, which was a meadow when Henri IV undertook to embellish this part of Old Paris. Clovis, the first king of the Franks to enter Lutetia, disputing the power of the Gauls with the Romans, established a church on Mount Lucotitius; this became the church of Sainte-Geneviève. Nearby, he built his palace surrounded by large gardens, including the one that became the garden of the Iycée Henri I7. There were thus two royal palaces on the left bank, with immense gardens that largely occupied what are now the 5th and 6th arrondissements of Paris. Childebert (1), son of Clovis, founded the abbey of Saint-Germain-des-Prés around the year 563. When the church of this name was built, he left Clovis' palace to live in the Thermes palace, to be closer to this church, to which he communicated through a gate in his gardens. He also had a residence in Nogent. Ancient historians, including Fortunat, report that this prince and Queen Ultrogoth spent their leisure time (1) Dom Bouilland. D9 cultivating the plants in their gardens with their own hands, which was enough to inspire other powerful figures to imitate them, and thus to make some progress in nascent horticulture. The other Merovingian kings certainly followed these examples, and it was clearly under this initial impetus that the progress of this art was to continue unabated. Charlemagne did not choose Paris as the capital of his vast empire. He did, however, occasionally live in the palace of Clovis, or that of the Thermes. He also wanted to have gardens around the palaces he owned in all the provinces of his empire. This attention to detail greatly encouraged agriculture and horticulture. In his capitularies, he himself indicated to his gardeners the plants they should cultivate, of which there were barely sixty or so. We'll look at the names of these plants in a later chapter. The abbeys (1) at this time were in full prosperity, notably those of Saint-Germain-des-Prés and Saint-Denis; they also had immense gardens run by courtier monks (2), some of whom were given quite important responsibilities. As Edouard André (3) says, "For men who were usually confined to narrow spaces surrounded by walls, garden design was understandably superfluous. The main thing for them was the produce: vegetables and fruit trees... Under feudalism, the same obstacles stood in the way of garden development. The high and mighty barons, confined to their castles with crenellated walls, constantly thinking of attacking their neighbors or defending themselves, had little leisure for the embellishment of their estates. As for the people, crushed under taxes and the omnipotence of their masters, they were condemned to remain for a long time to come in the darkness of ignorance and misery. (1) Félicie d'Ayzac. (2) Derived from "courtil", garden, meaning gardener. (3) Ed. André, l'Art des Jardins. 08 Several centuries passed without the creation of any major gardens in Paris, with the exception of the Pré-aux-Clerc, next to the Louvre. The Louvre, from Lupara (louveterie), was built on the orders of Philippe-Auguste in 1204 (1). It was a fortified tower used as a prison, and was razed under François I to make way for the present palace. During the reign of Charles V (2), Jean Duduy, the king's gardener, planted twelve thousand strawberry plants and seventeen hundred vines, which climbed up on all sides. Jean Baril was a trellis-maker, and arranged the trellises on the rue Beauvoir and rue du Coq sides. A small garden overlooked rue Fromenteau, with a gate through which the King and Queen went when they wanted to go to the country. Charles V, known as the Wise Man, was one of the kings who most encouraged horticulture. He was particularly fond of the Saint-Antoine district, where he had two hôtels built with extensive gardens. In these gardens, there were lots of vegetables and tall fruit trees in the open air, while dwarf trees and espaliers were still unknown (2). All at once, he had one hundred pear trees, one hundred and fifteen apple trees, eleven hundred and twenty-five cherry trees and one hundred and fifty plum trees planted in his Tournelles garden. At the time, there were no flowerbeds other than courtyards, no paths other than trellis-covered cradles, no ornaments other than arbors and green pavilions, and no water other than fish tanks. In the Jardin des Tournelles, there was a labyrinth called the Daedal (3), located at the end of a small wood called the Parc, which later gave its name to the Rue du Parc-Royal. A number of other streets were also named after these gardens, notably rue de la Cerisaie. (1) Ed. André, l'Art des Jardins. (2) Ed. André, Paris à travers les âges. (3) Delamare, Traité de Police. 508 It was under Francis I, during the artistic movement known as the Renaissance, that considerable changes were made to the layout and maintenance of Parisian gardens. At the king's instigation, eminent artists of all kinds were summoned from Italy, where an era of prosperity reigned for the fine arts. The gardens created around the transformed or newly-built palaces were designed according to Italian taste. To appreciate the importance of this movement, we can do no better than refer to Ed. André's admirable treatise on the Art of Gardens. The rebirth of garden design in the late 15th and early 16th centuries was to be found in Florence. Lorenzo de' Medici, nicknamed the Magnificent, protector of Michelangelo and renovator of the arts, wanted superb gardens. His creations have now partly disappeared under the transformations to which the città dei fiori has been subjected, but there are enough traces of them to demonstrate that they were inspired by memories of the villas of ancient Rome. The symmetrical, architectural genre was thus given pride of place. It is even said that the taste for decorating gardens with statues came by chance from Cardinal d'Este, who, having to create gardens on the site of the ancient Villa Adriana, wanted to make use of the numerous statues and ruins he came across there. In 1544 or 1546, in Padua, the Senate of Venice founded the first botanical garden, where two and a half centuries later, Goethe conceived his theory of the metamorphosis of plant organs. Immediately after the Padua garden, the one in Pisa was planted, closely followed by the one in Bologna. Similar establishments in Leiden and Leipsig date from 1577 and 1579 respectively (1). The medical faculties of Paris, several of which were already famous, soon recognized the usefulness of these institutions. The Montpellier botanical garden was created in 1593 by the learned Richer de Belleval, who devoted the most (1) Rome also had a botanical garden in the 16th century, entrusted to the care of Polea and Della Valle. In 1591, Ferdinand I, son of Côma de Médicis, had the first temperate greenhouse built in Pisa (Author's note). 08 large part of his fortune and his life. In 1605, the city of Giessen, in 1620 that of Strasbourg, then Alfort in 1625, Jena in 1629, were the object of similar creations. At that time, Paris still only had the garden planted by the apothecary Robin in 1570, under the name of "jardin des simples", where he taught the science of plants to a few pupils. It wasn't until 1626 that the Royal Garden of Medicinal Plants - now the Natural History Museum - was founded by Guy de la Brosse and became the glorious cradle of the great établisscment exemplified by the Buffons, Lamarcks and Jussieus. Clusius, d'Arras and Mathias de L'Obel had already given a salutary direction to plant description and cultivation: botany had truly been created. The art of gardening was to change considerably with the resources made available to it by Curpean horticulturists and explorers of unknown regions of the earth. Growing security had led to the demolition of many walls and fortresses, and people began to love the open air and the countryside, breathing at last after long centuries of suffocating in dungeons. The king set the example: he repaired Fontainebleau and built Chambord. To achieve this, he had called to his court, at great expense, the leading artists of Italy: Benvenuto Cellini, Andrea del Sarto, Rosso, Primaticcio, Leonardo da Vinci, Vignole, while Pierre Lescot, Philibert Delorme, Jean Goujon and other French architects, painters and sculptors, followed in the footsteps of these geniuses from the other side of the Alps... In the embellishment of its avenues and flowerbeds, Fontainebleau was influenced by the Italian taste, derived from antiquity and applied by Rosso and Primaticcio, decorative artists par excellence (1). Saint-Germain, Madrid near Paris, Blois and Villers-Cotterets received similar embellishments, traces of which have not come down to us, but which were obviously conceived in the same style. Fontainebleau remained in the same state until Henry IV. The Gascon king made a number of modifications (1) The Château de Fontaincbleau had been founded by Robert le Pieux towards the end of the 10th century, rebuilt by Louis VII in the 12th century, then embellished by various reis (Author's note). DQ capitales, and commissioned an Italian engineer, Francini, to execute his plans. The Luxembourg garden in Paris (1) had been "designed" in 1612 by Decamp, master gardener, who laid out the parterres, boulingrins, etc., as appears from a manuscript of Marie de Médicis containing her expense accounts and kept at the Biblothèque nationale. This garden, after the embellishments made by Jacques Desbrosses, was bordered by two terraced walls of unequal height, with flowerbeds, fountains, basins and gutters, yew and boxwood. Part of this parterre still exists near the palace. The Italian style thus dominated, albeit with a few modifications, in the embellishment of Paris gardens until the reign of Louis XIV, when the first French-style gardens were created. This style is due to André Le Nôtre, whose genius began to assert itself with the layout of the gardens at Château de Vaux, and whose glory is to have designed those at Versailles. This immense park has sometimes been criticized for being too monotonous and severe in style, but no one would deny that its majestic perspectives were in keeping with the monumental lines of the château it accompanied. Versailles remains, moreover, the most grandiose and perfect model of the French genre. Le Nôtre designed the garden at Sceaux, on land purchased by Colbert (2). He completed the Tuileries garden, which had been started in 1564 by Catherine de Médicis, then enlarged under Henri IV and Louis XIII. He also transformed those at Saint-Germain and Chantilly. (1) Founded on the site of the famous Chartreux nursery, which flourished in the 17th century under the direction of P. François (Albert Lenoir, Architecture monastique). Luxembourg also had some fine nurseries, but lost them in 1867. There is still, however, a model fruit tree nursery, where courses in fruit arboriculture are given free of charge. This public garden is the only one reminiscent of the old royal gardens, where fruit trees were planted and vegetables grown (Author's note). (2) Jules Janin, Marie Aycard, etc., Les Environs de Paris. 08 Le Nôtre's emulators tried to continue his traditions. Le Blond created Péterhof, in St. Petersburg, for Catherine the Great of Russia. Duizé designed the park at Marly. Desgodets planted the Château de Bagnolet. The Mansard brothers used the French style in all their creations. "But excess, which corrupts the best things, does not wait," says Edouard André (1). "The pruned trees and shrubs praised by Olivier de Serres at the beginning of the seventeenth century had degenerated into ridiculous fantasies. In the garden of the Abbé des Dunes, there were standing men-at-arms trimmed in boxwood. The Abbé de Clairmarais, in Saint-Omer, was surrounded by a flock of turkeys, geese and cranes, in yew and rosemary. At Chambaudoise, in the Beauce region, a whole collection of musical instruments had been carved into the green trees, planted in labyrinths. These excesses were to provoke a reaction in the opposite direction, and it was from this reaction that landscape gardens, also known as "English" gardens, were born. Indeed, William Kent has long been credited with creating this genre. Kent had begun Earl Gainsborough's gardens at Exton-Park in the regular style, but after a trip to the Continent, he finished them in the landscape style. On the contrary, the first landscape gardens were created in France, by Dufresny. This landscape architect, who had started out as a valet de chambre, asserted his ideas by planting the Parc de Mignaux, near Poissy, the gardens of Abbé Pajot, in Vincennes, and those of Chemin Creux and Moulin, in the Faubourg Saint-Antoine. "From a regular and flat site, he created a mountainous one, in order to vary the objects by multiplying them, and to guarantee neighboring views, by opposing them with elevations of earth that served at the same time as belvederes (2). (1) Ed. André, l'Art des Jardins. (2) Dufresny, Œuvres complètes. 200 In 1777, the Marquis de Girardin set out the fundamental principles of landscape style in a booklet. These principles were followed in the planting of Bagatelle, in 1779, by the Comte d'Artois, in the then Parc Monceau, designed by Carmontelle, and in the Petit Trianon, where Marie-Antoinette had her pleasures. During the last periods of the Revolution and the First Empire, the gardens were cluttered with statues, temples and symbolic monuments reminiscent of Greek and Roman mythology and history. The landscape style would also have fallen into disuse had it not been for Gabriel Thouin, who published the results of his work in 1819 (1). His finest creation was the SaintQuen park. From that time until the Second Empire, no major changes were made. With the exception of the Tuileries and Luxembourg, perfected according to Le Nôtre's principles, France's capital had few public gardens worthy of the city. It was at the beginning of the reign of Napoleon III that the Bois de Boulogne was transformed from top to bottom, first under the skilful direction of M. Varé, garden designer and contractor, then under that of the Service de la Voie Publique et des Promenades, which the Prefect of the Seine, M. Ilaussmann, had just set up by placing M. Alphand (2) at its head. It was at this time that the first municipal public gardens were created, which were later renamed squares. Those at Tour Saint-Jacques, Marclié des Innocepts, Place des Arts-et-Métiers, Place du Temple and Place de Sainte-Clotilde were the first to be planted. A little later, the Champs-Élysées were converted into magnificent promenades, adorned with beautiful clumps of trees and flowers. Parc Monceau was cleared of its (1) Thouin, Plans raisonnés de jardins. (2) Alphand may be called the greatest gardener of the xiXth century (Author's note). undergrowth, and its new style became the model of the luxurious genre. The Bois de Vincennes was then improved in the same way as the Bois de Boulogne. Finally, in 1864, work began on the "Carrières d'Amérique" and the "Buttes Chaumont", a haven for the disenfranchised in the center of one of Paris's most populous districts, and one of the most remarkable examples of a garden created from scratch on very uneven ground. The Buttes Chaumont park was completed in 1867. In the final years of the Second Empire, the Parc de Montsouris was designed and completed in 1877. This park covers almost 16 hectares. It was designed with a grandeur of views that make it one of the most beautiful landscape creations of modern times. Today, the number of public parks and gardens in Paris totals around one hundred; new squares are being added all the time, as and when the beautification of Paris requires them, and for the greater good of public hygiene. THE ESTIMATE OF THE OLD AND THE GREAT FOR GARDENERS The taste that civilized peoples profess for gardens makes them show a certain deference for skilled gardeners (1), and especially for those who create something new: using plants that previously lived in the wild and that nobody had thought of taking advantage of; improving other plants already cultivated, through selection, cross-breeding and grafting, which the Phoenicians were the first to practice. This people, who appeared on the Syrian coast from the earliest times in history, achieved a high degree of civilization; the practice of this art of fruit improvement is one of the many proofs of this. This is also sufficient to indicate that their other horticultural knowledge was on a par with this. They passed on the art of grafting to the Carthaginians and Greeks. The Romans received it from the latter, and spread it to the various peoples of Europe. Among these peoples, the art was at first considered more as a curiosity than as something useful, but it was not lost. Indeed, in the Middle Ages, to be accepted as a gardener in a good (1) Louis Noisette. Jigitized maison (1), it was necessary to know how to graft in slit and in escutcheon on trees and wildlings, such as pear trees, cognac trees, etc. It was only much later that the art of grafting was acquired. It was not until much later that La Quintinie brought grafting back into practical use, and it began to render real services in fruit improvement. Plato placed the art of the gardener between that of the physician and that of the geometer, and many princes indulged in it because it was highly regarded. Alexander the Great, whose conquests made him master of Asic, held the profession in such high esteem that he rewarded his gardener Abarthomius with the kingdom of Sydon (2). When Alexander heard that the king of Paphos had made himself unworthy of the crown, he asked if there was anyone left of the royal family; he was told that there was one named Alynomus, living from a garden he was cultivating. Alexander sent for him, and he was found in his garden, drawing water to water his plants. The king dressed him in purple and restored him to the throne of his ancestors. In the splendid days of the Roman Empire, one of the branches of the Valerian family, which had given masters to the empire, took the name of Lactucians, for having raised in its gardens a singular species of lettuce, more excellent than the others (3), and producing in greater abundance. This is probably Roman lettuce, which was not introduced into France until the reign of Charles V (4), brought from Avignon by Bureau de la Rivière. As we have seen, the Frankish kings spent all their leisure time in the art of gardening. Not until Charlemagne, one of the world's greatest conquerors, was he particularly concerned with the cultivation of his gardens. (1) Maison réglée, by Audiger. (2) Delamare, Traité de Police. (3) Delamare, Traité de Police. (4) Ménagier de Paris. niers, at that time, probably because the princes of the day wanted to keep all the honor for themselves. However, the taste they had for the upkeep of their gardens leads us to suppose that they were helped by obscure and modest gardeners with some experience in their art. Many centuries went by, and no great progress was made in the art of gardening in general. It wasn't until the fourteenth century, under Charles V, that this art, under the inspiration of the king, took off in a way that never stopped. From this period onwards, we'll find a few names of gardeners who have gone down in history. Charles V's gardener was called Philippot Persant, and his salary, which at the time seemed considerable, was sixty écus a year (1). The first names of gardeners we come across are, along with the one just mentioned, those of Jean Duduy, gardener to King Charles V at the Louvre palace, and Jean Baril, "trellis maker". The latter was undoubtedly involved in all aspects of arboriculture. Towards the end of the sixteenth century, a name that played a major role in French gardening appeared for the first time, that of the Mollets. "The Duc d'Aumale's gardener at Anet, Normandy, was the first of this small horticultural dynasty. His master held him in high esteem. Towards the end of the sixteenth century, the gardens of Anet were considered the most beautiful in France, thanks to their quality and the number of flowers grown. The second gardener, Claude Mollet, was trained by Anet in the practice of gardening, and was subsequently appointed gardener to King IIenri IV. In France, he created parterres à broderie, and in a rather naive book he wrote in 1614 (2), he published drawings of those he had executed (1) Ed. André, l'Art des Jardins. (2) C. Mollet, Traité des Plans et Jardinages. C first in Fontainebleau, then in Saint-Germain-en-Laye, in 1595. His three sons, whom he seems to have instructed more than himself, helped him realize his ideas (1). This man must have been a true practicing gardener, as his treatise indicates that with him, there was no theory, but only practice and an already well-developed knowledge of arboriculture and vegetable growing. Less educated than his brilliant successors, he seems to us more modest and more obscure, even though he knew his trade inside out. He undoubtedly lacked only the happiness of being placed at the head of a great undertaking, such as the creation of the gardens of Versailles, which came after him. Nevertheless, we rank Claude Mollet among the great gardeners who have gone before us. One of Claude Mollet's sons, André, succeeded him as intendant of the King of France's gardens under Louis XIII. Boyceau de la Baraudière, author of a Traité de jardinage, was asked to continue his work in the same capacity after his death. During the reign of Louis XIV, horticulture was honored in the person of André Le Nôtre, born in Paris in 1613, died in 1700. Le Nôtre succeeded his father as superintendent of the king's gardens. Having designed the gardens of the Château de Vaux for the minister Fouquet, he was called upon by the king to draw up plans for Versailles. Following his remarkable work, he was showered with dignities and honors. Louis XIV ennobled him in 1665. In 1678, Pope Innocent XI called on him for advice. "He had spread the love of gardens everywhere. Saint-Simon recounts many witty anecdotes about him. More than once, Boileau asked him for advice on his garden at Auteuil; Lamoignon took him to Baville; Bossuet himself chatted happily with him, and for a moment fell in love with (2) Ed. André, l'Art des Jardins. plants. It's surprising to think that the gardener to the great bishop of Meaux once said to his master: "If I planted Saint Augustine and Saint Chrysostom, you'd come and see them, but you don't care much about your trees (1)". Le Nôtre, who elevated gardening, not only by his talents, but by the charm of his relations and the distinctiveness of his person, has been called by the English author, Loudon, "the greatest French gardener who ever lived". Another distinguished gardener who shared with Le Nôtre, during the reign of "the Sun King", the favor of frequenting the Court as "conseiller-directeur des jardins des maisons royales", was La Quintinie, born in Angoumois in 1626, died in 1688. La Quintinie first studied to be a lawyer, then agriculture and gardening in Italy; he was then called upon by Louis XIV to create the vegetable and fruit gardens of Versailles, Chantilly, Rambouillet and many others. La Quintinic borrowed some of his techniques from Claude Mollet. He left a work on his art that was not published until after his death, in 1746. In this work (2), he is not afraid to say himself that he drew many examples from the "Parisian mareschais" (he is the first to refer to gardeners by this name). Having surrounded himself with competent men, he made rapid progress in vegetable growing. Agriculture," says La Quintinie, "is a truly noble art, and one that is even capable of giving nobility to those who make it their profession; so it is true that they are usually delighted that everyone sees their work; and when they happen to meet with success, their greatest joy is to declare to those who want to know, the (1) Ed. André, l'Art des Jardins. (2) La Quintinie, Instruction pour les jardins fruitiers et potagers. 4 means they have used to succeed. Instead of the common spirit of other workers, which is to make a mystery of everything, and to keep to themselves the enlightenment they have acquired in their art". These scholars, by virtue of the position they occupied, had no need to count, and rendered great services in all respects to the art of gardening. They were the most illustrious men of our corporation. After them came Gondouin, gardener to Louis XV, who followed in La Quintinic's footsteps, taking advantage of the progress he had made. But alongside these great names who have come down to us, how many generations of gardeners have remained obscure, working on their own account and who can be called public gardeners, since they supplied the markets with vegetables and fruit. Among these men who did not have the means to popularize their trials and improvements in cultivation. There were certainly some remarkable men who did not enjoy the glory of their innovations, which others, better placed to make a name for themselves, were able to take advantage of. This is the story of many inventors and innovators; simple, intelligent workers, finding and composing in their minds something new in the art they professed; but because of their situation, unable to put their ideas into practice, other more powerful, wealthier men seized upon them and became for posterity and history the true inventors, whereas they really only had to collect and popularize the ideas put forward by those more unfortunate than themselves. Sully, Henri IV's first minister, once said that "grazing and ploughing were the two breasts of France". In his time, gardening was not yet an industry important enough to be cited as a national asset. It was around this time that gardening began to flourish, and became completely detached from agriculture, taking the name of hortiD0 culture, as it did for the ancient Romans. As early as 1600, Olivier de Serres pointed out that the gardener was "the goldsmith of the earth", meaning that there was as much difference between a gardener and a farmer as there is between a blacksmith and a goldsmith. Horticulture was to grow rapidly, especially in the vicinity of large cities. There are several reasons for this: firstly, the flow of produce, especially at a time when means of locomotion were slow; secondly, the ease of obtaining fertilizers and transporting them on site, as cheaply as possible; thirdly, as men were more numerous and closer together, they more easily exchanged their ideas, theories, progress, etc. The selection of plants took place in these areas. The selection of plants took place more rapidly than in the countryside, where intellectual centers are more or less distant from one another. Paris, France's capital and most populous city, was no more backward in this industry than in others. Later on, we'll meet some of the eminent men who were involved in this art and helped it make giant strides. The Parisian horticulture of our century was well defined by our eminent geographer Elisée Reclus, in his Géographie L'n'universelle. He said: "C'est l'article Paris-agricole". We read in previous chapters that the term "mareschais" was first used by La Quintinie (I), and we found the word "courtilier" to refer to the monk-gardeners of the Middle Ages. In Old French, the garden was called a courtil, a word still used in Brittany. It's also the origin of the word courtilière, which refers to the insect that often wreaks havoc in market gardens. The Parisii had embraced Christianity around the middle of the third century, following the preaching of Saint-Denis and his companions, who were spreading the Gospel. In 360, a council was convened in Lutetia; it was on this occasion that it was first called Parisea Civitas, "City of the Parisians", and it was from the use of this locution that the word Paris took root. In 451, a young Christian woman, Sainte-Geneviève, the Virgin of Nanterre, saved Paris from the wrath of the invader Attila, king of the Huns. A few years later, it was she again who saved the capital from the resentment of Clovis, whose wife. Clotilde, niece of Gondebaud, king of the Burgundians (2), was already a Christian. Chief of the Frankish people, Clovis, catechized (1) See page 32. (2) Burgundians. by Saint-Remi, bishop of Reims, converted to Christianity after the battle of Tolbiac, which he won over the Alamanni. Following this conversion, the Parisians submitted to Clovis, who made Paris the capital of his kingdom. Even then, he said that the crown of the kingdom of France was worth a baptism, just as Henri IV would later say that Paris was worth a mass. It was after these memorable events that the first Parisian courtiers were established. Before Clovis built the church of Saint-Germain-l'Auxerrois, a village soon sprang up around it. Between this village and the city, vast marshlands stretched to the foot of the Montmartre hill. This marshy area was known in ancient Latin titles (1) as Lampella, meaning "small fields". It was part of this land that the early kings granted to establish the Paris cemetery, known today as the Cimetière des Innocents (the name came later from the fact that the victims of St. Bartholomew's Day were buried there). The cattle market was also established here, and later became the Halles Centrales de la Capitalc. Around the cemetery and market, the most marshy areas were transformed into courtyards. This is where we find the first courtiliers worthy of the name. These marshes were called the Valley of Misery. There's no doubt that this name comes from the misery our unfortunate ancestors had to endure to dry out, clear and cultivate these sad lands. In the end, it was in the village of Saint-Germain-l'Auxerrois that the first courtiliers settled, and long afterwards they formed the community of Maîtres-Jardiniers, whose grandsons have become the Master Gardeners of our time. (1) Delamare, Traité de Police. 99 When exactly did they begin to desiccate these lands? It's difficult to say, but if we put all the facts we've just cited together, it was certainly during the reigns of the Frankish kings who inhabited Paris. How many generations were born, toiled and died in these swamps? A very large number, since at the beginning of the fourteenth century (1), we find them in rue Merderel, near the Châtelet, at the Porte Montmartre, rue du Roi-de-Sezile, rue des Billettes, and so on. In any case, these early courtiers dried up the land for the construction of Paris's richest neighborhoods. As the city continued to grow, they retreated, starting again on other lands the work their ancestors had done on the first. They prepare the construction of the suburbs while supplying the city with all kinds of vegetables. It was their fate to work constantly on rehabilitating the land, to move backwards as the city advanced, and to start again on other plots of land, with no hope of ever benefiting from the added value they had given them; until the Revolution, which enabled them to become owners of the land they were cultivating, and thereby benefit from the increase in value that the expansion of Paris gave to the land around it. By following the expansion of the capital at the different periods when it took place, it will be easy to follow the gardeners in their peregrinations; this will be the means we will employ in this work, to achieve this goal and faithfully retrace the suburbs where they stayed, as well as the periods of their different retreats. (1) Guérard, Documents inédits sur l'Histoire de France. 208 Bguredp, COOgl0 LES DÉVELOPPEMENTS SUCCESSIFS du jardinage parisien AU MOYEN-AGE DEtiEed S GOO6 C Doibsd s (Oogle DES CITOYENS (1) Rien de plus divers, de plus discordant, de plus hétérogène que les populations, les états, les intérêts, les institutions, que la société présenteait en France, pendant les quatre premiers siècles de la monarchie. First, there were the conquerors and the conquered. The Franks, who came in great numbers during the Roman domination, had first established themselves as colonists, before becoming conquerors. Even the Merovingian kings ruled, or rather dominated, not so much the Gaulish country as the armed bands that roamed it, plundering friend and foe alike. In addition to the different races, there were free men, colonists and serfs. There were even several degrees of freedom and servitude. By studying these various categories of citizens, we can find out to which of them the ancestors of the market gardeners belonged. Inequality was also reproduced in terms of land: land was free, dependent or in servitude, and made up alleus, benefices or tenures. What's more, they had their own particular customs and uses, depending on the master and the country. So everywhere there was diversity and inequality, nothing was settled or definitive, there was struggle and war (1) L'Abbé Irminon. everywhere. Nothing presented a principle of life, order or duration: they were all elements of barbarism. The Franks were not very virtuous; likewise, the Gauls they subjugated were a bastardized people. Conquerors and conquered, all were in decadence. The political state, the moral state, the intellectual state, all remained the same from Clovis until the extinction of his descendants on the throne. The institution of the new religion alone took on great proportions and soon became the master of the country. There were then four categories of men: free men, colonists, leudes or vassals and serfs. Freedom diminished, and eventually the last three categories merged. The servitude of the first of the three was military; that of the second, landed, and that of the third, personal. War service was not dú by those who owned neither land nor serfs, and free men sometimes gave up their property to free themselves from this service. The free man was alienated with the land he inhabited, and passed under the domination of the new owner along with the serfs and their families. The leude, though dependent on a master, had far more freedom than the colonist or serf. He was generally attached to the lord when the latter was under arms, which frequently happened in these troubled times. The condition of colonists and serfs is the subject of the following two chapters. The colonist was a farmer attached to the land he cultivated, paying a certain number of royalties to the owner. Among these royalties was the dime. Tithing of the produce of the land had its origins in certain religious customs of ancient peoples. The Jews paid tithes to the Levites. Under the Merovingians, it was collected by the conquerors who became owners of the land, and it was only later, under Charlemagne, that it was reserved for the clergy. The dime consisted in the levying of a tenth of the products of cultivation, industry or commerce. It was paid either in kind or in money (1). The colonist was half free, half slave: 1° he contracted a true marriage; 2° he paid public taxes; 3° he could own property, but was not allowed to alienate it. As a slave: 1° he was not the owner of the colonial soil; 2° he was presumed to have a master or a patron; 3° what the colonist owned in his own right was called his "pécule" and could not be alienated without the consent of his master or patron; 4° the colonist was sold with the land he occupied; 5° the law declared him incapable of attaining honors, entering municipal offices or performing war service. He needed (1) B. Guérard (translation by Abbé Irminou). his master's approval to enter the clerkship, and if he did, it was on condition that he farm the land to which he was attached; 6° the colonist was included with the enclaves in the description of the land; 7° he could only bring an action against his master in three cases: when it concerned his origin, his condition, or the ownership of the land; when he was burdened with surtaxes; finally when the cause was criminal; 8° the fugitive colonist, like the slave, was deemed a thief in his own right; the master had the right to put him in irons and reduce him to the status of a slave; 9° personal punishments were, in certain cases, inflicted on colonists as on slaves, according to the brutality of the master. The colonial system, like most Roman institutions, fell into disrepair under the domination of barbarian peoples. What distinguished the Roman colonial system from that of the Middle Ages was that, under the emperors, the colonist was subject only to royalties owed to the master, whereas under the Frankish kings, the colonist descended to the rank of the unfree, subject to corporal services, later known as "corpées". These corporal services were, however, less severe than those imposed on serfs. To perform them, they owed their masters an average of three days a week, without pay. In other words, they worked half the time for free; part of the other half was taken from them by the dime and other duties at the master's discretion. Settlers were mainly established on church and monastery estates. Colonial land generally consisted of a manse (1), (we'll see later what the surface area of a manse was, often half a manse). The total fee for a manse could be (1) Manse, from the Bas-Latin mansia, itself derived from manere, to live on, to house. A measure of land deemed necessary to support a man and his family. The word mansia also gave rise to the word maïson. 99 be valued at a sum varying between two and three hundred francs of our current currency. It was not unusual for a single manse to be run by three or four settler households. In the vicinity of cities such as Paris, this was a very common occurrence. In fact, these settlers were able to engage in more remunerative farming than their fellow citizens living in the distant countryside, and didn't need as much land to support their families. In most cases, the manses they cultivated were described as ingenuous, livid or servile. We'll look at the difference between these labels and the degree of freedom they afforded the colonists later on. Although colonization was a regular state, the colonists were nonetheless subject to the common law of the time: the law of the strongest. As a result, they were often pillaged and robbed by powerful men, especially the ofliciers of royal lands. They themselves became plunderers when they felt strong enough. In the tenth century, many settlers became true landowners. From then on, royalties and services took on a different form, no longer representing the price of tenant farming, nor the charges of usufruct. They were feudal duties paid to the lords by the inhabitants of their fiefs. Villains are no longer challenged for ownership of their fields. If he has to fight, it's only to try to become a free man, by conquering his rights as a citizen one by one. THE CONDITION OF THE SERfs AND THEIR FIGHTING FOR FREEDOM Servitude has always been a social leprosy, from which many generations of men have suffered among ancient peoples, and even among modern ones, since it was not until the Revolution of 1848 that slavery was abolished in the French colonies. However, in our country, slavery has always been on the wane, officially speaking, for if the powerful, as civilization progressed, were obliged to let go on one side, they knew how to make up for it on the other to keep men in a more or less disguised state of servitude. After Caesar's conquest of Gaul, there was slavery, which reduced man to the status of a thing, to the level of a domestic animal. The Romans took their slaves to fairs, where they were sold like cattle. There were even, as today with horses, redhibitory cases, which had to be indicated at the time of sale. It was this terrible and unfortunate state of affairs that enabled Christianity to make rapid progress among the Gauls, a people so eager for freedom. The first evangelists from Asia Minor were persecuted by Roman and pagan jurisconsults. One of them, Ulspien, had written a treatise on the duties of a proconsul, outlining the punishments to be meted out to Christians. "It was not just a religious revolution, but a political and social one. In order to establish itself, Christianity had to overcome all kinds of enemies: governments, peoples, priests, pagan laymen, civil power as well as religious power, laws as well as morals (1)". These early Christians lived in community. They hid deep in the forests to celebrate the mysteries of their religion. Charity was their first law. A mass of slaves flocked around them, accepting their principles. All these newcomers in turn formed lay convents, where celibacy was not yet imposed. Accumulated suffering, spoliations and the rapacity of wealthy pagans had thrown the new converts into despair, but at one point, feeling strong enough to make their persecutors tremble, they took the offensive and organized bands under the name of bagaudes (2). They appointed leaders from among themselves. One of the main leaders was a woman: Victoria-Augusta, nicknamed the "Mother of Armies". The Bagaudes ransacked Roman villas and laid siege to cities. Around 270, they destroyed Antun. The insurrection became formidable in 284, with the accession of Diocletian, but his two generals, Amandus and Alianus, after ten years of heroic struggle, were crushed by Maximian at the confluence of the Seine and Marne rivers, at a place long known as Fosses aux Bagaudes, today Saint-Maur-les-Fossés. Later, there were further attempts at bagauderie, the last of which was destroyed by Aetius around 440. With the advent of Clovis, after his conversion to Christianity, (1) Guizot, Histoire de la civilisation en France. (2) From the Celtic bagad, insurgent. C tianism, slavery itself was replaced by serfdom. The hope of freedom and property may then have penetrated through some crack in the serf's hut. Nevertheless, this unfortunate still had, so to speak, neither city nor family before the law. Under feudalism, the serf freed himself and his property from the domination of his lord, no longer owing him everything, body and goods, but only a portion of his labor and income. The history of France was subsequently marked by the continual struggles of the enslaved against the dominators. Fifteen centuries later, this struggle culminated in the Revolution of 1789, which proclaimed man's individual freedom and equality before the law. But in these long stages of slavery or servitude through the centuries, there cu a great many kinds of serfs. There were : 1° those who were originally serfs, and those who, born free, had lost their freedom; 2° public slaves who belonged to the state, i.e. the king, churches or monasteries, and private slaves belonging to individuals; 3° urban slaves employed in the master's house, or in town, and rural slaves who worked in the fields, but who should not be confused with agricultural serfs; 4° agricultural serfs, attached to the cultivation of the land rather than to the personal service of their masters, were the same as those referred to in the third century by the Romans as servi tributarii. Their servitude was almost entirely real, i.e. restricted to the service of the soil, and their masters could dispose of them by transporting them from one domainc to another. After this rapid examination of the situation of men in this remote period, it is nevertheless easy to recognize to which class the first Parisian courtiers belonged. Established on an estate belonging to the king, they were half-free, half-slaves in the first centuries of church domination. Did their lot improve? This will be examined later in this book. The dogma of equality taught by Christianity, and the example of the first propagators of the faith, many of whom came from the lowest classes of society, gave reason to hope that the new religion would succeed in establishing the equality so eagerly sought by the underprivileged (1). As we have seen, slaves were the first to convert to Christianity. In the early centuries of the Church, it was customary to elevate men who had fallen into servitude to ecclesiastical positions. By the eighth century, however, there were so many serfs in the orders that the church resorted to injunctions to the contrary, prompting Michelet to say: "That the wooden cross would have saved men, whereas the golden cross adorned with diamonds has plunged them into the most odious servitude". In the Middle Ages, with the decadence of the Carlovingian empire, servitude no longer had any limits; it was more or less complete, depending on the temperament of the lords and great dignitaries of the church. The serf may no longer have been sold at fairs, as was the case with the Romans, but he belonged to the fiefdom to which he was attached and followed its fate. In other seigneuries, where the men were closer to the big cities, the lord was obliged to allow them more freedom. In the first centuries of the Church, as we said, the colonists were half free, half slaves; during feudalism they were reduced to the rank of serfs. However, century after century, serfs' revolts against their lords became more frequent and harder to suppress. Ideas of emancipation germinated especially in cities and towns. The secular and ecclesiastical lords, forced to make a few land concessions, and to allow these men a little more freedom, with the secret hope of attaching themselves to them even more, (1) L'Abbé Irminon. 908 could not prevent some communes from setting themselves up and freeing themselves from their despotism, even demanding charters and privileges. The emancipation of the communes is the greatest political event in our history (1), of which all the others were only the consecration and consequence. This act was purely revolutionary; the generosity of kings, bishops and lords had nothing to do with it. After some communes had drawn up their emancipation charters, some lords, unwilling to give them away and no longer daring to refuse them, decided to sell them. These charters were abrégements of fiefs, and were only valid as long as they had been confirmed by the king. It was Louis le Gros who was the first, towards the beginning of the twelfth century, to confirm them with the aim of weakening his vassals, while getting paid handsomely for his intervention. He would later go back on his word when the bishops and lords offered him a more considerable sum. When, in the eleventh century, the people began to count themselves and asked to be counted for something, there was not enough contempt at first, and then rage, to welcome the audacious claims of these serfs who wanted to be free. These enemies of the society of the time were running headlong into overthrowing religion, by enclosing the power of the bishop in the church, and the monks in the cloister; the family, in order to be able to dispose of themselves, as they wished, and of the fruits of their union; property, in order to found individual ownership, by guaranteeing their children their inheritance; and by ceasing to be, themselves, the property of others. All this to introduce a new element, a new order, into the enlarged society: the hitherto unknown Third Estate. Slavery largely disappeared in towns at this time, along with arbitrary pruning, corvée, mortaille and so on. The bourgeois became master of himself and his property, (1) Guizot, Histoire de France. he could give, sell and alienate during his lifetime or after his death; ceasing to be justiciable high and low, he could no longer be distracted from his jurisdiction, provided he gave good and sufficient security. This period was therefore the birth of the bourgeoisie, and these serfs of yesterday perhaps retained too much of the ruthless egoism of the parvenus for anything outside their association. But a careful examination of the state of society suggests that it could hardly have been otherwise. Although some authors claim that there were a few commercial rather than working-class communities before the communal movement, it's safe to say that the organization of the world of work in most large cities followed this communal movement. Fortunately for people, freedom is contagious, and the emancipation of large cities was the starting point. Workers belonging to the same trade wanted to take their place in the sun, but as they could not perform this act individually, they performed it as a community. The Romans had introduced the workers' guild (1), but this organization, like so many others, collapsed with Gallo-Roman society. Following the invasion of the Franks, some tradition of these guilds probably remained in the customs of the conquered countries, but what is most certain is that most, if not all, tradesmen were reduced to a state of servitude. Industrial and artistic work had lost none of its precious traditions, but the social role of the worker was nowhere to be seen. Courts, abbeys and castles had vast workshops, where serfs made clothes for their lords. With this way of doing things, how many artists in all professions have gone unrecognized! (1) René de Lespinasse. 909 WORKER COMMUNITIES It's hardly until the reign of Philip-Augustus that we find a few texts of worker community statutes. However, these successive concessions still offer only isolated regulations, lacking in coherence and extremely difficult to apply. The merchant and working classes suffered greatly from having no authentic laws to defend their interests, which were always compromised by taxes levied unevenly and most often arbitrarily. Philippe-Auguste had considerably enlarged Paris. The people felt stronger, and a few guilds, notably the butchers, talemeliers (bakers) and fripiers, had some of their prerogatives homologated, but these were not yet statutes. In those days, merchants had no means of obtaining protection from the municipal administration. The office of Provost of Paris was given on firm terms, and to the highest bidder. With such a procedure, it was bound to fall into unworthy hands; the provost compensated himself handsomely by taking from the world of work through exactions, rapine and iniquity. As a result, the entire mastery belonged to the dignitaries of the crown; it was a kind of fiefdom attached to the office held by the lord. The thirteenth century brought welcome improvements to this state of affairs. It may be called the great century of the Middle Ages, for it was a new era for the world of work, especially in the cities. The countryside benefited less from it, although there must have been a certain softening in its lot. We'll deal here only with gardeners, the "peasants of Paris", whose existence is hardly any different from that of their counterparts further from the capital, except that they are even more miserable than the latter, due to the higher cost of living. Around 1254, Louis IX changed the system for appointing the Provost of Paris. The office was no longer sold, but given to a man trusted by the king. Étienne Boileau's appointment was the consequence of a system of reforms applied by the king throughout his kingdom. It must have been an astonishing spectacle for this royal provost to bring order to the world of the trades, calling upon the large working-class family, represented by its jurors, whom he questioned about the traditions of their industry, consulting the few existing communities in the person of their syndics, who informed him of the few privileges they had acquired, and asking everyone for the right to work under the protection of the law.
