The Paris trades understood that it was better to be among the prolectors than among the protected. That's why they took their place in the feudal society of the time. This regulation has had its day. Unless we are narrow-minded, we cannot separate its application from the improvements that were then taking place in religious, political and social institutions. The wise Louis IX, canonized by the Church as Saint Louis, and his Provost, the illustrious Étienne Boileau, are therefore credited with the initiative to regulate the trades. Boileau could be called the protector of workers in the Middle Ages. He did not draw up the titles; the communities each drew up their own statutes and presented them to him508 for approval; he confined himself to deleting anything that might be contrary to the law, and to keeping the final statutes in the archives. Each community drew up its statutes as it saw fit. Some had more than fifty articles, while others had only five or six. Each trade transcribed the regulations in use in its community, but the man of progress that was Etienne Boileau, gave them such a perfectible character, that he was allowed to make the modifications demanded by the times. Thanks to this elasticity, Etienne Boileau's statutes remained for five centuries the code of laws, the arbiter of disputes between employers and workers, and earned them the name of the Golden Book of Trades. Around one hundred and twenty state bodies had their statutes approved. Courtiers were not among them. It did exist, however, with its own customs, but its members were few in number, uneducated and unfortunate; not a single man at the time could have taken the initiative to have his guild officially recognized. It was only much later that the guild accomplished this act, and when we arrive at this moment, we will salute the memory of the signatories who officially brought our trade to the number of Parisian guilds. The bodies thus constituted, with their statutes approved by the king, became collective seigneuries, when they had paid their dues to the king, or to the bishop, when they had paid in labor, in money in "guet" (1) all that feudal organization required of them; their situation, from the point of view of law, being the same as that of the nobility, supplying its men-at-arms, and that of the church granting the tribute of its prayers. In good standing with the king, his (1) From the German word Whata, to watch. Urban police force whose contingents were supplied by the inhabitants. C provost and his officers, as well as with the church, they stood proudly in their fiefdoms. No one would have dared to "cut" them arbitrarily; no one would have dared to molest with impunity a master, a valet, even a simple apprentice who had joined the workers' community. Individual freedom would then have had no effect; it would not have given independence, let alone security, since the laws took no account of private individuals, but only of constituted groups. The independent man could lay claim to no one, and was therefore dependent on all. On entering the community through the apprenticeship door, the young worker was faced with duties of various kinds, but also with rights, i.e. customs with the force of law; this was his worker's book. It's true that he was subject to the master's authority by virtue of his contract, but he benefited from his master's advice and that of the other workers, which provided him with serious compensation. As a member of a working-class community with its own code, he felt supported and defended, and indeed he was. Just as a man of the cloth is supported by his bishop, a man of the law by the Parliament and a cleric by the University, so the tradesman, supported by his employer, could appear before the corporation's jurors, then before the provost of Paris and the crown's grand officers; finally, in serious cases, he could go all the way up to the king. Urban workers had carved out a place for themselves in the feudal world, and could complain loudly and defend themselves against the enterprises of their enemies, opposing them with the industrial charter, the livre des métiers. Was the same true for field workers? The difference in their situation was profound; these unfortunate people, isolated on the surface of the territory, with no right to leave their fiefdoms, lived in close dependence on their lords, knowing only royalty in all its forms. There was no law, no labor organization to protect them, 008 only the lord's good or bad heart. Revolts were therefore frequent, and to reduce them, the bodies of the villains had to be crushed under the feet of the men of war. These unfortunates often preferred death to the state of servitude in which they were forced to live. Knowing full well that their urban brethren were better off than they were, they were bound to be even more exasperated by their situation. If, on the other hand, the urban workers rebelled, all it took to bring them back into line was to show them the page on which the jurors had written their obligations, alongside their privileges. Free from all exactions, sheltered from arbitrary measures, they had a sense of ownership and a love of home, the preliminaries of all emancipation. Masters owned trades they had bought or inherited from their fathers; workers aspired to mastery and apprentices followed the same path. It was a slow but sure ascent to freedom, given that these men owed the conquest of their independence solely to themselves. Gathered in their "chambres syndicales" (if we can use this term, unknown at the time), they deliberated on matters relating to their trades, with complete freedom, but with respect for existing laws, which form the basis of any civilized society. It was an apprenticeship in public life and a preparation for modern institutions. It was in this way that tradespeople, aware of the perils of isolation and the sterility of individual effort, achieved the greatest amount of freedom that could then be conquered, and the highest degree of influence to which they could aspire, through workers' association. Unfortunately, this institution, like so many others before and after it, was to deviate from its goal. In time, it became an instrument of oppression and a means of taxation. Other institutions, other mores changed our point of view, and directed our aspirations towards a different ideal. 909 Individualism, once a weakness, has become a strength; more equitable legislation has safeguarded the rights that trade statutes alone once protected, and made the collective stronghold, in which labor was forced to confine itself, less necessary. "Labor has left the corporate fortress, and destroyed the advanced works that bore the names of maitrises and jurandes, and defended their approaches (1). Louis XIV's advisors replaced these institutions with superintendencies and academies, i.e. a royal privilege for a popular monopoly. We shall see later that, at this time, the office of juror, formerly freely elected by the assembly of masters, became a lucrative function, bought from the State by private individuals. This state of affairs placed the guilds in debt, and forced them to increase the master's fees and jury visits. In other words, what had been wonderful earlier became an intolerable abuse. Finally, the economists of the late eighteenth century dealt the final blow to the feudal edifice. They abolished workers' communities, so that work could no longer be imprisoned within them, the spirit of the past could no longer hold sway, and individual effort would no longer be forbidden. This revolution, which came at the right time, would have been disastrous in the time of Louis IX, when, as we have seen, civilization was not yet sufficiently advanced to proclaim and effectively defend individual freedom. Five centuries later, it was to bring undeniable and immense progress. But does this mean that freedom was the last word in economics? Is everything in Etienne Boileau's Livre des métiers to be condemned? The answer lies in our own actions and institutions. Barely a century has passed since these institutions, full of abuses caused by the egoism of the powerful, were destroyed, and already we feel the need to reconstitute them in part by (1) René de Lespinasse, le Livre des Métiers. 909 adopting our modern ideas, our needs and our interests - in other words, by the same motives that guided our ancestors in creating them, and also because of new needs. Indeed, trade has taken on an extension that could not have been imagined a century ago. In the past, the trade of the old gardeners was absolutely local, whereas today, export has become the greatest concern of the market-gardening industry. Progress is so rapid today that the same legislation could not govern mankind for centuries; so it is quite possible that everything that seems just and equitable now will seem so in the future, just as what seemed abusive at the dawn of the Revolution had seemed wise to our ancestors. DEtEOOŒ First of all, it may seem to the reader that abbeys are irrelevant to the subject we are dealing with. However, in the course of this work, we shall see that gardeners, up until the Revolution, cultivated mainly the lands of these religious associations, and that monastic crops played a major role in their development.

in the progress of horticulture. With the conquest of the Gauls, the Frankish kings became the owners of a very large part of the land. Initially, they held an immense amount of land in favor of bishops and monasteries. It was on property that they founded the kingship. Later, when they wanted to make land concessions, they had to take from their own share or from property that had devolved to them either through confiscation, or through the death or flight of the Romans. Such was their short-sightedness that, after a few centuries, they were stripped almost completely of their possessions, while waiting to be stripped of royalty itself. As we have already seen, Clovis built a church on the right bank of the Seine, near Paris, and a town soon sprang up there. Childebert founded the abbey of Saint-Germain-des-Prés in the middle of a vast meadow, and endowed it with a large tract of land. These men, united in societies and organized administratively by religious brotherhoods, already represented a respectable force, which later imposed its will on the powerful themselves. It was from this association, which had eminent men and above all administrators (1), that the first application of a kind of cadastre, or classification of land since the domination of the Franks, emerged. Later. Dagobert founded the monastery of Saint-Denis, and gave it an immense amount of land. These monks (which was probably not their title at the time), or rather, for this period, these men of progress, having adopted the rule of Saint-Benoit, which consisted of manual labor and, above all, land clearing, cleared a large part of France, which had become uncultivated following the Roman invasion; the unfortunate Gauls having almost all succumbed to the tyranny of the conquerors. Saint-Benoît, a great man, rightly said that to clear the minds of the barbarians, it was first necessary to clear the land in which they lived, and thereby give them a sense of well-being unknown to them. These great associations, following his example, brought men back to the cultivation of the land, an indispensable quality for any progressive people. All around Paris, other abbeys were founded, less important than the two preceding ones, and perhaps under their dependence, which we don't need to examine. All these abbeys had very large gardens, and as monks subject to the rule of Saint-Benoit had to live on fruit and vegetables grown by their own hands, there is no doubt that it was among them that we find the first masters of arboriculturc and vegetable cultivation. Moreover, two monks in each abbey were entitled to mastery in each of the guilds practiced there (2). (1) Dom Bouillard. (2) Dom Bouillard, Histoire de l'Abbaye de Saint-Germaindes-Prés. 8 Needless to say, the most intelligent and educated were chosen. In this way, they could make progress in their respective arts. Félicie d'Ayzac describes the role played by the head courtier at the Saint-Denis monastery, a role not to be sneezed at. "The courtier (curticularis) is one of the dignitaries-beneficiaries and powers of the monastery. His name derives from the fact that he was in charge of the courtyards and courtils, i.e. the interior gardens belonging to the abbey. It was thanks to the courtier's care : that richness, freshness and a grandiose character strike and charm the eye in these silent gardens; that the hedges and walkways of hornbeam keep their regularity; that the quincunxes remain green until the last days of autumn; that the high shadows of the park are thicker every day and cast a more mysterious shadow on walkers; that the dwarf yew trees lining the flowerbeds and tracing the lines of the boulingrins retain their slender proportions and uniform silhouette; that the delicate shrubs and hothouse flowers, lined up in the courtyards in summer and sheltered in the refectory in winter, retain their brilliance for a long time to come, as well as their perennial fragrances. But the courtier's duties are not limited to this special care: he is also, and above all, responsible for overseeing the kitchen garden, espaliers, orchards, young prunings, vineyards, fields and meadows of the cousture. It is he who directs the cultivation, harvesting and income from many of the abbey's lands within the immediate radius. The courtilier also collected the cens of all the houses on some of the streets in the town of Saint-Denis, known for this reason as rue de la Courtille; that of three other houses in rue de la Fontaine and other properties of the same order. Some of these censes were ingenuous, i.e. consisting of a cash fee; others were 28 servile; the latter consisted of chores, labor, cartage, guard days, palisade work, barnstorming, earthworks, mowing, grape harvesting, brewing or pressing, piping or messaging, cutting and transporting wood, cultivating fields, and so on. After the reformed monks of Saint-Maur took possession of the monastery, the courtille des champs was ceded by them to Dom Etienne Cencier, sous-chantre and sous-cuisinier among the former monks; this monk then ceded it back to the abbey, with its courtyard, gardens, meadows and ditches covering an area of thirteen and a half acres, all of which was leased to Claude Beaucôté, gardener, for a fee of four hundred livres per year. The benefit of the "courtilier", conferred by the Court of Rome, gave this monk the right of medium and low justice in the entire domain of his property. His personal royalties stemmed from the nature of his management: the supply of all vegetables, greens and roots, i.e. cabbages, onions, garlic, pear, spinach, poreaux, turnips and roots, parsley, both for the kitchen and the refectory. He was also in charge of providing food for the Good Friday dinner: "a cargo of pears and spinach picked in the kitchen garden or courtyards, to be prepared with salt and water on that day only". This brief description shows the important role played by the master courtier in abbeys (1). His home was located alongside the treasury, and shared with it the premises of the former court or field house that King Robert owned in the eleventh century within the abbey walls. It was from the old sala that the courtier dated the acquittals of the cens and dimes sent to his suburbs and all over Europe to the tenants of his possessions. One of the leading courtiers in the sixteenth century was Dom (1) Félicie d'Ayzac, Histoire de l'Abbaye de Saint-Denis. O9 Louis de Lectier; under Henri IV, the courtier was Dom Jérôme de Chambellan. The knowledge, experience and skill these men brought to their profession enabled the abbey to produce exquisite products of all kinds. The system of cultivation adopted in ancient times was undoubtedly the one practiced by the first monks. By the time they had established cultivation not only in France, but throughout much of Europe, despite the invasions of the Barbarians, they had made numerous observations on how to improve the soil, as well as grains, vines, fruits and vegetables. For a long time, clearing the land and, above all, cultivating vines was the daily work prescribed to monks in accordance with the rule of Saint-Benoit. These monks also facilitated communications and encouraged industry in a number of ways, notably by perfecting the horse breed. The best vineyards belonged to them, and they already had a certain experience in choosing their land to produce good wines. The Côte-d'Or's main grands crûs were replanted by them. It's true that in the twelfth century, Abbot Suger, superior of the Abbey of Saint-Denis, brought in grape varieties from foreign countries at great expense, and specialist monks studied this crop in particular: the suitable soils, the exposure, one might even say the altitude, where these grape varieties should be planted. to harvest the world's first wines. Plans of monasteries from the first centuries show how vegetable gardens were laid out. They bear a strong resemblance to our own, with the name of each plant written in Latin on the square devoted to it: (cepas), onion; (poiros), leek; (apiumn), celery; (coliandrum), coriander; (anetum), anise; (papaver), poppy; (radices), turnip; (magones), carrot; (betas), pear; (allium), garlic; (ascolomis), shallot; (petrosilium), parsley; C (cerefolium), chervil; (lactuea), lettuce; (sataragea), savory; (pestinoeluis), parsnip; (caulos), cabbage; (getto), poirotte. With the exception of (magones), carrots, all these names are taken from the capitularies of Charlemagne (1). Later, vegetable gardens became more extensive, and in addition to those established within the monasteries, others were created in the surrounding area, also entrusted to the care of the master courtier. The long practice of these specialists taught them to know the most favorable exposures for particular fruit trees, as well as the most suitable soils for their cultivation, and how to improve fruit by grafting. The Carthusian monks had one of the finest fruit gardens in the world. In this garden, they had gathered together a large number of the fruit trees and varieties known at the time.
