(2) L'Abbé Irminon. 7I DE LA PROPRIÉTÉ TERRIENNE In a previous chapter, it was stated that the Romans, like all conquerors, had largely dispossessed the Gauls of their soil. When the Franks, in their turn, became masters of the country, they spared the first conquerors no more than the latter had spared their predecessors. The Frankish kings, in possession of almost the entire territory, were very generous to their vassals and especially to the fledgling associations. The latter took full advantage of the situation. Land under the Franks was granted in usufruct, i.e. in possession without right of ownership. All goods given to vassals by the king were to return to the domain after the death of the donee. This system enabled Charles Martel, for example, to seize the property of the clergy, as Dagobert had already done and as the men of the 1789 revolution would later do. Charles Martel gave this property to his soldiers as a benefit, so that all of a sudden, laymen found themselves in the place of abbots and bishops. During the tenth, eleventh and twelfth centuries, a large number of country churches were owned by laics. But Charlemagne, seeing that it was in his interest, and perhaps at that time also in the interest of civilization and progress, backed his empire on the power of these great associations, and gave them back a large part of their property, of which the Revolution of 1789 was to dispossess them momentarily for a third time. DIFFERENT KINDS OF POSSESSION (1) There were several types, namely: alleu, domain, colony or cencive, benefice, etc. The alleu is the most common type of property. Alleu appears to be nothing other than a patrimony, and was opposed to acquets. In the course of time, the name "alleu" was applied to everything owned outright, whether by inheritance, purchase or donation. Even the tenures of serfs, especially those of fiscaliers, i.e. royal serfs, were transformed into alleux, in the decadence of the Carlovingian dynasty, but most remained under the obligation to pay cens, and were therefore referred to as cencives. Land owned by monks from abbeys was more or less inalienable, and came to be known as mainmorte. In addition to their tenures, a certain number of tenants themselves owned property in the form of alleux, inheritance, propries or acquets, which they could dispose of as they wished. All landed property of a certain size consisted of two distinct parts: one, occupied by the master, constituted the estate proper; the other, distributed among more or less dependent individuals, formed what were known as tenures. The latter remained perpetually subject to the former, with obligations of various kinds. Tenures were divided into two sections, according to the obligations with which they were charged, and were either liberal or servile. In the first case, tenures were noble and owned (1) B. Guérard (translation by Abbé Irminon) by vassals, and were called benefices. The estate was the main part of the land, reserved by the possessor for direct collection of its fruits. Salic lands were noble lands derived from military benefices distributed by Clovis, and later by Charles Martel, to their principal lieutenants from the imperial domain left vacant by the demise of the Romans. According to Frankish law, daughters were excluded from any sharing with sons in the succession of Salic lands. Inferiority given to women, and from which they have not been able to recover until our century. In the second case, the land was despoiled and placed in the hands of colonists and serfs; it then constituted colonies and cencives. The land was divided into lots and distributed among people of low status. These people, by virtue of the lots of which they had usufruct, were subject to regulated obligations known as the law of the land. Usufruct was granted in several ways: perpetually and hereditarily, for life, for a term or at the master's discretion. Thus, the tenure of the colonist was hereditary and irrevocable; whereas the serf did not legally enjoy this property. From this account of land ownership, we can see that men had a greater or lesser degree of freedom, depending on the donation they possessed. Nothing was definitive, there was no stability, and each person's right was not clearly established. The free man placed himself under the protection of the powerful man, the serf under that of the free man. This practice of recommendation can only exist among barbaric peoples, devoid of the fundamental laws of society. In such cases, private force is most often used to make up for the absence of public force. The advent of Pepin-le-Bref paved the way for Charlemagne's heyday. He knew how to turn the diverse elements of his vast empire into the subjects of a great nation. This prince gave special encouragement to agriculture, and tried to put property and all existing institutions on a firmer footing. He had the officers of the estates give an account of their administration. Bishops, abbots and abbesses were ordered to submit annual statements of their property. Abbot Irminon, superior of the Saint-Germain-des-Prés monastery, was the first to draw up a sort of cadastre of the properties belonging to his monastery. He recorded the names and extent of the estates, the size and nature of the various holdings of which they were composed, and in general, the cultivation, produce, condition and administration of the land. It revealed the fate of the colonists and serfs, how many people made up a family, how they lived, to which class they belonged, and the services they were responsible for. In a word, he established not only the land registry, but a census, as accurate as possible, of the population existing on the abbey's lands. This was administration, order, political and rural economy. With the decadence of the Carlovingian empire, the tributary tenants, following the example of the vassals, appropriated the land of which they were merely the usufructuaries, and feudalism, as it developed, completed the process of fixing ownership in the hands of the possessor. Under Louis the Debonair, confusion became general, new men arose on all sides, petty vassals became great feudatories, and officers of the realm became independent lords. One settler, who had owned only as a farmer, became an owner and passed on his property to his posterity; another, who had lacked the audacity or strength to accomplish this act, fell into servitude. From this state of affairs, feudalism on the one hand, and the frequent uprising of serfs on the other. The difference between these revolts and those of the free citizens of antiquity was that the latter revolted for the love of freedom and the fatherland, while the former revolted out of sheer misery. It has been said that it was under Louis-le-Gros that the communes were officially freed, under what conditions, and what future this act brought to urban populations. DE COO In the following chapters, we will have occasion to quote an important article from Charlemagne's capitularies concerning gardens (1). At a time when very little was written, the article in question must have been of capital importance, along with others concerning cultivation in general. When you want to find out about the culture of our ancestors a thousand years ago, you don't always have all the information you need at hand. The main article dealing with the cultivation of gardens is article 70, which reads: We want them to have in the gardens, plants of all kinds, namely: lilies, roses, fennel, mint. sage, rhue, aurone, cucumbers, pumpkins, calabashes and Spanish artichokes, beans, cumin, rosemary, seille, glaieul, caraway, chickpea, serpentaria, anise, coloquintes, heliotropc, Athamante meum. sesili de Marseille, lettuces, spider's foot, rocket, cress alénois, burdock, poichot, maceron commun, parsley, cèleri, levêche, sabine, ancth, sweet fennel, dictame de Crête, chicory, mustard, savory, round-leaf garden mint, tanaisée, catnip, knapweed, garden poppy, chard, cabaret, marshmallows, tree and common mallows, carrots, parsnips, garden arrowroot, amaranth chard, kohlrabi, cabbage, onions, turnips and radishes, shallots, chives, garlic, madder, char(1) B. Guérard. Guérard. bounetier donations, bog beans, peas, coriander, chervil, épurges, orvale. Let the gardener have joubarbe on his house. As for trees, we want our stewards to have apples of various species: plum trees, idem; rowan trees, medlar trees, chestnut trees, peach trees of various species, cognac trees, avelin trees, almond trees, mulberry trees, laurel trees, pine trees, fig trees, walnut trees, cherry trees of various species. Apple names: Gozmaringa, géroldinéja, erevedella, spirança; some sweet, others sour, all for keeping, and those that are eaten as soon as picked, which are early. Mature pears of three or four species, sweet, cooking or late. In this collection of around seventy plants, including flowers cultivated at the time, medicinal and aromatic plants, we find all our old vegetables currently grown in our countries. There were also a dozen fruit trees that the emperor required to be planted in his gardens. Article 70 of the emperor's capitularies, needless to say, listed all the useful plants cultivated in gardens. Here are a few other articles from the capitularies that relate to agriculture in general, and will serve to edify us as to the interest this prince took in everything that depended on his vast empire. ARTICLE 1 "We want our lands, whose revenues we have allocated for our benefit, to be used entirely for our own purposes, and not for those of others". ARTICLE 2 "Let all those who belong to us be well cared for, and let them not be reduced to poverty by anyone". ARTICLE 3 "That our intendants refrain from putting them to their service, 00 C and from forcing them to do ploughing for them by chores, cutting, wood, or any other work; that they accept no present whatsoever, except a few bottles of wine, gardening, fruit, chickens, eggs". ARTICLE 4 "If our men wrong you by thefts or other faults, let them repair the damage, for the remainder of legal satisfaction, they undergo the punishment of whipping". ARTICLE 5 "When our stewards have to carry out work in the fields, they will regulate in each place how to do it". ARTICLE 6 "We want our intendants to tithe all produce to the churches, which belong to our ecclesiastics". ARTICLE 7 "That each steward perform the service prescribed for him". ARTICLE 8 "Let the intendants cultivate the vines well, and house the wine well". ARTICLE Q "That each intendant have measures in his district, muids, setiers, the silule being eight setiers, and corbus of the same capacity as those in our palace". ARTICLE 10 "Que les maires forestiers, proposés aux haras, celleries, remplissent bien leurs offices". ARTICLE 13 "That they take care of the stallions (waramones), if any die, replacing them before the season to put them with the mares". ARTICLE 14 That they take care of the mares, separate the foals in time". COOd 8 ARTICLE 17 "That they care for bees properly". ARTICLE 18 "That they keep chickens and geese in our mills, in proportion to their importance". ARTICLE 21 "That they have fish ponds wherever possible". ARTICLE 22 "Those who have vines should have no less than three or four crowns of grapes at home". ARTICLE 23 "On every piece of land, let there be cowsheds, pigsties, sheepfolds, goat and goat stables". ARTICLE 32 "That they take care to have first quality seed". ARTICLE 34 "To ensure that everything done with the hands is done properly". ARTICLE 37 "That they keep the fields and crops in good condition". ARTICLE 44 "That they send two-thirds of the produce each year". ARTICLE 48 "That the presses be in good condition, and that the grapes not be crushed with the feet". ARTICLE 62 "That they send every year, at Christmas, on separate statements, clear accounts of all income". ARTICLE 69 "That they give notice of the number of wolves each will have taken, in the month of May, that they hunt the cubs, either with poisoned powders and hooks, or with the help of pits and dogs". 2QM2ed D GOO8 We have only quoted the main articles of these capitularies, which sufficiently indicate how everything was regulated on the imperial farms; the others, which are not quoted, relate only to internal particularities that do not have the same interest for the reader as these. These capitularies show that civilization took a major step forward under Charlemagne, as he organized and regulated everything that contributed to the production of the land. The monarch's intentions towards his farmers were good, the only thing was to compel the intendants, sub-intendants and all the rest to carry them out. But these exploiters, instead of supporting the farmers, lived and grew rich at their expense, robbing them as best they could. During the decadence of the Carlovingian empire, the lot of farmers did not improve; for during feudalism, unlike the artisans of the towns who had organized themselves into communities to defend their rights and privileges, the unfortunate farmers had no organization to defend themselves, and remained subject to the goodwill of their lords, who treated them with no other rule than their own pleasure. UNDER THE CARLOVINGIANS It is absolutely essential to know the value of the weights and measures of this period, compared to the units of our metric system, in order to form an idea of the price of land, the value of rents or cens, the price of foodstuffs, and so on. In Merovingian times, weights and measures, like all other institutions, were in a state of disarray. In addition to the measures they brought with them from Germania, the Franks used those of the Gauls, the Romans, the Greeks and even the Jews. MEASURES OF LENGTH (1) The main measure used by the Franks was the foot, more or less the Roman foot, which measured Om 2963. The arpent of the Gauls, like that of the Franks, was equal to a square 120 feet on a side. The league contained 1,500 steps of 5 Roman feet, and was therefore worth 2,222m 25. Today's terrestrial league is given a length of 4444 meters, hence the custom of considering the league as measuring approximately four kilometers. (1) B. Guérard. paré à nos mesures, il y avait de 12 ares 64 centiares, de 13 ares 72 centiares, de 18 ares 26 centiares et de 25 ares. The acre was later enlarged. According to some accounts established on the basis of these data, it has been recognized that the ancients sowed their wheat more lightly than today. The ansange is a measure that remained in use around Paris until the fifteenth century, and was worth around 19 ares. The perch also varied from period to period and from country to country. The aune, used to measure cloth, was worth a foot and a half, or Om 44495. CAPACITY MEASUREMENTS The modius or muid would be worth around 52 liters today. Each country also had its own measures, with different capacities. This measure was used to measure grain, wine, water, milk, pulses and so on. The setier also varied in capacity. The hemin was also a measure of capacity (1). Abbot Irminon's hemin was worth one liter sixty-four centiliters, which was what the rule of Saint-Benoit allowed each monk to drink every day. There was also staupas, which was about half the hemin. WEIGHT The Frankish pound was worth 326 grams until Charlemagne, when it was raised to 406 grams. The ounce was one-twelfth of the pound; until 779, it was worth 27 grams, and later, like the pound, it was increased to 34 grams. The ounce was also a kind of money of account, and was counted in ounces, as well as in pounds of gold and silver. The pensa was used to measure a share, a (1) B Guérard (translation by Abbé Irminon). ration in general. It would have corresponded to 78 livres 79, Charlemagne's weight. CURRENCIES The Merovingian livre would have a value today of 69 fr. 57 c., the sou, which was the twenty-fifth of the livre, would be worth 2 fr. 782; the denier, which was the twelfth of the sou, would have a value of O fr. 2318. A gold penny worth 40 deniers would sell for 9 fr. 28. The pound of gold, which would produce 87 sous 3/20, would therefore be worth 808 fr. 40. Pepin-le-Bref's penny, being the twenty-second of the pound, would be worth 3 fr. 16 c. and the denarius of the same O fr. 2636. At that time, silver coins were manufactured at one twenty-second of their value. Pepin-le-Bref gave his monies one penny for every twenty-two pennies produced. Charlemagne increased the price of coins quite significantly. The Merovingian pound, as we said, had a value of 69 fr. 57, that of Charlemagne would be worth 86 fr. 97 c.; the sou, which was the twentieth, would now be worth 4 fr. 35 and the denarius of the same period O fr. 3624. This estimate represents the intrinsic value of the coins, but to get a more or less accurate idea of their relative value in our time, we need to quote the table drawn up by B. Guérard, Abbé Irminon's translator. This table required a colossal amount of work on the part of its author, and a great deal of knowledge to draw up. Therefore, we do not change any of the figures, but copy them verbatim. Relative value of coins before 755: one denarius 2 fr. 25; one silver penny 27 francs; one gold penny 90 francs; one silver pound 675 francs; one gold pound 7,841 francs. Relative value of coins from 755 to 778: Un denier 2 fr 56; un sou d'argent 31 francs; une livre d'argent 675 francs. Relative value of coins from 779 to 799: Un denier 3 fr. 52; un sou d'argent 42 francs; une livre d'argent 844 francs. Relative value of coins since 800: Un denier 2 fr. 35; un sou d'argent 28 francs; une livre d'argent 563 francs. After the discovery of America, gold and silver considerably decreased in value, and currencies followed suit. The ancient system of weights and measures certainly lacked the fundamental foundations of our metric system, which is based on our planet itself; but recognized by the competent authorities, it could render useful services to nascent trade and industry. Charlemagne prescribed it throughout his empire, but it was never properly observed, and despite his efforts and those of his successors, the institution went from strength to strength. Each province, each town had its own particular measures, and during feudalism, there were as many systems as there were seigneuries. It was the Revolution of 1789 that brought order to this institution, at least officially, by adopting the metric system. But customs are so difficult to eradicate that, a century after the Revolution, despite people's level of education, we still hear people talking about the old weights and measures, even in Paris. Certainly, in some provinces, the old system is still more widely used than the metric system, which is so simple and within everyone's reach. This institution, like so many others bequeathed by our fathers, is indestructible and imperishable. I say imperishable on purpose. Indeed, it could happen that any cataclysm would destroy all the types, all the standards of our weights and measures; all it would take is a single coin to reconstitute the system as complete and as perfect as it is today. To be edified on this subject, it is necessary to have attended, as a child, even if only for a few years, any of France's elementary school. 984-e8 GO LEUR LOCATION OU CENS SOUS CHARLEMAGNE A settler pays the abbey of Saint-Germain-des-Prés four silver cents (113 francs) for one year's arable land: one acre of vines (12 ares 64 centiares) and half an acre of meadow (6 ares 32 centiares). A courtil (cortilis) 120 feet long by 56 wide, or 5 ares 89 centiares, was sold for 20 sous d'argent, or 563 francs, nearly 1 franc per meter. In 867, 12 bonniers, 15 hectares that had cost five pounds of silver: 2,816 francs, yielded three sous of silver per year: 84 francs. A manse with 13 bonniers, around 17 hectares of arable land; 6 arpents, 76 ares of vines and 6 arpents 76 ares of meadow, yielded a cens of 5 sous 4 deniers, making an average of 8 fr. 57 per hectare. The abbey of Saint-Germain-des-Prés rents, of course, for the price of 9 deniers: 21 francs per year, nine days of arable land, located in a place called Chaisnes. Compared with today's measurements, a hectare of arable land around Paris used to fetch six to thirteen francs. Land prices have risen considerably since then. At the end of this century, in the suburbs of Paris, a hectare yields an average of three hundred francs in arable land; it's true that it's mainly special crops that are grown here, rather than grasses. In 794, a muid of oats could not be sold for more than one denier: 3 fr. 50; barley, two deniers: 7 francs; rye, 3 deniers: 10 fr. 50; and wheat, 4 deniers: 14 francs. It is estimated that the weight of bread is equal to the weight of grain, the muid being worth 52 liters, this gives us around 40 kilograms. One muid of wheat would have yielded 40 kilograms of bread. Milling and bread-making costs were estimated at a third of the value of the grain. Add 4.50 francs for these costs to the 14 francs for a muid of wheat, for a total of 18.50 francs. These figures bring the price of a kilogram of bread to O fr. 456 during Charlemagne's reign, a relatively high price for the time. As a result, people in the countryside ate only bread made from barley and oat flour, without removing the bran. Wheat bread was considered a luxury item. What can be considered one of the greatest modern advances is the lowering of the power of money over wheat, making wheat bread accessible to everyone. Since Louis XIII, wheat has risen by only 50 0/0, while all other commodities have risen by 300 0/0 (1). In our time, if we pay a high price for bread, relative to the price of wheat, we owe it to agiotage on wheat and flour. Using the calculation we've just made, wheat is worth around twenty francs per (1) Avenel (Richelieu et la Monarchie). (0 hundred kilograms, adding a third for milling and bread-making costs, this puts the price of bread at O fr. 27 per kilogram. But to this price must be added the profits of the miller, the baker and, more often than not, the middlemen who often take the lion's share. Nevertheless, these are the prices established for the two main stakeholders: the producer and the consumer. At the same time, a good horse was worth six sous d'or: 540 francs, and a good steer two sous d'or: 180 francs. These prices were exorbitant for the time, especially when compared to workers' wages. DEIESSS CO The love of society that men bring with them when they are born, and the mutual needs that they are continually obliged to meet, soon led the first men to approach each other and join forces, either to make their existence easier, or to defend themselves more effectively against their enemies. And so, from the earliest civilizations, we see them making attempts at association. Among the Romans, it was the collegium; among the peoples of Germania, it was known as the ghilde, i.e. a banquet at common expense. The purpose of these associations was to defend and help one another. There was mutual insurance for all the major accidents that can occur during a man's life. The qhildes appointed a leader from within their ranks, had a common treasury provided by the followers and the statutes, probably regulations agreed between them, which everyone had to respect at their word. What's more, in keeping with the spirit of the times, each association was under the patronage of a god or hero. The Germans retained the ghilde even after their conversion to Christianity. When the new religion became powerful enough to impose its doctrines, it was only necessary to substitute the invocation of saints for that of gods and heroes; in other words, to change the names, but without changing the principle. Moreover, the fundamental principle of Christianity was the association of the weak against the strong. Its followers knew how to make the most of these associations, and based on this principle, which is so typical of man, they gradually brought them into line with the spirit of the times. Admittedly, these developments were slow, as are all institutions created in a developing society. During the feudal era, ghilds were known as confraternities (confraternities were synonymous with meetings, associations or societies in our time). Today, when we say "confrérie", we mean a religious association. Christianity organized them in such a way that they had a major influence in Paris for several centuries. Especially in the thirteenth century, many had their statutes approved. Their organization was infinitely varied, depending on the trades they represented and the goals they set out to achieve. They followed the trend of the times, and almost all became religious. Despite many abuses, they remained generally moral, through the vigilance they exercised over the morals and conduct of each individual; they were often political, through their intervention in popular movements. These associations almost always worried the authorities, and on several occasions they were abolished by capitularies, and a synod (1) held in Rouen in 1189 reproduced these prohibitions. Despite this, they continued to exist in a spirit of mutual association. After the Wars of Religion, they were overseen by the officers in charge; banquets were attended by sermons, and each table had a pot de l'aumônc. They had dignitaries called provost, dean or alderman. All the emblems of the brotherhood were at home. This office was sought-after, but only the wealthiest could obtain it, as it was very costly for the holder. (1) Le Masson, Calendrier des Confréries de Paris. 908 Each brotherhood had its own banner, coat of arms, attendance tokens and mottos; they also had their chaplains, medals and statutes. Attendance fees were made of lead, sometimes gilded, in the case of the richest brotherhoods. With the invention of printing, these practices fell into disuse, and naive images were substituted to brighten up the craftsman's home. Each member received one as he or she entered, as a sort of diploma. These institutions, like all others, had their abuses. Inevitably, political passions took hold of them, and especially during the Wars of Religion, they became furious; a period to which some people today would like to take us back. The most frequent reproach levelled at them was: drunkenness among confreres (our fathers drank heavily when they were celebrating). There were many different kinds of brotherhoods: many devotional, banter-based, facetious, apocryphal, patriotic, scientific and literary, with a religious element at their core. The devotional brotherhoods included penitents of all colors: white, blue, black, red, gray, green, purple, etc. (1). Among the banter and bachique brotherhoods, one of the best known was the confrérie des fous or innocents; bachique: the drinking horn of the fashionable devotees of the time; satiriques: "les paresseux ou les saoulx d'ouvrer et enragez de rien faire, des baise-c..., des mal mariés, des cornards, dite des Jean". Among the serious ones, the bow and arquebus brotherhoods, the core of the French infantry that saved the monarchy at Bouvines; the first elements of our modern armies, so true is it that whatever has been organized by the public authorities has always been copied from private societies. (1) Le Masson, Calendrier des Confréries de Paris. C Among the scientific brotherhoods, we find the Pontiff brothers, who have facilitated communications and rendered great services to commerce and industry. That of the "master masons", who created and handed down to us the architecture from cathedrals to the humblest village churches - there were few other monuments at the time. The most illustrious of the literary brotherhoods was that of the Passion in 1438, which represented the mysteries of religion, and which Victor Hugo describes in Notre-Dame de Paris. This brotherhood is, in a way, the cradle of our French scènc, and from which the great authors, notably Molière, drew inspiration to create the theater; these entertainments so fashionable since that time. Many of these brotherhoods have now disappeared; the Revolution of 1789 destroyed a large number of them, especially those that were not serious. Of those that have survived to the present day, almost all have a religious character. A scientific brotherhood, that of the "Masonic Masters", has also survived the centuries and is still very powerful today. This brotherhood has taken the name of society and appears to be a mutual aid society, in the fullest sense of the term. One criticism we could level at it is that it has perhaps retained too much of its medieval practices, in its private ceremonies: emblems, rallying signs, etc., which are still very much in evidence today. If we have given all these details on brotherhoods, it's to clearly indicate the spirit of medieval men, who associated themselves for everything, even the most frivolous distractions; not even thieves had their brotherhoods. The oldest gardeners' brotherhoods in existence date back to the thirteenth century, at Saint-Roch, Saint-Nicolas-des-Champs, Saint-Yves, Saint-Sulpice and Mont Sainte-Geneviève; they were placed under the invocation of Saint-Jacques and Saint-Christophe. In 1415, under Charles VI, a 908 brotherhood at Saint-Josse was placed under the patronage of Saint-Fiacre. At the same time, another in Saint-Nicolas-des-Champs was given the patronage of Saint-Fiacre and Sainte-Véronique. In 1599, when the gardeners drew up their statutes and officially recognized their community, a chapel was granted to them in Saint-Nicolas-des-Champs. Saint-Médard has also had its own brotherhood since ancient times. The Revolution of 1789 destroyed all these associations, but in our century, as soon as the Bourbols returned, several were reconstituted, although not in the same churches, as the gardeners had retreated. Nevertheless, the Saint-Médard association was reconstituted in the same church; a donation had been made by a certain Durand to celebrate an eternal mass there on Saint-Fiacre's day, in honor of the former brotherhood. Almost forty years ago, we again saw the confrérie des jardiniers de Saint-Médard attend this mass. Its last president (or doyen under the ancien régime) was the honorable M. Charles Debille, descendant of an ancient family of market gardeners, about whom we'll have more to say later. Confraternities can be found in all religions and beliefs, since, as we have said, people are born to live in society and help each other. When they were more scattered and less numerous, they grouped together, forming hamlets, then villages, then towns and states. It was then that they formed guilds, choosing a god, a hero or a saint to patronize their society, according to their religious beliefs. At first, our ancestors placed their brotherhoods under the patronage of St. James and St. Christopher, and celebrated their feast day on July 25. Later, they placed them under the patronage of Saint-Fiacre and Saint-Véronique; of all these saints, only Saint-Fiacre remained. It's likely that this hermit, having devoted himself specifically to the cultivation of vegetables (D00), was closer to the gardener's guild than any other. What's more, he was an Irishman; generally speaking, one is not a prophet in one's own country, and the Irish, in those days, were held in very high esteem by the French; for the blood they had shed for our country. These societies were created mainly to help one another, the mutual aid societies of the time; but often the best institutions degenerate into abuses, especially if these charitable works are transformed into societies giving parties and banquets. What charity establishes, intemperance corrupts. A number of rulings by the Parliament, dating from different periods, forbid the establishment of brotherhoods, or the reunion of members of existing ones. François I, in an edict dated August 1539, abolished all brotherhoods of tradesmen and craftsmen. Only cloth merchants were exempted. Several other trades followed the same route as the clothmakers, and their brotherhoods were re-established. Charles IX, in an edict of 1560, ordered that the money and revenues of these brotherhoods should be used for divine service, and the surplus for the upkeep of schools or almsgiving. This re-establishment of the brotherhoods revived all the disorders that had been abolished. In a general edict issued in January 1563, the same king forbade them to hold any banquet or feast, on pain of a fine of five hundred livres for each person present. Following the example of the masters, the journeymen emancipated themselves by forming brotherhoods among themselves, and were forbidden to do so by an ordinance (1) issued by Charles IX on February 4, 1567, under penalty of a one hundred pound fine for each offender. Henri III confirmed this ordinance on November 21, 1577. (1) Ordinances issued by the kings of France. CONFRERIES D9 By parliamentary decree of December 13, 1660, it was forbidden to establish any assembly, congregation, brotherhood or community without the express permission of the king, and letters patent registered with the court. In compliance with all these regulations, the police magistrate issued an order on November 4, 1670, as follows: "The jurors and masters of the arts and crafts communities of Paris are forbidden to hold any assembly other than before us, and in the presence of the King's Prosecutor, on the pretext of raising money, confraternities or any other business whatsoever, except by virtue of our order and written permission, on pain of a fine of 100 livres against each of the jurors, dismissal from the jury, and a fine of 20 livres against each of the private masters who attend, with the exception however of the assemblies for the election of jurors, which will be continued in the future, on the customary days and places, in the ordinary manner". This was done and given by Messire Gabriel Nicolas de la Reynie, Conseiller d'Etat Ordinaire, Lieutenant General of the City, Provosté and Vicomté of Paris, on the fourth day of November, one thousand six hundred and seventy. Signed: De la Reynie de Ryants; Sagot, Registrar. Los confréries having lost their primordial character of mutual aid, to become simply religious societies, were to be swept away with all the old institutions, by the Revolution of 1789. We'll come back to the brotherhoods of our century in due course. AND STATUTES OF THE FORMER COMMUNAUTÉ DES MAITRES-JARDINIERS When Etienne Boileau urged workers' communities to draw up their statutes, to have them approved, to constitute a kind of workers' legislation, gardeners must already have been numerous under Louis IX, since they fed a population of nearly one hundred and fifty thousand inhabitants. But, either through ignorance or negligence, they never had their trade officially recognized. However, given the large number of guilds in the same situation, we are tempted to believe that all these "retardaires" enjoyed the privileges conferred on them by their respective guilds. The earliest document we have been able to find concerning gardeners is a police order from the Provost of Paris, dated December 30, 1376, during the reign of Charles V, signed by Hugues Aubriot: (1) December 30, 1376, Ordonnance du Prévôt de Paris, que les échalas de merrien à treilles et vignes, amenés à Paris, seront visités par les jurés : To all those who will see these present letters. Hugues Aubriot, chevalier, garde de la Prévôté de Paris, et commis(1) Delamare, Traité de Police. ollized Dy saire donné et réputé par le roy, notre sire, sur le fait de la réformation des métiers de la ville de Paris: Salut. Comme nous avons été et soyons certainement et suffisamment adcertenez, tant par la complainte de plusieurs marchands, bourgeois, et autres gens de la ville de Paris et des villes voisines, comme autrement deument que sur le fait de la marchandise du merrein à treilles et à vignes que l'on amène à Paris pour vendre, a eu au temps passé, et encore à grandes fraudes et déception pour le peuple, parce que les dites denrées ne sont pas bonnes, loyal, nor merchantable, but are defrauded and of smaller harvest than they should be, which things to the great prejudice, damage and lézion of the common people, and still the said commodities worsen and appetize from day to day by the malice and covetousness of those who intermingle, and could worsen and several frauds and mischief, be committed there, if brief remedy were not put there: Knowing that we, desirous of our power of good government of the city of Paris, and of the commodities which are brought there, and to reform icelle of better and better, to the utility of the public thing, whose cure and diligence belongs to us by the king, our sire, as well for reason of our office, as by virtue of the power, to us given, and committed in this partic, we made assemble, and come before us, several merchants and other people of the aforementioned city of Paris, expert and knowing in the aforementioned merchandise; et avec ce, avons fait venir plusieurs vignerons et laboureurs notables des villes voisines d'entour Paris, et des lieux où les échalas que l'on amènc, sont allouez et distribuez; et finalement par leurs avis, et par bonne délibération du Conseil, avons fait et ordonné certaines constitutions et ordonnances sur le fait de ladite marchandise, en la manière qui s'ensuit : ARTICLE 1 "The perches and trellises which will be in quarterons, will have each at the end of five feet, the bulk of a pulse provided, at least, and the dozens to the quantity". ARTICLE 2 The sixaines will have each at the top of three feet full fist of gros, at least ". ARTICLE 3 "The quartaines will have each perch, at the top of six feet, full fist of wholesale, at least; and for what the handles of all are not equal, but have the some bigger handles than the others, and bigger poulce also. The measure of the handle and of the poulce, at which they will be measured, will be made in iron calf, for more justly tested". ARTICLE 4 "As for the round and red ozier of Saint-Marcel, which is the best, we order that it will be made into good, fair and merchantable jars, some of which will be four feet long, and others two feet long, for those who do not wish to have such large jars as four feet long, and there will be no other ozier measured in fur, inside the said jars, nor otherwise, and the prunings of the surranez oziers will not be put there. ARTICLE 5 " The river ozier will have each jar, three feet and a half of link, and there will be no dry ozier nor furs any faulx surranés ". ARTICLE 6 "The trimmings of overripe faulx will be sold on one side, and will not be measured, nor put with the other ozier". ARTICLE 7 " Les échalas qui seront faits dorénavant et amenez à Paris pour vendre, seront bons, loyaux et marchands, et auront chacun la moisson de six pieds et demiy de long, et les plus courts ne seront que d'un pied moindres, qui seront fournis à la valeur des autres bons; et seront cinququantaine, et n'y pourra y avoir que dix de la petite moisson". ARTICLE 8 "We order that the playon will be all green ozier, and there will be no dry fur, ny de heurdry, and whoever will misunderstand in the things above said, or in any of icelles, it will amend to the order of us, or of our successors provosts of Paris, or of our lieutenants, of arbitrary fine, of which fine of the swears who are established for the roy on the aforementioned fact, will have the third part for their punishment, and the roy will have the surplus; and with this, the commodities which will be found false, or ORDINANCES OF POLICE parts of icelles, will make arses in line of justice; if it seems good to us, or to our successors, or to our lieutenants. And to maintain and guard the above-mentioned things, we order that there shall be four jurors on the merreins à vignes; that they shall be established by us, or by our successors, provosts of Paris, or our lieutenants, and shall have the third part of said fines. In witness whereof, we have caused these present letters to be sealed with the seal of the Provost of Paris. "Ce fut fait en jugement au Chatelet de Paris, le mardy, penultième jour de décembre, l'an de grâce 1376". This ordinance of the Provost of Paris really only concerns the materials used by gardeners, but we shall see later that the jurors responsible for overseeing the sale of all these products were the jurors of the guild of master gardeners, mareschers and preoliers of Paris and its suburbs. We'll also see that every aspirant to mastery had to produce a masterpiece of his or her own. The masterpiece required of gardeners did not consist of a real horticultural operation, but simply consisted of: "a quarteron of merriens, in good and sufficient work, according to the statement and report of the maistres jurer gardener". The second police ordinance (1) concerning gardeners is dated February 8, 1473; it completes the first, and still has little to do with gardening. "Ordonnance de Police concernant le commerce de merrien à treilles, oziers et échalas pour les regrattiers de ces marchandises et les jardiniers". Pour ce qui est venu a la connaissance de justice, par la complainte de plusieurs bourgeois de Paris, de plusieurs laboureurs, marchands mareschers, jardiniers, et aussi d'autres de la ville de Paris et de villes voisines, et autrement que sur le fait de la marchandise, d'échalas que de merriens à treilles et à vignes, et aussi des oziers que l'on amène à Paris, pour vendre à très grandes fraudes et déception ou préjudice de la chose publique, tant pour ce que lesdites denrées ne sont pas bonnes (1) Collection Lamoignon, t. IV, f. 750. 908 ny marchandes, mais sont fraudées, et de plus petit moison, qu'elles ne doivent être, comme autrement, qui est préjudice et lézion de commun peuple, et contre les ordonnances anciennes faites sur lesdites marchandises. 1°. - L'en commande et est enjoint par le roy, notre sire, et Monsieur le Prévost de Paris, à tous marchands quelconques qui feront, dorénavant, en cette de ville Paris, amener merriens à treilles, ou échalas et oziers, ou autres merriens à vignes, tant par eau qu'autrement, that first and before they bring the said merriens ashore, exhibit them for sale, or put them in the yard, they have them seen and inspected by the swearers in the said merchandise, who are known and established to do so, under penalty of a fine of forty sols parisis to the king, our sire. 2° item. - Que nul ne soit sy osé ni hardy de contrefaire ou vendre merrien en treille, qu'il soit levé de Seine ou d'autres rivières, pour celui qui sera venu de la rivière de Marne, pour ce qu'il n'est pas si bon, ny suffisant, mais qu'on y faire des différences, de toute ancienneté, accoustumées pour connaitre et discerner l'un de l'autre. That is to say, the said merrien from the river Marne, which is better from all antiquity, should be bound with double ties and rewound from above, and that which comes from the Seine, or from any other river, instead of from Marne, should not be rewound from above, and be tied only with a single tie, and that it not be tied at two feet from the wood, in order to better know the difference one from the other, so that the people will not be disappointed, on similar peinc as above. 3° item. - Que toutes perches à treilles qui seront en quarteron, ayant chacune de haut cinq pieds et gros d'un pouce fourny du moins, et les douzaines à la qualité. 4° item. - Que les perches qui seront en fizenaires, ayant chacune en haut trois pieds, plein poing à tout le moins. 5° item. - Que les querreines ayant chacunes perches en haut de six pieds, plein poing de gros, à tout le moins selon les moules, à ce ordonnés de grande ancienneté étant au Châtelet de Paris; sur lesdites peines. 6° item. - Pareillement l'en commande et enjoint que nul ne soit sy osé ni hardy de contrefaire ou vendre oziers qui sont d'autre lieu que de Saint-Marcel, pour celuy de Seinc, ou que le peuple en soit déceu, pour que celui de Saint-Marcel vaut mieux ORDONNANCES DE POLICE 909 que tout autre. Et que chacun jarbe d'oziers, rond et rouge de Saint-Marcel qui est le meilleur, soit bon et loyal marchand, et ait au-dessus du lien quatre pieds de tour, et la petite jarbe d'iceluy lien, deux pieds de tour, et que parmi lesdites jarbes ne soit pas meslé, nor mixtionné of other oziers, and similarly that the ozier of river ct of another place that of Saint-Marcel, have each jarbe in top of the link three feet and half of turn, without there being dry ozier, ny other fourrements surranés, on the aforementioned penalties. 7° item. - Also that all merchants who bring or cause to be brought and come to this city of Paris, to sell any ozier, whether from Saint-Marcel or elsewhere, that such oziers be brought down, brought and arrive at the Place de Grève, at the place so ordered, without selling with the other, on said penalties. 8° item. - Que les émondées de seaulx surranés, soient venducs d'unc part, sans qu'elles ne soient meslées et mixtionnées avec l'autre ozier. 9° item. - L'en commande et enjoint à tous marchands qui feront venir en cette ville de Paris, aucuns échalas pour vendre, qu'avant qu'ils exposent en vente, ne mettent sur terre ou en chantier, qu'ils faites iceux échalas voir, et visité par les jurez commis sur ladite marchandise (assavoir), qu'ils seront loyaux et marchands, sous poine de confiscation desdites denrées et d'amende arbitraire. 10° item. - Que tous lesdits échalas qui, dorénavant, seront amenés à Paris pour vendre, seront bien loyaux et marchands et qu'ils ont moisons anciennement ordonnées et accoustumées et ordinaires cy devant faites. C'est assavoir la moison de cinq pieds et demy de long, et les plus courtes de quatre pieds et demy de long, et qu'en chaque javelle, il n'y ait au plus de dix échalas de ceux courts, et qui n'ait que quatre pieds et demy et qu'ils seront tous bien et suffisamment fournis et tous cinquanteniers, sur lesdites charges. 11° item. - L'en déffend que aucun marchand regrattier, ne achète pour vendre depuis le matin jusqu'à quatre heures après-midy, aucuns échalas de quelques personnes, que ce soit, soit marchand forain ou autre, sur peine de confiscation desdites denrées, ou peine arbitraire; et que cependant les bourgeois, C manans et habitants de Paris en peuvent acheter et en avoir pour leur fourniture et provision. 12° item. - That no one of the said regrattiers be so dared or so bold as to go before the merchants who will bring the said commodities and that the said commodities not be bought or sold elsewhere than in the customary places, and, after the hour before said. 13° item. - L'en déffend aussi que nul laboureur, ny autres personnes, ne seront sy osé ni hardy de prendre ou livrer ceps de vignes, ne les couper et marcotter en icelle; sur peine de la heurt ou autre punition, peine et amende publique, ou autre à la discrétion de la justice. 14° item. - That no one shall bring to Paris, sell any hairy vines, or other vine plants, nor anted trees, unless he has a certificate from justice, or at least from the local parish priest, that he has taken and raised them from his inheritance and not from any other, and that the sale be made publicly at the customary place. That is to say, on the great bridge of Paris, and not elsewhere, under the aforementioned penalty and other arbitrary fines. 15° item. - L'en commands and is enjoined to the four swearers of said merchandise, that they well and faithfully inspect said commodities and merchandise before declared, and all faults that they would have and could have been made and committed in icelles, well and diligently report to us and to the procurator of the king, our sire, at the said Châtelet, and if trade is, the delinquents they make arrest by the sergeants of the said Châtelet and bring prisoners, to ester, to right, and of all fines which for this, will be awarded to the said lord. They will have the third part, and if will be all the commodities which will be found false or part of icelles, arses in sign of justice. 16° item. - L'en deffend que nul jardinier ne soit sy osé ni hardy, sur peine de quarante sols d'amende et de tenir prison, d'entreprendre besongne au-dessus de sinq sols parisis, s'il n'est maistre ou bachelier. 17° item. - Que nul ne soit sy osé ni hardy d'entreprendre. besongne au-dessus de cinq sols, s'il ne met par manière de chefd'oeuvre, un quarteron de merrien en bon usage et suffisant au dire et rapport des maistres et jurez jardins. 18° item. - Et pour ce qu'il est venu à la connaissance de justice, que plusieurs qui se disent jardiniers, vont par les hôtels O00 des bourgeois de cette ville de Paris; marchandans de faire leurs jardins, et qu'il arrive souvent qu'il faut abbattre et dépecer les ouvrages qu'ils ont faites; parce qu'ils ne sont bons ni suffissamment fait; en quoi iceux bourgeois ct autres avant jardins, pour grande somme de deniers, qui sont de grande perte ou dommage, et n'y peuvent avoir aucune restitution, parce que lesdits jardiniers n'ont rien. It is hereby forbidden for any gardener to undertake any work in excess of five sols, unless he has given full and sufficient guarantee of restitution, if applicable, for any damages and interest incurred by the said burghers and others, who have gardens, through the fault of the said gardeners. Done and given under our signet, on Saturday the eighth day of February, in the year of our Lord one thousand four hundred and seventy-three. Signed: DEPRASY. LE CORNU. This police ordinance supplements the ordinance of the Provost of Paris of 1376. It is a set of statutes, the first part of which, before dealing with trade, is a reproduction of the latter. The second part, the last three articles, deal with the privileges of the guild. It is certain that even before Etienne Boileau, these statutes existed verbally, but probably no abuses occurred, and it was when they did that the community felt the need to react. This ordinance is the first to deal with the work of gardeners, and is considered by some authors to be the oldest in the community. However, it is easy to see that it merely completes the first ordinance, just as the last articles of the last ordinance served as the basis for the statutes of 1599: 1576, August 29 Letters patent from Henri III, confirming purely and simply the regulations given to gardeners by the police ordinance of 1473. Lawsuits brought by gardener-jurors before the 1599 statutes. d August 11, 1576 Ruling confirming a sentence of the Hôtel-de-Ville for incompetence and referring a case brought by the gardeners to the Châtelet. "Between Pierre Boutault, Gcorges Destais and Thomas Frémit maistres jurés, et gardes-jardiniers de cette ville de Paris, appelans de certaine sentence, jugement et appointement contre eux donné par le prévost des marchands et échevins de cette ville de Paris, le seizième jour de janvier mil-cinq-cent-soixante-dixhuit passé, comme de juge incompétent d'une part; et lesdits sergents de l'Ilôtel-de-Ville, intimez. "And the said provosts of the merchants and aldermen intervening, on the other hand; without the qualities being able to prejudice. After Dennet, on behalf of the appellants, concluded in his appeal, that the Provosts of the merchants and échevins of this city of Paris, ordered that they proceed before them, to the summons given by them, to proceed before them, on the grounds that the said intimez claim that they cannot visit the merrien woods used to make gardens, and that it is up to them; the substitute of the royal prosecutor, in the Hôtel-de-Ville present. A requis être renvoyez par devant le Prévost de Paris, juge ordinaire; et que Desportes pour les intimez a dit: qu'ils ne font partie en cet appel, mais surtout que la connaissance en appartient au Prévost des marchands, et tendent à follement intimez; ont été ouis, ensemble le substitut du procureur du roy, en l'Hôtel-de-Ville, et l'un des échevins d'icelle; et que Brisson pour le procureur du roy, a dit : that the exploit is abusive, to make visitation of wood of gardening in the houses of the private individuals, he does not know with which title, they can have this power; the gardeners have their privileges checked in the Court, of céans, and make their reports of the malversasions to the Provost of Paris, who is ordinary judge. The Court says: that it was badly exploited by the sergeants of the Hôtel de Ville, badly ordered by the Provost of the merchants and aldermen, sends back the parts by in front of the Provost of Paris. or his lieutenant, to make them right, condemned the intimatez ès expenses, costs of the Court of appeal. "Fait au parlement le onzième jour d'août 1578". Signed: DU TILLET. Arrêt du Parlement concernant l'ordonnance de police de 1473: 1889, June 27 "La Cour fait inhilition et déffense auxdits jurez jardinsiers, troubler ou empêcher ledit Rigault, vendre ou faire vendre, aux places publiques, ou autres lieux de cette ville, et à tels jours que bon lui semblera, les fruits et herbes creus et recueillis sur les héritages qu'il tient et valoir entre ses mains, lesquels fruits et herbes, pourront être visités par lesdits jurez maîtresjardiniers, tant esdites places que jardins, sous dépens". These lawsuits show that although the old community of gardeners did not yet have definitive statutes, it did enjoy certain privileges, especially the right of inspection, both at market disputes and in the gardens. In those days, even more than today, custom was the rule. It also happened that these representatives of authority wanted to arrogate to themselves rights they didn't have, and that the courts were forced to call them to order so that they would simply return to their privileges. FIRST STATUTES OF THE COMMUNITY OF MASTER GARDENERS 1599, November New statutes presented by the gardeners in seventeen articles, and letters patent from Henri IV confirming them. "The community of master gardeners of our good city, suburb, suburbs, provostry and viscounty of Paris, most humbly point out to Your Majesty that, from time immemorial, they have been numbered among the sworn masters of your city of Paris, and governed by ordinances and statutes particular to their trade, which they have peacefully enjoyed without contradiction until now. Nevertheless, in the shadow that the suppliants would not have obtained letters of confirmation from 0 your Majesty, some private individuals are trying to disturb them, and undertake on their trade, so that the suppliants doubt that this undertaking, if it were tolerated, would harm them and their successors, and hinder the said undertakings, commit several abuses and deceptions which are not to ratify and confirm the articles which follow, and the suppliants will be all the more obliged to pray to God for your noble prosperity and health". 1°. - No one may become a master gardener, in this city, suburbs and suburbs of Paris, unless he has been received as a master in the said trade, and has made a masterpiece by his own hand, as is customary. This masterpiece will be awarded and evaluated by the sworn members of the said trade, in the presence of four former bachelors. 2° item. - Before the masterpiece is given by the swearers to the said journeymen who wish to become master craftsmen, the said swearers are required to inquire into their good life and morals, the master craftsmen they have served, and where they have served their apprenticeship, in order to give or refuse them the said masterpiece, according to the report they find. 3° item. - After the said inquisition has been made, the said journeymen, who aspire to the said maistrise, will be required to make a masterpiece, as will be devised to them by the swearers of iceluy done and perfect, and will make the said swearers their report in the accustomed manner. 4° item. - That no one shall be accepted for master craftsmanship, in order to attain the said maistrise, unless he has been apprenticed under a master of the said trade, in this town and suburbs, for the time and space of four years, and furthermore, served by the masters, after his said apprenticeship time, for the space of two years. 5° item. - The children of the said maistres will be admitted to the maistrise, without any masterpiece or experience, after they have been certified as having been apprenticed, either to their fathers or elsewhere, for the said period of four years. 6° item - That the said maistres jardiniers, after being admitted to the said trade, will give to the confraternity of the said mestier, their right to it, and to maintain the divine service, the sum of thirty-two sols, which they will be obliged to give to the confraternity maistres. 7° item. - That no master may withhold, debauch 08 or give work to any journeymen of the said trade, while they are allotted to another master, unless he first knows from his master whether he is satisfied with him, under penalty of six sous fine applicable as above. 8° item. - That each apprentice of the said trade shall be required, as soon as he is obliged, to pay to the brotherhood of the said trade, for the fees of the said brotherhood, and to maintain the service, the sum of twenty-four sols parisis, and the master with whom the said apprentice will be, shall be required to advance this sum. 9° item. - The widows of maistres, as long as they remain in viduité, will enjoy the same privileges as their deceased husbands, but if they remarry, or fail in their viduité, they will lose the said privilege, and will no longer be able to engage in the said trade. 10° item. -- The said widows may have apprentices who were obligated to their deceased husbands complete their apprenticeship under them, so that they may maintain and practice the said gardener's trade, and so that they do not remarry others of the said trade, otherwise the said widows will be required to place the said apprentices with the mayors of the said juries, who will be required to have them complete their apprenticeship under another sufficient master of the said trade. 11° item. - The said widows may not, as long as they continue to practice the said gardening trade, take on any new apprentices or have them obligated, but will have them trade and merchandise with a journeyman who requires the status. 12° item. - That all persons whatsoever are forbidden to bring or cause to be brought any melons, cucumbers, artichokes, herbs and other things dependent on the said gardening trade, unless they are masters of the said trade, except the burghers of the city and suburbs of Paris, who may have gardens and property, who may bring or cause to be brought on market days, which are Wednesday and Saturday, all kinds of produce growing in their said gardens; which produce. will be inspected by the said swearers, and if they are good, will be exhibited for sale; if they are not, they will be confiscated, and the person who exhibits them for sale will be fined twenty sols, half of which will go to the King, and the other half to the said swearers of the said trade. 008 buy any thing dependent on the state of gardening, in any place other than the said market hall and public markets, so that the swearers have knowledge of it, nor even hold in their houses, or elsewhere, their fruits and herbs at night, insofar as they will be subject to rot and attract the bad air. And to avoid the said abuses that may be committed, the sworn members of the said trade may search the houses of the said sellers for the faults and abuses that they find there, report them in the customary manner, and the said offender may be fined and punished if he so desires. 14° item. - That all persons are forbidden to smoke any garbage or pig droppings in order to plant or sow any goods that depend on their gardening; to avoid the abuses that are committed there, and could be committed hereafter, to avoid contagious and other diseases, on pain of having their goods spilled, and being fined two écus for each offender: 15° item. - That it is forbidden to all masters of the said trade, to purchase from other masters, any trees, or other goods to bring them to sell on the bridge of the said trees, or other public places, on penalty of confiscation of the said trees, and of two écus fine, applicable as above; but may the said masters, sell their trees and flowers there as of any seniority. 16° item. - That all master bachelors and journeymen of the said trade, shall be bound to recognize their former master-jurors, and to show them the respect, honor and reverence due to them. Assavoir à leurs anciens et à leurs jurez, chacun en son temps de ladite charge de jurez, comme ils ont promis lorsqu'ils ont été receus audit dudit mestier. 17° item. - That for the preservation of the said trade, four swearers of the said trade shall be raised, in the same form as the swearers of the other trades, by the community of the masters of the said trade, by whom swearers shall be made all necessary visits to be made to the said trade, both in the said city, suburbs and suburbs of Paris, without the said suburbs being required to request a license from the high justiciars, whatever privilege and right of high justice they may have, given that it is a question of 103 C police, of which, connoissance belongs to the Provost of Paris. DECREE OF HENRI IV CONFIRMING THESE STATUTES November 1599 "Henry, by the grace of God, King of France and Navarre, to all present and future: Salut. Ayant fait voir en notre conseil, la requête et articles à Nous en iceluy présentez par nos bons amis les maistres et communauté du mestier de jardinier, en notre bonne ville, faubourgs, banlieue, Prévosté et Vicomté de Paris, concernant leur état et mestier, à ce qu'il nous plaise, iceux ratifier et confirmer, ensemble l'avis, à nous donné par le Prévost de Paris, ou son lieutenant. Qüi, le substitut de notre procureur général en ladite Prévosté; suivant le renvoi que nous leur auraient fait, à cette fin par nos lettres patentes, le tout y attaché, sous le contre-scel de notre Chancellerie : Scavoir taisons, que nous désirons subvenir auxdits maistres et communauté du mestier de jardinier, en notre bonne ville, faubourgs, banlieuc, Prévosté et Vicomté de Paris, de l'avis de notre conseil et de notre certaine science, pleine puissance et authorité royale; conformément à ledit avis, même ayant égard, que par le moyen de l'entretennement et observation desdits articles, confirmiez, ratifiez, approuvez et authorisez; confirmons, ratifions, approuvons et authorisons par ces présentes. Voulons et nous plaist qu'ils soient, dorénavant, gardez, observez et entretenus en tout et chacun leurs points, sans il y soit contrevenu en quelque sorte et manière que ce soit, sur les peines y contenues. "If we mandcment, to our friends and seal advisors, the people holding our court of Parliament of Paris, and to the said Provost of Paris, or his lieutenant, that the said articles and the said present, they make register, maintain, keep, observe from point to point according to their form and content; the suppliants and their successors of the said gardener's trade, compelling to make themselves suffered and obeyed by all those who it shall appear, and who for this purpose shall compel by all ducous and reasonable means, notwithstanding any opposition or appellation for which we do not wish to be deferred; for such is our pleasure. And in order that it be a firm and stable thing forever; We have put our seal on these presents, excepting in other matters our right and the right of others in all matters. "Donné à Paris, au mois de novembre, l'an de grâce milcinq-cent-quatre-vingt-dix-neuf, et de notre règne, le onzième; ct au reply est escrit, par le roy, notre sire, à votre relation". Signed: THOMAS These statutes are the most important act of the ancient community of master jarniniers. From the documents that preceded them, it is easy to see that the community had only incomplete legislation, based on the police ordinance of February 3, 1473. However, as we've already mentioned, around 1260, when Etienne Boileau had a register drawn up and deposited at the Châtelet to record the customs practiced for centuries by the various Parisian trades, our ancestors were already numerous, but they neglected this formality which gave official recognition to their trade. What is certain is that these time-honored practices had the force of law, and successively produced the documents we have just cited. These statutes of 1599 were skilfully drafted for their time, and provided a sufficient guarantee in every respect for the community of master gardeners. They cover the rules of apprenticeship, the rights and duties of journeymen, the masterpieces accomplished 99 to achieve mastery, and the privileges of masters' sons and widows. The election of jurors, their functions and rights, the prohibition against poaching journeymen from one another, the obligation to inquire when hiring someone they didn't know, where he had apprenticed, with which masters he had worked, the reasons why he had left the house: in short, the community policed all its members. As a result, it was not uncommon for journeymen to stay with the same masters for a number of years. Hence the mutual esteem that existed between them, with the pastoral existence so dear to our ancestors. We'll come back to this subject at another time, and try to explain, as far as possible, the reasons why these pastoral customs changed. Masters were not allowed to buy goods from other masters for resale by regrat. They could not smoke their land with pig manure, Paris sludge that was too fresh, or fecal matter, the infection of which, said the Prévost de Paris, could cause contagious diseases. This was a hygiene measure taken by the police against gardeners, given that many of them lived in the city, or even on its outskirts, in fairly densely populated suburbs. Even today, don't we see these police measures taken against the sludge of Paris! The capital's neighboring towns no longer welcome them. The railroad stations where they are shipped create problems for farmers, giving them only a few hours' latitude to unload their wagons and transport the contents to a certain distance from their homes. All these difficulties increase the cost of the city's cleaning service. As a result, the city council has asked the Hygiene Commission to look for a practical way of getting rid of this refuse. The idea is to create large furnaces to incinerate it. This would produce chemical fertilizers that are easy to trans09 port to all parts of the country, but what is indisputable is that the Paris suburbs would suffer a great loss if this system were adopted. Growers and horticulturists within a fifty-kilometer radius have protested against this method, but the idea is still being pursued. However, large-scale cultivation would like nothing better than to receive the slush from Paris, but say the growers we spoke to, that the railway companies should give us the time we need to remove it. Certainly, today, the capital no longer fears famine, as in the old days, thanks to the rapid means of communication, the food we need comes from all sides, according to the season. It is no less true that this great suburb, which supplies the lion's share of the vegetables consumed in Paris, is entitled to some consideration from the big city. It has not even been proven that the price of these products would not rise significantly above current prices. The old police regulations before 1599, concerning gardeners, had to do with trade, the visit of jurors to markets and gardens. Four different people had the right to sell fruits and vegetables from their gardens in the public markets of the city of Paris: master gardeners, journeymen, suburban showmen, and the bourgeois of the city and suburbs who owned gardens they sold. There were regulations for all these different conditions, prescribing what they had to observe, and whose purpose was to provide citizens with an abundance of the simplest and most common healthy foods, often the only ones used to feed the people, and which even the rich would find hard to do without. The maitres alone had the right to display their wares for sale on the mornings of every weekday, ORDONNANCES DE POLICE 908 at the halle aux poirées, from the halle aux blés to the rue Saint-Ionoré. This community was the only one whose journeymen could trade like the masters; in the past, this was only granted to those who owned gardens or marshes that they managed, but since 1627, this right has been extended to those who rent them. Nevertheless, with these old conditions, they were still required to endure the visits of the jurors, to bring their goods to the market hall and other squares only on Wednesdays and Saturdays, which are market days, and to take their place there only after all the masters. These same journeymen also contributed to the community's expenses, even though they were not yet part of it as masters. The burghers of the city, suburbs and suburbs, as well as the showmen of the small surrounding localities, had only the same right as the journeymen gardeners, to exhibit their trees, fruit and vegetables for sale, on both market days, on condition that they also suffered the visits of the jurors. The community left no stone unturned to defend its interests, and it's easy to see that freedom of trade was very limited, and competition virtually impossible: such were the customs of the time. During the nearly two centuries that these statutes remained in force, they underwent no major changes, but simply followed the spirit of the times, one might say the whims of kings. Whereas under Louis XIV, instead of the communauiés appointing their jurors, they were appointed by the king, and the jurors became, so to speak, public servants. This state of affairs could only result in flagrant abuses, which helped to push the ancien régime into the abyss, perhaps even faster than it should have, had it respected the ancient regulations of the workers' communities. A word, in passing, about the signatories of the statutes of 18 1599; of these nine signatories, only one family has kept its name in the guild to this day. The first signatory is a Jean Le Bouteux, which certainly means that he was the principal, and what we today call the president. In our century, we also find a Le Bouteux, as one of the principal founders of the first mutual society created by gardeners. This venerable dean of our corporation was vice-president of this society for almost forty years, and at the end of this century, he is still its honorary vice-president. Another name that has left its mark on the history of gardens is Pierre Lenostre, who must undoubtedly have been one of the ancestors of the great landscape architect who designed the park at Versailles, and thus created a new art of garden design known as the French garden. We've already had occasion to talk about this illustrious gardener. Letters patent from the late King Louis XIII, confirming the statutes of the gardeners: "Louis, by the grace of God, King of France and Navarre, to all present and future, greetings: Our beloved masters and community of the gardener's trade, in our good city, suburbs, suburbs, Provost and Viscounty of Paris : We have been informed that for all time and antiquity, they have been numbered among the sworn maistres in our city, and that they are governed by the ordinances and statutes of the aforementioned trade, which they renewed on October 15, 1599, and which the late King Henrile-Grand, our honored lord and ayeul, would have advised the Provost of Paris, or his lieutenant; and oüi le substitut du procureur général, confirmé et approuvé par ses lettres patentes du mois de novembre ensuivant ledit an, registrées au Parlement de Paris, le 17 avril 1600, dont ils ont toujours paisiblement joui jusqu'à présent, craignant iceux exposants que pour n'avoir depuis notre avèncment à la couronne, obtenus nos lettres de confirmation d'iceux statuts et privilèges, pour lesquels ils ont payé la finance, à laquelle ils ont été taxés en notre conseil, nos officiers ou autres, voudussent les troubler; O8 they have most humbly begged us to grant them. To be enjoyed by them and their successors to the said profession, fully, peacefully and perpetually, just as they have enjoyed and gently used, enjoy and still use at present. We hereby instruct our friends and advisors, the people holding our Court of Parliament in Paris, the provost of the said place or his lieutenant, that these presents be registered, and that the contents hereof be suffered and left for the exhibitors, fully, peacefully, compelling to the observance of the said privileges and statutes, all those whom it may please; for such is our pleasure. "Donné à Paris, au mois de juin, l'an de grâce mil-six-centquarante-cinq, et de notre règne le troisième. "And to the reply is signed by the king". RABOIS.
