These various lawsuits provide ample evidence of the community's interest in asserting its privileges: they also demonstrate the no less active interest of the companions in reacting against these privileges. It has been said that the organization of workers' communities was part of the feudal system of the Middle Ages. So, when the great reformer Calvin came to preach his doctrines in Paris in the 16th century, they disrupted the existence of the old communities, which set out to maintain the use of courteous arms between industrial competitors; they were violently opposed by the Paris bourgeoisie, who represented them. After the Edict of Nantes, the king obliged tradesmen to accept several religionists, as they called them, into their ranks. The aim of the authorities, in imposing these men on the various organized guilds, was undoubtedly to force them to obey an order from the king, to ensure the supremacy of the Catholic Church. As these religionists were the most literate, this gave them much greater authority over their fellow members. Despite this, many men in the communities, already enlightened and with a certain independence, preferred to go into exile and take their industries to foreign countries, rather than bow to this arbitrary act (1. This unfortunate act cost our country dearly, says Michelet. The act of revocation was signed by the king on October 16, 1685. On October 19, the Paris police brutally told the tradesmen that they had to convert immediately. They were terrified, but raised no objections. The king thought it was all over, and on the 20th authorized Protestants to assemble for a joint declaration of conversion. Those who resisted were looted and robbed, and some were murdered. Women in particular, whose freedom of conscience is even stronger than that of men, had to endure the most odious outrages. Many of them, to force them to give in, were locked up in the convent of the Nouvelies Catholiques, and subjected to all the horrors that an undisciplined soldiery is capable of inflicting on a woman who is handed over to them bound hand and foot. Surely some of our old grandmothers must have suffered these outrages. Whereas before these ridiculous constraints, which had nothing to do with trades, the groups of citizens who made up the ancient communities, constantly in touch with each other through common interests (1) Michelet, Histoire de France. 10, drew up their own little code, and the state intervened only to approve their decisions, taken among peers, and to reap a certain profit from them. Despite the abuses that these societies perpetuated for their own benefit, and which the Revolution did away with by proclaiming individual freedom, it can be said that they had a raison d'être, which argues in favor of the time when they subsisted. The masters upheld the rights and privileges that the ancient nature of their guild gave them, or that they had arrogated to themselves by forming organized and recognized communities. It's true that, from time immemorial, people have had to band together to defend their interests effectively. Unfortunately, the interests we support become abused with time and new men. Even so, it's hard to let go of them when they've been enshrined for centuries. To be fair to the old community of master gardeners, it must be said that they had almost relinquished all their privileges by the time the revolution broke out. This abnegation by our old masters in favor of their privileges is proof of the independence of their character and the generosity of their ideas. If not these men, at least their sons, will be found later on, and we will trace the services they rendered to the corporation, by taking the initiative, at their peril, to introduce new crops. The old community also had to support various lawsuits against the regrattier fruit growers, who were organized as a community long before the gardeners. An article in the statutes of the community of grain growers governed the right of gardeners to sell seeds. It's almost certain that both of these industries originated in gardening. In the early centuries, growers sold their products directly to consumers, as the city was sparsely populated and not very large, 28 but gradually, as the population grew and the city gradually expanded, new needs arose. This led to the creation of the specialties we have just mentioned, which were careful to set themselves up as communities, with their statutes approved by the public authorities. Another specialty more closely related to gardeners was the community of bouquetières, whose first statutes are included in Etienne Boileau's livre des métiers. The fashion of adorning oneself with natural flowers was very much in vogue in the Middle Ages. At joyous gatherings, flower chapels were woven and offered to friends. The art of making chapels was a favorite pastime of the grandes dames, and our Parisian ladies were already distinguished by their good taste and genius for innovating everything to do with grooming. At first, florists copied these grand dames, but in time, they quickly became artists in their art. The community was free, exempt from watch and taxes, authorized to work at night at certain times "establi pour servir les gentuez hommes". At this time, they made wreaths of flowers and herbs, and above all chapels of roses, diadems, crowns or circles, much used in processions; they reserved the right to sell bouquets, hats, wreaths and garlands of flowers in the streets and at church doors; anyone contravening these regulations was fined five hundred pounds. During the season, bouquets of violets, roses and carnations could be sold, but not upright. Gardeners sold their flowers only at the IIalles and Quai de la Mégisserie, until eight o'clock in the morning. Flowers had to be fresh, and sales were supervised by community jurors. Acacia was banned from Corpus Christi processions. All flower chapels had to be set against a background of greenery. Statutes of the Chapelières de fleurs inscribed in Etienne Boileau's guestbook. 1° - Quiconques veut estre chappellières de fleurs à Paris, estre le puet franchement, pour tant ce qu'il sache fère le mestier et il ait de coi. 2° - Quiconques est chappellières de fleurs à Paris, il puet ouvrer et fère ouvrer de jours et de nuiz, de fleurs et d'herbes. 3° - Quiconques est chappellières à Paris, il ne puet ouvrer, ne fère ouvrer au jour de dimenche de nul chapel, se ce n'est de chapiaus de roses, tant sculement, tout comme la seson des roses durent; et se le faisoit, il seroit à 5 sols de tournois d'amende à poier au roy. 4° - Nus chappellières de fleurs ne doit ne peut cueillir, ne fère cueillir au jour de dimenche en ses courtuiz; nules herbes, nules fleurs à chapiaus fère ne à mangier, en celle jornée, qu'il ne soit à 5 sols de tournois à poier au roi. 5° - Quiconques est chappellières de fleurs à Paris, il puet porter et faire porter, vendre ses chapiaus à Paris, en quelque lieu qu'i voudra, et à toz les jours de la semaine, pourtant qu'il trouve place wide. 6° - Nus chappelliers de Paris, ne doit nul coustume, nul paage, ne nule droiture de chose nule qu'i vendent, n'en achaptent apartenanz à leur mestier, soit qu'i envairet de dehors la ville, ou qu'i envoit l'dehors la ville, soit sur cheval ou à col. 7° - Nus chappelliers de fleurs de Paris ne doit point de guet, parce que leur mestier est frans et que il fu establi pour servir les gentuiz hommes. 8° - Si prend oume du mestier se sont ascenti, un preud'oume de leur mestier soit mestre du mestier, li ce que preud'oume doit jurer devant le Prévost de Paris, que il le mestier gardera bien et loiaument, et que toutes les mesproustures qu'il saura qui fétes seront u mestier devant dit, que il au Prévost de Paris ou à son commandement fera scavoir au plus tost que il pourra par reson. Article 4 of the statutes sufficiently indicates that the bouquetières were gardeners, as it forbids Sunday flower-picking in the courtyards. The husband, father, brother or any other worker was responsible for growing the flowers in the garden, and the wife for selling them, according to the customs and fashions of the time, exactly as they are today, with the difference that each one organized himself as he saw fit. These statutes were renewed on August 21, 1677, and make it clear that the community is independent of any other. We note in these statutes certain articles that concern the sale of flowers in the olden days, and which are still in use today, notably: flowers in crates or pots must be sold at the Quai aux fleurs and not at the Ilalles Centrales. 1677, August 21 Statuts des bouquetières, in 21 articles, and letters patent from Louis XIV confirming them (1). ARTICLE 1 No woman or girl may become a master flower-maker in this city and suburbs of Paris, unless she has been a master of the said trade, and has made a masterpiece by her own hand, in the manner accustomed from time immemorial, which masterpiece will be given to aspirants by the jurors of the said trade, in the presence of four former bachelors only, who will be called in turn and by order of the roll. (1) René de Lespinasse. ARTICLE 2 No girl or woman shall be admitted to the master's degree unless she has served her apprenticeship with one of the master craftswomen for four years, and then served the master craftswomen for two years; the apprenticeship certificates shall be notarized in the presence of two jurées and duly registered in the chamber of one of the king's attorneys. And apprentices will be required to pay three pounds to the brotherhood's (boète) at the time of the said patent, which will be advanced by the mistress, who will be reimbursed by her apprentice. ARTICLE 3 Before granting masterpieces to aspirants who present themselves for acceptance, the jurors will be required to ascertain their good life and morals at the home of the mistresses with whom they have served their apprenticeship and as companions, and according to the report made to them, may grant or refuse them masterpieces. ARTICLE 4 The aspirants will make their masterpiece with their own hands, in the presence of the jurées and four former bachelières, who, after it has been made and perfected, will report on it and take the aspirant before one of the king's procurators at the Châtelet, to administer the oath and be received by him in the customary manner, and all the mistresses will be warned to come and see the masterpiece, if they so wish, and attend the administration of the oath, but without charge. ARTICLE 5 The aspirant will be required, at the time of her reception, to put ten livres into the confraternity box, pay sixty sols to each of the jurées and thirty sols to each bachelière, and the ordinary costs of justice, without it being permitted to make her pay any other costs, even when they are voluntarily offered, under penalty of fine and restitution of quadruple against the jurées, bachelières and others who may have received them. ARTICLE 6 Daughters of mistresses may be admitted to the master's program without having completed any masterpieces or experiments, provided they have served as apprentices to their mothers or other mistresses for four years, pay only thirty sols into the brotherhood box, and pay half dues to the jurécs by way of recognition. ARTICLE 7 Those who wish to be received by virtue of letters, without having served an apprenticeship, will pay thirty livres into the confraternity box and will be received by making light experiments, and will pay the said jurée fees. ARTICLE8 The right to display and sell all kinds of bouquets, hats, wreaths and flower garlands shall belong solely to the maitresses bouquetières. Any person who is not of the aforementioned quality is forbidden to make or sell any bouquets, nor to resell or peddle them in the streets or at church gates, under penalty of confiscation of their merchandise and a fine of five hundred pounds, half of which will be for the benefit of the King, and the other half for the benefit of the community of maitresse bouquetières. ARTICLE 9 All kinds of persons may nevertheless sell bouquets of violets, roses and carnations during the seasons, as in the past, with the difference that they may not be mixed with any other flowers, nor may they use canetille to decorate and tie them ARTICLE 10 Masters of the community of gardeners may not display or sell their flowers; whether they are picked, or in pots, crates or mannequins, at church doors, or elsewhere, in front of the stores and squares of the master flower sellers; they must bring their picked flowers to the market every weekday, and their flowers in pots, crates or mannequins to the Quai de la Mégisserie on Wednesdays and Saturdays in the usual manner. ARTICLE 11 In order to preserve the freshness of the flowers, gardeners and other inhabitants of the surrounding suburbs and villages who bring them to the halles, will be required to bring them there very early in the morning and sell them immediately, either to the bourgeois, or to the flower sellers, and will be required, after having completed their sale, to withdraw in summer at eight o'clock, and in winter at nine o'clock, with a prohibition on selling flowers after the said time has passed, on pain of confiscation and fine. ARTICLE 12 Flower sellers will be required to use fresh and new flowers in their bouquets; they are forbidden to use old, wilted flowers, or those that have been salted during the summer, under penalty of confiscation and a fine of ten pounds, applicable, etc. ARTICLE 13 Bouquet-makers are similarly forbidden to use any accacia flowers in their bouquets, nor to make any wreaths or flower hats for Corpus Christi or other holidays, unless they put some green underneath, subject to the same penalties as above. ARTICLE 14 Each bouquetière merchant may not have two apprentices at the same time, and so will be required to be content with having only one; may, nevertheless, with the said apprentice, have one or more companions to help them do their work, and the daughters of maitresses whom they have working for them, will not take the place of an apprentice. ARTICLE 15 No mistress may give work to a girl unless she is a mistress, apprentice or companion. All mistresses are forbidden to take away companions who are rented to another mistress, or to give them work, unless they have first asked permission from the said mistress and know that she is satisfied with the said companion. ARTICLE 16 All mistresses of the said trade are forbidden, under the same penalty of a ten pound fine, to meet gardeners and other persons when they bring their flowers to the market, so that they may be sold and quoted among themselves. 4 ARTICLE 17 For the preservation of the aforementioned trade, there will be in the future, as in the past, four jurées, two of whom will be elected each year before one of the King's procurators at the Châtelet, one a woman and the other a girl, as is customary, These jurors will take care to prevent companies that could be made on the aforementioned trade and will make visits to the maitresses, at least four times a year, and will be paid twenty sols for each visit, may nevertheless make a greater number, but will only be paid the right for four visits, per each year. ARTICLE 18 We forbid all persons who are not maitresses of the said trade, to sell flowers or bouquets at church doors, on street corners, or to carry them into the houses of the bourgeois; nor regratiers and coureuses, to peddle them in the streets, and maitresses bouquetières, to have them sold by the said unqualified persons: all under penalty of confiscation and fine both against the one and the other ARTICLE 19 If any mistress, apprentice or companion of the said trade were convicted of misconduct in her honor, she would lose her privilege, and if she were an apprentice or companion, she would be unworthy of attaining the master's degree. ARTICLE 20 During their first year as jurors, the last two jurors will take care of all matters concerning the brotherhood, which they will continue to maintain in the church of Saint-Leufroy, as they did previously, and will have the customary service said there. ARTICLE 21 The community will be composed of women and girls only, and no boy will be allowed to become a master, or to enter into the said trade, or to make or sell bouquets. We forbid all mistresses to give work to any man, nor to have their bouquets sold by them. To these causes, Louis, by the grace of God, King of France and Navarre, to all present and future; greetings: Nos chères et bien-aimées les jurées et anciennes bachelières de la communauté des maîtresses bouquetières, chapelières en fleurs de notre bonne ville de Paris, nous ont fait remarquer qu'il y a très longtemps que leur art est établi dans ladite ville de Paris, en communauté et corps de métier, qu'elles ont eu des statuts très anciens qui leuront été accordés par les rois nos prédécesseurs, qui contiennent plusieurs beaux droits et privilèges, pour la perfection et la conservation de leur métier; but that through the passage of time, and the negligence of those who preceded them in the office of juror, the originals of the said statutes have been lost, and whatever diligence the aspirants have been able to make, they have been unable to recover them, in such a way that, although the substitutes preserve among themselves the rules of discipline. that they know have always been observed in their community; that they have continued to maintain their confraternity and the divine service that is customary there, that they still have their juries, their apprenticeships, their visits, as they have had from time immemorial, like other communities, they cannot, nevertheless, retain the rights of their trade, nor prevent the undertakings that are daily made by gardeners and other persons, without quality, on their community, if they are not given a name. To this end, they have collected the old memoirs of their statutes and, in accordance with the custom and tradition of their community, have had new ones drawn up, which they have most humbly begged Us to grant them and order them to execute. And having been informed that this community has been established in our good city of Paris for a very long time, that they have their confraternity established in the church of Saint- Leufroy, where they are accustomed to have services said every Sunday for our prosperity; that they have continued to elect jurors every year, to receive maitresses and to take on apprentices, that it would be unjust for the loss of their statutes, which is more excusable, to cause the ruin of the community and state of those who compose it, the just and innocent means of earning their living and supporting their families. Having also considered that the art of flower girls provides a very pleasant and innocent ornament for people of status, and often even serves pious uses of religion, being used to adorn altars and churches, and that it is in the public interest and of our grace, to preserve all the trades formerly established, and give them a means to support themselves in the true rules of justice and their establishment. 909 To these causes. Donné à Versailles le vingt-et-unième jour d'août, l'an de grâce mil-six-cent-solxante-dix-sept, et de notre règne, le trente-cinquième. These new statutes show that the community of bouquetières is completely outside the gardener's guild, since article ten prohibits gardeners from selling their flowers anywhere other than at the Halles and Quai de la Mégisserie. There is no longer any mention, as in the first statutes, of a ban on Sunday flower-picking in the courtyards. Under Louis XIV, this community consisted solely of flower merchants, not gardeners. The communities of master gardeners, bouquetières and regratiers des fruits esgruns (I) were classified as small trades. In the letters patent of Louis XI. containing the distribution of bourgeois, merchants and artisans, under certain banners, for the guard and safety of the city of Paris, tradesmen are divided into fifty-nine banners, according to the Reverend Father Le Masson, in his calendar of the brotherhoods of Paris. The gardeners occupied the fifty-fifth rank and the other two came after. Each banner had a principal and a sub-principal elected every year on the day after Saint John's Day. They were eligible for re-election and were required to swear an oath of loyalty to the king, and to lead the community in arms in defense of the state and the king. The banner was only to be taken out on the king's orders. It was kept by the principal, or failing him, by the sub-principal. To be eligible for these functions, one had to have been a master for at least six years. The communities of the various trades, part of the feudal organization of the Middle Ages, had their own mottos and arms, like the lords and princes of the church. (1) Sour fruits and vegetables. The coat of arms (1) of the community of master gardeners was Sable, with three garden lilies Argent, stems and leaves Vert, two in chief and one in base, and a chief Azure charged with a sun Or. Its motto, inscribed on a token dated 1556 and belonging to the Monnaie collection, depicts an axe-wielding hand amidst a garland of flowers and fruit, with the legend: Manus, fortis, divitias parat (THE STRONG HAND PREPARES RICHES) The axe, in those days, was as much a gardener's tool as the spade. We have seen in their statutes that the masterpiece required to be admitted to the master's program would today represent the work of a trellis-maker rather than a gardener. On another token, which appears to have been made when the community renewed and completed its statutes in 1599, and which became the actual legislation governing the gardener's trade until the Revolution, is written in exergue. Egestatemo, perata est manus remissa (NOVEMBER 1599) In a short work by Mr. Georges Gibault on the former community of master gardeners, published in the Journal de la Société d'Horticulture de France, February 1896, we note the festivals attended by the community in common with the communities of other trades. The pompous procession that preceded the King of France on his solemn entry into Paris was always a great feast for Parisians, and a magnificent sight to behold. The king usually spent the night at the priory of Saint-Ladre (1) Depping, Livre des Métiers. at the top of the faubourg Saint-Denis. At eight o'clock in the morning, placed on a platform, he saw the delegates of his good city parade before him: at the head the mendicant monks, then the clergy of the parishes, then the university followed by its rector, finally the bodies of the city i.e. richly costumed men, chosen from the various trades; some carried with them, either the tools or the insignia of their profession; the others organized militarily in the company of pikemen and harquebusiers, marching seven by seven with fifes and tambourines, all clad in engraved and gilded helmets and cuirasses. The procession then accompanied the king to Notre-Dame (1), through streets adorned with triumphal arches, decorated by the hands of the greatest artists. On June 16, 1549, there were fifty (2) gardeners at the entrance of King Henri II, where the contingent of each of the most powerful guilds reached eighty, while others had only five members in their ranks. The Provost of Paris set the contingent for each trade in the absolutely binding summons he sent to its leaders, and equipping the men was a heavy burden; (3) "and to provide for the fraiz, will be able to compel all those of the said mestier, both in the city and in the suburbs, the strong carrying the weak, etc.". In 1610, when Queen Marie de Médicis was due to enter the town, the gardeners were to provide twenty-six men. Together with the millers, turners, basket makers, etc., they formed a company of one hundred and sixty-four men, commanded by a captain, Sieur Mailly, a lieutenant and an ensign, both shoemakers. The company was therefore made up entirely of small tradesmen, occupying the lowest rank in the classification of trade categories. (1) Entrée de Charles IX dans Paris. (2) Histoire générale de Paris. t. 1. (3) Registre de la Ville, folio 102. 006 La Communauté des maîtres jardiniers had its office on rue des Rosiers, now part of the third arrondissement of Paris. This was more or less where they lived in the 16th century, and where their numbers were greatest. After this social and corporate account of our ancestors, it remains for us to talk about the brotherhoods. Detixed ? GOC DES ANCIENS JARDINIERS A foreigner who took a mercenary into his service, under Charlemagne, paid him three gold sous per year, 270 francs, or 0 fr. 74 per day. Needless to say, this man and his family could only eat oat bread, and not every time they were hungry(t). The polyptych of Saint-Remy de Reims (information on our ancestors' way of life in this remote era can only be found in these precious documents) sets the price of nine days' work at four deniers: 9 fr. 40, or around 1 fr. 05 per day, after the year 800. This is already a small increase on the previous example. On this subject, I'd like to borrow from my excellent friend, Mr. Georges Gibault, a little pamphlet he published in the Journal de la Société d'Horticulture de France, entitled: La condition et les salaires des anciens jardiniers. This little work is very conscientiously done, and required a great deal of research on the part of its author. It deserves to be quoted in full. If we reserve the name of horticulturist for the cultivator of plants destined for the trade, as correctness demands, all that remains to designate the horticultural professional in general is the vague term gardener; an unfortunate word if ever there was one, since it has created a regrettable confusion between the man of art truly worthy of the name and the laborer who calls himself a gardener. Indeed, the general public, always superficial, is all too unaware of the difference between the various categories of people uniformly referred to as "gardeners". However, some are eminent scholars, artists and authors; others are often distinguished specialists in a most difficult profession; as for the science of simple gardeners' helpers, it's limited to handling the spade and watering can. There can be no doubt that the ill-defined name of gardener, and the resulting disadvantageous comparison for the able practitioner, may have contributed to fixing the latter in a condition inferior to his real merit. Gardening already had against it the old prejudices that discredited work on the land; moreover, the majority of gardeners personally attached to the service of a master, found themselves placed on the fringes of domesticity, less favored than craftsmen and other tradesmen whose nature of their work made them more independent. Are these not the main causes of the current inferiority of the gardening profession? Indeed, while most tradesmen have always followed an upward trend towards a higher social status and better material conditions, gardeners have not been so happy. We must hope that the future will remedy this injustice. A great step will be taken in this direction when the gardener's merit is better known; when we can better appreciate the amount of intelligence and the variety of knowledge required by a profession for which too many people profess only the very relative esteem accorded to purely manual trades. p09 It is probable that the most ancient civilized peoples, such as the Egyptians and Assyrians, already distinguished gardeners from farmers. Tomb paintings prove that Egypt, two or three thousand years before our era, had pushed the luxury of gardens very far, whose cultivation certainly required special workers (1). The Romans used only slave labor to cultivate their gardens. Wealthy Romans owned and employed slaves of all professions, from teachers to shoemakers and gardeners, for their personal service. As a result of advances in gardening, the latter were already specialized: hortulanus seems to indicate, in the texts, the gardener in general, often also the flower gardener; olitor, which took its name from olus, vegetable, took care of the vegetable garden, and topiarius or artist gardener, took care of the decoration of pleasure gardens. In a familiar letter, Cicero wrote: "I have paid a compliment to your gardener decorator (topiarius), who has covered the entire main building with ivy, from the base to the terrace (2)". Topiarii and viridarii were particularly skilled at pruning green trees into the extravagant shapes that were so popular. Under their skilful hands, Yew and Boxwood were transformed into figures of beasts and various objects, while Rosemary and Alexandria Laurel (Ruscus hypoglos- sum) drew in huge letters the name of the gardener or that of the owner. According to Pliny and other authors, these singularities were among the main attractions of beautiful gardens at the time. Because of their highly appreciated talents, topiaries appear to have been treated with some consideration. "... There are certain slaves," says (1) Perrot et Chipiez. Histoire de l'Art dans l'antiquité, t. I, fig. 256, 257, 259, 260. (2) Cicero. Ad. Q. III, 1. Cicero, like the stewards and gardeners (topiarii), who consider themselves far above the rest... (1) ". During the first period of the Middle Ages, the gardener was found only in abbeys. Monasteries were the main centers of civilized life. They preserved the faint remnants of the arts, sciences and letters that had escaped barbarism. As the monastic rule imposed manual labor almost everywhere, gardening was in the limelight. A monk, who was one of the abbey's principal officers, was responsible for overseeing the cultivation of crops; he took the title of hortulanus or gardinarius (2); at the same time, he oversaw the orchards, vineyards and ponds; it was his duty to ensure the decoration of the church with appropriate flowers (3). To carry out the work, he employed religious courtilliers (curticularii) (4); he also had recourse to the corvées owed by the abbey's serfs (5). It's only in the 14th and 15th centuries that the documents preserved in the archives, especially the expense accounts, which are of such great help in understanding private life in the Middle Ages, begin to shed some light on the living conditions of the ancient gardeners. The first forerunners of our modern gardeners to appear in history were, of course, the gardeners of the great lords. The insecurity of feudal times meant that only kings and princes could establish which(1) Cicero, Paradox. v, 2. (2) Gardener; word derived from the Germanic Gartem or Garden meaning garden. (3) Alicia Amherst. A History of Gardening in England, 2nd ed. p. 10 ff. (4) Jardiniers; from courtil, still used in some provinces to designate the cultivated enclosure next to the dwelling. (5) B. Guérard. Polyptique de l'abbé Irminon, t. 1, p. 631. the outskirts of their manors or in the fortified outbuildings of castles. As a result, the documents we're talking about come mainly from the royal house of France, the powerful and sumptuous house of Burgundy, the archbishops of Rouen, King René of Anjou and other high-ranking personalities. The already great luxury began to extend to the gardens. Appropriate decoration was needed to harmonize them with the sumptuousness of the homes, furniture and clothing. As a result, from the 13th century onwards, courtyards and vineyard trellises were being built around wealthy residences. The préau, which gave rise to the modern parterre, was a lawn with geometric lines, adorned with shrubs and flowers and surrounded by grass seats. In the following century, this primitive parterre was embellished with carpentry arbors and luxurious pavilions built on artificial mounds. Then came labyrinths, trees pruned in the ancient tradition, grottoes, parquet floors and compartments. The gardening profession thus seems to have originated in the need for special men capable of adorning pleasure gardens with certain accessories which today are more the domain of landscape architects and the horticultural industry. Depending on the taste of the day, the gardener raised the clod (1) to make the courtyards, carpentry work was carried out to build rustic pavilions, trellises and arbors, green trees were sculpted and compartments were designed. Until the 17th century, the gardener seems to have been more a "trellis-maker", a garden decorator than a plant grower. In 1599, the masterpiece required by the old guild of Parisian gardeners for admission to the master's degree program still consisted of simple char(1) Sod removed with a spade. 08 penterie work. The axe was then considered the main emblem of the gardener's trade, quite distinct, it seems, from the vegetable-grower's trade in the Maîtres-Jardiniers-Préoliers-Maraïchers guild (1). If we consider that almost all of our ornamental plants were introduced recently or not very long ago, and that horticulture was in its infancy in those distant days, with neither the pruning of fruit trees nor the art of early produce, we will be less surprised to see the cultivation of many large gardens requiring only the temporary labor of simple field workers. In 1302, in Picardy, Countess Mahaut d'Artois kept "three valleys busy weeding roses" for three days (2). In 1311, she hired laborers to plant "mesliers (Néfliers) pumiers, peskiers, francs-mouriers...". (3). A large hospital, that of Hesdin (Pas-de-Calais), employed only day laborers: Year 1326 "Pour I ouvrier qui fouy les aires (qui laboure les planches" udit gardin par (pendant) v jours..." Year 1333, "pour fouyr les aires et espandre le fiens (manure), II povres vallets, cascun (chacun) XXVI journées et demi..." (4). The head gardener's auxiliaries, hired on a yearly basis, were not employed on a permanent basis either, and were also paid a daily wage. Alicia Amherst notes the same situation in England in A History of Gardening in England (2nd ed., pp. 104-105). (1). "Pour avoir coupé, latté, plié, lié et tourbé (2) la tonnelle dedans et dehors, 4 livres tournois (3)". The very frequent employment of women in garden cultivation is noteworthy. This is nothing out of the ordinary; in fact, many women still take part in the hard work in the fields today. In those days, women took on the menial tasks of gardening. Weeding and picking flowers and aromatic herbs, whose importance in the domestic economy was singularly great in the past, were mainly reserved for them. In the 14th century, in the gardens of the Château de Rouvres, belonging to the Dukes of Burgundy, women carried manure, earth and sand in baskets and on their heads. In March, the accounts show that two quarters of strawberry plants are being planted in the "jardin Madame"; young girls are busy with this work, as well as with the "esboicher" of lavender. A quarter of land is "reffouit" to plant Aulx; the men make the rays and the women plant; and the girls are busy weeding "toulz les fraisiers du jardin Madame, hoster l'erbe et ordure, nettoyer et sarcler le lis et l'osilctte"; they then pick the seeds of "pourros, blettes, parsley": they rastellent (rake) the sown beds (4). (1) Archives de la Seine-Inférieure, G. 438. (2) Sod or clod, patch of turf removed with a spade. (3) Archives de la Seine-Intérieure, G. 490. (4) E. Picard. Les Jardins du Chateau de Rouvres au XIVe siècle. weeding was done by women in the old gardens. What's more unusual is to see real women gardeners employed as heads of cultivation in royal houses. Under Louis XIV, the practice was commonplace at Versailles, the Tuileries and the Château de Saint-Germain. An earlier example can be found at Charles V's usual residence, the Hôtel Saint-Paul in Paris, as witnessed by a payment order dated 1362: "A Jehanne la Bouchière, concierge et jardinière de noz jardins de Saint-Mor (1) et de nos hostel d'emprès SainctPaul-lès-Paris, vous bailliez.... the sum of thirty-six livres on what li is or could be due because of the works she has done and is doing from day to day in our said garden (2)". We can assume that, despite her gender, Jeanne la Bouchière possessed the qualities that make an excellent gardener: hard work, order and good taste, since her services were so highly appreciated. Later, when Charles V extended his Hôtel Saint-Paul, a gardener was needed. In 1377, we find the first mention of a gardener named Jehannin for this royal residence, who received a salary of 140 francs that year (3). Another of Charles V's gardeners earned 60 écus a year. This brings us to the difficult question of valuing, in modern money, the salaries of these gardeners of past centuries; a valuation that can only be approximate, for while on the one hand we can know the intrinsic value of ancient coins, which is determined by weight and title, on the other hand we can only guess at their value in terms of money. (1) Hôtel enclavé dans la propriété royale, whose buildings and gardens entirely covered the present-day Saint-Paul district. (2) Bibl. nationale, Mss. fonds fr. 25,701, n° 195. (3) L. Delisle. Mandements de Charles V. 6C 3 The same cannot be said for the power of money, in other words, the purchasing power of money (1). However, despite the varying assessments of historians and economists on the relative value of ancient currencies, it is generally agreed that free labor was well paid in the Middle Ages. The gardener's situation must not have been bad, any more than that of other workers. The scarcity of labor, due to the small size of the population, kept wages high - a reaper, for example, earned around 5 francs a day in our currency in 1200. - But the reign of Louis XII saw the peak of high wages. With the growth of the population and the discovery of America, wages began to fall steadily, while the price of basic necessities rose. As a result, due to the low price of food and basic necessities, the worker of the Middle Ages was happier than the worker of the Ancien Régime, and even than the worker of the first half of this century. A gardener who was paid 3.50 francs a day in the 14th century received only 2.10 francs in the 16th. Employed on a year-round basis, such servants were paid 330 francs in 1490, and 227 francs a century later (2). Today, leaving aside those of the privileged, gardeners' salaries range from 1,100 to 2,400 francs a year, with accommodation but without food. They must always have varied according to eircumstances; perhaps the differences were even more appreciable in the past, but it is impossible to account for them exactly because of the difficulty we have mentioned of knowing the relative value of money. According to accounts compiled by M. E. Picard, the wages of Maître Jehan, one of the Duke of Burgundy's gardeners, amounted to 60 francs in 1372; he also received 30 francs from the Duchess for the upkeep of the "jardin Madame". In this garden, gallantly reserved for the mistress of the house, roses, flowers and aromatic plants were cultivated for many domestic uses: flower hats, jonchées, the manufacture of perfumes and eau de toilette. Sweets, of course, were not forgotten, as strawberry plants played an important role. Payments in kind were customary at the time. In 1338, gardeners at the aforementioned Château de Rouvres took on the upkeep of the gardens for the company. The contract stipulated that they would receive certain products in kind: "XII aunes de drap et III émines de froment." Under a contract signed with Gillet Desportes on August 18, 1453, this gardener-eoncierge undertook to guard the manor of Chanzé, belonging to René d'Anjou, and to maintain the gardens for 10 livres a year (about 180 francs today), plus 2 pipes of wine, 1 pig, and 6 setiers of rye (1). In 1375, Amiot Arnaut, receiver of Philippe-le-Hardy, Duke of Burgundy, noted in his accounts: "To the gardener of the Seigneur de la Rivière, for the gift that Monseigneur li made when he came to enjoy the gardens of the said Seigneur: I franc". In 1384, the new petits Pois appeared on May 17, and "Jean du Jardin" re(1) Archives du Pas-de-Calais, A. 315. (2) A. Lecoy de la Marche. Extraits des comptes et mémoriaux du roi René, p. 103. On this occasion, Q received 16 sous as a gift from the Duke of Burgundy (1). Considering the obscure workers whose memory we are recalling, it seems that they must have occupied a very modest place in the society of their time. From the 16th century onwards, however, we see a number of people who have risen far above their predecessors, despite being called gardeners themselves. The great movement produced by the Renaissance brought about a renewal in architecture and, consequently, in the art of gardening. The gardener-artist appeared at this time, intent on creating a setting around the chateau or bourgeois residence that highlighted the architectural lines of the edifice, while delighting the eye with decoration executed according to the fashion of the day. Among those garden designers who were the predecessors of Le Nôtre and La Quintinie, and whose names are virtually unknown, is Pierre de Mercolienne. He was Louis XII's principal gardener, taking the title of Doyen du Plessis, gardener to the king. He received 116 livres 5sols (approx. 1600 francs) for his fees from January to July 1506, when he laid out and planted the gardens of the Château de Gaillon, owned by the cardinal minister, Georges d'Amboise (2). In the 16th century, gardeners and hydraulic engineers from Italy were highly regarded. In 1570, Bernard de Caruesse, "intendant des plants du jardin des Thuilleries", was in charge of the garden's planting and upkeep, receiving a salary of 400 livres a year (around 3,800 francs) (3). Another Italian family, the Francini, was called to France by Henri IV; for almost two centuries, its members directed the construction of the (1) Mémoires de l'Académie des sciences, arts et belles-lettres de Dijon, 1858-59, p. 112. (2) A. Deville. Comptes de dépenses de la construction du château de Gaillon, p. 86. (3) Léon de Laborde. Les comptes des bâtiments du roi (1528-1571), t. II, p. 348. D0 caves, water distribution and elevation in the royal parks. According to the information provided by the Comptes des bâtiments du roi, the organization of work in the royal gardens was based on the entrepreneurial principle. A plant and garden steward appears to have exercised general supervision. Most often, he was a designer of embroidered and compartmentalized flowerbeds. Next in line were the regular gardeners. Each was assigned to a specific department, and was responsible for the upkeep or, to use the official term, "maintenance" of part of the garden, in return for an annual allowance. A commitment (1) determined the obligations of the person entrusted with the upkeep of the flowerbeds, orangeries, fruit trees, paths and palisades. Palisades were the name given to the straight hedges or walls of greenery made up of pruned trees or shrubs: hornbeam, yew, boxwood, etc., which lined the paths in the old French gardens. In 1541, "Quentin l'African, dit le More", whose name fairly indicates a foreign origin, was responsible for maintaining the garden of the Enclos de l'Etanq, at the Fontainebleau palace. The payment order concerning him (2) grants him 240 livres per year, both for his wages and for the cultivation costs and salary of his assistants. The royal scribe also reminded him that he must not sell or give away any of the garden's produce; he could only take what he needed. The administration of the kings of France was singularly paternal to the servants of the royal estates. Under these conditions, the gardener's job became a kind of function or office that was passed on in survivorship (1) J. Guiffrey. Comptes des bâtiments du roi sous Louis XI)', t. I, p. 806. (2) Léon de Laborde. Les comptes des bâtiments du roi (1528-1571), t. I, p. 175. 208 from father to son. Dynasties of gardeners thus succeeded one another right up to the end of the monarchy, at the Louvre, Fontainebleau, Château de Saint-Germain and other locations. Let's just mention the Saguières and Nivelon families, whose names already appear in the Comptes des bâtiments du roi in the 16th century. The Mollets succeeded one another from father to son at the Louvre and the Tuileries. The Bouticourt family were caretaker-gardeners at the Palais-Royal. The La Lande family was so numerous in Saint-Germain that it's hard to distinguish the different members of the family (t). The principle of survivorship in the position of royal gardener was so well established that it extended to the widow, sons-in-law and even unmarried daughters of the deceased gardener. As a result of this singular practice, many women were entrusted with the direction of a crop or maintenance, in the gardens of Louis XIV's châteaux. According to the Comptes des bâtiments, Françoise Lenostre, from the family of the famous garden designer and widow of Simon Bouchard, was in charge of the Orangerie des Tuileries around 1665, after her husband's death: her daughters succeeded her in this role in 1672. In the same garden, in 1667, the widow Carbonnel was entrusted with the upkeep of various paths and palisades. At Fontainebleau, the widow Debray was responsible for half of the large parterre known as the Tybre; the widow Le Fèvre was responsible for the fruit trees (2). At the Château de Saint-Germain, in 1681, three gardeners' widows were employed in the same capacity. In 1749, Sieur Boisvinet "and his mother" were in charge of the Chancellerie and Chenil gardens at Versailles, while the widow Pimpare tended the garden at Plessis-les-Tours, an old (1) J. Guiffrey. Comptes des bâtiments du roi sous Louis XIV, t. I, p. XL. (2) J. Guiffrey. Comptes des bâtiments du roi sous Louis XIV, t. I, p. 74, 147, 214, 654, 750. C château inhabited by Louis XI. and probably quite neglected at the time (1). The traditional love of the job was a characteristic trait of the old gardeners. Usually, in provincial châteaux, the care of the garden did not leave the same family, as long as the lineage of these devoted servants lasted (2). They were no longer privileged; they fell into the category of gardeners known as "bourgeois house gardeners", a category that became numerous with the development of wealth in the middle class. Records of these modest gardeners became commonplace from the 17th century onwards. But it's mainly in notaries' minute books, so rich and so little explored, that researchers can find traces of their existence (3). In those days, gardeners were employed by private individuals for a period of varying length, and their employment was enshrined in a notarial deed. Today, lack of job stability, the independent spirit of employers and employees, and other factors have led to the abandonment of old customs and practices. However, some provinces have retained the use of year-round rental contracts for domestic servants and farm workers. Among so many acts of this kind, a contract from 1583 seems typical enough for us to dispense with citing others: "On April 10, 1583, according to a contract signed in Paris, with Bergeon, notary: Bernardin Prévost, knight, king's advisor, etc., and his wife Madeleine Potier, made the following agreements with Nicolas Yon, master gardener of Paris: Yon undertakes to cultivate and maintain the adjoining garden (1) L'Etat de la France, par les Bénédictins de Saint-Maur, t. I, p. 402 et 468. (2) A. Rabeau, Les Artisans et les domestiques d'autrefois, p. 307. (3) Louis Morin. Notes sur les jardiniers troyens, in Annales de la Soc. hort. de l'Aube, t. IX, n° 8. 08 to Sieur Prévost's Charenton country house; he undertakes to maintain the borders, supply flower and vegetable seeds, mow, fill and trim the beds and compartments, plow the vegetable gardens in due season, prune and prune the fruit and other trees, clean and rake the paths, maintain the palisades, in a word, keep the garden in good and sufficient condition. The owners are careful to specify which plants are grown on their estate and which must be returned when the contract is terminated or ends. These are, for the borders: Rosemary, Thyme, Lavender, Aspic, Marjoram, Sage, Rue, and IIysope. Roses come in two varieties: white and red. Ornamental and greenhouse shrubs include orange, lemon and pomegranate trees. There's a big difference between "anthés" or grafted orange and lemon trees, and wild ones. Sieur Yon may not touch the products of the former; as for the sauvageons and "pouceuses" (the name given to the cuttings in the deed), he is free to sell them and make use of them, provided he returns the same number when the contract comes to an end. The number of grafted orange trees is not specified in the deed; the wild ones are quite numerous; there are 18 very large ones, 76 in pots and an almost equal number in tubs where they are several together. As an interesting detail, let's note Madame Prévost's reserve of fragrant plant clippings and rose blossoms; she probably used them to make eau de toilette and those ointments and unguents with which all bourgeois homes were abundantly furnished. Mr. Yon would have no right to the fruits either, which were all reserved for the owners, especially the Grapes. The fruit trees formed avenues; Sieur and Madame Prévost reserve the right to increase the number and extent of these avenues as they see fit, without Sieur Yon, who receives no income from them but must nevertheless maintain them, having the right to complain. Finally, the owners reserve the right to take any vegetables and salads they please. The remainder will belong to Sieur Yon, whose share, as we can see, is not very large, since it consists only of the sale of orange and lemon trees (I) that he can produce by cuttings or seedlings, and in what the owners do not want in the way of vegetables. The sieur and dame Prévost sign in fine handwriting, and poor Yontrace struggles underneath with a spade, an instrument of his trade which he undoubtedly knows better than a pen" (2). The conditions, as well as the often multiple and meticulous obligations imposed on gardeners, were bound to vary according to circumstances. Certain deeds illustrate the long duration of commitments to domestic service, and the persistent custom of payment in naturc. In 1638, Louis de Proisy, baron de la Bove, signed an agreement with his gardener, Louis Normand, notary in Bouconville, for the cultivation of his gardens for six years. The gardener will have "6 cartels" of rye per year, a two-month-old pig, the surplus of vegetables not needed for the Lord's kitchen, and half of the apples and pears (3). Other contracts obviously included wages in money; but, as we have already said, the worker's pecuniary situation during the last two centuries was not advantageous, whereas in the past, wages of 2 or 3 sous a day represented, in reality, a daily pay perhaps higher than that of today. (1) It's true that they were very expensive in those days. (2) Magasin pittoresque, 1888, p. 182. (3) Archives de l'Aisne, E. 412. For gardeners employed on a daily basis, we find in Orleans (1), in 1602, salaries of 8 sous (O fr. 95); 12 sous in 1660, or O fr. 97 only, following the gradual lowering of the rate of the monetary unit, the livre tournois, which became the current franc at the time of the Revolution. In Normandy, in 1731, gardener's days cost 20 sous or a livre (O fr. 95) (2) and in 1734, a salary of 6 sous (barely 0 fr. 30) (3). By way of comparison, at the same time and in the same region, a carpenter earned 16 sous, a bricklayer 12 and a washerwoman toiled all day to collect 3 sous. From 1600 to the beginning of this century, the purchasing power of money was, depending on the year, two or three times greater than today. Multiplying the above figures by two or three would give the equivalent in today's money. These were meagre salaries. If we move on to gardeners paid by the year, we see a further extreme diversity in wage rates. In Rouen, in 1669-1685, a gardener at the Jesuit College received 40 livres (60 francs) (4). In the same city, at the Nouvelles-Catholiques convent, wages were 120 livres (177 francs) in 1684 (5). According to a register of wages paid to employees of the Abbey of Saint-Martin-au-Bois, in 1685, Antoine Montigny served as gardener at a wage of 100 livres (118 francs) and food; in 1699, he was reduced to 75 livres a year "if he is given wine, and 105 livres if he is not". Double or triple these figures, which only indicate the intransigent value, to get roughly the equivalent in today's money. 5) Id. at D. 497. 99 still only 75 livres, and the gardener's assistant is entitled only to food and "something a year to help him maintain himself (1)". In one château, around 1738, the gardener received 100 livres (around 100 francs), while the cook and valet were paid 150 livres and the coachman 120 (2). During the course of the 18th century, other gardeners were paid varying wages of 200, 250, 400 livres, and even 850 livres with food, by a Marquis de la Ferté in 1767. This generous landowner, who paid his gardener so well, granted only 350 livres to the cook, 300 to the valet and 80 to the coachman (3). More favored in terms of wages were the royal gardeners, to whom we referred earlier. According to the Comptes des bâtiments du roi Louis XIV, merit and talent were also rewarded with generous bonuses. La Quintinie's fees amounted to 2,000 livres a year, usually doubled by a bonus of the same amount - 12 to 13,000 francs of our currency. But the elimination of part of the salary was just punishment for negligence and carelessness in service. In these registers, so interesting for the history of gardening under Louis XIV, we read during the year 1679: "To François Francine, for the upkeep of the fountains and grottoes of the Château de Saint-Germain: 1,200 livres; given the decline of most of the grottoes, he will only be paid 800". The widow Bellier, in charge of maintaining the château's vegetable garden, also saw her wages reduced from 600 to 450 livres (4). André Le Nôtre, the great garden architect, received 1,200 livres per year. Charles Mollet, another draughtsman (1) Archives de l'Oise, Il. 382, 406, 407. (2) H. de La Ferrière-Percy, Journal de la comtesse de Sanzay, p. 94. (3) Archives de l'Eure-et-Loir, E. 1668. (4) J. Guiffrey. Comptes des bâtiments du roi sous Louis XIV, t. 1, p. 1221. O8 de parterres, had 1,000 livres. Fleurant, gardener at Château de Clagny, had a salary of 1,200 livres, as did Trumel, gardener at the Pépinière Royale du Roule, an establishment that seems to have been the beginning of a kind of horticultural school. Louis XIV's concern for everything to do with gardening led to the appointment of a 300-lb. teacher, Sieur de Beaulieu, who taught writing and arithmetic to the boy gardeners (1). If the elementary education of gardeners in the good old days left much to be desired, their professional ability was also much weaker than in other professions. Nothing was rarer than a gardener skilled in his art. And nowhere were the conditions for learning the trade more defective, until quite recently. With the exception of a certain amount of practice in manual work, which is easy to acquire, what kind of students could be provided by routine teachers who, like all ignoramuses, were full of presumption and stubbornness! Under the old monarchy, the only enlightened teachers came from the royal gardens. The more we study the history of gardening in France, the more we will recognize that the pronounced taste of most of the kings of France for beautiful gardens was, in this country, the main driving force behind the progress of horticulture. Do we not owe the admirable creations of Le Nôtre and La Quintinie to the generous initiative of one of them? (1) J. Guiffrey. Comptes des bâtiments du roi sous Louis XIV, t. 1, p. 294, 1089, 1350. ANCIENS TRAITÉS DE COURTILLAGE The oldest treatise on courtillage written in French, that we have found, was published in 1393, and is signed: un bourgeois de Paris. At that time, it's almost certain that none of our ancestors could read or write. Looking closely at his work, this bourgeois de Paris must have been a judge at the Châtelet, probably living next door to some intelligent marager who dictated the cultivation he did. When those in the trade read this treatise, which we will reproduce verbatim, they will easily recognize the hand of a fairly skilled practitioner for his time. TRAITÉ DE COURTILLAGE Primo. - It should be noted that whatever is sown, planted or buried, must be sown, planted or buried in damp weather, in the evening or in the morning, before the heat of the sun and during the waning of the moon, and must be watered on the pié and the soil and not on the leaf. Item - When the sun is shining, it must not be watered, but in the evening and in the morning, nor must cabbage, percil or other greens be watered, as the heat of the sun would bake the cut and the soil, and so it would never be watered in this area of the cut. Note: In rainy weather, it's a good idea to plant, but not to sow, as the seed is retained by the ratel. From All Saints' Day onwards, there are bog beans, but to prevent them from freezing, we plant them around Christmas and in January and February, and at the beginning of March; and we plant them at different times, so that if some are frozen, others are not, and when they rise from the ground, as soon as they begin to grow, we must harrow them and break the first germ, and as soon as they have six leaves, we must sourfouir them (put earth on top). And of all these, the first to come are the most expensive, and must be eaten the day they are harvested, or else they will turn black and sour.
