Item - You can look under the leaves of the choulx and there you'll find a large gathering of white mittens in a heap, and know that this is where the caterpillars come from, and for this reason, cut off the place where this seed is and getter loing. Leeks should be sown in season, then replanted in October and November. If you want to have seedless grapes, take in crescent (of the moon), in the time that the vine is planted. That is to say, in February, plant a vine with the root, and bone the shovel on one side and the other. Then gnaw the stock and bind all along with black thread, then plant the stock and smoke with good manure and stuff with earth the hole above the joint of the stock. If you want to enter a cherry or plum tree on and inside a vine stock, then in March split it at four canes near the end and bone the mould on both sides, and there make the place of the kernel of a cherry stone and put and enclave it in this slot and bind with thread the jointed stock as before. If you want to insert a vine into a cherry tree, prune the vine, which will be planted and rooted for a long time next to the cherry tree, and in March around Notre-Dame (the Annunciation, March 25), pierce the cherry tree with an auger of the size of the vine stock, and in the hole of the cherry tree, bend the vine stock so that it is at least one foot long, then cut it on both sides of the cherry tree ; i.e. clay, moss and drapeaults, so that no rain can reach the hole. Item - The vine stock must be escorched and its bark peeled and ossified down to the green, in all only as far as the inside of the cherry tree is concerned, for if this is done and the bark is peeled and ossified, the sharp end of the stock joining the sharp end of the cherry tree will consolidate one to the other, which would be prevented by the bark of the stock if it remained there. Once this is done, leave them together for two years and then cut the vine behind and below the cherry tree joint. Item. - On a trunk or stump of oak, bury ten or twelve trees; that is to say, in the month of March, around Notre-Dame, garnish with as many grafts of various fruits as you wish to bury, and saw through the oak or tree you wish to bury; and have your grafts trimmed on one side, as much only as a blind quince, if as it is cy, and so much that the bark of iceluy graft is whole of the one of the sides and without being escorchée, or started, then ficherez your grafts between the bark and the flesh, or the sharp of the graft towards wood, or the sharp of the oak. Then trim and cover with loam, moss and drappeaults, so that rain, snow or frost can't interfere. Si vous voulez garderroses en yver prenez sur le rosier petits boutons qui ne soient pas espanis et les laissiez les queues longues, et entassez en un petit tonnelet de bois, comme un tonnelet à composte et sans cau. Make sure the barrel is well sunk and tightly bound so that nothing can get in or out, and bind two large, heavy stones to the two ends of the barrel, and place the barrel in a running river. Rosemary - Gardeners say that the seed of rosemary does not come from the soil of France, but whoever plucks and desmensbreroit from a rosemary tree, old small sprigs and stretched them by the end and planted them, they would come back, and whoever wants to send them far away, J'ai oüi dire à monseigneur de Berry que en Auxergne à trop grosses cerises que en France pour ce qu'ils provignent leurs cerisiers. This document is the oldest we have been able to find specifically concerning market gardening. Some of the accounts and expenses of large houses or congregations also contain chapters on gardening, indicating the crop grown at the time, but in a more indirect way. One of the first practicing writers to deal with D00 vegetable growing, and even more generally, is Olivier de Serres (1). Although he was more of a farmer than a gardener, he nevertheless deals with the cultivation of all the vegetables known in his day. The time of sowing, the various care to be given to them, is more or less what we saw in the previous treatise. But where Olivier de Serres becomes useful to history is in indicating the introduction of new vegetables and plants. He mentions a new cabbage grown in his country. The Caulifion of the Italians, which is indeed our cauliflower, he predicts a bright future for it, given that its beauty alone, and its delicacy to boot, will see it accepted on every table. The famous agronomist with his cartouffle, which is indeed our potato, probably so small at the time and of low yield, did not have the same eye as for his caulifiori, and yet the former spread millions of times more than the latter. It was certainly impossible at the time to judge the bright future of the illustrious solanée. He also discusses Nicotiana, named after Jean Nicot, King Henri's ambassador to Portugal. This plant," he says, "is native to hot countries, so it must be sown in the shelter of the north. The smoke of patum mêlo, also known as tobacco, taken through the mouth with an appropriate cone, is good for the brain, eyesight, hearing and teeth, and relieves the stomach of phlegm when taken in the morning on an empty stomach. Nor could he have foreseen the source of wealth for his country that this plant would be a few centuries after him. He was one of the first to deal with the cultivation of melons, even though this plant had already been known for some time. We'll talk about this crop in another chapter. (1) Théâtre de l'Agriculture The reader has already seen, and will see again in the remainder of this work, that the ancients attached great importance to the different phases of the moon when sowing and planting. Olivier de Serres does not combat these influences, but he is right to say that "we must not put off until the new moon what we can do at the end of the previous one". One of the principles of mastery, he says, is to know how to sow your seeds in good time to obtain good plants, according to the climate in which they are grown. As for the gardeners working in the gardens of the great, Audiger's Maison Régée, published in 1527, will teach us the knowledge required of them to hold these jobs. At the time, this knowledge was such that anyone wishing to engage in this art had to study in depth with different masters, to learn all that was required of him. You had to know a great deal about agriculture; these ancients weren't yet talking about horticulture, despite the fact that the Romans had had a fairly advanced horticulture several centuries earlier. Knowing how to graft in slits and escutcheons, knowing about graincs, onions, cayeux, flowers and so on. In France, when it was fashionable to have trellises and cradles, the gardener had to know how to manage and care for them. He was also required to have a good kitchen garden, growing all the vegetables known at the time. He was also responsible for purchasing the merry-go-rounds and osiers he might need for his trellises and cradles. The gardener, says d'Audiger, "is often a servant of the house. His duty is to take care of the garden when he is in charge. He must know agriculture, both for trees and flowers, and know how to decorate the garden, according to the times and seasons; he must know all kinds of plants and fruits; he must know how to graft well and enter into splits and escutcheons on trees and wild plants, such as pear trees, cognac trees, etc.; he must keep the garden tidy and tidy. He must always keep 908 flowerbeds clean, mow boxwoods at the right time, know about seeds, onions, cayeux and rare and foreign flowers, and know how to sow and plant them well, so that they come up again in spring. He must not be lazy, to water his garden during the great droughts. He must also know how to prepare potting soil, both for orange trees and for late bloomers. He must know his way around orange trees, how to clean them of bugs and prevent ants from spoiling them. Don't get them too wet, lest they die, and put them in the greenhouse in winter and outside in summer. He must take care to clean the alleys properly, prune the trees and trellises when they need it, as well as the trellises and cradles. Plow the paths and resoil them from time to time. He must take care to smoke the base of fruit trees from three years to three years, removing the wood that embarrasses them too much, which prevents the fruit from growing. Weed the flowerbeds, remove the weeds that choke the flowers, and keep the ponds and fountains in good condition, if any. The gardener must also know how to make a good vegetable garden, sow, plant and bring in all kinds of vegetables, make good beds to bring in melons, not let his garden run out of anything, have artichokes, celery and cardoons, and always lettuces according to the weather, keep everything very clean, get rid of all sorts of nasty weeds, and remove caterpillars, snails and other vilenesses which often do great damage to gardens and destroy the most important things in them". You couldn't ask much more of a gardener these days, for to know all these different branches of horticulture in depth, it's scarcely necessary to have a man to learn them. And yet, after d'Audiger, it was a servant who was asked for this amount of horticultural instruction, when it should have been said that this man was an artist in his art, worthy of standing alongside the greatest artists in the various other arts. 28 Until the end of the 17th century, no specialist writer, apart from Olivier de Serres who wrote on agriculture in general, dealt specifically with horticulture. The most complete treatise on this art under the ancien régime was written by Claude Mollet, gardener to Louis XIII, and published by André Mollet, probably his son and gardener to Queen Christine of Sweden, around 1700. Here is the extract from the prévilège: Nous, Christine, par la grâce de Dieu, reine de Suède, etc., à tous ceux qui ces présentes lettres verront, salut : Honorable et industrieuse personne André Mollet, maistre de nos jardins; s'étant proposé de faire imprimer un traité qu'il a fait en trois langues, se scavoir: suédoise, allemande et française de la culture, utilité, plaisir et ornement des jardins, intitulé: le Jardin de plaisir, et désirant sur ce, nos lettres nécessaires. We have authorized and do authorize by these presents signed by our hand, that he may print, sell and distribute the said book called: le Jardin de plaisir, however and during the term of ten years, starting from the day and date that the said book will be completed to print, making for this purpose very express inhiliations and deffenses to all booksellers and printers or others of what quality and condition they are of our kingdom, country and land of our obeyssance, to print or make print the said book, or to bring some or make bring from outside, under the name interposez to sell and distribute them in our kingdoms, without the permission of the said Mollet or his heirs, on pain of a fine of 400 dol of silver, half of which will be payable to our Chamber of Accounts, and half to the said suppliant without any reduction and costs, damages and interest and confiscation of all copies which may be put up for sale, or otherwise against the content of these presents. For such is our pleasure. Given at Stokholm, March 12, 1651. TRAITÉ D'HORTICULTURE DE CLAUDE MOLLET CHAPTER PREMIER To fertilize the land, use preferably ox and sheep manure: for bedding, horse manure; to improve the land, let the manure season and rot in a low place in winter; it will have less vermin and be fuller of humours for our use. Pigeon droppings mixed with fallow deer, deer or sheep droppings are very good for orange trees. As for pig manure and fecal matter, they would be rather harmful to gardening. The best season for fertilizing is autumn. CHAPTER II OF THE PINEGARDEN Prepare the soil and its amendments for all species of trees in a place close enough to water, make boards four to five feet wide, sow the seeds in rays made neatly a foot apart. Other beds for pits, such as cherries and plums. Large pits, such as apricots, peaches and almonds, should be planted and soaked in water beforehand, or in fresh sand to soften them. If you have rare plants, put them in pots or crates. All these operations should be carried out during the new moon. The following year, the young trees should be transported in a straight line, in small rows about four feet wide and one foot apart, and the second year they can be grafted. Regetons can also be used, but it should be noted that they will not make such good plantos. CHAPTER III GRAFTING FRUIT TREES CHAPTER IV VARIOUS TYPES OF GRAFTS CHAPTER V VINES. - How to plant, prune and propagate grapevines Grapevines can also be grafted in a slit or approach, as long as it is as low as possible. The way to get black and white grapes on the same vine. Plant two vines right next to each other, weld them together with wax, so that they form a single stock, producing different grapes. CHAPTER VI ROOTS AND WEEDS Choose a damp spot, put in a lot of manure, plough and level the ground to sow and plant according to the weather. Let's consider when to sow and replant the most useful vegetables in the kitchen garden. First. - Onions are sown around the August moon and replanted around the March moon. All kinds of onions can also be sown around the February moon in temperate areas; but in cold countries, they can only be sown in March, when the ground has thawed, and as soon as possible so that they can be replanted and grow. Leeks should be sown on the new moon in March, as early as possible in these quarters, so that they have time to strengthen and be replanted on the new moon in June. It should be put in the ground early enough to turn white, as it is best eaten in soup during the winter, when no other herbs are available. It keeps well in sand in the greenhouse, and lasts until spring. Garlic is more suitable for planting than for sowing, because it multiplies strongly in its bulb, as do the small ones (cieüe); this is why it is more expedient to pluck and replant them. It should be noted that all these roots so infect the soil where they have been, that it must be purified and amended before sowing or planting anything else. Beet is a very good and healthy root, and is sown in the full moon, as early as possible in the spring. Turnips can also be sown when the moon is full, but to get them early, they need to be sown on layers of manure; similarly, to get them in any season, they need to be sown every month on earth, and require sandy soil. Parsnips and carrots should be sown during the febrier moon in temperate countries. But in these areas, it's best to sow them before winter, otherwise they won't have time to grow. Chereus, which we call sweet roots here, are very good and multiply greatly, which is why they cannot be escharpir and replanted rather than sown, and this in the new moon of March. Wild chicory is sown in the full moon of August. Selciferia is sown in the full moon of March, or earlier, as are the roots of coarse parsley. After the roots come the herbs of the kitchen garden. These include cauliflower, white cabbage, Savoy cabbage, green cabbage, red cabbage and others, but cauliflower is the most delicate to eat, and is also the most susceptible to the ravages of time, and for this reason it should be sown on layers of horse manure at the end of the March moon, and replanted in the full month of May, in well-smoked and prepared soil, and to preserve it in (hyver), it should be replanted in sandy soil. Apple cabbages, both red and white, can be sown at the end of summer, when the moon is full, and replanted with new ones in spring. Milan or Savoy cabbages, and all others generally, can be sown in spring on the full moon of March, for replanting on the full moon of May. Lettuce is a very good herb, both as a salad and cooked in a pot. There are several species, and to get them soon, they are sown on layers of manure, in the new moon, as soon as possible. It is replanted in the course of the same or following moon. But to have them all summer long, you have to sow them every new moon of the month and replant them at the end of the month. There is another kind of lettuce called romaine or spanish, which is an excellent salad, it is sown in the month of march, and replanted in the new moon of may. Bind with straw to blanch. Endive is also very good, both in salads and cooked. We have two kinds, one curly and the other not; the first is the better. It is sown at the time of the moon, and replanted at the time of the moon when the cold has passed. It can be tied with straw to whiten it, and the one you wish to keep for (hyvuer), should be sown around August. Then, when the frosts start to come, transplant it in sand into the soil, taking care that it doesn't get wet when tightened. The best for this purpose is frisée. Borage and bugloss are sown on the new moon in March, and are good and refreshing in soup. They have small blue flowers, which are very suitable for salads and tables. 908 Spinach, parsley, chervil and sorrel are sown at the new moon in August and March; what is sown in March goes to seed in August, so can only be used until July and August; but what is sown in August does not go to seed and can be used in autumn and even in winter. Poirée à large cosse, or white chard, is sown during the March moon, and is also replanted during the following moon. Pimprenelle is sown on the new moon in March or April, as are round sorrel, stag's horn, watercress and savory. Purslane is excellent both in salads and pickled in vinegar for (hyvuer). It is sown on the new moon of March, or April in these quarters on layers of manure, to have some early, and consecutively every new moon, you can sow some to have some at all times. As for tripe-madame, tarragon, mente, baulme, coq and petites cieüses, they can be sown sooner (escharpir) than planted, multiplying greatly in their roots, they are transplanted in the full moon of March or April. It should be noted that to sow early in these cold countries, it is a good idea to place two or three feet of newly made horse manure on the site to be sown during the winter, so that the warmth of the manure prevents frost from penetrating the soil; then, when the soil is lifted in spring, it will be ready to sow whatever you wish. CHAPTER VII The fruits of the kitchen garden are : melons, cucumbers, pumpkins, artichokes, peas, broad beans and other vegetables, of which the melon is quite difficult to get in some places in temperate countries, and consequently much more so in these northern climates; therefore great care must be taken to grow good ones; and this will be done by means of layers of long horse manure, about three feet high and four wide, on the surface of which we'll put four to five inches of compost, or small used manure, taking good care that the layers are sheltered from the north, as much as possible, and well exposed to the midday, then when they have passed their great burning heat (which can be seen three or four days later by sticking your finger in them), we'll sow melons in them; we'll sow melons in them shortly beforehand, in small holes in order, and two seeds at D8 each hole, and this during the March moon, fitting glass or panes of glass over the said layers to prevent the winds from harming them, and to receive the sun's rays more forcefully; then cover them at night with straw mats to protect them from frost; and when they have three or four leaves, replant them on other layers lower than the first ones and without small manure, ploughing the underside of them, so that the roots of the melons can take nourishment from the earth. They are to be watered often, but first the water must be seasoned by the sun's rays, and by arrousing them without wetting either the leaf or the fruit. Then, when it's time to warm them up, put new manure all around them, about a foot wide and a little higher than them. Then it will be necessary to prune them, leaving them only three or four arms, i.e. branches, always removing the false flowers, and most of the fruit from the end of the branches, so that the fruit close to the foot can benefit more, which is usually the best. Another strong layer is used to transplant melons, which in France we call couche sourde; This is because, having dug two or three feet of earth of suitable width, a trench is made, which is filled with new horse manure and then with earth above, so that the said layers are a little flattened and taller towards the midday, and after having made holes at equal distances, the said melons are transplanted into them, taking good care, when lifting them from the first layer, to remove their roots; ains leave them a small clod, which can keep it in its first place, otherwise our labor would be vain and of no effect. Cucumbers can be seeded and replanted in the same way as melons; they are not so delicate, nor are they tender in bad weather; so they can be transplanted into the ground, with just a little manure at the base and in the hole. Pumpkins can be cultivated in the same way, but as their fruits are very heavy, it is a good idea to support them with poles, as they would take up a lot of land. They also need to be trimmed often. Artichokes are one of the best fruits in the kitchen garden. The best species is the least pungent. They can be sown in the new moon in March, and replanted when they have three or four leaves. They require 900g of rich, well-smoked, fresh, moist soil, and a good deal of watering. They can be kept on their own land by (hyvuer) surrounding them with manure and earth on top. But they will be safer in these quarters in the greenhouse, for in spring to disjoint and separate them, then transplant them as said is. Their pods are excellent to eat when blanched, thanks to the manure with which they are surrounded for twelve or fifteen days. They can also be blanched in the soil to hyvour them. Asparagus is also excellent, it is sown in the full moon of March, and replanted two years later in the same lunc ct in beds or beds well prepared and a little low, so that they can have freshness and moisture, and in these beds, it must be planted in rows and (esgalle) distance of a foot from each other. It is not required that they have more than a foot and a half of good bottom, so that they can heel more, and produce more fruit, not finding suflisant food at the bottom. Peas come in different varieties. The early ones are sown at full moon, as early as possible to have early ones. There is a type of pea that is called "sans gosse" (without kid), as it is eaten with the pea in it, and is very good. It is necessary that all beans be branched. Broad beans are planted during the April-May moon. Fennel is sown during the full moons of March and April. We'll now move on to plants and shrubs, both fruiting and sweet-smelling. Those with fruit are: the groseliers, of which there are three species, namely: the one that comes in bunches, which is white and red and very good for preserving; the third yields fruit that is quite the opposite, namely: separated from each other, it is very thorny, so its fruit is not as good as that of the other two species. The vinette thorn, or barbaris, is very good and suitable for candying, as well as for eating (crude) with meats and sauces. It is similarly thorny, but the leaf is very beautiful, so it can be planted in pallissades in some places in the pleasure garden, which it is advisable to do in the same way for (groseliers), but only in the kitchen garden. All the aforementioned shrubs provinate easily and take cuttings very well. The best-smelling shrubs are lavender, sage, rüc and rosemary, hyosope, thyme and marjoram, which are sown in the new moon of March on layers of manure, if you like, so that 200 of them come earlier, and they are replanted in the full moon. They also easily take cuttings and marcotes. CHAPTER VIII OF THE FLOWER GARDEN The main flowers grown were: roses, marigolds, peonies, wallflowers, tulips, anemones, ranunculus, irises, etc. How to sow them, how to grow them and how to plant them. How to sow, grow and reproduce them. CHAPTER IX WILD TREES Oak, chestnut, lime, elm, sycamore, beech, hornbeam, privet, white thorn. Fir, juniper, holly, boxwood, cypress, which must be protected from the cold, etc. CHAPTER X: ORANGE, LEMON AND MYRTLE TREES. SPANISH JASMINS AND OTHER RARE TREES There are two species of large and dwarf orange trees; the latter are put in pots or vases, so that they can be transported to rooms, tables, etc.; they bear many flowers and their fruit is very small. There are several species of large orange, but they are very heat-loving, and in our countries you need an orangery to hybridize them. Orange trees are easy to graft in escutcheon, approach and cleft. Pomegranates should be kept in the same way, although they are less delicate. There are two varieties, one with fruit and the other with double flowers. It can be grafted in slit or approach form. There are several species of jasmine, of which the Spanish jasmine is the most highly esteemed for its fragrant flowers. The common jasmine is the most robust, but its flowers are smaller and less fragrant. The evergreen myrthes give a good scent. Both males and females bear a small white flower, almost like a white thorn. They are easy to provine and marcott, and can also be grafted. The fig tree can be preserved in the same way and can bear fruit when ripe; it can be pickled and takes cuttings well. There are several types of laurel: common laurel, tin laurel, oleander, white laurel, red laurel and cherry laurel. The latter takes cuttings again, while the others marcott. As all these trees are in boxes, the soil must be renewed every three or four years; mix pigeon, fallow deer and sheep droppings with strong, clayey soil, and even add city sludge. CHAPTER XI DES ORNEMENTS DU JARDIN DE PLAISIR This beautiful and complete horticultural treatise is a striking proof of what we have said in placing Claude Mollet among the great gardeners of past centuries. Although this treatise was not published until 1700, it was certainly written in the first half of the 17th century, since the extract from the privilege authorizing it is dated 1651. In this work, one senses more the skilled practitioner, the man who knows by long practice of his craft, than the theorist who has less hands-on experience. We have reproduced, in extenso, what relates to market gardening, to give the reader a better idea of the progress made in this field over the two centuries between the time the treatise was written, and the time when the treatise on courtillage was published by a Parisian bourgeois in the Menagier de Paris. In the latter, there is no mention of layers; all cultivated plants came in their natural state, and the number was very limited, just a few in addition to those found in Charlemagne's capitularies. Mollet, on the other hand, shows us how to grow plants in layers, and even how to grow plants in layers in the open air. He tells us about the cultivation of melons, already done with a certain art. What's surprising about this cultivation is that Olivier de Serres, who wrote before him, mentions the glass bell and Mollet says nothing about it. It was certainly well known in the south of France, where Olivier de Serres lived, long before it was in Paris. Mollet was growing cauliflower, which was a new plant for him, when Olivier de Serres had been growing it half a century earlier. As for the unfortunate cartouffle, he didn't say a word about it; it was so hated, so feared as a violent poison, that its cultivation was banned everywhere. That's how damaging the errors of scientists and great men can be to people. Finally, Claude Mollet's work was to pave the way for the success and glory of La Quintine, France's most famous, and perhaps the world's most famous, market gardener. As far as this author is concerned, we will only talk about his new, i.e. forced, cultivation; for the others, it would be repeating what Mollet said. But before going into these details, let's examine what our illustrious colleague thought and said about gardeners, and market gardeners in particular. In his supplication to the King, he flattered himself that the treaty he had the honor of presenting to him would contribute to making good gardeners. This part of gardening, which combines pleasure with utility, provides the means to make pleasant vegetable gardens, and to raise good fruit for every season of the year. Some of the first gardeners," he says, "who settled in the vicinity of the big city, had the idea of growing a special crop of grass, anticipating that they would be able to sell it in the public markets. For these gardens, they chose low-lying areas where the soil was greasy and damp, such as dried-out marshes, or marshes that they dried out, so much so that in common parlance, they were called mareschais, or marsh gardeners. As the city's population grew, the marshes became too small to supply it with produce. Human industry has multiplied these kinds of gardens to the point of making 908 of them in very arid and sandy places, ensuring that frequent watering and ample manure fertilization make up for the lack of a good soil. So there are several categories of gardener: the mareschais, who cultivate vegetables and herbs; the florists, who are especially concerned with the cultivation of various flowers; and those who devote themselves to making nurseries, who didn't yet have a name, but who could be called, as La Quintinie put it, pépiniéristes. As for me, he says, "my particular inclination is to apply myself to the cultivation of vegetable and fruit gardens". In addition to the beauties he found in them, he also discovered considerable shortcomings, especially in the gardens of large landowners who had gardeners at their service, and his treatise, he admits himself, was aimed more at them than at specialists. I don't claim," he says, "that this type of cultivation exists today, and I confess in good faith that the knowledge I have of vegetable gardens comes particularly from having had frequent discussions with what are vulgarly called 'skilled mareschais'. Cultivation, in general, includes a large number of plants, and it is not possible for any gardener to apply himself to cultivating them all with art. Some gardeners devote themselves to a certain crop, which is well done, and neglect the rest, which is cultivated with the same art by other specialists. But the gardener who has to run a master garden in a large house must know everything he needs to produce every month of the year. The great master confesses in good faith that what he knew about vegetable gardens, he had learned from skilled mareschais. Let us hasten, for the respect we owe to his memory, to have the same good faith towards him, by declaring that his experiments in forced cultivation have become the basis for the cultivation of market gardens in our century; and have enabled several 908 thousand families, not to make a fortune (one does not make a fortune in cultivation, when that is all there is to it); but at least to raise their families honorably. This is more than enough for market gardeners to honor his memory with a title of gratitude. FORCED CULTIVATION OF QUINTINIA In January. - Lay early cucumbers, melons and lettuces. Warm up asparagus. To do this, dig the path two feet deep and a foot and a half wide, fill it with large, warm, well-burned manure, and after a fortnight, mix it with new manure. Repeat this operation as often as necessary, about every ten or twelve days. So far, modern market gardeners have not changed this method of heating asparagus. They used to cover the asparagus claws with dry manure, and when they appeared, they put a glass bell on top, which they covered with manure at night; those who had glass frames put them on top of the bells. Today, bells are no longer used for this type of cultivation; the author says that many were broken, and we have no trouble believing him. Only glass frames are used, covered with mats sufficient to withstand the most severe frosts. He claimed that a lot of new manure was coming in, and it's exactly the same now. La Quintinie is the innovator of this type of cultivation. He reheated sorrel, patience and borage beds using the same procedures. Raising strawberries on beds, to have them in April and May. He probably raised them in pots and placed them on warm beds, which he omits to mention in his treatise. Take horse or mule manure, two-thirds new (i.e. fresh), and one-third dry. Spread the manure, roll it up so that all the ends are in the middle, and the edge forms a sort of back; let it stand to even out the layer; its height should be two or three feet when you make it, and loaded with potting soil. 08 By this means, when you establish a square, the paths are empty; you fill them in as the heat drops, and if necessary you turn them over as for the paths that heat asparagus, in order to warm up the layer. This explanation was given to me by former market gardeners when I was young, as some of them established this kind of layer right up to the middle of our century. In the same way, for bell beds, the compost was placed on top of the manure, and heated using the same procedures. Market gardeners called these layers "layers à ployants". Today, labor is too expensive to use our ancestors' methods. It took two men a whole day to make a layer. With overheads rising all the time, and forced vegetables cheaper than in those days, young people have to look for ways to make a living. This is one of the main reasons why forced cultivation is no longer carried out with as much correctness as it was by the market gardeners of the first half of the century. Needless to say, La Quintinie, like his predecessor Claude Mollet, cultivated cucumbers and melons. He also grew several types of lettuce. His superiority over his predecessors lies in the fact that, in his royal kitchen garden at Versailles, he brought together all the new utensils for forced cultivation, such as cloches and glass frames. In a word, it was he who carried out the first in-depth studies for these various cultivations; he succeeded more or less well, as happens with everything that is done by trial and error. Nevertheless, the impetus was there, and his successors took advantage of his experiments to perfect his type of cultivation, and it's already been a century since market gardeners have tackled it. 14 UNTIL PHILIPPE-AUGUSTE'S CLOSURE From the time of Roman domination, the town that became the capital of France grew from strength to strength. At first, it overflowed its island, especially on the left bank. When Julian built his palace there, he brought with him a population center, particularly for the literati. It was to remain forever the school district. A little later, at an uncertain date, the city expanded a little on the right bank. Walls were built at the same time as the grand Chatelet tower to defend the city on this side. Petit Chatelet was on the left bank, at about the same point as Place du Petit Pont. Whereas under Roman rule, the city had expanded more on the left bank, the opposite occurred under the Frankish kings. It was under Childebert I that the town of Saint-Germain-l'Auxerrois came into being. The Louvre, built in 1201 under Philippe-Auguste, was constructed on land belonging to the abbey of Saint-Denis. It is almost certain that a large part of the right bank belonged to this abbey, just as the left bank belonged to that of Saint-Germain-des-Prés. Later, other, less important abbeys were founded near Paris, giving rise to the following towns: Bourg-l'Abbé, which was dependent on Saint-Martin; Beau-Bourg on the 10 Temple lands; Thiboust, which took its name from an ancient family that included Guillaume Thiboust, Provost of Paris around 1300; and Saint-Eloi, where Saint-Paul church now stands. On the left bank: the town of Saint-Germain-des-Prés; the town of Sainte-Geneviève. Between these towns and Paris, there was arable land, courtils and marshes (1). We have also seen that the kings of the first dynasty had donated land to establish the Paris cemetery and cattle market. For a long time, the term "culture" was used in the names of Paris neighborhoods and streets, as a reminder of the ancient market gardens of the time. On the north side, there was the Saint-Eloi cultivation, and near Saint-Paul church, the house of the farmer who ran it. Then there was Sainte-Catherine, Saint-Gervais, Temple, Saint-Martin and Saint-Lazare. The left bank between the towns of Saint-Germain-des-Prés, Sainte-Geneviève, and perhaps Saint-Marcel, had once again become the fertile land seen in Gaulish times. Above all, there were immense vineyards planted by the Gauls (2). It was these vines that produced the wine known as vin de Lutèce, which became the delight of several Roman emperors. Philippe-Auguste, fearing invasions from the Normans, who had plundered Paris several times, decided to annex a large part of these villages to Paris. He had the enlarged city surrounded by strong walls, nine meters high and three meters thick, flanked by towers from place to place to keep an eye on the enemy. From far and wide, monumental gates gave access to the city. The work, begun in 1191, took twenty years to complete. (1) Delamare, Traité de Police. (2) Delamare, Traité de Police. C north side, opposite the Louvre. At this point, there was a gate called the Porte du Louvre. From there, the cloture passed between the rue du Louvre and the rue du Coq, crossed the rue Saint-Honoré, where there was another gate; it continued between the rues d'Orléans and de Grenelle, crossed the Hôtel de Soissons and the rue Coquillère, where a gate was opened, later called the porte Coquillère, after a notable bourgeois living there. The cloture then crossed rue du Jour, derived from "séjour", because the kings had a pleasure house there; it crossed rue Montmartre with porte Montmartre and Saint-Eustache; continued between rue Tiquetonne and pointe Saint-Eustache, crossed rue Montorgueil, where another gate was later opened. The fence then passed between rue Pavée, rue du Petit-Lyon and rue Mauconseil, crossing ruc Saint-Denis, above Saint-Jacques-de-l'Ilôpital. At this point, there was a gateway known as the Porte aux Peintres. These walls passed between rue du Heuleu and rue aux Oües, crossed rue Bourg-l'Abbé, where there was a false door, then crossed rue Saint-Martin, opposite rue du Grenier-Saint-Lazare, continuing this ruc, crossed rue Beaubourg with a false door of the same name. Rue du Chaume, at this point, the walls turned as they crossed Rue de Paradis, in the middle of the Convent des Blancs-Manteaux, where there was a door named : Porte Barbette, in honor of the bourgeois of that name, who lived there and was provost of the merchants. They turned between the Rue des Francs-Bourgeois and the Rue des Rosiers. They crossed rue Saint-Antoine, where there was a gate that took the name of the former porte des Baudets, then rue de la Mortellerie, crossing various monasteries, where there was a gate called porte des Béguines, and ended at the river with a gate called porte Barbette. On the left bank, the fence began opposite the place where it ended on the other side of the Seine, in the place called la Tournelle, by the rue du Cardinal-Lemoine, crossed the rue Saint-Victor, went up behind Sainte-Geneviève, rue des Fossés-Sainte-Geneviève, rue Soufflot, Monsieur le Prince, passage du Commerce, and ended at the Institut where stood a strong tower called Philippe Hamelin, name of the provost of Paris at that time. On the left bank, the gates were: Porte de la Tournelle, Saint-Victor, Saint-Marcel, Saint-Jacques, Porte Gibard, since renamed Porte d'Enfer. The Buci and Saint-Germain gates led to the abbey of Saint-Germain-des-Prés. In these parts, there used to be a saulsaie on the banks of the river, where the inhabitants went to cool off in summer. Until 1550, the path that followed these extramural walls was lined with meadows; in 1609, these were converted into houses. At the time of Philippe-Auguste's death, there were still vast marshes, arable land, meadows, vineyards and vacant lots within the walls of Paris, which were built on under subsequent reigns. Philippe-Auguste's enclosure, built between 1190 and 1211, had raised the population of Paris to around 100,000, a fairly respectable agglomeration for a time when no produce came from afar; it required a certain number of market gardeners to supply the city with fruit, vegetables and grass. On both the left and right banks of the Seine, these walls encompassed a number of hamlets dependent on the surrounding abbeys. We won't deal with the left bank here, as there were very few market gardeners at the time. However, there must have been a few on the Place Maubert side, a fairly marshy area. We can see that there was a confraternity in Saint-Sulpice, but the gardeners on this side were probably more interested in growing trees and flowers, and we'll find them later on the Saint-Marcel side. This is probably what prompted special men to create the King's Garden, D8 now known as the Jardin des Plantes, in these parts. Even today, most of the major horticultural establishments are still on the left bank. The right bank, on the other hand, was much more marshy at the time, and the majority of its inhabitants were market gardeners. All the towns between the Seine and the heights of Charonne, Ménilmontant and Montmartre, contained a large proportion of these specialists. As a result of this expansion, a number of them found their way into Paris, and the same thing happened several times later. As these lands became more valuable, they were built on, and the maragers retreated outside the surrounding walls. DERECdO GO FROM CHARLES V TO LOUIS XIV Outside the walls established by Philippe-Auguste (!) there were abbeys with coultures that were rented out to the maragers, notably the Saint-Eloi cultures near Saint-Paul, the Sainte-Catherine culture, du Temple, SaintLazare, Saint-Honoré, etc. It's clear that there were abbeys with coultures that were rented out to the maragers. But as the city grew in population, more market gardeners were needed, and those who were displaced were forced to relocate further afield. In 1383, Charles V resolved to expand his "good city" of Paris, but this time only on the right bank. He tore down Philippe-Auguste's walls and built new ones, starting at the Arsenal on the Seine. It was then that he had the Bastille built as a state prison. The new walls roughly followed boulevard Beaumarchais, passing through the gates of Saint-Martin and Saint-Denis. From there, the walls turned to cross the Place des Victoires, the Palais-Royal and enclosed the Louvre within Paris. As a result, the districts of Saint-Paul, Sainte-Catherine, le Temple, Saint-Martin, les Filles-Dieu, Saint-Sauveur, etc., which had been mere suburbs, found themselves enclosed within Paris. By this new (1) Delamare, Traité de Police. expansion, the city, twenty years later, was increased by half and had one hundred and fifty thousand inhabitants. This new enclosure enclosed almost all market gardeners in Paris. As commerce and industry developed, their land was taken over. But it was mainly in the 16th century, from the time of François I onwards, that these districts began to be populated. In 1544, the nuns of Sainte-Catherine sold what was left of their land and gardens, with the buyer required to build and lay out streets. The lands of the Saint-Eloi culture near Saint-Paul were sold and built on around the same time. Around 1600, during the reign of Henri IV, the town was still surrounded by the meadows of Ile Notre-Dame and Ile du Palais, and to the north of the town by marshes and farmland. The king decided to have all these areas built up. The nuns of Saint-Anasthase sold the land they had in the Temple, Saint-Antoine and Saint-Gervais districts to private individuals. Place Royale was built, as were Rue de Cléry, Rue du Mail, Rue Neuve-Saint-Eustache, Rue des Fossés-Saint-Augustin, Rue des Victoires, Rue Neuve-des-Petits-Champs, Rue Richelieu, Rue Sainte-Anne and Rue Neuve-Saint-Honoré. The abbey sold a garden where the Rue Saint-Benoit was built. This enormous expansion of Paris took place under Henri IV and Louis XIII, bringing the population of Paris to 300,000 by 1600. From then on, the capital's population grew rapidly. It was also around this time, in 1600, that the gardening guild expanded. Cultivation took on a new lease of life, as sailors began to bring in plants from foreign lands, and the powerful encouraged horticulture by employing foreign scientists in their gardens. It was also at this time that the gardeners had their statutes officially recognized, entitling them to cerO tain privileges and definitively classifying their guild as a Parisian profession. In 1603, the gardeners rented a large part of the Temple grounds from the Prior, which they converted into vegetable gardens (1). At first, they built lodges for shelter. Later, the Grand Priory authorized them to build small cottages to house themselves, on condition that they be demolished and the materials taken away at the end of their leases, if he did not prefer to retain them and reimburse half their value, according to estimate. These leases were granted at a rate of four pounds per acre. In 1608, when the king conceived the plan to transform this district, all this land was acquired by Pijon, a bourgeois from Paris, for forty-four thousand livres, obliging himself to build there according to the plans given by the Duc de Sully, and to pay, to the order, six hundred livres of rent, of which he would charge by portion the houses he had built. In this treaty, we can see that the Order first received the purchase price of the land, and, in addition, a perpetual annuity to be deferred in parts on the sub-purchasers. As for the unfortunate "loçataires maraichers", there is no mention of them in Delamarre's Traité de Police, written at the beginning of the 18th century. As the author makes no mention of compensation for tenants, it's likely that they received little or nothing. The Saint-Honoré district between this faubourg and Montmartre was still swampy in 163l. These various documents show that market gardeners occupied all these districts until the beginning of the 17th century. Under Louis XIII and Louis XIV, the city expanded even further. From a population of 300,000 in 600, it reached 500,000 in 1700. Louis XIV, on the strength of his greatness, renown and comp(1) Delamare, Traité de Police. 90 sion on the fortified borders that Vauban had established, made Paris an open city. He demolished the remaining walls of Philippe-Auguste on the left bank, and those of Charles V on the right. On this side in particular, he created the beautiful boulevards that have become the center of Parisian activity. After this demolition, the suburbs grew as fast as the city, giving the capital a huge extension. It was in these faubourgs that we find the gardeners of the Revolution, who returned to Paris for a third time. In 1784, the fermiers généraux proposed to Louis XVI that a wall be built around the city, with gates at the main crossroads, in order to more effectively collect duties on goods entering Paris. This plan was carried out under the orders of Ledoux, the king's architect. These were the walls that men of my generation knew, since they were not demolished until 1860. The main landmarks on the left bank are Place Denfert-Rochereau, where the octroi offices built by Ledoux have been preserved as a historic monument. The Pont de Bercy links the left-bank boulevards with those on the right. On the right bank, the Place de la Nation, formerly the Barrière du Trône, and the Place de la Chapelle, with their offices, have also been preserved. Further west, the Arc-de-Triomphe de l'Etoile, above the Avenue des Champs-Elysées. At the time these walls were built, the capital contained 620,000 inhabitants within its walls. AND LES HALLES DE PARIS The first market in Paris was established in the city (1), roughly where Notre-Dame square is today. The main trade was in grain, which was transported by land from the surrounding area. At first, the capital had two small ports on its river: Port Saint-Landry and Port aux Eufs, near the Grand Pont. It was only under Louis the Younger, in the 12th century, that Port de la Grève and Port Pépin were added. These ports were not designed to receive grain, but only saltworks, timber and a few wine boats. The abbey of Saint-Denis levied duties on all goods traveling on the Seine (2). It also levied a tax on fruit and vegetables grown on its lands; this tax was costly, paid on a retail basis, and less so if the sellers of these products, the so-called "small trades", took letters from the abbot, which were also taxed. Generally speaking, however, the abbey leased out this collection in order to obtain a fixed annual manse. This farmer did not fail to exploit as much as he could the farmers who brought their produce to Paris. The city's first market has long since disappeared. The oldest surviving market is the Grand Pont, now known as the Quai aux Fleurs. This market has always (1) Paris through the ages. (2) Félicie d'Ayzac. COOg C has retained its customs in terms of selling days, which have always been and still are Wednesday and Saturday of each week. There are many police ordinances concerning the exchange of money on the Grand Pont de Paris; we will cite those of Philippe-le-Bel, issued in 1304, which read as follows (1). February 1304. - 1°: The exchange of Paris will be on the GrandPont, on the Grève side, between the great arch and the church of Saint-Leufroy. 2°: The exchange will not be carried out elsewhere, on pain of confiscation of the goods exchanged. 3°: If any of those involved in the exchange, by giving notice, give rise to confiscation, they will receive the fifth part. March 1304. - 1° : It will be solemnly shouted in all the baillages, the vicomtés, the prévotés and other places, that nobody, under penalty of confiscation of the goods, sells the sextier of the best wheat, measure of Paris, more than forty sols parisis and the sextier of other lesser wheat in proportion. 2°: The sextier of the best beans and barley, measured in Paris, will be sold for thirty sols; the sextier of the best oats for twenty sols, the sextier of the best bran for ten sols, the least in proportion and so on for the other grains. 3° : Whoever has of the above-marked grains, what is necessary for feeding his house and sowing, will be obliged to send the surplus to market, not all at once but little by little, to be sold at the price fixed above. 4°: If, after the public proclamation that will be made, any grain is found in excess of the quantity granted to them, the grain will be confiscated for the benefit of the King. 5°: The aforementioned ordinance will be published by the bailiffs, at the places they will mark. They will enforce it and severely punish those who contravene it. 6°: And so that the aforementioned ordinance is put into effect, Nicolas de Laigny and Guillaume de Tournay, appointed by other letters, will search for grain, and compensating the measure of one place with that of another, they will reduce them all to those of Paris. (1) Ordinances of the kings of France. of the place where the exchanges were to take place, but also fixed the price of the main commodities. Transporting goods from one place to another wasn't easy; there were only rivers, and many of them were hardly navigable. Paris owes its prosperity, in large part, to its geographical location: placed between the Brie and Beauce regions, it was able to be fed by land, in the early centuries, from these fertile regions; crossed by the Seine, just below the confluence of the Marne, a little above the confluence of the Oise, this network of major rivers contributed in no small measure to its rapid growth. It was under Philippe-Auguste that wheat began to be transported by water, as the area around the capital was no longer sufficiently productive to supply it (1). This gave rise to new ordinances to prevent hoarding. Under the aforementioned ordinance, farmers were required to bring their produce to market, not all at once, but in instalments and at different times. Likewise, bakers were not allowed to stock up on goods; community jurors strictly enforced these regulations. Almost all merchandise was subject to a fee upon arrival at the market, and we will now list the fees charged for gardening in general (2). From these documents, concerning the exchange, or trade in supplies for Paris, it can be established that, since the time of the Frankish kings, there has been a market around the Chatelet on Wednesdays and Saturdays. This market was subject to the various ordinances of the kings, for the foodstuffs sold there. In our century, it has become very important and exclusively dedicated to horticulture: trees, shrubs, flowers in cuttings, pots, crates and so on. Only cut flowers can be sold at the halles, and the former at the quai aux fleurs, which stretches along both banks of the river from Hôtel-de-Ville to Pont-Neuf, using the bridges nearby. Le Chatelet and its surroundings were known to the ancients by the Latin expression Porta parisiensis. For some authors, this meant Porte de Paris; for others, l'apport Paris (market); in the end, Chatclet was both. "Estour Chatelet" wrote around 1430 (1), Guilbert de Metz, "vendoit ou sel, fruits et herbes, et aussi y faisoit on tout l'an chappeaux, couronnes de diverses fleurs et verdeurs". (1) Paris through the ages. Old-time drawings show vegetable and flower merchants sheltered under awnings around the Chatelet. The Letters Patent of Charles VI, May 13, 1416 (1): "Desirons que devant notre Chastelet, ait une notable place belle et spacieuse, ordons que la boucherie soit du tout, démolie, jusqu'au rez de terre, sans y réserver rien, et ordonnons que l'écorcherie qui était derrière le grand pont de Paris n'y soit plus" (2). This later became the poultry market. LES HALLES CENTRALES It has been said that the Frankish kings owned a large territory on the right bank of the Seine called (campella), and that they donated land to establish the Paris cemetery outside the city. Not far from there, the cattle market was established; the wheat market we saw in the Cité was transferred there, and later, when Philip-Augustus annexed these districts to Paris, he had two large enclosed and covered market halls built (3), and brought a fair there, which he bought from the monks of Saint-Lazare, in 1183. Letters patent in the form of a transaction were signed between Philippe-Auguste and the Chapter of Paris in 1222, to regulate this purchase. These letters patent were called Charta Pacis, and stipulated that the bishop would have full justice in the old town of Saint-Germain-l'Auxerrois, in the Culture l'Evêque and in the Clos Brunel; in other words, the annexation of these towns to Paris by the new enclosure. (1) Ordinances of the kings of France. (2) Paris through the ages. (3) Delamare, Traité de Police. were located on the bishop's land, the king agreed that for three weeks at a time, i.e. one-third of the time, the provost of Paris would deliver wheat measures to the bishop's officers, and that they would exercise the emoluments; in addition, said officers and servants would enjoy the franchises. The "Halles des Champeaux" were to become the "Halles centrales", and would continue to expand as the population of Paris grew. The old regulations show that the halle aux poirées (pear market) was located between the rue Saint-Honoré and the halle aux blés (wheat market), in the side streets of this crossroads, as this trade has always been conducted in the street. In the past, only master gardeners were allowed to display their wares at this market every day of the week, and until around 1860, they didn't pay the City of Paris any market fees. I remember being told by elders, when I was young, that this was a gift that had been made to the master gardener community in past centuries. I haven't been able to find this document anywhere, nor is it cited by any author who has written on the subject. However, Delamarre's Traité de Police is very comprehensive in this respect. It must simply have been an old, time-honored custom, and one that resisted even more than half a century after the Revolution. ON THE ORIGIN AND HISTORY OF VEGETABLES Vegetable herbs are indispensable to human nutrition. The Latins called them olera (1) (vegetables). That's why gardeners were called Olitores, and the herb market, Olitorium forum. The successive introduction of vegetables into the crops of the Middle Ages marked, as it were, the stages of progress achieved by gardening. Improvements to vegetable crops were to run parallel to the development of the gardening guild. Without wishing to research the exact origins of vegetable plants, which would be beyond the scope of this book, we will instead focus on the place they occupied in the cultivation of our ancestors. We will therefore cite them below, in order of importance, wherever possible. 1 - Cabbage and cauliflower Among these plants, the most famous was cabbage; the Latins called it Brassica. From a therapeutic point of view, it was placed above all other medicines: (1) Olivier de Serres. llized Oy GO0 it was a universal remedy. Chrysyppe de Gnyde, a famous Greek physician, wrote an entire volume on the properties of cabbage, both as a food and as a remedy. Pliny felt the same way about this plant, and said: "It's no wonder that the Romans kept all the doctors away from their city for many years, and that they used cabbage to cure all illnesses. . The ancients claimed that of all the virtues of cabbage, none was more certain than that of guaranteeing and curing drunkenness. They derived this fact from the antipathy between Cabbage and Vine, and from the fact that wine also retains the same aversion. This is what the poet Alexis expresses in Athenaeus, with these lines, speaking of the Sybarites, great drunkards: Yesterday, my friend, you drank only too much, By such a great excess your head is heavy. Would you like to cure it? Instead of such a syrup: You must only fast and take under the tent, A dish of boiled cabbage from the hands of a servant. In Henri Estienne's Maison Rustique, published in 1597, we find a whole treatise on medicine, with vegetable plants. He says: Le premier bouillon de chou rouge, avec beurre ou huile et sans sel, lâche le ventre, mûrit la toux et rend la voix plus agréable. Creamed with sugar, it's ideal for asthmatics and lung sufferers. Cabbage seed in broth or powder is excellent against children's worms; used in another way it gives milk to wet nurses, the juice or juice fights venom taken from eating mushrooms, otherwise it cures burns, good remedy for curing gout, etc. In a word, it was a panacea. Cabbage has been around since time immemorial, and is native to all temperate countries. The flora of Asia mentions it along with turnips and rabe, which were grown as a single crop. The Hebrews had no names to distinguish these varieties from one another; on the contrary, the Arabs had a name for each of them. The ancients were already aware of how easily this plant degenerates. If you sow old cabbage seed," they said, "you'll get Raves, and if you sow old Raves seed, you'll get Cabbages. That's where the proverb comes from: "Sow cabbages and you'll get Raves". This proverb implies that an undertaking had a completely different event and purpose than the one proposed. In the 16th century, only a dozen varieties of cabbage were known. Olivier de Serres, writing around 1600, said: "We believe we recognize in the caulifiori of the Italians, our flowering cabbages, which are still quite rare in France, but will hold an honorable place in the gardens thanks to their delicacy, and which will be mentioned as something proper and useful". So, around 1600, cauliflowers were little known in France, and it's worth adding that Olivier de Serres lived in the South of France, in Montpellier, and that cauliflowers were not yet grown in Paris. They were known as brassica cauliflora, or brassica cypria, because they originate from Cyprus. Around 1700, gardeners were still saying that the best seeds came from Italy. The famous agronomist Olivier de Serres was right when he said that cauliflowers would take an honorable place in gardens. Today, they are even grown outside gardens, in open fields covering hundreds of hectares. In Paris, they're available all year round, with the first plants coming from Algeria, followed by those from Provence. Then Paris market gardeners supply the halles from May until the big frosts, i.e. the beginning of December: growers around St-Germain bring them at the end of summer and throughout autumn. PenO08 dant l'hiver, les Bretons qui en font une culture spéciale sur le littoral de l'Océan, protégés de la gelée par le gulf-stram, approvisi onnent la capitale et les autres grandes villes. 1l. - SALADS Chicory and lettuce were sometimes confused by the ancients. Wild chicory, now cultivated with great improvement, grows spontaneously throughout Europe, except for the north. In the past, it was also widely used medicinally as a depurative. Chicory has been cultivated for a very long time, and now has a very large number of varieties. When blanched, it makes one of our best salads. Chicory has more or less the same properties, and there are also several varieties, but they are less distinct from one another than curly endive. Endive, a large-rooted chicory similar to coffee chicory, belongs to the Mediterranean region, and has been cultivated since Greek-Roman times. There are two species of watercress: 1° Cresson Alénois A olere (to feed), because when mixed with meat, it gives it a good taste; it was once sought-after for certain properties of its seeds. Its cultivation dates back to ancient times and is now widespread. The Persians held it in such high esteem that they fed it to their children, to give them strength and courage. 2° Watercress was held in the same esteem, and has also been known for a very long time. It is picked along streams or near fountains. Today, some growers have devoted themselves specifically to this crop, which they cultivate with a certain art in specially established "cressonnières". Lettuce was called lactuea by the Latins, because of the milky juice it was filled with. The ancients spoke of them28 with the same praise as cabbages. It has been said that one of the branches of the Valerian family, which gave masters to the Roman Empire, had taken the name of Lactucians, for having bred a singular species in their gardens, which we suppose to be our Romaine(l). The latter is said to have been brought to Paris, during the reign of Charles V, by Bureau. de la Rivière, the king's favorite. This great figure must have visited Avignon several times, but in May 1389 he was there with Jeanne, Countess of Boulogne and Auvergne, whom he had proposed in marriage to Gaston Phébus, Count of Foire, his guardian, on behalf of the Duc de Berry. It was this princely marriage that brought Romaine to Paris. Wild Lettuce grows spontaneously in all temperate countries, and has been cultivated for over two thousand years. From a medicinal point of view, Doctors Estienne and Liébault considered it a very good remedy: "it refreshes the heart, softens the stomach and makes the good blood abound. Its juice, mixed with rosé oil, soothes headaches; it is used in gargles, mixed with pomegranate juice, for inflammation of the gullet. The seed, crushed with white poppy seed, tempers the ardors of Venus. Lamb's lettuce grows wild in all temperate countries. It seems to have originated in Sardinia. It is grown nowadays only around large cities. Dandelion, like Lamb's lettuce, grows wild in all temperate countries. For the last ten years or so, Parisian market gardeners have been cultivating the improved Dandelion; it is already much larger than the wild Dandelion, and has certainly not yet reached all the perfection it is capable of. It makes an excellent winter salad. Now grown by market gardeners, it (1) Le Ménagier de Paris. 4 there is no doubt that it will reach a degree of perfection that will place it on a par with the other plants cultivated by these specialists. Purslane was once one of the most widely grown lettuces. It has been cultivated since ancient times. It was used medicinally to combat many illnesses. 111 - MELON The melon is thought to have come to Europe from several different countries, including India and Africa (1), where it may have been cultivated at the same time. The natives of the countries where it grew wild were perhaps not long without recognizing its pleasant smell and delicious taste. Hence the honors of cultivation, just like the cucumber whose cultivation dates back thousands of years. But despite their antiquity, these plants have not been as useful to people as cabbages and onions; moreover, their cultivation is only easy in warm climates, where it is quite elementary, requiring no great preparation of the soil, nor much fertilizer. In Algeria today, the Maltese who grow this crop plant the seeds in situ, just as they do in France for beans, only they water them when the need arises. The Romans also cultivated melons, which they confused with cucumbers, proving that they were not very sweet. In their vast empire, they cultivated these plants in warm climates, but did not introduce them to the Gauls, whose climate was resistant to this crop. There is no mention of them in Charlemagne's capitularies, nor in the Ménagier de Paris, which seems to define Parisian market gardening at the end of the 14th century. Its importation into Paris may date back to the early 16th century, under François I. It is likely that in the (1) De Candolle, Origine des plantes culticées. 06 south of France it was cultivated before this time. The first Cantaloupe melons were brought from Armenia to Italy around the 15th century (1), and the first green-fleshed melon grown in France, near Rouen, was brought from Africa by a monk from Grammont in the 16th century. The "maraîchère" variety is less delicate, and until the beginning of this century, when market gardeners began to have frames, it was cultivated by them much more than the cantaloupe. The latter were probably only cultivated in the gardens of great people, where there was everything needed to establish this crop. When I arrived in Paris around 1860, I still remember seeing market melons being grown by a few market gardeners, albeit in the late season and on top of the soil; they planted two rows per layer and pruned very short, although they produced fine fruit when sufficiently watered, but left much to be desired in terms of quality, compared with today's varieties. At that time, the south of France still didn't send any; they were sold at almost the same price as today's good ones. It's worth adding that these vine-ripened fruits were still preferable to Cavaillon melons, which ripen by force in the straw, and of which many fruit stores are overcrowded. The ancients took great care in planting and re-growing their melons. Let's listen to Olivier de Serres on this subject: "The hottest part of the garden is chosen for the melon patch. In temperate countries, a layer two or three feet high should be planted, sheltered from the wind, along a wall. The maistrise for this plant consists in uprooting it to replant it, as these plants fear being disturbed in their roots. (1) Louis Noisette, Manuel du Jardinier. 98 To produce melons within twenty-four hours, enclose the seed in baskets or earthen pots, among the febrile and well fertilized earth, before having soaked the seed in wine or other liquors, one or two days. And then bake the baskets or earthen pots in the oven as soon as you have baked the bread, where for the remaining heat of the batch, in one day and one night the seeds will grow and rise from the earth. In cold weather, place them in large crates on wheels, to shelter them on bad days and especially nights. To protect these plants from the cold, place glass covers over them. These covers are large hats shaped like bells, wide at the bottom, or like still hats, with no rim at the end. Their size is a foot in diameter to wrap around the melon pit. The bottom of the pit should be heated with recent horse manure (1)". This treatise on the cultivation of melons, published by the greatest agronomist of the 16th century, shows that this crop was not within the reach of many people. It's also the first time we hear of these large glass caps, fashioned as bells, whose description gives us a good idea of our first garden bells. In a more recent work published by M. Delamarre around 1700, entitled Traité de Police, the Melon is also mentioned. It is, he says, one of the most difficult plants to cultivate. "To choose the best ones: those with a good smell, the heaviest, the thickest tails; they can also be recognized by the number of small marks imprinted on their bark, like so many nail strokes, which means, according to gardeners, that they have been grown by an overabundance of sap which they have been better nourished than the others". (1) Olivier de Serres, Théâtre de l'Agriculture. 208 These signs mentioned by the author, for recognizing the quality of a good melon, are absolutely correct. We might add that when the fruit has been well nourished by sap, and has reached maturity, the tail detaches from it, which gardeners call "decerner". This is a sign to be added to those cited by Delamarre. Sometimes, when the fruit has reached full size, in stormy weather, the sap causes it to split. To avoid this accident, which takes away much of their flavor, the gardener is obliged to twist or make an incision in the branch carrying them, to stop the excess vegetation. We'll come back to this cultivation in a moment. Ancient authors tell us nothing about the pruning they underwent; in all likelihood, none at all. 1 Doctors Charles Estienne and Jean Liébault, in their Maison Rustique, published in 1597, treat melon cultivation as a perfume. They claimed that horse manure was detrimental to its quality. "To guarantee them from veroniss and pussons, sow origene around them, or iquez branches between the plants. To make a fragrant, good-smelling pompon, as soon as you have removed the seed from the melon and wiped it dry, place it among seiche roses or musk seeds, and keep it that way until you need to sow it, and if you like, sow them together, or soak their seeds four days before sowing in damask water or perfume; by the same means, give them such odor and flavor as you please; if you soak their seeds, in such liquor before sowing. as in mead, or rose water, powdered with sugar or musk; however, watering takes away much of the said odor, and even flavor. To make pompons or sweet cucumbers, soak the seed in very sweet water, or honey; and to make them sweet, in ewe's milk or mead and sow them, then when they are sown, powder them with very dry earth and water them a little. The woman being in her months, by pourmenant by the boards of pompons, squashes and cucumbers, makes them seicher and die: the fruit which escapes will be bitter. Pompons, which smell like roses, are very useful for quenching thirst during a burning fever. Our ancestors made great use of perfumes and sought every means at their disposal to create them. Lavender, balsam, civet, etc. were among the perfumes most often used by princes. These men, in constant fear of being poisoned, had numerous manuscripts on how to make their own perfumes. This was an excellent reason to propagate these selected plants. The melon seed was one of the four cold seeds used in medical emulsions. Ancient doctors, and even many modern ones, claim that melons can cause fevers in autumn. That's why, under the ancien régime, the police magistrate would issue an ordinance prohibiting the sale of melons at this time of year. Today, everyone is free to sell melons for as much as they're worth. IV. - OTHER CUCURBITACES CUCUMNS, VEGETABLES AND CUCUMBERS Squash, gourds and calabashes. These fruit-vegetables are also native to warmer climates. They are mentioned in treatises on plants (1) a century before our era. The ancient authors included them under the single genus of serminous plants crawling on the earth. Charlemagne cites them in his Capitularies, proof that they have been cultivated in our countries since antiquity, probably imported by the Gauls. As far as medicine is concerned, the ancients claimed that nothing (1) De Candolle, Origine des plantes cultivées. GENERAL CONSIDERATIONS more sovereign for soothing the ardor of burning fevers, tempering thirst, loosening the belly, than the frequent use of pumpkin juice cooked without water, in a new earthen pot, placed in the oven; nothing is better "for dryness of the tongue, for thin, feverish people, than the use of pumpkin pulp, or syrup made from their juice". The cultivation of these plants is widespread; there are hardly any villages where they are not grown. As the Pumpkin is in the same family as the previous ones, its cultivation is not much more delicate. However, to obtain beautiful fruit, when they are specially cultivated, they need certain care. These plants require a lot of water once the fruit has set and begun to swell, leaving only one or two fruits per plant. In our countries, if you want to be sure of success, you must raise the plant under chassis, and when spring is cold and rainy, it is not harmful to plant them under cloches to facilitate recovery. But I'm talking here from a market-gardener's point of view, and this plant is now only exceptionally cultivated by them. The capital is supplied with these fruit-vegetables by growers in the outer suburbs. The cucumber is thought to have originated in Tartary and India. It has been cultivated in these countries for at least three thousand years, and was introduced to Europe by the Arians. Its cultivation is hardly more delicate than the previous one, when done in its season. Also cultivated under Charlemagne, and continuously since. In the Ménagier de Paris treatise on courtillage, published in 1393, only squash is mentioned, but the author, not being a professional, and writing under the dictation of a practitioner, probably included this plant and others of the same family, such as cucumbers and gherkins, under the word courge. All authors since that time who have dealt with this subject, speak of the cultivation of the Cucumber. It's one of the easiest market garden plants to force, and doesn't need as much sun and daylight as the melon. Provided it has the necessary heat, it grows easily. In Belgium, and even in England, this crop is grown on a large scale with the help of thermosiphon heat. Since coal is cheaper than in France, it is difficult for Parisian market gardeners to compete. Twenty to twenty-five years ago, this crop was quite lucrative, but it's tending to disappear, and soon it will only be grown in middle-class homes as early vegetables. There are two types of cucumber: green and white, and today there are a number of varieties of each. The gherkin is a small cucumber that is picked before it reaches maturity. The gherkin cucumber has long since become a well-established variety, grown in open fields like the pumpkin. It is grown by farmers around Paris, within a radius of forty to fifty kilometers. V. - THE TOMATO AND THE AUBERGINE The tomato is relatively new to our countries; like its congeners, the potato, it comes to us from America, and was brought to Portugal at the beginning of the 18th century. In these countries, it can be cultivated without art, whereas in ours, to have a profitable crop, it must always be sown (hot) and transplanted in the same way; it is hardly before May 15 that we can risk putting it (in place), and even then sometimes there are little frosts which do it considerable harm. It's a fact that, before it entered the market, it was first cultivated in the gardens of the great and good, and it's only in this century that its cultivation has made great progress in Paris. Market gardeners first cultivated it extensively, until the middle of the century, when a few grew it as early crops, but with the rapid means of p08 communication, the Midi sent some, and today Algeria supplies a considerable amount, making it difficult for Parisian market gardeners to compete, given that this plant is very easy to grow in warm climates. Growers on the left bank of the Seine, near Lonjumeau, Monthléry and Arpajon, who have very hot soils, have begun to cultivate it in open fields, and it now covers tens of hectares. As a result, whatever the season, market gardeners find it hard to compete with the competition from either the Midi or the outer suburbs, and its cultivation is increasingly abandoned by the latter. We will, however, come back to its cultivation, the new varieties that have been obtained through selection, etc. Eggplants originated in India, where they have been cultivated for a very long time. Its cultivation spread to Africa before the Middle Ages. Europeans only discovered it in the 17th century. Like the tomato, in our climate it has to be grown in (hot) frames. Market gardeners have also grown it, but not as extensively as the tomato. Moreover, this plant has not entered the popular diet to the same extent as its sister, the tomato. By its very nature, it is not capable of rendering the same services. Its taste is very bland, and it needs special preparation to be good to eat. We grew it ourselves for some fifteen years, at a time when the Midi shipped very little of it, and even then it was worth the effort. Today, however, it has been completely abandoned, so to speak, and perhaps something could be done. Instead of trying to grow it early, in July and August, we could plant it later, for example after the Carrot of the cloche layers, prune it to keep it bushy, as low as possible, and as soon as the cool nights arrive, place two boxes one on top of the other O0Q and put frames over them. This wouldn't be expensive, since that's when the frames are stored. In this way, Aubergines could be sold from mid-August to mid-November. It's an idea that I'm putting forward, but it's up to any practitioner to try it out. Cultivation is becoming so difficult for market gardeners, that it may not always be a question of trying to obtain plants early, but also of delaying them as much as possible, as our ancestors did for certain plants. VI. - BULB POTATOES AiL, ONION, PEAR. CIBOULE, CIVET. Garlic originated in Sicily. It is a widespread plant that spreads easily. Its cultivation dates back to ancient times (1). In Egypt, the plant was so highly esteemed that it was long included among the gods. The Greeks, on the other hand, hated it, and even condemned certain offences to eating nothing but garlic for a certain number of days. The Latins had neither the veneration for garlic of the Egyptians, nor the contempt of the Greeks; they considered it a useful food for country folk and sailors. Eaten on an empty stomach, garlic was the theriac of rustic people in times of plague; it was also used against certain illnesses. The onion is one of the oldest known vegetables, its origins lost in the mists of time. The Americans knew it before Columbus discovered their country.
