The Egyptians paid it divine homage; it is, along with cabbage and turnips, one of the oldest known vegetables. Before people began to grow cereals, these two vegetables were the staple of their diet. It was mentioned by Charlemagne in his capitularies. In fact, we (1) De Candolle, Origine des plantes cultivées. 98 have published this list, which is undoubtedly the sum total of the vegetables and flowers our ancestors were growing at the time. Like cabbage, it was used medicinally in a large number of cases. Tobacco being unknown, it was used as a substitute for it, to be inhaled through the nose to "purge the brain". Leeks, which the ancients called "porreau" or "porrée", are native to the Mediterranean region. The Latins called it porrum, because its root is not round like that of other bulbs. It was not known under Charlemagne, and is not mentioned in his capitularies. In the past, its seeds were used to throw into barrels to prevent the wine from becoming sour, or if it was sour, to bring it back to good quality. It was also claimed that leeks cooked under ashes had great virtue for dissolving drunkenness. Ciboule comes from Siberia, and was unknown to the ancients. It arrived in Europe via Russia in the 16th century. Civet is widespread throughout Europe, growing wild in Italy and Greece. VII. - ROOT VEGETABLES BETTERAVE, CARROT, PANAIS, RADIS, SALSIFIS, etc. Beet, cultivated for its roots, originates from the Mediterranean islands to the Caspian Sea. Beet juice "sucked through the nose purges the brain". Carrots, parsnips or pastenades, and Chervis, are old plants cultivated since the time of Charlemagne, and played a major role in people's diets before the popularization of the potato. Carrots in particular are an excellent vegetable for all kinds of stews. Some varieties are used as animal feed, and belong more to the field of agriculture than horticulture. C Ancient physicians claimed that these plants had the virtue of inducing urination. The leaves "crushed and put on wounds are very beneficial", says Henri-Estienne. Turnips belong to what the ancients called the "Raves" family, but are milder and only eaten cooked. They were called Napus by the Latins. Before Jenner's discovery of the vaccine, doctors recommended them to "push out" smallpox and measles; mixed with a decoction of lentils (1). The Egyptians made good oil from them. Radish originated in the temperate regions of the ancient world, but has been grown in our gardens since historic times. Horseradish comes from Russia, its name derives from the strong root, and it is commonly known as "German mustard" or "Capuchin mustard". Ancient doctors believed that horseradish root, when chopped and mixed with white wine, caused profuse urination. "Drunk with mead, it cures jaundice. The leaves, cooked in soup, relieve inflammation of the liver and spleen. Horseradish was generally used against all urinary tract diseases. Salsify grew wild in Greece in the 16th century, and Olivier de Serres described it as a new crop for his country. Scorsonera, or Spanish salsify, was not yet cultivated in the 16th century; it was known only in the wild, being spontaneous throughout southern Europe. It was called the "viperine" plant, because the ancients claimed that it was an antidote against the bite of vipers. VIII. - VEGETABLES GROWN FOR THEIR LEAVES SPINACH, GARLIC Spinach was unknown to the Greeks and Romans; it was new in the 14th century; the Ménagier de Paris mentions it in (1) Henri Estienne. 008 his treatise. Spinach is thought to have come to us from the Orient.

It is good for those whose voice or breathing is impeded, or who cough a lot" (1). Sorrel, or Acetasa in Latin, meaning sour or acidic, is a widely-used vegetable herb. It has been cultivated for a very long time. It has some medicinal properties, but is less widely used today than in the past. Patience has more or less the same properties. Poirée or Bette is a very old plant, highly esteemed by the Greeks, while the Latins, on the contrary, despised it as a dirty, tasteless food. Bonne-Dame or Belle-Dame is a bland, tasteless and tasteless species of arrowroot. Cultivation of this plant has now been abandoned. IX. - VEGETABLES GROWN FOR THEIR SEEDS Beans are an old vegetable of obscure origin, due to the antiquity of their cultivation. Ancient peoples all cultivated it, as do modern ones. In the ancient pagan cult of the Romans, fava beans were included in sacrifices made in honor of the goddess Carna. She played an important role in ancient Italy. As religions succeeded one another and left indelible traces in human mores, the custom of putting a broad bean in cakes on the Feast of the Three Kings undoubtedly originated. There are several varieties, including the marsh bean, once cultivated by the market gardeners of Paris, but nowadays, in general, the bean belongs to the domain of agriculturc. The Hlaricot (Phaseolus vulgaris) was already cultivated by the ancient Greeks in remote times, under the name of fasiolos. The bean was probably known in Asia and Africa for a very long time. Charlemagne (1) Henri Estienne. 08 had it grown on his farms. But it was mainly after the discovery of America that it spread to Europe. In the 16th century, gardeners called it "Turkish bean". At the beginning of this century, until around 1855, market gardeners grew it as an early crop. This crop was extremely labor-intensive, and the increasing cost of cultivation, combined with competition from products from the south of France, forced them to abandon it. In Algeria, the crop is grown on a large scale, and in January we saw beans being picked for shipment to France, as the colonists say. After Algeria, the South of France sends us some, and then the suburban growers supply the halles. We can these vegetables all winter long, even when there are new ones, so that everyone can eat green beans all year round. Shelled beans are also supplied by growers in the suburbs. As for dried beans, they belong to agriculture and are imported in large quantities from Hungary. Peas are also very old, cultivated by the Greeks before our era, they were imported into our country by the invading Gauls. Their cultivation went through much the same phases as that of beans. Lentils have also been cultivated since prehistoric times. X. - STRAWBERRIES A number of strawberry species are native to most of Europe. The Greeks and Romans did not cultivate them. We have seen that in the 14th century, Charles V had them planted in the garden of the Louvre, more as a decorative plant than for production. Olivier de Serres says of this plant that "it is so easy to pick up that only small tokens with a few Q subtle filaments are needed to grow. Suffering equally well from cold and shade, they come naturally, without cultivation, through agrarian forests, among the clump of trees. However, they become engrossed as they are treated. So to grow large strawberries, transplant them to a sunny spot in the garden, weed them well and water them in dry spells, cut off any running shoots and force them to stay in their lodges. But to produce larger strawberries in greater abundance than the common variety, scront strawberry plants require special treatment. This is by not allowing them to climb to the ground, but forcing them to rise to the top, shaping themselves like a small shrub. This is done by cutting them off with the fingernail, as they naturally want to climb to the ground. For this reason, the plants are planted a foot apart, in rows. The soil of which, after cultivation, will yield strawberries exceeding in size, goodness and abundance, the common ones, and in such qualities will render themselves almost admirable, if the breed of itself is good. Given that by such treatment the most savage are freed". This quote shows that the famous 16th-century agronomist already knew how to improve the wild strawberry. This crop, so defined at the time, has been continued. It has been greatly improved by the introduction of Chilean and Carolina strawberry plants, whose hybridization with our own varieties has produced large-fruited strawberry plants. It's not surprising, then, that we now have several hundred varieties of strawberry, each more beautiful and better than the last. As a result, strawberry cultivation has expanded considerably. For half a century, strawberries have been grown on hundreds, if not thousands, of hectares of land around the capital. The strawberry is not only consumed immediately, and is already needed by the four million inhabitants of the Scine department, when its price is affordable for 906 everyone; but, in addition, confectioners make excellent jams from it, and distillers a liqueur they call: la fraisette. The strawberry is a very healthy food, although picked in the woods, in the wild, where it can be soiled, or even tainted by the venom of certain beasts, we know of no example where it has never harmed anyone: "Among other conveniences that they would bring", says Doctor Charles Estiennc in the 16th century, "the juice or wine that is expressed from strawberries, is sovereign to remove the redness and small scratches that come to the face, from heat of liver, even to soothe the redness of the eyes, and erase the spots and pimples of ladrerie". XI. - ASPARAGUS Asparagus was highly esteemed by the ancients, and is no less so by moderns. The Latins called it Asparaqus. Old authors mention it as a wild plant in the earliest times. We ate it in Algeria in 1892, and it is much more bitter than the cultivated variety. Very good for the stomach and considered a tonic, it has the disadvantage that, almost as soon as eaten, it imparts a bad odor to the urine. Our ancestors under Charlemagne knew nothing about growing asparagus, since it is not mentioned in his capitularies. If the scholars of the time had known about the works of the ancients, who spoke of it, they would not have failed to draw the monarch's attention to the virtues of Asparagus, which was then growing without use. In the 16th century, Olivier de Serres was cultivating it, and does not mention it as a new plant, although he does say the same about cauliflower. It's true that Asparagus hadn't had the honors of divinity like Cabbage, or even Lettuce. Asparagus must therefore have been first cultivated in France, between the 9th and 16th centuries; it was imported in 908 by some obscure and modest man, at an unspecified date. La Quintinie is the first gardener known to have forced its cultivation. XII. - THE ARTICHAUT AND THE CARDON Cardoon is thought to have originated near the Mediterranean, indicating a very ancient culture. The Romans ate the receptacle bearing the flowers, while we eat the fleshy leaves. Cardoon has always been considered an excellent food. According to some authors, Artichoke is a derivative of Cardoon. For others, it's a very old plant known to the Romans. It was also known to the Egyptians, but had not yet become a food. Its root was once recommended as a cure for dropsy. Celery is widespread in all climates; it is an old cultivated plant with a number of varieties, of which celeriac is the most distinctive. XIII. - THE CONDIMENTS CHERFEUIL, PERSIL, ESTRAGON, etc. Chervil has been cultivated since the Christian era. It is an excellent condiment that "stimulates the appetite and purifies the blood". Parsley is an old local plant, which ranks first among the herbs used to season stews. Charlemagne had his gardeners cultivate it in all his domains. Its flowers are much sought-after by bees. The ancients also used it extensively in medicine, notably to make women lose their milk when giving birth. Tarragon, called "stragon" by ancient gardeners, has the same virtues as arugula, but these herbs should not be eaten alone, because of the "excessive heat they bring to those who use them". As a result, they are rarely used as a condiment in salads and sauces, along with many other herbs such as salad burnet, savory, perce-pierre, etc. XIV. - PINEAPPLE The pineapple originated in America, but was introduced early by Europeans to Asia and Africa. It was not until the reign of Louis XV that it was brought to France. Having received two carnations of the fruit from America, the King entrusted them to his head gardener at the Versailles kitchen garden, one of the Lenormand family, who from father to son governed the garden for almost a century. But cultivation requires so much care in our countries that it will never be commonplace, and even today it is only done by a few specialists, who find it hard to keep up with the competition from hot countries. Whatever happens, pineapple cultivation will not be completely abandoned in our countries; if the specialists who grow it for the trade are forced to abandon it, for lack of sufficient income, it will still be grown in the gardens of the great. The majestic bearing of its fruit, its size, goodness and finesse will always make it a first-rate ornamental plant. XV. - THE POTATO The potato, like the tomato, is a relatively new vegetable, imported into Europe by various routes in the 16th century. It was only towards the end of the 18th century that it was vulgarized and became an established part of the European diet. Certain vegetables, such as cabbage and onions, played a major role in the diets of ancient peoples; when wheat and other cereals were O8 cultivated, they eclipsed vegetables. The importation of the potato into Europe has certainly not eclipsed the precious grasses, but after wheat, it is the potato that plays the greatest role in the diet of modern peoples. Today, we even wonder how workers of all professions, in all countries, lived when the precious tuber was unknown: "It is the most beautiful conquest that Europe has made over the new world" (1). Originating in America, it comes from the land of gold, and has rendered more services to peoples than the vile metal (2) used to incite men to exploit one another. When Christopher Columbus landed in America, the potato was already being cultivated by the natives, at least in temperate countries. Cultivation began in Chile, then spread to Peru and Bolivia. The Chileans first harvested it in the wild; it bore little resemblance to today's potatoes, they called it "papas" and the Indians called it "batata", a word that has since been adopted by the Arabs to designate it. The first imports did not excite the enthusiasm of the farmers to whom they were entrusted, due to the smallness of the tubers; they were rather gnarled roots than anything else. It's obvious that if they'd only had the shape of today's tubers, they would have attracted attention more quickly. Olivier de Serres, in his Traité de l'Agriculture, mentions them vaguely, a century after the discovery of America. Some authors even claim that he confused it with the Jerusalem artichoke; only, this tuber was so small that he could not foresee the glorious future of his "Cartouffle". (word taken from the German Kartoffel: Potato). (1) Ernest Rose, Histoire de la pomme de terre (2) Victor Hugo. D00 In 1620, Gaspard Bauhin mentions the potato in Franche-Comté; as coming from Italy and Switzerland, under the name of (Cartouffli), which can only be the same plant mentioned by Olivier de Serres, under the name of (Cartouffle) and which became the (Tartouche) of the Francs-Comtois and Burgundians. In our youth, the ancients still used this term to designate the potato. At that time and in those countries, the potato's detractors spread the rumor, which had become legend, that the plant should not be eaten, as it could cause leprosy, being a member of the Belladonna family, and all the plants of this family are a very violent poison. To bolster their legend, they claimed that a ruling by the Besançon parliament forbade this crop, on pain of fine. Treated in this way by the powers-that-be, the potato was unable to make any serious progress and remained a plant rather despised than tasted. However, in 1655, it was given the honor of entering the King's garden (Muséum or Jardin des Plantes), and being cultivated there. Various farmers in their respective countries tried to cultivate it, but it was so feared by the people that nobody wanted to eat it; it was barely fit, it was said, for animal feed. It's so true that legends take root in the minds of men, that it took the perseverance and tenacity of the illustrious Parmentier over the last two centuries to popularize it and give it the place it occupies in people's sustenance. Parmentier used a number of stratagems to achieve his goal. At a court celebration, when potatoes were in bloom, he attached one of these flowers to the king's buttonhole, and the potato made its entry into the court. Charming and innocent flowers, wherever you come from, your presence is always welcomed by men, and in all their gatherings, gay 906 or sad, you are always welcome. And you, modest flower of the Potato, if your presence has contributed to the popularization of the tuber that gives birth to you, by the services it renders to mankind: be the first and most blessed of all flowers. Parmentier obtained permission to plant a few acres of the much-discussed plant, choosing between the Grenelle plain and the Gravelle plateau. He chose the latter to demonstrate to his opponents that the plant he so insistently advocated could grow in any terrain, as the Gravelle plateau is almost arid. Twenty years ago, another distinguished agronomist, Georges Ville, chose the same site to demonstrate the use of chemical fertilizers. But back to Parmentier, these potatoes were to be shared among the unfortunate people of Paris. Some of them began to steal them, so he had them guarded; the great agronomist said that his trial did not fail its purpose, for certainly the thieves were not rich people. Afterwards, he offered a dinner especially for the plant's detractors. This dinner, from soup to desserts, was composed of dishes, of which the Potato held the most arge place. Everyone dined well and no one was inconvenienced; Parmentier had won his case. From that moment on, the potato took its rightful place in people's diets. Its cultivation has continued to increase ever since (1). In 1789, an estimated 4,500 hectares of potatoes were planted in France, and a century later, in 1889, more than 1,500,000 hectares. In France, the area planted to potatoes already exceeds that of barley, reaches that of rye, half that of oats and a quarter of that of wheat. These figures illustrate the importance of (1) Elisée Reclus, Géographie universelle. 009 cultivating this precious tuber at the end of our century. It's true that this plant alone would be enough to feed entire peoples. When bread is too expensive, it replaces it in many cases, so there is no famine wherever the potato is grown. This marvellous Solanée has revolutionized agriculture, because it belongs more to this science than to horticulture. It's a source of new wealth, unknown to ancient farmers. It is estimated that the 1,500,000 hectares planted in 1889 produced a harvest of 135,000,000 quintals, worth approximately 600,000,000 francs. These figures speak for themselves (1). The number of varieties is prodigious (2), says M. de Vilmoin in his book on vegetable plants. We could count several thousand if we wanted to record all those that have been obtained and recommended over the last century; varieties suitable for all soils, all zones, all climates and easy to grow in all circumstances. At the beginning of the century, the market gardeners of Paris cultivated it as an early crop, but for the same reasons given for other vegetables, they had to abandon it. Settlers in the vicinity of Algeria's major ports grow this crop on a large scale, shipping it all winter and spring to France. Their planting begins as soon as the first rains arrive in September, and continues throughout the winter, so that they supply French markets with new vegetables long before we can plant them in our climate. (1) Elisée Reclus, Géographie universelle. (2) Vilmorin, Les Plantes potagères. 908 That's how far this vegetable has come in a century, a relatively short time in the history of mankind, and the services it has already rendered to people. Yet the man who has devoted all his strength, all his faculties, his whole life to popularizing it is barely known to rural populations, when the names of the great destroyers of men are on everyone's lips. This benefactor of humanity should have a statue in every major rural center, so that no one is unaware that he is the greatest man of modern times, because he knew how to provide food for people. But, alas, the agronomist, the farmer, the winegrower or the gardener are never appreciated for their true worth by the people they feed. Some plants that once enjoyed the honors of cultivation are now neglected. Laceron, for example, was cultivated and its roots eaten as a salad in Italy during the winter. The Italians found them sweet and pleasant-tasting. Ancient doctors used the stalk, which is full of yellowish milk, to cure certain illnesses, notably earache, etc. Today, except when fed to rabbits, Laceron is considered a noxious weed. In the past, market gardeners grew not only vegetables, but also medicinal and aromatic plants. These included Mallow, Marshmallow, Gentian, Passerage, Valerian and Angelica. The latter was still cultivated by some people as late as 1860. Among the aromatic plants: Rhue, Mint, Thyme, Savory, Rosemary, Anise, Fennel, Lavender, Pepper, are still grown by some, but in small quantities. LES JARDINIERS DE PARIS O08 FLOWERS, FRUITS, TREES CONSIDERED FROM A MORAL POINT OF VIEW In the previous chapter, we spoke of the role played by vegetables in the history of peoples. In ancient religions, certain vegetables were honored by divinity for the services they rendered. Today's more civilized people may no longer worship plants, but they still seek in them an allegorical image for their passions, afflictions, games and mourning. Vegetables offer analogies with our feelings, no less fertile than flowers and trees. By choosing the rudest, as it were, the most misunderstood of all: the Raves, they will illuminate the issue with a torrent of light, and prove themselves worthy of the rank assigned to them by morality. It is a nursery of beautiful analogies, says a great philosopher, that the bourgeois families of Raves, Beets, Carrots, Salsify. Celery, etc. Their collection represents the cooperators of the agricultural tram: each of these vegetables allies itself with the class of which it is the portrait. The large turnip remains at the table of large farmers; the turnip, less rustic, can appear on more distinguished tables. The carrot represents the experienced gardener whose usefulness is everywhere in evidence. Celery, with its acerbic flavor, gives the idea of those chamO pterous loves, where peasant men and women court each other with their fists. The fava bean, as we have said, played a role in ancient pagan religions, and to this day, the custom persists in Catholicism of putting one in the cake eaten with the family on the Feast of the Kings. The Athenians ate chickpeas (Pois lizes, Pois pointus, Garbanzos) on certain feast days. Some southern countries, in the minds of the people, have retained fidelity to old customs; for example, eating chickpeas on certain holidays of the year. Such is the case in Italy, Spain and even the south of France. In Montpellier, for example, people used to say that when Jesus Christ entered Jerusalem, he passed through a "sesierai" (from Cicer, the Latin name for this pea), a field of chickpeas, and that it was in memory of this day that the custom of eating these peas was perpetuated. In ancient times, the Roman nobility used to throw vegetables at the people to demonstrate their superiority. Flowers have always provided garlands on feast days, depending on the climate, the season, the customs of the country and the status of those using them. Laurel adorns the foreheads of warriors and studious schoolchildren, ivy that of poets, myrtle crowns the heads of happy lovers. The flower is part of every ceremony, happy or sad, and takes its place everywhere. Even the most modest of celebrations is accompanied by flowers, which means that the rich flock to them in heaps. When it's a funeral procession, the most modest man has a wreath of flowers, and when it's a man of note, like our two Presidents of the Republic, who died within the space of a few years, you'd have to count by the cubic meters the flowers of every kind and provenance that are brought there. In even the most modest of homes, everyone tries to raise and care for a few flowers. In Paris, our squares rival the finest princely parks of yesteryear. Water features, cascades and fountains, with numerous swans and ducks from all over the world frolicking in their midst. Ornamental trees, shrubs of all kinds, flowers and lawns forming wonderfully tasteful copses, beds and lawns. Some gardens are planted in the style of Le Nôtre, in the French style, in the large parks; others are planted in the English style, according to the layout of the gardens. But all planted with an art that does credit to the gardeners of our time. Not even our cemeteries are true parks. The alleys are planted with ornamental trees of various species, each bearing the name of the tree it is planted with. The death squares are surrounded by flowerbeds planted with shrubs of all kinds. Citizens keep as many flowers as possible on the graves of their loved ones. Seen from afar, it looks more like a magnificent park than a necropolis. Those unable to erect a monument of any kind on the grave of a departed family member plant flowers which they tend religiously until the body is raised, which is done every five years, and the bones join those of our ancestors in the catacombs. Trees, regardless of their usefulness, their products or their pleasures, are more or less bound up with our ideas and affections; they are, for us, old acquaintances. It is in this respect that certain trees, which live for many years, present themselves to our imagination, when their presence strikes our sight; it is no longer just a tree adorned with all its attributes that comes to our mind, but a sort of old friend, who brings back to us the beautiful days of our childhood and our most tender adolescence. When you've been away from home for thirty-five or forty years, and you're about to return, sometimes your father's house has disappeared; all you know are a few roughnecks whose hair, like ours, has turned white and who bend their backs under the weight of the harness. So, when you come across one of these giants of the flora, a walnut tree, for example, you recognize it immediately and imagine that it recognizes you too. You stop under the shade of this old tree, which is still green despite having seen several generations of men born and die beside it. We're tempted to ask his forgiveness for the lacerations we've made with picks and sticks to steal some of his fruit. This little war exercised our skill and developed our strength. The nuts obtained by right of conquest were again the instruments of new games, which often provoked battles between comrades; and in the end they remained with the victor. But this didn't happen without being reported to the teacher, who in turn punished both winners and losers. Some of these old trees were also witness to the first kiss we shared with a little girl our own age, in our tenderest adolescence. A very chaste kiss, it has to be said, but one whose memory remains forever etched in our minds. Already bordering on old age, it seems to us that if this tree were to speak, it would reproach us for the ill-treatment we have inflicted on it; it would take its revenge by saying: once you didn't respect me, now you're old, your skull is bare and I've stayed green. Instead, he still shades you with his superb, haughty head. When we see these trees, which were old when we were young, in a short space of time, our whole existence is retraced in our minds. For haven't they also seen the funeral convoys of our dearest loved ones pass by at their feet? How many have disappeared since the time we left the country? So it gives us a certain satisfaction to see these old trees again, witnesses to all our joys and sorrows. Doesn't the weeping willow announce regret and sorrow in a very touching way? Doesn't it inspire sentimental melancholy, and its overall bearing invite you to reverie? His head, bowed over a grave, offers the image of a being overwhelmed by grief. The gentle, sentimental poet Alfred de Musset expressed his dying wish that one of these trees be planted on his grave in Père-Lachaise. This wish was fulfilled by his friends. The tree has grown since the time of his death, and today it completely covers the grave of one of the greatest poets of our century, and at least the most sentimental. DEuEed (OOS C We have seen that, under Henri IV, Louis XIII and Louis XIV, the capital had expanded considerably, and the gardeners who occupied the Saint-Eloi (where Saint-Paul Church is), Sainte-Catherine, Saint-Martin, Filles-Dieu, etc. estates retreated, all of these districts having been sold to middle-class citizens who built there. At the end of the last century, and even at the beginning of this one, the gardeners moved to the suburbs, outside the walls of Charles V, near the Bastille, rue de Lappe, which was cut through the middle of a marsh, and named after the owner; rue de la Roquette, in the grounds belonging to the Hospitalières of that name, rue Popincourt, on the site of the canal, faubourg du Temple, Saint-Martin, rue des Récollets, near the hopital Saint-Louis, rue des Marais du Temple, and further on, rue de Paradis, rue Bleue, then the passage Saulnier, which was opened in 1787, by the owner of that name, in the middle of his marshland. There was also the ruc des Porcherons, now rue Saint-Lazare. An honorable dean of Paris gardeners, M. Le Bouteux, by virtue of his family's seniority in the trade, tells us that his father-in-law was a gardener on the site of the Passage Tivoli, his grandfather started out on rue Saint-Lazare, and later moved to rue des Récollets. It's also likely that some of them set up shop on the Champs-Elysées side of the Seine, where they were to be found until the beginning of our century; allée des Veuves, which is now avenue de l'Alma. Crossing the Seine at the Pont de la Concorde, there were no other bridges downstream, but at Gros-Caillou, when the Ecole Militaire was built in 1767, some of them were relocated, and we've known them in these parts since 1870, and a little further up, in the Necker district. To find other market gardeners on the left bank at this time, we need to return almost to the banks of the Bièvre, near the Jardin des Plantes, rue de Buffon, rue de Poliveau. This street ran all the way to the Seine, at the site of the Orléans railroad station. It began near the church of Saint-Médard, but there were many more horticulturists on this side. The market gardeners, who had moved back to this point, were probably descendants of those who had inhabited the Plaee Maubert for several centuries. PARISIAN MARAICH GROWERS The cultivation of Parisian market gardeners has followed in small steps the progress made by La Quintinie and his successors in this art. Most of them had neither horse nor carriage, and drew water by hand, which was moreover not deep, inhabiting all the marshy areas. In addition to the ordinary crops grown by their ancestors, they used air beds to force lettuces, turnips and radishes. A few had bells, at first in small quantities, and then a few bells were used to cultivate lettuce until the end of the last century and the beginning of this one, when frames were added to the equipment used by the ancients, and from then on market gardening made rapid progress. Some had horses and carriages, and delivered manure to their colleagues. They were as much valets as gardeners. However, M. Le Bouteux, who quotes this fact to me, says that his grandfather, who did 50Q of this trade, had several thousand bells, raised seedlings in winter for other growers, and heated asparagus with bells using the system defined by La Quintinie. Already in the last century, the pleasure of eating asparagus before the season was given only to the king, as his famous gardener had said a century earlier. It's certain that all the greats who owned gardens wanted to imitate the king. Their gardeners followed in La Quintinie's footsteps, and when you don't have to count the cost price, the difficulty is not insurmountable when you know the culture to be established. This was not the case for market gardeners, who are everyone's gardeners. The question was whether this costly crop would be profitable enough to grow. Of course, it was only started on a small scale, so that we could judge what it could produce. Nevertheless, it's true that the first to try to imitate the great gardeners in this forced cultivation, were bold pioneers who paved the way for their sons and prepared the ground for the introduction of all the forced cultivation that the latter were to do, and which has become, today, the basis of Parisian market gardening. These great market gardeners, who had horses, also had cranks. The crank is very old," says Malpeyre in his Maison Rustique (1), "and its installation could cost three or four hundred francs. But most market gardeners didn't have the means to do so; nor were their establishments large enough to require such a tool. When they lived in marshy areas, it's quite possible that they had gutters where water ran continuously; later, they watered with bailers like the hortillons of Amiens and certain other towns. (1) Malpeyre, Bixio et Bailly, La Maison Rustique. 7 In hot countries, such as Algiers, watering consists of running water through gutters dug between each bed. In all these large marshes along the Agha Bay coastline, the water is shallow. A Noria pump set up in the highest part of the marsh and operated by a mule-drawn merry-go-round, throws a considerable quantity of water into a large cement basin, surrounded by mandarin trees, in which the gardener washes his vegetables, and only has to worry about getting it to flow where he wants to water. This is how the Arabs water their date palms in the oases. Their main occupation is to make the water flow, and steal it when they can. This is also the method used by the city of Paris to use its sewage pipes on the vast estates it irrigates. Water is pumped into the main sewer in Clichy, pumped back up and distributed to the irrigated land. Here, in addition to the watering effect, the water deposits all the fertilizers it contains in the soil it flows through, and returns to the Seine clear and limpid enough to be drunk. We've tasted it at Gennevillers and Achères, and all that's left is a faint taste of its primitive state. Let's return to the Parisian gardeners of the last century. Those who drew water by hand had wells in place in their marshes. Digging them was not very difficult: a hole a metre or two deep, with a barrel knocked down into it to support the soil, often sufficed. With the introduction of hand-cranks, as soon as you could afford to install them, there was only one well in the highest part of the marsh, and water had to be distributed everywhere. Wood was cheap in those days, so two planks joined together to form a gutter and sunk to ground level were used to lead the water into barrels; then came sandstone pipes laid with fountain putty or bitumen, sunk deeper into 98 the ground, passing through all the barrels, filling them one by one or all together if the ground was flat. I remember seeing this system again in old establishments when I first arrived in Paris. For watering, market gardeners used watering cans known as "turn-over" watering cans. They were carried with the knob backwards; when they reached their destination, a flick of the wrist on the handle was enough to turn them, and the other handle, placed behind them, was grasped to empty them. You can still see a few of these in the homes of old gardeners, but they're becoming increasingly rare. I still used them a year or two after arriving in Paris: like all apprentices, I sometimes dropped one on my bare feet, of course, I was told, to get the job into my head. The main crops for the majority of market gardeners were, in addition to the old-fashioned crops, a few layers in the air to advance radishes, turnips, parsley, sorrel and lettuce. In autumn, the gardener's science was to keep the plants that came in that season, such as cauliflower, scarole, celery, etc., as late as possible in the winter. During winter and early spring, parsley, sorrel, wild chicory, etc. were sold in "calais" (a kind of small, elongated, round, slightly pointed basket), in which fish arrived. Melons, cauliflowers, Romaine and other vegetables were mounted on hoods and sold in batches. All this was carried to the market halls on the backs of hottriaux, and when there was a lot of merchandise in the spring, it was necessary to make several trips and walk most of the night. In other words, market gardening has always been one of the most fulfilling jobs that men can do. LEASE PRICES The reader will have seen that under Henri IV, the Grand Priory had 09 leased to market gardeners the lands dependent on the abbey of the Temple, at the rate of four livres per acre, certainly swampland and without a house, First, he allowed them to set up lodges or huts for shelter, then he authorized them to build small houses on the land, which they either took with them or left to him, paying them half their appraised value. In a lawsuit brought by a certain Le Bouteux against Le Coq, lord of the Château des Porcherons for a question of mitoyenncté in 1762, it can be seen that this lord had ceded to a man named Louvet, by an authentic deed, lease with annuity, dated April 24, 1725, half an acre of marshland, located in rue des Porcherons, now rue Saint-Lazare, in return for fifty pounds of ground rent. This land was subject to an additional five sols de cens. In our century, the reader will find rent prices in the chapter: Historique sommaire de la corporation maraîchère pendant le siècle, written by the author to serve as a retrospective exhibition at the 1900 Universal Exhibition. MECOS GO AT THE END OF THE LAST CENTURY & THE BEGINNING OF THIS ONE Under the reigns of the last kings of the ancienrégime, particularly Louis XIV, the capital expanded greatly. The suburbs were rapidly populated, especially those on the right bank, after the demolition of Charles V's walls. We have seen where market gardeners used to live, and as these areas were built up, they retreated into the suburbs. At the end of the last century, when Louis XVI had the city walls built, this time not for the defense of the city, but to facilitate the collection of goods entering Paris, they still found themselves almost entirely enclosed within the city. The population of Paris grew even faster than in the previous two centuries. With the sale of the Lationaux estates, many farmers of all kinds became owners of the land they were cultivating; and so began a well-being unknown to their ancestors. This," says Michelet, "was the beginning of a large number of agricultural and horticultural families: a respectable beginning, since it was founded on the faith that the farmer placed in the revolution, in the solidarity of the pledge that it delivered to him". This is how our institutions have become solid and durable; slowed down at times, their development continues unabated. This is because they are not simply based on the mobile ground of cities, which rises and falls, where people build and demolish. Rather, they have entrusted themselves to the land and to the people of the land: this is the durable France, less brilliant, less turbulent than the city, but much more solid. It would seem that their ideas have been the same for many centuries, but what is truer is that they advance by an insensible and latent work, as is the case with any modification in the great forces of nature. In a century's time, this population of farmers will still be whole and strong, going about its business as it does today, thinking as it ploughs, and cultivating its land; it will continue to preserve the benefits that the revolution has brought it, while the ephemeral population of the cities will long ago have buried in oblivion, along with its bones, all its political systems. Around Paris, the revolution found these men of the soil eager, like all their counterparts in the countryside, to own the soil they cultivated. For the supreme goal of the peasant, like that of the market gardener, the Parisian peasant, is to own the soil he moves so often. It's as if, by turning it over and over again, he's settled in and would like to stay there forever. The sale of national property was the beginning of the fortunes of our vicious market-gardening families, like a large number of farming families. The acquisition of their land, much of which belonged to the nobility and religious communities, made them Jacobins, and thus attached to the work of the revolution. Those who were not revolutionaries became so out of self-interest; their families' future depended on the success of the insurrectionary movement. As the aim of the movement was to liberate mankind, the men of the earth hoped for an immense benefit. 98 The nation, through the sale of national property, had in one fell swoop created a million property owners in France, that is, as many patriots attached to its fate, and by this very fact, had become invincible. Nor should we look for the success of the doctrines of the men of 1789; the interest of the greatest number. This is not a new, unprecedented or spontaneous act. It is the legitimate repetition of the great insurrectionary movements; we have already seen it happen when Christianity took root in our country. In the Middle Ages, great movements also took place for this persevering love of the purchase of land by those who cultivate it. Hymen sacré, legitime de la terre et du laboureur. In saying legitimate, this expression has a profound propriety. The man who cultivates the land has never asked for it to be given to him for free. This object of all his wishes, of his faithful passion, he seeks to acquire from his savings, through constant and superhuman efforts. The revolution is therefore the good mother of the farmer in general; it broke down the barrier that prevented all citizens from becoming real property owners. It made it easy for anyone who owned a garden, a piece of land or a vineyard to acquire it. It is in this way, by making the greatest possible number of owners, that she is at once the benefactor, the friend and the savior of the greatest number, temporarily agitating the world only to give it peace. By inviting the farmer to acquire, by marrying him to the land, the revolution made him even more thrifty, more industrious, if that's possible. The farmer felt that, while he had new rights, he also had new duties: to prosper, to expand his plot of land and to raise his family honourably. And so it is that we still find today, in our old market-gardening families, the taste for work, order and thrift that was bequeathed to them by their fathers. This is the moment to recall that they have remained faithful to the old 900 motto of the guild of master gardeners of past centuries: Manus fortis, divitias parat LA MAIN FORTE PREPARE LA RICHES When the ancients recount how their fathers lived, we are astonished by the effort made by this generation: a Spartan lifestyle, abstinence to excess, which no worker would accept today. Although gardeners, then as now, had peaceful and austere morals, there were nonetheless violent types among them, as in all societies and corporations. History relates that during the troubles of the 1789 Revolution, a troop of prisoners, made up of nobles, most of whom were men who had marked their opposition to the movement in a most odious manner, was brought in carts to Versailles. Escorted from the front and the back, it was assaulted from the flanks by a confused troop who surrounded the carts and jumped in. A gardener who had worked on Mr. de Brissac's land, and had been expelled by him for a trivial reason, said to her: "Do you recognize me? At the same time, he grabbed him by the ruffle and broke a stoneware milk jug he was holding over his head. This was the signal for the general massacre of the troop. In another era, such acts would have been unforgivable, but in these troubled times of our history, we must excuse them. Indeed, this generation carried in its heart the hatred that the suffering of twenty generations had accumulated there. These men, most of whom came from families of serfs, had heard their fathers and grandfathers tell of all the misery the old society had made them endure, and some had suffered it themselves. It's hardly surprising, then, that among them there were exalted, violent individuals capable of committing the most cowardly murders, of the kind we've just mentioned. If we relate these facts, it is as a faithful and impartial historian, to demonstrate that in the most peaceful and peaceful guilds, there are beings ready to satisfy their vengeance when the occasion arises. Men of the revolution, whatever your virtues, whatever your vices and faults, you have undergone the final clash of the old world against the new; a clash that has no equal in the history of peoples. You have endured it more or less stoically, but you have transformed the world no less, giving it freedom, emancipation and hope. Whoever you are, we, your great-grandnephews, salute you as heroic men. LES JARDINIERS DE PARIS DIntiXedE GOO MARAICHÈRES In the previous chapter, it was said, according to our great historian Michelet, that at the time of the Revolution, many agricultural and horticultural families had been formed, but there were also very old families in the market-gardening guild, dating back several centuries, notably the Dulac family, which is very numerous today in the horticulture of the Paris region, and which has provided eminent practitioners. Under the reign of Charles V, market gardeners of this name dried out the marshes of the "Coulture Sainte-Catherine", and are surely ancestors of those of today. The first signatory to the statutes of the community of master gardeners, in 1599, was a Le Bouteux, of whom one member still remains, one of the deans of the guild, having been born around 1615, but having no male heirs, this name is tending to disappear from the guild of gardeners. Other families also date back several centuries: the Debilles, the Stainvilles, the Hébrards, etc., all names that we'll find among the first market gardeners who began growing early vegetables. Another no less ancient family, which to my knowledge no longer exists in the gardening trade, is the Debergue family. This family may not have left as lasting a corporate mark as others, but one of its members was immortalized in 1870, during the Franco-Prussian war. Paris was overrun by the German army, isolated from the rest of France, and consequently without news of troop movements outside. The capital sent news to the provinces by balloon, but received none. Several prominent men, including Gambetta, left Paris in this way to organize national defense in the provinces. A few brave men, risking their lives, crossed enemy lines to bring news to Paris. One of these was a descendant of the Debergue family, who was captured by the Prussians and mercilessly shot, along with one of his colleagues. A monument has been erected in their honor in Bougival, on the very spot where these brave men, victims of their patriotism, fell victim to the bullets of the victors. Such men honor their families, their corporations and France itself, the family of us all. Debergue, may your name remain forever engraved in history, as a martyr to patriotism; may your memory be hailed by future generations as a hero. We, your contemporaries, modest workers like you, place a laurel branch on the monument raised in your honor to consecrate your heroism. Indeed, as I write these lines, it is almost thirty years since the terrible year passed, and yet the event held every year at the monument to these heroes, on the anniversary of the battle of Buzenval, grows in importance from year to year. Speaking of the character of market gardeners, which consists above all in becoming owners of the land they cultivate, taking into account the spirit of conservation of patrimonial property in children; of the location that gardeners occupied at the beginning of the century; of the rapid expansion of the capital since that time; of the prodi909 243 gious increase in land in all these districts; the reader will not be surprised at the great fortunes owned by the grandsons of our old market-gardening families at the end of this century. These fortunes have a tendency to increase, as the grandsons continue the tradition of their ancestors, buying the land they occupy themselves, and the same progression exists for land further from the center, as for that occupied by their grandfathers. 9gues9 COO 2 Google ====-- PEVOLUTION DE LA CULTURE MARAICHERE depuis La Quintinie Douxed . (OOQ 6 DoueedS GOOglo The cultivation of early vegetables, inaugurated by La Quintinie in the last century, could only exist to satisfy the whims of kings and the powerful. As already mentioned, the gardeners of the big houses who engaged in this type of cultivation didn't have to worry about the cost price or the selling price of the plants they grew. Of course, it was to their credit that they produced plants out of season, and thus gave satisfaction to the masters who employed them; but it was a big step from there to seeing this cultivation done by gardeners from all over the world. The former grow crops with money, while the latter have to make money with crops, and the difference between the two is enormous. Those first market gardeners who practiced forced cultivation drew their ideas from the Versailles kitchen garden, but once again, it's certain that this royal kitchen garden, where money was spent without counting the cost, did our profession a great service by experimenting with new crops, which the artists who took over the management of the establishment could do without running the risk of losing a penny if they didn't succeed. Nevertheless, I'm certain that not a single Parisian market gardener graduated from the Versailles school as a pupil, and that many went on to become teachers. Which school did these teachers attend? Surely it was the practice and routine of past generations that had trained them in their art. Indeed, La Quintinie himself acknowledged that he had learned a great deal from the "Parisian Mareschais". Even during the Renaissance, when cultivation had its masters, didn't we see the potato, that precious tuber, take two centuries to become popular? This is a valuable example of how many centuries it took for other, much less useful plants to gain the honor of cultivation in earlier times. Our masters from the beginning of the century, who had lived under the ancien régime, sensed that, with the revolutionary turmoil behind them, a new era was opening up for them and their descendants. Many had become landowners, that is, in a state of stability that enabled them to break out of the old routine, and establish new cultures, where some probably committed more than they possessed, having for themselves only the hope of success, without having the certainty of it. There's no doubt that there was a lot of trial and error, and harsh criticism, even from colleagues who aren't looking for anything and wait until the initiator has found something useful to take advantage of it without a moment's hesitation. Some probably became discouraged, while others took up what the first had abandoned, to try and do better. These early masters of the gardeners of our time perhaps already foresaw the extension that their trade would take with the century that was beginning. They did not create these new crops simply out of egoism, which is a human trait. But inspired by a new spirit, after the great social movement that had just taken place, they understood that if man has strict moral duties and obligations towards himself, he also has them towards posterity, and that on pain of lying to his natural destiny, he must cultivate his intelligence in such a way as to bring it up to the level of the general knowledge of his time, for all that he must bequeath to his children. And once this first duty has been accomplished, he must not limit the scope of his action to material and personal interests alone. No one can blame him if he gives a large share to egoistic feelings. But he must link all his aspirations to the ideal that, alongside our ephemeral selves, there is something permanent and superior: posterity. This ideal is the desire not to be completely useless here on earth, and to contribute even the smallest stone to the social edifice to which countless generations before us have contributed. It's the formal and unshakeable will to help posterity, in every circumstance and according to its means, to rise higher and higher to a more general well-being than that of our ancestors. It's up to us, in our turn, to pass on to future generations, not only intact but enlarged, the precious heritage of which we are merely the custodians. The first market gardeners of this century were therefore initiators, and are those to whom it is given to introduce their fellow human beings to a higher realm, to open up a path and walk straight ahead, without worrying whether they will be followed. The initiator is the man of genius, recognized by the fact that, being ahead of the others, he is not at first understood by them, but astonishes, worries and scandalizes them. So, to cite just one example of the new cultivation methods introduced into market gardening at the beginning of the century, we'll take the bell bed to cultivate Romaine. Those who devised the counterpO0 planting method were not the first to do so; their experiments had to be criticized; today, however, this method is quite simple - it's just a question of knowing how to use it. The old-timers used to make ployant layers, i.e. they built them up to just the width of the frame or the three rows of bells, and rolled up the manure to form the edges, leaving the paths empty to warm up as and when needed. This work was very labor-intensive, and I remember hearing a former gardener tell me that two men shouldn't have all the fun of making an eighteen-panel long belfry bed in one day. This is the kind of layer that La Quintinie made, and that our ancestors copied when they started growing early vegetables. It has to be said that labor was cheaper than it is today, and early vegetables were rarer and generally sold at a higher price. Here are just a few of the main market gardeners who began forced cultivation. Around 1776, Legrand, on rue de la Santé, at La Glacière, was heating rose bushes in situ, using layers of sludge; carelessly, he placed a chest and three glass frames on the end of a bed of Alpine strawberries; The first dozen he harvested were sold for twenty-four francs, and although the author reporting this fact neglected to indicate the time, it was probably in the course of May; since then, prices have fallen significantly. In 1788, Decouflé was growing peas and beans in frames and carrots on beds. The cultivation of peas and beans must have been abandoned by market gardeners, because of the competition for these products from Algeria and the South of France. Around 1791, Stainville, allée des Veuves (Avenue de l'Alma), on the Champs-Elysées, was forcing fine Chicory. When we say "forcing", we must assume that he first had the idea 009 of sowing it on a bed and transplanting it in the same way, to obtain a late spring crop, given that until then this salad had only been grown in autumn. Autin followed in his footsteps. The secret of how to grow the plant early and prevent it from bolting was revealed; other market gardeners did better. The market gardeners of Vaugirard and Grenelle, in particular, cultivate it early on beds. They made it a special crop for most of the century. The first white asparagus to be heated under a frame, using the system that still exists, was produced by Quantin around 1792. This system consists in digging paths about Om65 wide; throwing the soil into the boxes to butter the asparagus, so that they are white when they grow; filling the paths with hot manure, which is reworked every ten or fifteen days, depending on the weather; bringing in new manure each time to mix with the previous one; and finally, keeping the paths continuously full up to the top of the boxes. This type of cultivation is very costly, and asparagus must be sold for at least ten to twelve francs a bunch to cover it. The troubles of the Revolution halted the progress of this crop for some time. Afterwards, it was quite lucrative for those who cultivated it, especially in sandy soils, which are more suitable for asparagus than clay soils. Today, as the Midi sends out more and more of them, it is to be feared that the younger generations will not always be able to keep up with the competition, especially in the late season, and that this crop will largely disappear at the end of winter and the beginning of spring from market gardening, as we have seen with other vegetables. At the beginning of the century, Marie was forcing green asparagus. It consists in: pulling up asparagus claws at least two years old, in the fields, as a large quantity is needed, and planting them touch by touch on a strong C layer, to develop the eyelets; when they reach a length of twenty-five to thirty centimeters, they are picked and bundled for sale. The claw is then removed and burned. This type of cultivation is now done only by a few specialists, using the thermosiphon, because to do it profitably, you have to raise the plant yourself, and this requires the cultivation of many hectares of land. One house in particular does it in a big way: Compoint, in Saint-Ouen. The company's latest exhibit at the agricultural show delighted all visitors. The head of the company recently won first prize at the International Horticultural Exhibition in St Petersburg. The first market gardeners to use frames were Stainville, Marie, Debille, Hébrard, Marcès, Quentin, Valet, etc., followed by François, Fournier, Heude and Jaulin. By 1820, they were in general use, and the number of frames has continued to rise ever since. By this time, France had regained its tranquillity, and trade had taken off in a way that would only be temporarily interrupted by a few temporary disturbances. A few began growing lettuce in combination with carrots, followed by cucumbers and cantaloupes. Until the introduction of frames to their crops, market gardeners grew little more than "maraicher" melons. Some continued to grow them in cloches on soil until around 1865. I've still seen this extremely robust variety of melon being grown. The beds were not turned over, but simply ploughed into the compost, and two rows of melons were planted in each bed under cloches. They were pruned very short, and once they had stopped (in trade terms), i.e. once we had chosen the most beautiful one to be the fruit that was to remain, we removed all those that had formed so as not to harm the growth of the favorite. They were watered copiously every day. Eventually, however, this variety disappeared 906 from market gardening, as the quality of the produce was generally inferior to that of the Cantaloups grown today. They were replaced on Paris markets by imported Cavaillon melons. The working-class population eats these Cavaillon melons, which ripen in the straw or in the fruit tree stores, and are certainly no match for our old market-garden melons, which ripened on the plants that bore them. This is a consequence of free trade. It's quite certain that in the days of the "maitres", juries would not have allowed the sale of a large proportion of these fruits, which are liable to cause disease, and which, for consumers who are not connoisseurs, contribute to depreciating the value of those grown by Parisian market gardeners. Around the same time, other market gardeners were experimenting with different crops. Besnard was planting cauliflowers under frames around 1812. Those already named with new names, such as Dulac. Chemin, began to make bell beds to grow Romaine. In 1815, the Société d'agriculture awarded a gold medal to Mr. Pierre-Simon Marcès. This market gardener grew cucumbers, peas, beans and strawberries. He was also one of the first to grow Romaine on bell beds. Mr. Debille also won an honorable mention for his forced cultivation. From then on, market gardeners were more likely to give lessons to the Versailles kitchen garden than to receive them. However, the garden was not left behind. M. Edy, head of the establishment, experimented with all types of forced cultivation. Messrs Grison and Gontier followed in his footsteps, the latter receiving a gold medal from the Horticultural Society in 1834, for the application of the thermosiphon in forced cultivation. In 1827, almost at the time of its foundation, this same Society had awarded a prize to Mr. Découflé for all his forced cultivation. In spite of all this encouragement, market gardeners contributed almost nothing to the Society; they have continued to err in the same way ever since; this is because they have no clientele; they take their produce to Les Halles and sell it at the market. This is the real reason why they are not interested in making presentations to this Scientific Society. However, it must be recognized that the Horticultural Society has rendered great service to the market-gardening guild, and to horticulture as a whole, in the popularization of new plants, and in that of improvements obtained on older cultivated plants. Thus, in vegetables in general, there are today a large number of varieties that date back only to this century, and which have taken on great importance in cultivation and trade. Deuixed G0 MARAICHERS IN THIS CENTURY During the course of this century, the population of Paris quadrupled or nearly quadrupled, causing frequent displacement of market gardeners living in the suburbs. Towards the beginning of the century, we find the biggest nuclei in the vicinity of the Bastille, the Temple and Saint-Martin suburbs, the Saint-Lazare district and the Champs-Elysées. On the left bank, at Gros-Caillou, near the Ecole Militaire and a little higher up. And on the banks of the Bièvre, from its mouth and up the Faubourg Saint-Marceau. After the Revolution, as the value of land increased in all these areas, tenants, with their leases terminated, had to go further afield to establish their crops. Those who owned their land, finding it more profitable to rent it out to industry, also placed their children on other, less expensive land near Louis XVI's walls, either inside or outside the city. On the Bastille side, the Canal Saint-Martin displaced a number of them around 1820, and neighbors who were not in the way were displaced by the industry this outlet brought to these neighborhoods. At the Champs-Elysées, too, as land became more valuable, market gardeners had to cross the Seine and settle in the plains of Grenelle and lower Vaugirard. On this side, the Ecole Militaire and the Champ-de-Mars had displaced some of them, while those living on the Gros Caillou also had to retreat further into the plain where they were settled. Contrary to what had happened in previous centuries, it was in this area, the 15th arrondissement of Paris, that the largest group of market gardeners was established during the last two-thirds of this century. It's true that they found all the elements they needed to grow their crops here: strong, clayey soil on a sandy base on the banks of the river; sandier and lighter soil as you move up towards Vaugirard. Water, always the essential element sought by market gardeners, is shallow and plentiful. A word of history about these neighborhoods (1). Valboitron was later called Vaugirard, because Abbé Gérard, superior of the Abbey of Saint-Germain-des-Prés, had a farm built there that bore his name. On the basis of old plans, this farm would have included the whole of the 15th arrondissement, as well as parts of the 14th and 7th arrondissements, extending over the territory of Issy and belonging to that parish (2). Issy took its name from Isis, because the Gallo-Romans had built a temple dedicated to this goddess. The land of Issy was donated to the abbey of Saint-Germain-des-Prés; it included all the territory of the farm already described, Issy, Vanves, Malakoff, Chatillon, Fontenay, Clamart and Meudon. Around 1342, Vaugirard was established as a parish, and a hamlet was built around the church, which later became the commune of Vaugirard. The lower part of this territory, which later formed the commune of Grenelle, remained under arable cultivation and was given over to other farms, notably the Grenelle farm, which some old-timers still know. Up until a third of this century, part of the 15th arrondissement was still farmed. (1) Dom Bouillard. Histoire de l'Abbaye de Saint-Germaindes-Prés. (2) Abbé Irminon. 109 marshy, a small branch of the Seine separated from the river at Champ-de-Mars, cutting the plain in two and joining the river at Ile Séguin. It's probably because of this island, which has long since disappeared, that Parisians refer to this district as l'ile des Singes. The street that today bears the name of Lourmel was called chemin des vaches at the beginning of the century, and the local elders say that it was these animals that cleared it to go grazing. It later took the name rue des Marais, probably also because it was later inhabited by many market gardeners. We insist on this district, because it's where we practiced our trade as market gardeners for almost a quarter of a century, and I remember that at the beginning, this land still felt its marshy state, even though it had already been cultivated for twenty-five years. Today, the land is crossed by two major thoroughfares, Rue de la Convention and Rue Herr, and a factory is already operating on the site. The 15th arrondissement is the largest of the twenty that make up the capital, bordered on one side by the Seine and on the opposite side by the Chemin de fer de l'Ouest railway line, Having rid itself of the Champ-de-Mars, which served as an army parade ground, and of the barracks which will inevitably be relocated near the new parade ground, and which constituted a sort of zone between it and Paris, this arrondissement will be one of the most densely populated in the capital in the course of the century that is about to begin. Cultivated land is beginning to thin out: in ten years' time, it will be counted. All that will remain are a few lost corners, like the ones we've seen left in the various precincts of Paris. Those in the Faubourg Saint-Marceau have retreated up the Seine towards Ivry, still close to Louis XVI's walls, or Glacière, up the Bièvre. Between 1840 and 1850, a great advance was made with the creation of railroad lines, all ending in the capital; passenger stations were set up inside the city, not far from the walls of Louis XVI, and freight stations in the suburbs outside the city, also at a short distance from these same walls. In other words, all these new facilities had to be built on the large areas of land occupied by market gardeners. As a result, many were expropriated and had to move their establishments further afield. These shopping centers also obliged the city to create large access roads through all this land, resulting in further expropriations. As commerce expanded, industries, factories, construction sites and even neighborhoods were built around the new stations. The market gardeners gradually disappeared from Vieux l'aris. Dg184S GOC For the first time, we have used a term that was previously unknown to us: Expropriation. In fact, the great industrial revolution that the creation of railroad lines was to accomplish obliged towns and major railroad companies to acquire large tracts of land; the legislators of the time, in order to facilitate and develop industry and commerce, passed a special law called : Expropriation Act, dated May 3, 1841. This law sets out the procedure to be followed to force landlords to sell their property, and tenants to give up their leased premises, either to the state, to cities or to large corporations, when it is in the public interest to do so. Firstly, a decree declaring the expropriation of the designated premises to be in the public interest, followed by various formalities, including unc, the constitution of a jury responsible for setting the allowances due to the expropriated for the purchase of their properties, and to the tenants for the termination of their leases or the damage caused to their industries in whole or in part. It is quite clear that the legislators of the time were of the opinion that the expropriated should always be compensated with a certain mark-up, for the properties sold, or the industries that had to be moved elsewhere, or abandoned, at the expropriated parties' peril. This law, like many others, gave rise to many abuses. We have seen expropriations that were amply paid for, and others where the interested parties were wronged; this is often due to the way the jury was constituted. These kinds of cases, which are really no more than expert appraisals, are usually handled too legally by the lawyers on both sides. Having had the honor of sitting as a juror on several occasions in different cases, I can say that I have often heard the lawyers on both sides of the case argue beside the point, thereby confusing the jury. The expropriating party makes derisory offers to the expropriated parties, who in turn make exorbitant demands. On both sides, there is no reliance on justice, but simply on the jury's greater or lesser competence. The jury, always made up of very honest people, is never guilty of anything other than incompetence. Thus, in various expropriations of the same trade, such as that of gardener, we see people receiving twice as much as others placed in exactly the same conditions. To give just one example, let's look at the most recent expropriations of gardeners for the opening of the rue llerr, in the 15th arrondt of Paris: almost all of them were heinously wronged. These facts are absolutely regrettable in the times in which we live: to seize the property of others, in the name of the law, and not to pay for it what it is really worth. The legislators who made this law had not foreseen this case. We estimate that the displacement of a market gardener, who almost at the end of his lease has five or six hundred frames, three to four thousand bells, irrigation, etc., is today worth twenty-five thousand francs, at the very least, after which the jury must take into account the years of lease remaining, established crops and a host of other cases that can raise the allocation to twice this sum. For partial expropriations, there are also many cases to be examined, and D08 must be competent to deal with them. We are particularly insistent on rental expropriations, as it is on this point that the most misunderstandings occur. This sixty-year-old law is already too old to remain fair, given the progress made in our century. The jury should be recruited by other means, so that it is composed of more competent men. At present, all guilds are unionized and elect experts; these men, chosen by their peers, are ideally suited to sit on expropriation juries. These experts would be chosen according to the industries to be expropriated. One of these men, who would bring out the real value of the case in question, before his colleagues from other trades, accustomed as he was to settling such disputes, would give fairer justice to both parties, than the randomness of an honest jury, there is no doubt about it, but more or less competent and capable of being led one way or the other, by the pleadings of this or that lawyer. JUSQU'EN 1860 With the help of the law of expropriation, it became easy to undertake major works, either for the state, for towns or for large companies that could obtain the decree of public utility. Since then, these companies have continued to expand their domains, and the capital has made numerous street openings and widened other thoroughfares, as the streets of old Paris became insufficient to meet the needs of the transactions that commerce imposed. Almost entire districts were demolished, and the industries that had been established there were forced to retreat to the outskirts of the city. Louis-Philippe's government also built fortifications around Paris, on average about two kilometers from Louis XVI's walls, thus encompassing part of the surrounding villages. It's quite certain that the engineering officers who built these fortifications hoped that in the near future, they would form the limits of the capital, which they did less than twenty years later. While we're on the subject of this project, let's also mention that the same government had forts built two kilometers away from these fortifications, to cover the capital, with a road running past each of them and linking them all, called the strategic road. After these major commercial moves, the main market-gardening centers were located between Louis XVI's and Louis-Philippe's enclosures. On the right bank: Bercy, Reuilly, Charonne, La Chapelle and Plaine Monceau; on the left bank: Grenelle, Vaugirard, Montrouge, a few at La Glacière, but as always, more and more horticulturists on this side, and in the Gare d'Ivry district. Although not an integral part of the big city, all these neighborhoods encompassed by the fortifications were gaining considerably in population and trade, and this in turn drove up land prices enormously. In this incessant game of backwardation, the last became the first. Thus, at the beginning of the century, those near the Louis XVI fence were the furthest away, and became the first half a century later. Towards the middle of the century, some of them crossed the fortifications to settle in neighboring villages, such as Charenton, Saint-Mandé, Vincennes, Montreuil, Pantin, Aubervilliers, Saint-Denis and Clichy on the right bank. On the left bank: Issy, Vanves, Montrouge, Gentilly and Ivry. Those remaining in these countries today are closest to Les Halles in Paris, where they all trade. If this game goes on for a few more centuries, one wonders where the market gardeners will be in this day and age. However, with the hindsight they have gained in the last forty years of the century, it is safe to assume that they will remain where they are now for a long time to come. Despite the fact that the demands and needs of all kinds imposed by this enormous agglomeration of citizens, the entrenched camp that is the capital today, the immense network of railroads, canals and consequently stations, warehouses, workshops, docks, ports, etc., that commerce requires, and even cemeteries, will still force some market-gardening centers to move backwards, this time into the Seine-et-Oise département. Q AT THE END OF THE CENTURY In 1860, all the communes enclosed within the fortifications were annexed to Paris; the walls built by Louis XVI were demolished, and the octroi was transferred to the fortifications: this is our present-day Paris. The gardeners, or at least most of them, were once again enclosed within the city. Some of the villages annexed to the city became as commercial as the center, thanks to the large-scale commercial installations that had been built there since the creation of the railroads. The right bank, in particular, quickly became denser than the left. Passenger and freight stations, canals and harbors, the wine warehouse at Bercy, markets and abattoirs, all contributed to the creation of large industrial and commercial districts. Much of the industrial activity took place on land vacated by market gardeners. Landowners benefited greatly, while other market gardeners were expropriated as a result of the opening of new roads. Finally, those who owned their land and already had a certain fortune, opened up streets and other lots on long leases, to enable tenants to build on mutually-agreed terms. As a result, the right bank of Paris now boasts only a few 2,000 market gardeners lost behind buildings or land belonging to religious communities, which industry has not yet been able to take possession of. On the left bank, in the XIIIth and XIVth arrondissements, there are also almost none left; the XVth arrondissement alone still has a number, but with the construction of major thoroughfares over the last ten years, they will rapidly disappear. Many of those that had retreated outside the fortifications have also been relocated. Firstly, on the Charenton and Saint-Mandé sides, by the extension of the Bois de Vincennes. A large proportion of these went to start building the new districts of Maisons¬- Alfort, Alfortville and Créteil. Others headed for Bobigny, a village on the other side of Pantin that is still the largest grouping of market gardeners. In Pantin, Aubervilliers and La Plaine-Saint-Denis, many were displaced by the numerous railroad lines criss-crossing the area, the stations they required and the trade they brought with them. These retreated to Sains, Pierrefite, la Courneuve, Bobigny, Draney, le Bourget and Bondy. Similarly, in Clichy, they were driven out by the railways, docks, warehouses, etc., to settle in Asnières and Gennevilliers. On the left bank, those of the XIIIth went to Ivry and Vitry, the first of these towns taking on such considerable importance that soon the last market gardeners would disappear. The XIVth has rejected its own in Bagneux and Arcueil. Thirty years ago and more, in the XVth arrondissement, as the establishments located near Louis XVI's old walls disappeared, they moved further out onto the same plain, to Issy-les-Moulineaux. However, since the 1889 Exhibition, the Champ-de-Mars could no longer be used as a cavalry maneuvering ground, and another large area of land was needed for this purpose. The military engineers chose the Plaine d'Issy to set up this maneuvering ground, and the gardeners had to abandon the site once again. This time, cornered at the end of the immense plain, where we first saw them set up at Gros-Caillou, they had to retreat to all sides, as they could no longer find suitable land for their crops in this region. DgttixedO GO AND MODERNES In chapter XIII of this book, we listed the main tools used by gardeners in Charlemagne's time. They were few in number, and some of them would still belong today to other trades rather than to gardening. However, the spade is certainly the first tool used by men to cultivate the land, having preceded the plough by a number of centuries that is difficult to determine; the hoe or pickaxe is also very old, as are the sickle, the scythe and other tools, such as the cognée, the auger and the saw, which are more woodworking tools. Some wooden utensils, such as the van and the basket or banne, were also used for a variety of purposes.
