Later, the "rastel" or rake was added to the gardener's tools, followed by the watering can, the two- and three-pronged fork, and the wooden and iron shovel. It's clear that, until market gardeners started using forced cultivation methods, this equipment hardly increased at all. Wooden tools and utensils were joined by the hood, so widely used in the last century and at the beginning of this one for preparing and transporting produce to Les Halles. WATERING It is in watering systems that progress has been most noticeable, especially in our century. The first gardeners established in swampy areas were content to let water run off in gutters, and this was sufficient with the freshness of the soil they were cultivating. Later, on drier soils, they had to water with a scoop, then as the soil became healthier, they were obliged to dig holes from place to place to obtain sufficient water and draw it with a bucket, using a pole, Then came the pulley suspended from the top of the well with a rope and a bucket at each end, the water was poured into a barrel and then drawn off with watering cans, which have also undergone many transformations. The first watering cans, known as "turn-over" watering cans, were carried with the apple or "bouffe" at the back. They had a handle connecting the "bouffe" to the body of the watering can, and another behind, almost at the bottom. To fill them, all you had to do was press down on the "bouffes", and they would fill up very quickly, immediately being grabbed by the first handle indicated to carry them, and when they reached their destination, a little flick of the wrist would make them turn and the handle on the other side would come to the hand, so that they emptied just as quickly, and as soon as they were empty, they were returned by the same means. All the old-timers who were used to this system preferred it to the new ones. Admittedly, it had its merits, but what's certain is that it was less affordable than today's "pouring" system, which simply consists of carrying the full watering can by the top of the handle and pouring the hand down to the other end to empty it, then bringing the hand back up to refill. But the elders of the last century, and those of the beginning of this one, still had the watering can to turn over, in accordance with their principles of mastery and companionship, from which it was not necessary to depart. Then came the hand crank, which is very old, but not used by our ancestors, as the gardening establishments were not large enough to use this DO8 tool, which requires the use of a horse. So it was only towards the end of the last century that a few gardeners began to use them; little by little, they came into general use, but until the first quarter of this century, many continued to pull water by hand. Around 1835, piston and merry-go-round pumps made their appearance. This new tool solved the problem of great depths and enabled market gardeners to tackle the plateaus around P'aris head-on. Before the crank, there were several wells in each marsh, so that there were no fixed pipes. As we've seen in some provincial towns, there were probably portable pipes to carry water in barrels to a certain distance from each well. With the use of cranks, a system of fixed pipes came into use, initially in sandstone welded with fountain putty, later with bitumen. The barrels were filled one by one, the well being located on the highest point of the marsh; later, they were filled one by one by means of a discharge at each of them, the pressure not being strong, many market gardeners closed these discharges with a simple plug of rags, others, afterwards, put in taps. so that the expense was more considerable than today to install an irrigation system. (1). Fully laid sandstone pipes cost seventy-five centimes a metre. Barrels that had contained oil were the only ones suitable for this purpose, and were worth twelve to fifteen francs a piece, and the taps adapted to the rejects eight to ten francs. The installation of the crank cost four to five hundred francs, the first were supervised by a person, usually a bastard, to empty the buckets, and others were emptied by means of a hook. One of the first market gardeners (1) Moreau, Histoire des Maraichers de Paris. cited for this installation was a man named Bourgeois, at Petit-Grenelle. The merry-go-round pumps that replaced the cranks did nothing to change the watering system; the first to use them were Séguier, Autin and others. Around 1860, Ponce Isidore, in Clichy, and Niolet, in Vaugirard, came up with the idea of raising the water in a reservoir to water by pressure. Like everything else that started out, this system had many detractors; the old-timers were adamant that it would never be worth anything, for various reasons: 1° they didn't know how much water they were giving the plants; 2° watering would not be done equally everywhere, and so on. Despite all these criticisms, the system was generally adopted, and not a single Parisian market gardener watered in any other way. The first irrigation systems installed were far from the perfection of those used today. Some were made with sandstone pipes that were not strong enough to withstand the water pressure. For the distribution of water in the marsh, until the copper taps with screw thread, which we use today, there were many trials. I remember seeing a simple rubber hose fitted to the end of the T, onto which the watering hose was fitted; to stop the water flow, this end of the rubber hose had a ring with a small chain that was used to bend it, by means of an accessory existing after the T; the chain was hooked to hold it in this state, and the water was stopped. Other market gardeners used flaps that were kept closed by water pressure alone. Nowadays, everyone has come to rely on copper taps of various types; there have also been cast-iron taps, abandoned long ago as they could not be repaired. When market gardeners settled on the high plateaus around Paris, they could only find water at very great depths. Some D08 establishments needed several horses in summer to accomplish this task, so the idea came to some of them to operate the pump mechanically. One of the first to install a steam engine was M. Trousselard, in Bagneux. This method required a lot of inconvenience, the fire had to be maintained continuously, and as the machine was not large enough to require the presence of a special driver, the system was not followed, and has even been completely abandoned in the face of new modern advances. Later, Mr. Narcisse Laurent in Grenelle installed a pulsometer. This device raises the water by steam pressure alone, but the system can only be usefully employed at shallow depths, and consequently could not provide all the services that market gardeners expected of it. At the 1889 Exhibition, mechanical engineers presented gas and oil engines. These devices are designed to produce the power needed; they take up very little space and are very easy to operate. Once in motion, there's no need to worry about gas engines; as for oil engines, you just have to make sure they don't run out of fuel. Young market gardeners quickly get used to operating and maintaining them properly. For the last ten years, a large number of these devices have been in operation in the marshes around Paris, and, just as with ireigations, in twenty years' time we'll be counting those who don't have one. As we enter the twentieth century, mechanical power has taken its place in the work of market gardeners. There's every reason to believe that it won't stop now. Our great-grandnephews will cultivate larger tracts of land than we do, and equipment will increase proportionately. From this will also come new, more economical means of cultivation than ours. 00 LES CLOCHES The first time this type of equipment was mentioned in this book was when Olivier de Serres wrote about it in his Théâtre del'Agriculture, published in 1600 (1). The great agronomist depicts our cloche clearly enough for us not to be mistaken, for before this all-glass cloche, there was the verrine, a kind of tiny greenhouse with an iron or cast-iron frame and glass panes, used only in princely gardens (2). The smallest verrine cost five or six francs, a price that prevented our ancestors from being able to afford a certain number. Claude Mollet, in his fine Traité d'Horticulture, makes no more mention of the glass cloche in the chapter on melon cultivation than in the other chapters. He did, however, write after Olivier de Serres, and was more of a specialist than he was. But Oliver de Serres lived in the South of France, while Claude Mallet lived in Paris. It would seem from these facts that the bell comes from Italy, where the glass industry had made great strides. So it's hardly surprising that, at a time when communications and means of transport were so difficult, the glass bell was known in the South of France more than half a century before it was in Paris. It was not until the reign of Louis XIV, when La Quintinie travelled to Italy to study his art, that this object came into great use in the Versailles kitchen garden, as the great gardener used it to force asparagus. From that time onwards, they were used more frequently, and it's likely that some master gardeners began to own a few. According to Malpeyre, they were about the same price as they are today, despite all the progress that has been made in their manufacture. (1) Chapter XXII. (2) Malpeyre, Maison Rustique. 96 Olivier de Serres tells us that the bell has a diameter of one foot at its base and a similar height; the glass was leaded. Nowadays, it has been enlarged in width and height, and great improvements have been made in the manufacture of glass. An average bell for growing vegetables in Paris should be 0m35 to 0m36 high and Om4l in diameter at the base. The glass is slightly blued to prevent sunburn. In the first half of the century, white glass was produced, but practitioners have recognized its defectiveness, and today there are almost none left. The number of bells used by Parisian market gardeners at the end of this century is estimated at five or six million. Towards the middle of the century, Messrs Moreau and Daverne estimated the number at two million two hundred thousand, a figure that has almost tripled in the last half-century. The use of this equipment, which is certainly the most important for today's market gardeners, seems to date back to the mid-seventeenth century. Olivier de Serres didn't know about them in 1600, although he did know about the cloche, while Claude Mollet, half a century later, spoke of glazed frames, although he didn't know about the cloche. It was not until the reign of Louis XIV, therefore, that all the equipment used by marachiers for forcing vegetables was brought together and used in the Versailles kitchen garden. In his royal kitchen garden, La Quintinie brought together all the equipment that the progress of the time had made in this new art of forcing plants, and of commanding nature, as it were, even when snow covers the earth. The illustrious monarch, so proud of his kitchen garden and so satisfied with the produce his illustrious gardener served him out of season, could not have foreseen that this royal fantasy would, two centuries later, become an important branch of our national industry, with business worth hundreds of millions of francs in our beautiful country of France. Today, the forcing of trees, vines, vegetables and flowers represents a surface area of many square kilometers; I estimate that Parisian market gardeners alone heat four to five million square meters of land. So, like the bell, the glazed frame was at first only a luxury item for gardens.

of the greats. This is more or less the role it played until the end of the last century. At that time, a few gardeners began to use them. From what little has been written on the subject, and from what the ancients have told us, bells were used more for breeding spring plants. At the beginning of this century, the bold pioneers of the market-growing guild, who were trying to make money from a crop hitherto considered a luxury, were not afraid to increase their installations, at their peril, with this important equipment. At mid-century, Messrs Moreau and Daverne estimated that the number of glazed frames used by Parisian market gardeners stood at three hundred and sixty thousand; by the end of the century, this figure had risen to one million. The same authors counted eighteen hundred market gardeners in Paris and its suburbs. This number has decreased over the years, as crops have become more specialized. A certain number who were counted as market gardeners at the time, because they grew vegetables and flowers, have become florists for us; those who continued to grow vegetables have given up growing flowers. Half a century ago, market gardeners specialized by district. Those in the Faubourg Saint-Antoine had more frames and bells than those in Vaugirard and Grenelle. The latter were especially busy forcing curly, fine or Parisian chicory, while the former were forcing asparagus, melons and cucumbers in their prime; carrots, lettuces and Romaine on bell beds, etc. This would have been to deviate from the norm. It would have been a breach of common sense to move a crop from one district to another. In Vaugirard, the old culture of open-air beds, especially for Radishes (the Raves had been abandoned), still lasted a long time. I remember seeing former market gardeners growing Radishes all year round on their open-air beds; this culture, well done, was worth another. Finally, this district also specialized in growing summer cauliflowers. The market gardeners of the Faubourg Saint-Antoine and other districts, after their early crops, grew all the vegetables that their ancestors had cultivated. From 1860 onwards, with the rapid communications brought about by the railroads, market gardening underwent a revolution. The Midi began to ship its produce, and northern countries, notably England, began to demand produce from the Paris market. These commercial outlets proved the Faubourg Saint-Antoine greengrocers right, and all market gardeners, in every district, increased their equipment in frames and cloches; after 1870, Vaugirard was home to establishments as large as those on the other side of the Seine. It was around this time that a new crop of early vegetables was added to those already in existence. We're talking about the forced cultivation of turnips. The ancients, even in the last century, were already cultivating Radishes and Rays on open-air beds. It is therefore astonishing that this forced cultivation of turnips has taken so long to spread among modern market gardeners, as this plant is in the same family as those we have just mentioned. The care it requires is hardly more difficult than for its congeners, except that it needs a little more heating, and the roots must be kept tender. Some market gardeners first sow it in other crops, without any particular care, which always gives 008 a bad result. However, some young growers, seeing the potential of this crop, took it up in a big way, with Messrs Narcisse Laurent, Durchon, Beaufils and others leading the way. This time, this innovation of forced cultivation began on the left bank, contrary to everything that had happened until then. Forced cultivation of turnips was very profitable for a number of years; today it belongs to the public domain, if I may put it that way. However, it has not done little to increase the number of trays used by market gardeners; I estimate that half of these trays are used in spring, after they have produced one or two harvests of lettuces in autumn and winter. It can therefore be said that the market gardeners who innovated this crop have done a great service to their corporation and to the various industries that depend on it, especially the chassis manufacturers. Finally, it is worth adding that the cultivation of other early vegetables has followed the same progress, which explains the rapid increase in this equipment since 1850. PROGRESS ACHIEVED IN CHASSIS MANUFACTURING The first chassis were made entirely of wood. They consisted of a frame and three timbers placed at equal distances to support the glazing, giving four rows of panes. There were also some with four small timbers, giving five rows of panes. This was probably the oldest system. I haven't found anything written on the subject. Market gardeners, or rather the manufacturers who worked for them, adopted a uniform measurement, which is 1'30 wide by 1u35 high, but horticulturists have larger ones, which are most often used at the same time to cover their 00 greenhouses. As "market gardener" frames are uniform, when one of these practitioners sells his equipment, he can easily get rid of it, since his colleagues can use it. Glazing is done by overlapping the panes by about a centimeter from the top, to facilitate water drainage from the bottom of the frame. The last tile, improperly called the "base" by tradesmen, rests on the bottom rail, which is the real base of the sash, covering it by two centimeters. A groove exists in the wood on each side and in the top rail to fit this glazing and to putty over it. Until the middle of the century, frames were made from driftwood. The wood was cheaper than today, and the sapwood was removed, so the frames were able to withstand humidity long enough without suffering too much, especially if they were well maintained with coats of paint. Until then, early fruit and vegetables were not grown until the end of December or the first days of January, whereas now many market gardeners take out their frames in October and don't bring them in until the end of June or the beginning of July, depending on the temperature. The only progress we've made on this tooling has been to replace wood with iron. Firstly, the three small timbers have been replaced by three equally grooved iron rods; consequently, there is no change in the glazing, except that the panes are a little wider, and the iron takes up less space than the wood, resulting in a welcome reduction in shade for the plants grown under these frames. Some builders made all-iron frames, but this system has its drawbacks: firstly, they are heavier if you don't want them to whip, hotter in summer, colder in winter, and don't stack well when they need to be retracted. This system has not been adopted by market gardeners, and there are very few of them in their equipment. Another system of double frames forming a two-slope greenhouse is also available on the market. Gardeners in some provincial towns use them, but there are very few of them in the equipment of Parisian market gardeners. The most popular frame in the last thirty years is the one designed by M. Vélars père, known as the "Vélars frame". A number of imitations have been made without any advantage over the first. This chassis consists of a frame whose top and two sides are made of oak and whose bottom is made of wrought iron, with brackets rising up to a third of the way up the frame and fixed to the wood with screws. This iron advances under the uprights in all their thickness with a screw and four others in the bracket which goes up inside along the leaves. THE COFFERS As the word suggests, this part of the equipment is designed to support the frames and surround the cultivated plants. Although market gardeners have abandoned the old method of folding layers, they have retained the idea of maintaining the warmth of the layers, by filling the paths with dry manure as much as possible, according to time and need. This part of the equipment is certainly the most costly to maintain, as it is constantly exposed to warm humidity. No economical means of preserving wood have yet been found. At the Versailles kitchen garden, we saw a trial of a tarred trunk, and the melons inside were burnt; the heat of the layer and the sun made the tar run, and the gas released burnt the plants; the head gardener told us that sulfating produced the same effect. Many different types of trunk have been tried, and all have varying degrees of disadvantage, which we shall now examine. The tarpaulin or fixed trunk is rarely used by market gardeners, and mobile, portable trunks are needed, given that crops are constantly changing place, and that 908 it must be easy to place plants at varying distances from the glass. The crutch chests: at the end of each board, there was a very narrow head with a foot on which two iron rods were adopted, where the end of the board was passed, there was at four or five centimeters from the top, a small notch to pass the rod, so that the board was fixed just at the height of the head, and as in all the other chests; the frames were supported by bars, these with dovetails. The good thing about these chests was that no holes were made in the layers with the heads, but the bad thing was that it was difficult to raise them when necessary, as the whole row was held together, and had to be able to be raised all at once. The nailed trunk has a head at each end and is therefore independent. The old market gardeners didn't have many others, with dovetail bars When there weren't many frames, this was the simplest, and when they were no longer needed, they were stacked; But nowadays, with so many frames, they take up so much space that hurricanes sometimes break them, and the sun and rain cause the boards to warp and wear, making them difficult to stack. Next came the sliding trunk, exactly the same as the nailed trunk, except that instead of being nailed, it has two pegs at each end of the head, with a notch in the board to pass the peg through and hold it at the required height. At the end of the pitons there's a hole to pass a rod that secures the board to the head across its entire width. At the end of the rows, the heads are Om35 wide and the others Om18. The first trunks in this system had hooked bars; the wooden bar was just as wide as the trunk, and an iron hook was fitted by means of three screws. The disadvantage of these bars was that as soon as one screw was missing, the bar could no longer be used. O0 Another bar system consisted in the hook being fixed from below, to avoid fatigue, and raised at right angles to the end of the bar, to hook in like the previous system. In this day and age, wood is generally replaced by iron; bars are made from one-piece T-iron, bent at each end to form the hook, with the edge of the T underneath bent just to leave the thickness of the board and form a stopping point. This system is undoubtedly the best and least expensive, although it requires a greater advance of funds at the outset, but lasts a very long time. In all trunks, there are two cleats attached to the bottom board, to prevent the frame from sinking when it is given air or raised for one reason or another. At first, these cleats were simply two small pieces of wood nailed to the outside of the bottom board, extending beyond it by the thickness of the frame. Mr. Velars, who designed a frame system, came up with the idea of making iron cleats. This cleat is hooked onto the board; the iron is bent under the frame in a small notch in its thickness to grip the board internally. This system is very practical, and indestructible, given that they can be removed when the trunks are picked up, or fixed with a nail through a hole cut in the iron and passing through the board. So, from my point of view, this is the best trunk you can build: Om35 oak headers at the ends of the rows, Om18 inside with pitons and rods to secure the fir boards, the bottom one a little narrower than the top one, iron bars and cleats. PAILLASSONS Gardeners have been using this accessory for a very long time, even before they had bells and frames, and its manufacture has remained unchanged and familiar to all practitioners (1). Since not everyone needed a large quantity, and there was always time to make them in winter, this issue was considered negligible. With the increase in labor and the quantity needed by market gardeners, the time is ripe to reduce the cost price. The Syndicat alone, with its cooperatives, will be able to make major savings in this area. These companies have already dealt with the purchase of twine, which is the smallest part of the business, and yet they have achieved a result, the first step in this direction. Later on, we'll be buying straw in bulk for all the members, so it's no surprise that our little nephews will have their own factory, mechanically of course, where associates will find the number of straw mats they need every year, at a much lower price than they currently pay. I'm trespassing on the future here, but by examining the past and studying the issues seriously, we can well foresee the future. It is up to the younger generations to study these questions and to try, by every means in their power, to bring down the cost price of their products, if they want to compete advantageously against the competition which will not cease to grow. Old-timers can only enlighten them with their experience, and that's what I'm aiming for in writing this story. SMALL TOOLS Great improvements have been made in this area over the course of the century. All tools are much lighter, and therefore less tiring. When we had a heavy spade or pitchfork in our hands from morning to night, it took more effort to use it, or we produced less work with the same amount of fatigue. This (1) This description is given in our treatise on vegetable growing. 508 It's only in the last quarter of a century that we've had the American fork. In our time, this tool has overtaken the spade, which was almost the only tool used by gardeners in past centuries. The manufacture of the latter has also improved considerably over the olden days; much less heavy as we say, these tools penetrate the earth better by the effect of their shape alone. The same applies to all other tools. There's nothing more to be said about the watering can, since it's been done away with almost everywhere, except for a few light sprinklings. NEW EQUIPMENT In recent years, new equipment has been added to the existing range. The Decauville narrow-gauge railroad has been introduced into the marshes. I've seen tracks with turntables, and probably other tracks with points, but it's already a small network. Small wagons, pushed by men, run on the rails, transporting manure, soil, goods, etc. These plants were the precursors of the new railways. These plants were the forerunners of the large plants of the twentieth century, when these wagons would be powered by automobiles. As we saw in the previous chapter, at the end of this century, mechanical power replaced animal power to pump the water needed for irrigation. In fact, the horse does enough to bring in the manure needed for farming, and to transport the produce to the Halles Centrales, which is becoming increasingly remote as market gardens move further and further away from Paris. As market gardeners moved farther and farther away, they cultivated larger tracts of land, and ploughing was done mechanically. Inventors of agricultural implements are keeping a close eye on the transformations that progress is bringing to this type of farming, and as soon as they see the usefulness of a 908 special plough for market gardeners, the latter will not be long without an embarrassment of riches to choose from. Here again, I'm foreshadowing the future, but when eighty-year-old men tell us that they've still seen market gardeners pulling water by hand, and when we consider the successive progress made since that time in Parisian market gardening, we can well foresee the progress that the twentieth century will see. Dnied9 GOO AMONG THE OLD AND THE MODERN At the time when the statutes of 1599 officially established the market-gardening guild (1), new crops such as melons began to enrich the market-gardening scene. There is no doubt that the drafting and application of these statutes enabled the corporation to make progress in regulating all aspects of its trade. However, despite the fairly slow progress made in the trade, such as the first air beds, where lettuces, turnips and radishes were grown, and the first trials of forced cultivation, based on La Quintinie's method, trade was very limited. Nevertheless, it's fair to say that market gardeners were never idle, and when they didn't have early crops to grow, their art consisted in preserving vegetables that had arrived in autumn for most of the winter. As these plants became rarer, they sold all the more expensively: cauliflowers in particular fetched prices that would be considered fabulous today: some old-timers told me that they sold for as much as two or three francs apiece. Greens such as parsley and sorrel were also very expensive, as were scarole and celery, whose late sale remained quite lucrative for two-thirds of this century. (1) See the statutes of the ancient community of gardeners, chap. 19 of this work. The first market gardeners to cultivate early vegetables sold them at a very high price, but as we have said, this cultivation was done on a small scale, after much trial and error, and often false maneuvers that took away part of the profit. It's rare for the inventor to benefit from his invention; the profit is more often than not for those who come after him; they benefit from the experience of the first, and make improvements to the innovation that enable them to earn a higher return. It was not until after the Revolution, therefore, that trade grew significantly. The social state having been transformed, many more average fortunes having been built up at the expense of the great fortunes of past centuries, and the city growing rapidly, the introduction of forced cultivation among gardeners everywhere enabled well-to-do people to indulge in fantasies that only kings and the powerful had been able to enjoy in previous centuries. A little later, when these forced cultivations became general, they lost their value, the trade always being local, so that the market gardeners increased their costs, the profits were hardly greater. On this subject, I remember hearing a great agronomist of our century, M. Georges Ville, say in one of his lectures that "agriculture did not make a profit"; when, he said, "the farmer has withdrawn the interest on the money committed to his enterprise, the emoluments due to the manager and the director who take the lion's share for the work carried out on the farm, there is nothing left. If he has a little money, it's a saving on his personal well-being". This reasoning by a competent man can be applied to the market-growing corporation in general. However, there was a period in this century when the profession of market gardener was significantly better than it was before, and than it is today; for we have arrived at a time of transition; as we have said: the advent of the great NC industrial establishments, if I may use this expression, where the large workforce will be mechanized. This period of prosperity began with the creation of the railroads, and the city's population doubled in thirty years. In fact, according to the various censuses taken in this century, the capital had 910,000 inhabitants in 1836, and 1,825,000 in 1866. Market gardeners already had a certain amount of equipment for growing early vegetables, and these products were in demand on foreign markets, especially in England and Belgium, while the Midi region did not yet have a suitable crop to compete actively with market garden produce. As a result of these circumstances, in autumn, following the old system of preserving plants, they were sold for part of the winter, as well as or better than in the old days. When summer was dry, and production in the Paris suburbs was lacking, the market gardeners' trade also increased. I estimate that this period began around 1855, and began to decline around 1880. For the past twenty years or so, production has been steadily increasing, while revenue has been declining in the same proportions. This too-short period of prosperity for the market-growing corporation has doubled labor costs, rents and general expenses. The conclusion to be drawn is that today, market gardening is scarcely more lucrative than it was sixty years ago, but there is still a higher level of well-being, and people still live better than they did then. It's up to the younger generations to react, by looking for new ways of growing crops, by replacing manual labor with mechanical means, and in short, by using everything possible to reduce the cost price of their crops, to enable them to sell their produce cheaply 1008 and thereby compete advantageously with the Midi, thanks to its climate and the ease of transporting its produce to Paris. The market-growing corporation is made up of independent, active and hard-working men, and there is no reason to doubt that the intellectual level of the younger generations will enable them to make the progress necessary to make their industry more flourishing than ever. This is the most ardent and sincere wish I can express as I write the history of their ancestors and foretell their own. D8t-e45 GOO Few trades require such basic and inexpensive working clothes as market gardening. Bosses and workers, being occupied with the same tasks, wear the same effects. Comparing the clothes our ancestors wore with those worn today, I feel that on this side, there has been little progress. If, on the one hand, work clothes have hardly been improved, market gardeners, on the other hand, suffer as much as before, if not more, from the ravages of time. In fact, and quite to the contrary, because of the large-scale cultivation of early fruit and vegetables in our time, and also because of the remoteness of the establishments from the capital, market gardeners are even more exposed to bad weather than in the past. In the past, our elders and we ourselves still wore a limousine to drive the car; this garment completely enveloped the man and, with its double pelerine, guaranteed against the heaviest rains. The paletots I see worn by today's market-garden workers are a poor substitute for this garment, which is still very much a rarity in the trade. Another garment that has also disappeared is the "coltin". Made of coarse twill ticking, it was used in the marshes, especially to carry the "hotriau", and to protect against the rain and cold, it was a kind of overcoat for all kinds of work, and very long to get through in the rain. This garment has not been replaced, not even advantageously. Often, the same paletot used for the car is also used in the garden. When it gets wet, it dries on the back, which can lead to illness. So much for current clothing defects. As for progress, we see only the following: The blouse, which the ancients used to wear, has been abolished and replaced by the bourgeron, which modern industry supplies cheaply. Similarly, the canvas or coutil cotte is tending to disappear, to be advantageously replaced by coarse velvet pants, also supplied cheaply. The shoes used by market gardeners have not changed: clogs, made entirely of wood, are the best. Galoshes don't provide the same service, especially in winter, when feet are constantly numb. In this respect, the fashion of the ancients was still preferable: clogs made entirely of wood, with bare feet inside, with fresh straw artfully woven under and over the foot, and renewed every day. This trim, however rustic and primitive, defies the best stuffed shoes. Clogs are also useful for ploughing with a spade. The practitioner who knows how to handle this tool uses the clog as a support to turn the "bêchetée" of terrc. For a long time to come, this shoe will continue to be used by market gardeners during the winter months. In winter, our ancestors wore black woollen caps, which they called "black radishes". These bonnets have been replaced by caps of all kinds; it's all a question of fashion. For summer, everything is simplified; a pair of pants, a canvas or cotton shirt, a wide-brimmed straw hat, bare feet - that's the market gardener dressed. For those going to Les Halles, a burgundy coat and selvedge shoes or slippers complete the outfit. Women have kept pace with the progress that modern industry has made in clothing. I've been told by former market gardeners that at the beginning of the century, some women still walked barefoot in the marshes, but this fashion has long since been abandoned. Some still wear it, but others have replaced it with more modern, less expensive garments. What is disappearing completely among young women is the old hairstyle worn by our mothers and even our wives: the marmot, which we describe for the lay reader as nothing more than a handkerchief or scarf artfully tied around the head. The marmot has been worn with some coquetry by some women. Today, it has been replaced by woollen kerchiefs, bonnets and other more modern headgear. In all things, you have to keep up with the times, provided your health doesn't suffer. For the market-gardener, there's no improvement in her work; the establishments are bigger, farther from the capitalc and these two conditions mean more trouble for her, since she has to go to the Ilalles almost every day, whatever the weather. Despite this, our daughters are as healthy as their mothers and grandmothers. of contemporary times DOuXed D (OOC OEUEed S GOOgIC - * -- - - -.- -. - The revolution of 1789 came at the right time to proclaim and effectively defend individual freedom. Few today would deny the immense progress it has brought to society in general. But, as with all things, no matter how good they may be, there is another side to the coin. The esprit de corps was very strong in the old workers' communities, where everyone's rights and duties were well established. This was partly broken by the proclamation of the principle of workers' freedom. By creating individualism, the power of the money man was exaggerated, and the old edifice disintegrated, little by little, under the dissolving action of self-interest. Workers are no longer bound by any contract with their bosses, and sometimes display a certain rebellious independence towards them. The latter, for their part, are free to dismiss them whenever they see fit. The two become indifferent, if not downright hostile. If relations between employers and employees have cooled to such an extent in the world of work, isn't it because the former appear to the latter to be nothing more than labor contractors, who employ them and pay them more or less well, according to their needs, leaving them free to seek better conditions with other bosses? The modern system has thus created individualism, with its initiatives and responsibilities, its chances of success and opportunities for fortune for the few, but also its isolation, weaknesses and inconveniences for the many. The old guild is no longer there to protect all concerned by its statutes. Was this state of affairs immediately felt by our ancestors? The answer has already been given in the course of this book. The ancient community of master gardeners did not wait for the revolution to abandon its privileges. They had given them up of their own accord, so that when lightning struck, it changed almost nothing in the social state of the men in our guild. Family spirit and family life continued to exist between bosses and workers, as before, but weakened the further one moved away from the Revolution, so today this family life is tending to disappear. In all the specialties that modern progress has created in Parisian horticulture, the only ones left to feed their workers are market gardeners. We're still looking forward to the time when everyone on both sides will be free to have lunch as they please, which will happen faster on large farms than anywhere else. In other specialties, workers start their day at such and such an hour and finish it at the same time; some are paid by the day and others by the hour, exactly as in industry. In this century of progress, freedom and competition, it's true that citizens should have the greatest possible amount of individual freedom. But alongside the strong, the intelligent or simply those to whom fortune smiles, aren't there the weak, the disinherited, etc., who need to be protected? Employers and workers settle their disputes before the Justice of the Peace: that's common law. A large number of guilds settle their disputes before industrial tribunals. Isn't this a throwback to the Middle Ages and the statutes written in Etienne Boileau's Golden Book? The 009 regime set out in the statutes of ancient workers' communities has left many traces in our modern institutions, because it contains a shred of economic truth, and represents the patriarchal and protective side of the weak against the strong. While recognizing that the regime of individual freedom is more favorable to great initiative; that the commerce of this century would be hampered by the economic system of the Middle Ages; we must agree that for far less gifted natures, the opposite occurs. In the great working-class family, there have always been and always will be, alongside the strong, weak, sickly, unintelligent, short-sighted or poor managers of their own interests, as well as old, weakened or infirm workers. That's a whole world to protect. Should all these men be allowed to deteriorate, through the free-for-all, often into dullness and sometimes even into dishonor? Should we also force these old veterans to beg for bread? Once the great revolutionary turmoil had passed, the men of this century realized that it was necessary to reconstitute the associations of the Middle Ages on a different basis. The statutes of the old communities, destroyed by the Revolution, placed work under the hand of the Eglisc, whereas our modern societies are secular and liberal, freed from all religious suggestion. Our mutual aid societies and old-age pension funds have replaced the box of the old workers' communities. Professional unions are the image of the statutes of the workers' communities of the Middle Ages, linked with the ideas and economic and social needs of the modern world. Other associations, such as mutual insurance companies against hail, storms, etc., aim to protect each other, as far as possible, when one of these scourges brings ruin and desolation to one or more members of the association. Cooperative societies form a collective capital, enabling the most disadvantaged to buy their produce as cheaply as the rich. Like the societies of the Middle Ages, there is no doubt that these various groupings are the prelude to great social and economic transformations in the centuries to come. OOREed9 GOC During the Revolution, as the people of the countryside began to feel freer, they came to Paris from all surrounding countries. As trade expanded, the Normans, the majority of whom had been the source of Paris's first market gardeners, preferred to trade rather than farm; many became fruit growers, even buying squares of standing produce from the market gardeners, particularly Romaine. In our century, the majority of workers are descended from the Yonne and Nièvre departments. A number of large market-growing families are already from these departments. Half a century or more ago, a certain number of workers saved on their wages, got married and took up establishments that were then within everyone's reach. Some became owners, and like their colleagues from older families, benefited from the added value that the expansion of Paris gave to these properties. After the wars of the Revolution and the Empire, which cost Europe a million men, a few workers made beautiful marriages, either with their boss's daughter, or that of a friend; soon, there was no longer any distinction between provincials and Parisians. The worker who became boss would bring in a brother, cousin or friend who followed the same path, if he was hard-working and thrifty. It's interesting to add that all these hard-working, industrious and thrifty men who made up the market-gardening guild in the first half of this century were animated by a spirit of solidarity, which is tending to disappear from the mores of market-gardeners. These men fought to see who could establish the most workers and advance the highest sum. Let us take this opportunity to pay a warm tribute to the memory of my family's benefactor, the Honourable Charles Debille, whom we have already mentioned on another occasion, and whose parents, along with others, were the first to try forced cultivation. This good man advanced my eldest brother, like so many others, a sum of money to establish himself, and he later did the same to his entire family. I cite this fact because it's particular to me, but almost all the old market-gardener families are in the same situation, and there are hardly any provincial workers established in Paris who are not indebted for a similar kindness, to one of those old families we've mentioned several times. Isn't this the purest socialism imaginable? These modest, hard-working and thrifty men, who could have enjoyed greater well-being than they did, sometimes went to great lengths to raise their workers to their level, and were honored when the latter succeeded. In today's world, however, factories are becoming less and less accessible to workers, as their purchase price rises day by day. On the other hand, many workers are prone to the vice of the century: alcoholism. Many of them, even those who had no intention of settling, have lost the thriftiness that characterized their ancestors. The latter amassed a small nest egg and returned to their homeland, cultivating the plot of land they inherited from their parents and expanding it with the proceeds of their savings. O08 "Today, some have taken on that rebellious independence of which we spoke earlier; others, who might have had good dispositions, have found themselves sometimes put off by unjust bosses; from this comes misunderstanding between the two parties, instead of the reciprocal esteem of patriarchal life that once existed. So it's almost an exception for a worker to stay with the same boss for several years, which used to be the rule. But we should also mention the role of women in market gardening. The market gardener's job requires technical knowledge for the woman at least as much, if not more, than for the man. In fact, in addition to the work she's responsible for on the farm, a woman has to learn how to trade, as she's in charge of selling the produce at the Halles. Anyone who is familiar with the Halles trade, with the variation in prices, sometimes from one day to the next, knows just how important it is to keep an eye on the arrival and demand for a given product, when another is neglected; given that everything is generally sold on the same day, what remains loses its freshness for the Lendémain, and selling is often made all the more difficult. As mentioned in the chapter on Halles et Marchés, market gardeners have always sold their produce in the streets around the Halles, at fixed locations; sales end at 7am in summer and 8am in winter. As soon as the bell was rung to announce the end of the sale, any remaining goods had to be transported to the storerooms, otherwise they were left to be pillaged. From this brief description of the market gardeners' trade, it's easy to see what skills the market-gardener must have to ensure that she doesn't miss her sale, under the best possible conditions. according to the course. Given the special skills that market gardeners must possess, market gardeners generally 299,008 marry market gardeners; the latter accompany either their mothers or their bosses to the Halles, where they learn the trade, and are thus up to the task when they are called upon to work on their own account. When it comes to marrying off their children, market gardeners have preserved a custom handed down from their ancestors, one that no longer exists anywhere that I know of. They hold "picnic" weddings; friends and relatives are invited, and there are many of them, with all families allied to one another. Everyone pays their own way at the banquet held on the wedding day, and it's not uncommon to see two hundred people attend such banquets, where harmony never ceases to exist. It's yet another trait of customs bequeathed to the guild by the ancient community of master gardeners of centuries gone by, who themselves had inherited it from the "ghilds" of the Franks. These primitive men were already organizing "picnic" or communal banquets. So true is it that there's nothing new under the sky, and that men always retain the same temperament; but for the subject at hand, it's also proof of the good harmony that reigns within the corporation. WORK In all times, and still today, the job of market gardener is one of the hardest that men can do. In the past, when they were not far from the Halles, they carried their produce there on their backs, i.e. on hoods. In spring and summer, when the produce was more plentiful, they made several trips, so they had to be on their feet part of the night. Then the water was hauled by hand and the watering cans carried. This type of work lasted for the most part until the first quarter of this century. Workers earned twenty-five francs a month in summer, and twelve to fifteen francs in winter. They were also provided with food and lodging. Female workers, if any, earned between twelve and fifteen francs a month (t). The women, for whom the trade was perhaps even more arduous, did the same; they stayed at Les Halles to sell and deliver goods. The maid, commonly known as the "fille", was usually a child of the household at the time, and would bring back from the Halles, on a hood, the various utensils, such as mannes, manettes, clayettes, manivaux, etc., that had been used to package the products. When she returned home, she and the (1) These details were provided to me by former market gardeners. manager would prepare the vegetables for the next day's sale. In this respect, nothing has changed. It's worth saying here that, since time immemorial, market gardeners have taken a siesta during the day, in the four hottest months of the year. This siesta, which lasts an hour and a half on average, cuts the day in half, and anyone who has been in the trade knows the well-being it brings. The only complaint young people have about what all market gardeners call "the sleeping hour" is that it's too short... Later, the introduction of the horse and carriage brought relative well-being for everyone, but especially for the bosses, who hardly ever left the house. The women and the "boy" in charge of driving the carriage continued to go to the Halles to sell vegetables. It was at this time that the cultivation of early vegetables took on a certain importance, necessitating the use of much more manure to build up the beds. Once the produce had been deposited at Les Halles, the man in charge of driving it would bring back a wagonload of manure, which he would load at the ad ministrations or at the homes of private individuals with stables in Paris. Afterwards, he continued to work in the marsh with the boss and other workers, if any. Until around 1860, the "girls" continued to bring back from Les Halles the various accessories used to transport vegetables. As the Halles began to move further away, some of them brought only the clothes they needed to make the morning sale. When the first bateaux-mouches were installed, it became customary for many market-gardeners living either upstream or downstream from the river not to return from Les Halles on foot, and this practice quickly spread to other districts, whatever the means of transport (omnibuses and streetcars). It also did away with the custom of carrying a hood. From then on, the girl would pack the above-mentioned garments and return home 508 by the easiest and cheapest means of transport. In the work by Messrs Moreau and Daverne, we find that women earned an average of two hundred and forty francs a year around mid-century. In addition, they made profits in the marsh, such as cauliflower leaves, which were removed when this vegetable was prepared for sale, baby carrots, chickweed when it wasn't weeded in time, etc. They also made profits in the "Illes d'Or", where they worked the marshes. They also made a profit at Les Ilalles, carrying goods when they were sold, either to the merchants in the pavilions, or to the city fruit growers who left their cars with the guards around Les Halles. Today, they earn an average of one franc a day; they are housed, fed and laundered, and they also enjoy the profits we listed above, which have increased with the growth of the establishments. It's one of the hardest jobs a woman can do, but it's also one of the ones where she earns the most; we're talking, of course, only about purely manual jobs. The same authors we quoted above also tell us that, at the time they were writing (in 1845), men earned eighteen to twenty francs in winter, plus two francs a Sunday (this was called the "loan"). In summer, they earned thirty to two francs a month, plus a loan of two or two francs fifty. In my day, from 1860 to 1870, the average was forty francs a month in winter and sixty francs in summer. Today, prices have risen from fifty to sixty francs in winter, and eighty in summer. Food and lodging are always extra. With bosses and workers all working and living together, the way of life has improved a great deal, and market gardeners today live much better than most workers. 008 After 1870, as the spirit of independence grew among workers, some bosses agreed with their workers to abolish the Sunday evening meal, which was initially paid for by those who agreed to this deal. The practice then became widespread, and everything was included in the monthly wage. The remoteness of the establishments from the capital, forcing the man driving the car to eat outside in the morning, and the suppression of the Sunday evening meal are the prelude to the moment when the workers will no longer be fed by the boss. It's quite possible that this will lead to a new sense of well-being on both sides. LES JARDINIERS DE PARIS From this point of view, the old well-off families can be blamed for not giving their children enough education. It was legendary among them to believe that it was useless to be educated in order to do their job. I've noticed that, among men of a certain age, a large number don't even have the education of a common peasant. Even more so, they lagged far behind the well-to-do peasants who were already giving their children a secondary education. Our old market gardeners, however intelligent but uneducated, didn't at first understand this new benefit of the Revolution: education for the people. With compulsory education enshrined in our laws under the Third Republic, the children of market gardeners are no longer below those of other corporations. But the time is not far off when this will no longer be sufficient. With the trade tending more and more to become an industry, and establishments growing steadily away from the capital, there will be new means of cultivation, new fertilizers, etc., all of which will entail bookkeeping in good order, and knowledge of agricultural chemistry, in order to analyze one's own soil and fertilizers, in a word, to break out of routine and enter the realm of science. Some crops will disappear. New ones will probably emerge, and we'll have to study them before applying them. The GOOs 6 trade will make it increasingly possible to specialize crops. The manures used on the farm will no longer be sufficient to fertilize the soil; chemical fertilizers will have to be used, and to use them economically, it will be necessary to know the dominant element of each plant, i.e. the element it absorbs in the greatest quantity; it will also be necessary to know which elements the soil we are cultivating contains. In short, it will require a wealth of technical knowledge that already belongs to the domain of modern horticultural science. Today's market gardeners are already suffering the consequences of their lack of education. The forcing of plants brings with it new diseases, in the face of which they are forced to sit idly by, not one of them having the education necessary to carry out reasoned experiments to combat these diseases. Some of these diseases, such as lettuce and romaine sucker (peronospora gangliiformis), melon nail, grey mould and rust, which attack other plants, have already caused enormous losses to the corporation. Lastly, crop specialization destroys the rotation system so much advocated by the old-timers in all types of cultivation, and which still has its raison d'être, despite all the fertilizers. After a certain period of time, when the same crop is grown on the same land, the land becomes exhausted and the harvest is hardly remunerative. So, by analyzing this land, we could give it back what the crop has taken away from it; or we could change the crop in time to avoid losses. It is only with this special knowledge that future market gardeners, with large farms, will be able to make a profit. 4 PLAYED BY CERTAIN MARAICHERS From a political and communal point of view, some market gardeners played a certain role in the 19th century. During the terrible year of 1870-71, Becquerelle, from Ivry-sur-Seine, was appointed mayor of the town during this troubled period. This almost uneducated man imposed himself through his dignified, energetic attitude and his life of integrity. Threatened several times with being shot, either by the Communards or by the Versaillais, as the belligerents were then called, he never flinched or lost his cool. This heroic attitude saved Ivry from the horrors of civil war. This brave old man belonged to the generation of 1848; he had retained all its principles. He never acquiesced to the imperial regime that France had given itself, or rather allowed to be imposed. On every occasion, he held high the flag of the Republic. So, to consecrate his long life as a sincere republican, he expressed the wish that the Marseillaise should be played at his funeral, the moment his body was lowered into the grave. I attended the funeral, as did a large crowd, and the whole town of Ivry had come to salute the memory of this energetic man of integrity. When the Société musicale d'Ivry, who had made it their duty to accompany this brave old man, played the first stanzas of our national anthem, a shiver ran through the audience, unaccustomed to hearing it on such occasions. One sensed that this man of principle had possessed broad and generous ideas. Another example I'd rather not mention is that of my elder brother. A resident of the 15th arrondissement of Paris for some fifteen years, he was recommended by his friends to be appointed deputy mayor of his arrondissement in 1878, when Jules Grévy became President of the Republic. He held this post for three years. The voters of the Javel district then appointed him to the posts of town councillor and general councillor of the Seine, which he held until 1889. For one year, he was even vice-president of the latter assembly. Around this time, he was struck down by a serious illness, which took its toll on him two years later. Since 1870, a number of market gardeners have been elected municipal councillors, most of them living in the Paris suburbs. Today, there are a number of them. Some are mayors of their own communes. Among them is Mr. Duvillard, who has been mayor of Arcueil for many years. Among those who live in Paris, several are members of charitable organizations, such as welfare offices, school caisses, municipal crèches, and so on. With this presentation, we wanted to prove that, with their freedom and independence, the market gardeners of the end of the century can and already do play an important role in society. A number of them have received or are receiving honorary distinctions. M. Laizier, founding president of the Société de Secours Mutuels, was a Chevalier de la Légion d'Ilonneur. Ponce Isidore, expert arbitrator with the Tribunal. Civil for many years, was chevalier du Mérite Agricole. My eldest brother also held this distinction, thanks to the services he rendered, as a general councillor, for 08 the creation of horticultural schools, especially for morally abandoned children. The same goes for Mr. Georges Chemin, thanks to the selections he made from various plants, including the tomato which bears his name, and the Golden Celery which he succeeded in fixing. We should also mention Mr. Duvillard, as professor of vegetable cultivation for the Seine department and the Chambre Syndicale des ouvriers jardiniers. Several others, such as Messrs Stainville, Hébrard Alexandre, Ilébrard Laurent, for services rendered, either to the Horticultural Society, or to the Société de Secours Mutuels-des jardiniers et horticulteurs du département de la Seine. A few others have been awarded this decoration for a variety of other reasons. Shortly before the publication of this book, Mr. Narcisse Laurent was awarded the Cross of Chevalier du Mérite Agricole for his participation in the International Horticultural Exhibition in St. Petersburg. Mr. Narcisse Laurent had long been known in the horticultural world for the impetus he gave, long ago, to his cultivation of early vegetables, by installing steam circulation in his beds, and as one of the main innovators of new forced cultivation methods. The market gardeners who have written and left their mark on the history of their corporation are extremely rare. Messrs Moreau and Daverne, however, wrote a Manuel de la Culture Maraîchère and an Exposé Historique de leur corporation around mid-century, from which I was able to draw some information. Later, Mr. Isidore Ponce also wrote a small work on the same subject. That's about all the literary works I could find, written by market gardeners themselves. 1 May posterity salute the memory of these kind-hearted men who honored the guild in the history of our country. 8 MARAICHERS Here are the main localities occupied by market gardeners at the end of the century. Starting as we always have, on the banks of the Seine, upstream from Paris and on the right bank, we find Alfortville, Maisons-Alfort and Créteil, these three countries situated between the Seine and the Marne, in a beautiful plain that awaits the market gardeners of the centuries to come. A small group exists in Champigny on the right bank of the Marne; closer to Paris, there are still a few market gardeners, in Saint-Mandé and Vincennes, but they will disappear in a short space of time. Then it's on to Bobigny, home to the largest group on the right bank, and La Courneuve, Drancy and a few other nearby villages also have market gardeners. Next, Stains, which already contains a small group. Then, scattered among the factories and railway lines, the remaining ones in Aubervilliers and Saint-Denis, which will also soon disappear. Finally, there's a large group in Gennevilliers and Asnières, on the peninsula that bears that locality's name. A number on this side irrigate with sewage from the city of Paris. As for the few that remain in Clichy and Saint-Ouen, they are so lost among factories and built-up areas that, for a hundred different reasons, not a single one will be left before long. Then you have to go all the way back to Billancourt to find a few; but on this side, space is so tight that they can't increase, hemmed in as they are by the Seine, which bypasses this locality, and by the population, which is also increasing rapidly in these parts, close to the Bois de Boulogne. On the left bank, the marshes of Issy-les-Moulineaux are severely eroded by the maneuvering area. In Malakoff, those who live in the upper part of the country will be able to stay there for a long time to come, barring unforeseen events. Châtillon could take in a few, but the land is already too valuable for market gardeners to settle there, given that land cannot currently be paid for at more than three to four francs a meter to establish a marsh. On this side, moreover, the terrain is too hilly for further development. Montrouge still has a number of market gardeners, but these will disappear as the land is taken over by industry. However, those near the Bagneux cemetery will be around for a long time to come. The largest group on the left bank is at Bagneux, while Gentilly, Arcueil and Larue, on the banks of the Bièvre, have a few. Ivry will shortly be sending the few remaining to Vitry and Choisy-le-Roi, which already have a small group. On this side, as on the right bank of the Seine, a beautiful plain awaits the market gardeners of the centuries to come. They will undoubtedly build up settlements of the kind we anticipate, and of which we will give an overview in a subsequent chapter I CURRENT VALUE OF MARAIS AND THEIR RENTAL AREAS With trade and industry having made more progress in our century alone, than in the previous twenty centuries, the land in and around Paris has considerably increased in value 1). The land occupied by our ancestors at the beginning of the century (near Charles V's enclosure) was then worth twelve to fifteen thousand francs per hectare; today it sells for five to eight hundred francs per meter. The rental of this land, for market gardening, was worth five to six hundred francs per hectare, including the dwelling house. By 1815, this same land was already worth between eighty and one hundred thousand francs per hectare. It was therefore in the second half of the century, i.e. since the creation of the railroads, that they acquired such a considerable value. The land occupied around mid-century by the children of the former (in the vicinity of Louis XVI's surrounding walls) was worth between twenty-eight and thirty thousand francs per hectare, and now sells for between one hundred and three hundred francs per metre. At that time, rent was already doubled, with prices ranging from thirteen to fourteen hundred francs per hectare, including the dwelling house and outbuildings. Throughout the 11) Moreau et Daverne ; Hlistoire des Maraichers de Paris. 145COOS C century, these conditions have not changed. When we speak of "renting a marsh", we always mean including the dwelling house and outbuildings. At the end of the century, land near Louis-Philippe's fortified enclosure, where there are still a few market gardeners disappearing as leases come to an end, was worth twelve to fifteen thousand francs per hectare at mid-century. They are now selling for between twenty-five and fifty francs a metre, depending on the district in which they are located. It's understandable that with this prodigious increase in the price of land, market gardeners, whether owners or tenants, can't cultivate it for long. The former, who have crossed the fortified zone to settle in the neighboring communes, are also benefiting from a fabulous increase in land prices. The capital suffocates within its walls, and land is exorbitantly expensive. The major industries that need a lot of space are moving out of Paris to set up in these communes: the result is this enormous increase in land, especially on the banks of the river, a canal or a railway line; in other words, the whole of the outskirts of Paris is in these conditions, albeit more or less advantageously. The largest groups of market gardeners are currently to be found in the last communes of the Seine department. Suitable land can be bought for between three and four francs a metre, of course, unless there are exceptional circumstances that cannot be used as a basis for general assessments. The cost of buildings, fences, wells, etc. amounts to around twenty thousand francs, bringing the price per hectare of market garden land to between fifty and sixty thousand francs. The rental of these establishments is currently worth between two thousand and two thousand four hundred francs per hectare. The century that is about to begin will not pass without further expansion of the capital. There is already a 98 project to dismantle the fortifications in the north-western region of Paris. The population centers of Boulogne, Neuilly-Levallois and Clichy will thus be incorporated into the capital, which will only be limited in this direction by one of the bends in the Seine. These communes have become real cities. Progress will perhaps be slower in the rugged, quarry-strewn region stretching from Châtillon to Bicêtre, south of Paris. Nevertheless, as the capital continues to expand, we can expect a steady rise in the price of all the land around it. DEIESS Q OF THE ESTABLISHMENTS TRANSACTIONS TO WHICH THEY GIVE RISE At the end of the last century, when market gardeners owned very little equipment - most of them had neither horse nor carriage, no chassis, only a few bells - establishments were not yet sold en bloc. I had before me the minutes of a sale of the equipment of a market gardener, which was not the least important for the time; the total of this sale amounted to fourteen or fifteen hundred francs, including the furniture. It was only with the introduction of cranks in market gardening establishments, which began to constitute an establishment in its own right, that market gardeners adopted the mutually beneficial system of selling their establishments in their current state, a custom that has continued to this day. In fact, the seller always makes a profit by selling everything that makes up his establishment as a whole, while at the same time transferring his right to the lease, or, if he is the owner, granting a lease to the buyer. The latter then takes over the business, having bought everything: seeds, plants and merchandise of all ages, and from the day after his acquisition, he continues to sell the products likely to be sold. In this way, an establishment is more valuable in spring than in autumn. Both parties are aware of the advantages and disadvantages of the seasons, and this facilitates transactions. We also take into account the remoteness of the Ilalles, the quality of the land, and the length of time it has been used for market gardening, although less so today than in the past, when it was felt, for example, that summer cauliflowers could not have been grown in areas other than Vaugirard and Grenelle. Depth and abundance of water were also taken into account. It's true that now, with the use of motors, depth is becoming a secondary issue; but abundance is still, as it always has been, the primary question. At the same time, the quantity and quality of all the equipment are examined. Furniture alone is not included in these transactions, and the buyer must follow the commitments made by the seller with regard to the establishment. More often than not, the latter gives the former staggered deadlines for liquidation. This approach makes it much easier for young people to establish themselves. This is especially true for those from the provinces, who are generally not wealthy. A few small savings from the young man or woman, together with a certain sum advanced by a friend, or as we said in another chapter, more often than not by a boss, form a payment to the conptant, giving a certain guarantee to the seller. The remainder is paid with interest at the set times. In most cases, the two parties know each other, or the buyer is recommended and supported by a serious person. It may be added that this is one of the trades in which there are the fewest cases where commitments are not kept. The first establishments sold in this way were worth between two and five thousand francs, depending on the size of the equipment; their price has risen with the progress of the industry. By mid-century, they were already worth between eight and twelve thousand francs. With the introduction of irrigation, prices rose even further, and all equipment increased considerably, with average prices rising from fifteen to twenty thousand francs. Today, with new advances such as engines, Decauville railroads, etc., the average is between twenty-two and twenty-five thousand francs. To set up an establishment at the beginning of the twentieth century, taking into account all the advances mentioned above, we'd need to operate on a plot of land of at least fifteen thousand meters, on which we'd put a thousand frames, five to six thousand bells, horse and carriage, motor to operate the pump, irrigation, Decauville railway, etc., i.e. we'd need to spend thirty thousand francs to organize such an establishment. These figures show that the market-gardening industry in Paris, like all other industries, is set to become capitalist; the number of establishments is declining day by day, as those that remain grow in size, and it is already becoming difficult for workers to set up on their own. Such is the force of things, a force against which nothing can be done. The day when products can all be sold by auction, making it easier for growers to specialize their crops, capitalists will set up ćtablissements on hectares of land, with equipment whose scale we cannot predict: farms that will have a manager and a director, exactly as is already the case in large-scale farming. DURING THE XIXC CENTURY ----------------- As far as market gardening is concerned, we can say that, as in all industries, if trade has increased considerably, it is in the interests of consumers in general. We have seen that, at the beginning of the century, the produce of early crops became more accessible to a certain category of citizens, but they were not yet within the reach of all pockets, whereas today almost everyone can afford the fantasy of eating, for example, a Laituc in the middle of winter. In other words, production has risen sharply and prices have fallen accordingly. But with costs rising in general, how does the producer manage to make a profit? Well, where our ancestors struggled to sell a thousand lettuces, for example, we sell fifty thousand today. Produce a lot and sell it cheaply - that's the motto of modern trade. Market gardeners, who have always done their business on a cash basis, generally don't keep books; however, some young people, without having one as such, are beginning to keep track of the business they are doing. As the farm expands, it will be necessary to keep proper books, just as in all industries and businesses. It is therefore only possible to estimate their sales over the course of the century. To guide us in this evaluation, we shall base ourselves on a work done in 1845 by two intelligent practitioners, Messrs More au and Daverne, who wrote the history of their corporation in a manner judicious enough to fix the historian. These authors tell us how much equipment was used in their guild at the time, and how much business was done annually. They estimated it at thirteen or fourteen million francs, including the product of certain crops, such as mushrooms and many flowers, crops which have been transferred to other specialties. Taking all these facts into account, the practitioner can, by deduction, roughly establish the vegetable trade that the ancients did at the beginning of the century. Having established the value of forced cultivation at the time when the authors we have just quoted were writing; having deducted the product of mushroom and flower cultivation, we estimate that Parisian market gardeners, at the beginning of the century, sold three to four thousand francs worth of vegetables. Until around the middle of the century, trade was only local, but with the city rapidly increasing in population, and containing many more average fortunes than under the old regime, trade in general was affected by this well-being. So did the corporation's particular trade, which had grown from three to four million francs at the beginning of the century to thirteen or fourteen million francs in 1845. It was not until the trade became international, therefore, that it took on a very large dimension. This expansion will only increase in all countries as new commercial outlets open up. Today's market gardeners have become completely O08 specialists, growing only early vegetables and salads, then in full season. We estimate that they cultivate a thousand hectares of land, with a thousand frames and five to six thousand bells per hectare. This land, covered with the equipment we have indicated, yields an average of thirty thousand francs per hectare. The Paris market-gardening industry therefore delivers thirty million francs worth of production to the trade every year. This represents a tenfold increase in sales over the course of the century, but taking into account the fall in food prices, we can estimate that it is fifteen times higher today than it was at the beginning of the century. We have already mentioned that market garden crops will be forced to become even more specialized. Competition from products from the south of France will force market gardeners to abandon certain crops, such as the forced cultivation of asparagus in the off-season, just as they have abandoned others such as Ilaricots, Peas and Potatoes. But other forced crops, such as Lettuce, Romaine, Turnip, Melon etc., will continue to expand and will withstand all kinds of competition for a long time to come. Later on, we'll see market-gardening establishments that will be veritable factories for the production of one or other of these plants. MUSHROOM GROWING We saw in a previous chapter that mushroom growing became specialized during the 19th century. Let's try to retrace the phases this cultivation went through. The ancients knew about edible mushrooms; they considered them very good, but very dangerous to health and even to life. "Being of a very cold and damp nature, they must be used sparingly, but they are especially dangerous by accident" (1). The Latins called them fungi, a name which, according to some authors, comes from funerals, and others from the number of murderers. Oh, what gluttony! said Pliny, what a shameful attachment to the pleasure of the mouth, to expose oneself to death in order to satisfy it. Mathiole said: you'd have to be a fool to want to satisfy your taste at such a price. Alongside the excellent edible mushroom, cultivated in such abundance in the ancient quarries around Paris, other varieties of the most poisonous species grow in the wild. This family, so numerous (mushrooms are the masters of the earth), presents (1) Delamare, Traité de Police. a few pleasant-looking genera, such as the false oronge, whose bright red globe stands out against the carpet of greenery; but when you think of the deadly venom this mushroom contains, beauty disappears, what does the envelope matter, when the heart reveals only gall and poison. We'll deal here only with cultivated mushrooms, the others being outside our scope. Parisian market gardeners have been cultivating them for over two centuries; unfortunately, these modest men have neglected to write down or have written down their cultivation methods. La Quintinie, who admits to having learned a great deal from the Parisian mareschais, must have learned this culture from them. He's the first author I've come across who talks about it, albeit very vaguely. I believe that the famous grower must have considered this crop a negligible quantity, or he made some very serious omissions on this subject in his treatise on cultivation.
