For the edification of the practical reader, we will quote verbatim what he wrote: CHAMPIGON LAYERS "The difference between those which are to produce plants by their heat, and those which are to produce mushrooms, consists, firstly, in the fact that the latter do not need to be sunk into the ground, like the others which are sunk in by half a foot, unless it is to be what we call deaf layers. Secondly, this difference consists in the fact that the first ones must be regularly placed and joined on top, instead of the others being in the shape of a humpback". Certainly, if the layer is laid out in this way at the end of winter, mushroom spawn, or mycelium, may form in the autumn; this happens quite frequently, even in layers built for forcing vegetables. But this is only a random occurrence, and does not constitute cultivation as such, since it is only due to the state of decomposition of the manure, the weather it has been in to achieve this result, etc. The cultivation of mushrooms, as mentioned above, carried out by market gardeners over two centuries ago, either in the cellar or outdoors, only became more widespread in this century, when it began to be used in the quarries from which the ashlar, masonry and plaster used to build the great city were extracted. The first quarries used for this purpose were in Passy (probably even under the Palais du Trocadéro), Montrouge, the XIVth arrondissement and parts of the XVth and XIIIth arrondisements of Paris. The first specialists to do so were Legrain in Passy, others of the same family in Montrouge, Daniel in Vaugirard, Marchand near La Glacière in the XIIIth arrondissement, Arbot in Châtillon, followed by Brique, Burvin, Moulin, Sanguin, Tarenne and others. It's hardly possible to compare these early mushroom growers with the large farms run by their grandchildren today. Some of them had neither horse nor carriage, and like the market gardeners, they carried their produce to Les Halles on hoods. The mushrooms were put into "maniveaux", a type of small, round, slightly splayed basket; these maniveaux were stored in another basket called a "clayette", which held between twenty and thirty, and so the sale was made. In the Maison Rustique by Malpeyre, Bixio and Bailly, written at the beginning of this century, we find a treatise on mushroom cultivation. What's certain is that, according to the testimony of the ancients, market gardeners were already cultivating them at that time, but as this treatise was not written by practitioners, they missed out on many small details. Nevertheless, it's already a much more complete treatise on cultivation than La Quintinie's. C Here's an extract from that treatise: COUCHES A CHAMPIGNONS "The construction of mushroom beds differs completely from other beds. Donkey manure is the easiest to turn white, followed by mule and horse manure. Market gardeners obtain horse manure, arrange it in regular heaps, Om60 high, tread it and water it according to the weather, leaving it like this for a fortnight; at the end of this time, they reshuffle it, taking care to change the one on the surface with the one in the middle. When the manure has reached the desired point, i.e. unctuous, fatty, it is laid out in roof-shaped millstones, Om80 wide by Om55 high at their base. These millstones are best built in a cellar, rather than in the open air, and are lined from distance to distance with small pieces of a special substance known as mushroom spawn. It's already a culture: there's the preparation of the manure, the assembly of the millstone, the dimensions of which seem enormous, as we'll see from the following treatise written by a true practitioner, the planting of the millstone, if I may use that term, or the grafting; what market gardeners and mushroom growers call "lardage". This is the beginning of the art of mushroom cultivation, but I repeat, our ancestors cultivated them long before this time with a certain art. In Delamare's book, written at the beginning of the last century, the author says that gardeners have found the secret of producing mushrooms all year round. Here again, I declare that it is regrettable that these practitioners have not written down, in a work of some kind, the names of the initiators of this new crop which has taken on such great development at the end of our century. It is however 909 easy to prejudge how gardeners started it. Seeing. mushrooms growing around their manure heaps, or as I said on old layers, where the manure had just decomposed to achieve this result; the mycelium gaining the neighboring parts from where it had formed; Intelligent men, taking these observations into account, have had to prepare manure to bring it to the state in which the mushrooms grew; place small pieces of spawn, or mycelium, in the manure thus prepared, cover the whole with earth and, after a certain time, harvest mushrooms, of which there is nothing to fear. As for wild mushrooms, I don't deny that there are some excellent ones out there, but according to competent people, there are some that are excessively poisonous and bear a strong resemblance to the former; it's not uncommon to see almost entire families poisoned by these terrible cryptogams. Finally, let's mention the Traité de culture des champignons, by Mr. Duvillard, professor of market gardening in the Seine department and member of the Chambre syndicale des ouvriers jardiniers. This treatise expresses itself in these terms: A USEFUL AND PRACTICAL WAY TO PRODUCE MUSHROOMS (1) "Take fresh horse manure, as much as possible, and make a square of it one to one-and-a-half meters thick; the size will be in proportion to the quantity you wish to establish. It is always preferable to "cut down" more manure than less, as the surplus is never lost in cultivation. This operation (1) In another work, entitled Culture forcée et ordinaire des plantes potagères ainsi que des champiquons, we publish a treatise on mushroom cultivation and on the choice of the best whites to use, etc. 509 is called "abattre" (cutting down) the manure into planks. Care must be taken to place the manure always in the same direction, so as not to have to pull on it to "turn it over", an operation that is performed twice, every eight days. This operation will be carried out twice, every eight days. It consists in taking the manure, the last to be placed, and mixing it, taking care that the edges are put back in the middle of the heap, and the middle on the edges. Once these two operations have been completed, the manure must be left for a few more days before it is put to work, i.e., put into millstones. If, during the preceding operations, the manure should sometimes be too dry, care should be taken to water it by gauge, as the beds are established, and especially on the strawier bed. "The manure, thus prepared, is made into millstones with a thickness of 0m40 at the base, ending at zero, over a height of Om40 to Om50. This operation is called "mounting the wheels" by the specialists. The millstones must be well made, the manure must not be put into tampons, and must be well trodden with the hands and knees. A few days after this operation, when the grindstone is no longer too hot internally, the mushroom spawn can be added. This is called "larder"; you need to prepare pieces of spawn, called "mises", Om10 square by OmOl thick, and place them 0m40 apart, in two rows, the first row as low as possible and the second in the middle of the wheel. Leave in this state for twenty to twenty-five days, then pat the sides of the wheel with your hand, putting a knee on the top to support it throughout its thickness, and knocking off any bits of manure that may be sticking out, so as to make its surface as even as possible. "At this point, we check the work the spawn has done in the wheel, and proceed with the "gobetage", which consists in covering the wheel with uncultivated soil, preferably subsoil, and not more than a centimetre thick. "The fungus will form in this soil and appear two or three weeks later, if all the work has been well done, if the quarry has always had the same temperature and a regular draught. If the soil becomes too dry, it should be watered lightly with a fine-headed watering can. "When we set up millstones outside, we have to wrap them in large dry manure, which is renewed as often as it gets too wet. This is called putting on a "shirt", and on top of this a thickness of manure, varying according to the weather, to maintain normal heat". This treatise is undoubtedly the clearest, most precise and practical guide to mushroom growing, and the ultimate in mushroom cultivation. However, despite all this hard work, sometimes you just can't get it right, from a financial point of view of course, because you still have to reckon with the weather. When the weather is soft and rainy, and the temperature heavy, the quarries lack air and "sulk", as the mushroom growers say. In such cases, they set up long wooden chimneys over air intake holes, and light a strong stove at the bottom of the quarry to draw in the stale air it contains and renew it. Despite all their efforts, they still don't achieve the same results as when the weather is dry and cold. When the millstones sulk, "molles" are formed, the mycelium penetrates the earth, and the fungus doesn't form, representing a leprosy on the surface. In this case, remove all the diseased parts with the soil, and gobble again. If the weather changes, and the air in the quarry is renewed, the disease sometimes disappears, but always with some benefit to the grower. The purity of the spawn used for larding also needs to be taken into account, as this spawn can be diseased and eaten by another, more voracious cryptogam, which mushroom growers call 908 "verdigris". Care must be taken to peel the spawn before introducing it into the grinder, so that it does not contain this parasite, which devours everything and produces nothing. This description of mushroom cultivation will enable the layman to judge the care, technical knowledge and inconvenience involved in making a living from this crop. The Pasteur Institute, which has rendered eminent services to science and humanity, is already working on sterilizing mushroom spawn. There can be no doubt that the study of this question, placed in the hands of scientists, will yield practical and satisfactory results. So there's a long way to go from the mushroom production of yesteryear to the millions of kilograms that mushroom growers bring to the IIalles today. Today, it's a veritable industry. There are around one hundred and fifty mushroom growers in the départements of Seinc, Seine-et-Oise and Seine-et-Marne, who produce around two million running meters of mushroom wheels. Their union estimates that the average input is twenty thousand kilograms per day, but with great variation; there is much more in winter and spring than in summer. Given these figures, it's easy to establish the annual production of this new industry. It would produce around seven million kilograms of mushrooms a year, with an average sale of 1 fr. 20 per kilogram, giving an approximate income of 8,500,000 francs. This industry employs around eighteen hundred workers, some of them year-round; these are true mushroom workers; the others are sort of laborers, finding work in the worst season of the year, i.e. winter. Almost all market gardeners are able to do this work. They are paid from O fr. 45 to 0 fr. 60 an hour, depending on their abilities. Mushroom growers also employ eight to nine hundred horses to transport their manure, gobet soil, 909 produce to Les Halles, and the body of millstones to the stations, which is bought by large-scale cultivation, because when the millstone no longer produces mushrooms, the manure is very good as fertilizer, and sought after by growers in the greater suburbs for certain crops. Those farthest from Paris have their manure taken by rail to the nearest station to where they slaughter their floors. They pay an average rent of O fr. 15 per meter of gallery they occupy. One meter of millstone requires around 1.50 francs worth of manure, which represents 3,000,000 francs worth of raw material from the capital. The sale of millstones is estimated, on average, at O fr. 50 per metre, giving a profit of 1,000,000 francs. In addition to this, they spend a great deal of money to obtain virgin blanks, and they also prefer the blanks from millstones built outside for larding in quarries. Some market gardeners set up millstones to be lifted in white, i.e. at the moment when the fungus begins to appear and form on the surface; at this moment, the whole millstone is invaded by the mycelium, it is sliced with a long knife like a lump of butter, and removed in wedges from Om15 to 0m20 thick. It is in these wedges that the bets are prepared, as described in Mr. Duvillard's treatise. In short, these abandoned quarries, once used for the construction industry, are a source of income for the owners of the land, sustaining a large number of working-class families and increasing the wealth of our national production. It's worth adding that mushrooms are never wasted; when they grow in greater abundance, they are preserved, as are vegetables such as peas, beans, tomatoes, etc. Many of these preserves are shipped to our customers. Many of these preserves are shipped abroad, which also increases our export figures. This is another important branch of vegetable produc006 tion, if I can use that term, that our ancestors created. Let's add that at the International Horticultural Exhibition just held in Saint Petersburg, a member of the mushroom growers' union, Mr. Lécaillon, exhibited fresh mushrooms. They arrived in very good condition, but two days later they were spoiling, so it's only a question of shipment, a question that will soon be resolved by trade, to supply these distant markets with our fresh products and thereby create a major commercial outlet for this industry. This small exhibition, which was only an ess ai, was rewarded with an average silver medal. The mushroom growers, being absolute specialists, set up a trade association to defend their interests in every respect. Dgiised 9 GO AGAINST INSECT PESTS AND PLANT DISEASES In a previous chapter, we said that market gardeners had remained helpless against the ravages of insect pests and parasitic diseases, either due to a lack of education or to a lack of awareness of the need to combat them. As we shall see, the means employed to combat these scourges have remained rudimentary. Among the insects harmful to market gardening, one of the most dreaded is the courtillière or cricket mole. This dreadful insect grows as big as a man's thumb, working its way through the soil in exactly the same way as a mole, cutting and gnawing at everything it comes across. It is especially formidable in the layers. Establishing its lair at the bottom, beneath the manure, it can only be tracked down when the topsoil is removed to reconstitute new layers. The most practical way for gardeners to destroy it is to pour oil or kerosene into the holes where it settles, by pouring water over them. A drop of oil or kerosene that reaches a courtillière is sure to kill it. But these insects have nests with hundreds of eggs, so that enough eggs always escape to spread, and it's almost impossible to get rid of them completely in the soils they like. During spring and summer, at night, the courtillières come up for air. Cats, which are very fond of them, also destroy a certain number. White grubs, called horseflies by gardeners, are only to be feared in areas that have recently been converted to marshland, where they wreak considerable havoc. In old marshes, where the soil is oily and often disturbed, there are very few of them. Various aphid species also attack market garden crops. One of the most fearsome is the black aphid, which attacks melons. Some years it comes in such abundance and so quickly that it's hard to get rid of. To obtain this result, gardeners use nicotine cut with water to water the affected plants; but this operation must be carried out as soon as the fly that indicates its arrival is seen. This is because if the operation is carried out when there is a large number of flies, the plant would have to be destroyed at the same time as the insect. It's fair to say that the cure is worse than the disease. Caterpillars and larvae of all shapes and colors attack plants in different ways; some through the roots, others, in greater numbers, through the leaves. The only way to get rid of them is to look for them and crush them. However, for those that eat cauliflower leaves, you can get far fewer by watering often; the leaf, which is continually wet, keeps away the moth that produces them. The flea beetle, which destroys sorrel and young cabbage plants, can be controlled by frequent watering, and young cauliflower plants sometimes have to be shaded in late spring to get rid of it. The guillotine, or white worm, attaches itself especially to the 900 roots and collar of cabbages of all varieties. This forms a kind of bulge at the root, and the plant perishes. Slugs are very common in certain areas, especially in autumn. To destroy them, powdered lime is spread on the soil. Affected slugs are burned, but often take refuge under the leaves; where they cannot be reached, care must be taken to find them and collect them. In certain crops, we also make small piles of leaves on the ground, where they take refuge. If left unchecked, these insects can wreak havoc. Grey mould is caused by a tiny acarus, a small insect that attacks the parenchyma of plants, especially as they age. Cucurbits, beans and celery are particularly susceptible. A radical cure for this disease is not yet known. Romaine blight, in spring, is due to the action of a microscopic fungus that spreads through sudden changes in temperature, cold sleet, etc. Melon blight, which affects early and late-season melons in particular, also appears following cold dews. There is as yet little agreement on how to treat this disease. How many studies have yet to be carried out on these various diseases, whose effects often compromise market garden crops? Eggplant cultivation has been abandoned, tomato cultivation severely compromised, not only because of the competition it has established elsewhere, but also because of the diseases to which these plants are prone when the cool nights of September arrive. But one disease that was hardly known, or at least did not cause serious losses, before the introduction of early crops, is (miller) (Peronospora gangliiformis, berh), which attacks Lettuces and 008 Romaines grown under glass. Plants grown outside are easily cured when the weather turns dry and warm. As for early plants grown under frames and cloches, when they are affected they do not recover. The disease progresses more or less rapidly, depending on the weather, but it is extremely rare for it to disappear entirely. This parasite feeds at the plant's expense, drying out the affected leaves and causing it considerable damage. During wet winters, this disease is the source of losses that can already be quantified in millions of francs. This forced cultivation begins in early autumn and lasts until late spring. Some twenty years ago, a small group of market gardeners got together to look for ways to combat the miller. No appreciable results were achieved. One day, this idea will be taken up again, and let's hope and wish for the younger generations that they will be more successful than their elders and that they will know a practical way of avoiding losses which will become considerable in their crops, due to the very extension of these crops. While the success of this small association may have been limited, we must bear in mind the generous goal it set itself, a goal we do not despair of seeing achieved one day or another. So, for the edification of those who may later wish to take up this idea, we publish below the association's articles of association. They are followed by the important report to the Acacémie des sciences, made on this subject by Mr. Max Cornu, the learned botanist who now holds the chair of cultivation at the Muséum d'histoire naturelle in Paris. STATUTES OF THE ASSOCIATION "The committee set up to combat plant diseases is offering a bonus of ten thousand francs to the person who can provide the means of radically curing the malady it calls le (meunier). This bonus will be given under the following conditions, which aspirants must comply with in order to compete. ARTICLE FIRST Aspirants will carry out their own experiments, or have them carried out by members of the committee, if they deem it appropriate. These experiments must be carried out before a committee, as stipulated in article 4, which will judge beforehand where they should be carried out; however, they must be carried out in the homes of committee members. Aspirants will be required to carry out several experiments in different districts with the same product, in the course of the same year. ARTICLE 2: The products used to carry out the experiments, as well as the time spent using them, will be at the expense of the aspirants; in no case will they be able to claim compensation from the committee. ARTICLE 3: The length of time required by the committee to perform the experiments, in order to be entitled to the bonus, is three consecutive years of experiments performed with the same product, and having each given a radical cure of the disease. ARTICLE 4: A report on each experiment will be made by an experimental commission, taken from the committee, which will have judged the effects produced by the experiments, as well as the time spent using the products, and their cost price. A duplicate will be in the hands of the committee, and the other in those of the aspirant, who will sign them, as will the commission who attended the experiments. ARTICLE 5. Aspirants will have to comply with the arbitration of the committee, which will have to rule on all the reports made during the three years required for the experience of each product. ARTICLE 6: The time spent using the products and the products themselves may not exceed in expense more than one tenth of the price of the plants they cure. If only one preservative is found, it must not exceed one-twentieth; otherwise there would be no result satisfactory enough to award the prize, and the competition would remain open until such time as the committee deems necessary. ARTICLE 7. The committee reserves the right, in the event of several candidates achieving the same result, to award the prize only to the one offering the most practical and economical means. ARTICLE 8. For each experiment carried out by the aspirants, a sample must be taken and placed in such a way as to ensure its safe keeping and that it cannot be distracted by anyone. These samples will be deposited in a place chosen by the committee and the aspirant, so that the responsibility of both is well covered. At the end of the three-year experiment, the committee and the aspirant will take back the samples. The committee will have the right to have them analyzed, if it deems this necessary, in order to ascertain whether the same product has been used for the three years in front of the com missions. In the event that these samples do not conform exactly, the premium will be refused and the experiments continued, if the committee deems it appropriate. 08 When an aspirant, after having fulfilled the aforementioned commitments, has supplied a product recognized as good, he will be acknowledged by the committee, and the premium will be paid to him by the said committee, three months after the signature of this acknowledgement. ARTICLE 10: In acknowledging the discovery, the Committee reserves the privilege of the discoverer. The latter, in exchange for the deed, must give the Committee the manner in which his product is to be composed and used. If he has a patent, he must relinquish it to the Committee, and if he has none, he will not be entitled to take one. ARTICLE 11. It is understood that once the act has been received, the aspirant who has won it may neither sell the product, nor give instructions on how to make it to anyone other than the Committee, under prejudice in damages to the Committee, ARTICLE 12. Any person wishing to compete must contact the Committee Chairman to find out the conditions to which he must commit himself by signature, failing which he will not be able to compete for the prize. Committee Chairman, CURÉ Rue Lecourbe, 315, Paris. Secretary. DUVILLARD. Here is the important report presented to the Académie des Sciences on November 18, 1878, by M. Max Cornu on this subject: MALADIE DES LAITUES "Market garden crops have been invaded for several years by a special disease, which has received the popular name of Meunier. Meunier attacks varieties of Lactuca sativa (Lettuce and Romaine); it impedes the development of these plants, stains, dries out or corrodes the leaves. "The damage is so considerable that a small group of market gardeners, about twelve in number (1), have decided to offer a prize of ten thousand francs to anyone who can put a stop to it. "The disease is produced by a parasitic fungus, Peronospora gangliiformis Berck, which frequently attacks other plants (groundsel, lettuce, etc.), and in particular artichokes, where the same disease is masked by the down of the leaves, without anyone seeming to care. "This species, like all its congeners, attacks living plants, which it more or less exhausts, and on which it produces dark patches of browned, dried-out tissue; it is the direct cause of the disease: there can be no doubt about that. In this respect, consult M. de Bary's (2) remarkable work on the fungi of this group, and read his account of the experiments he carried out. "We know that the dreaded disease of potatoes and tomatoes is due to a Peronospora (P. infestans Mont.), and that our vineyards are threatened by a similar parasite; I have repeatedly insisted on this danger (3). Dr. Wittmack recently reported a species (P. sparsa Berk), which is devastating rose crops near Berlin, as well as in several parts of England. A study of Lettuce disease may be of some interest, by (1) M. Curé (de Grenelle), president of the Committee; M. Duvillard, (de la Glacière), secretary. (2) Ann. des Sc, nat, Bot, 4e série, t. XX, p. 5, 1863; 13 plates (see especially page 59). (3) Sav. étr. t. XXII, n° 6, p. 35; 1873, - Comptes rendus, sóance du 23 juillet 1877; c'est le P. viticola Berk, et Curt. 1008 attirant l'attention sur les moyens à employer dans la lutte et sur les altérations déterminées par les parasites. On lettuces, these alterations are of two kinds and very different: they can be insignificant or disastrous, depending on the case. "When we tear off a flap of epidermis from a Lettuce plant attacked by P. gangliiformis, we observe conidiophorous filaments, emerging through the opening of the stomata, as in other Peronospora. They are grouped in twos or threes, or solitary; their upper part is variously branched; the whole simulates a small tree. The ramuscles are dilated at the tip and bear three to six sterigmata, which give rise to the spores or conidia. The conidia are broadly oval, with an incomplete papilla; germination gives rise to a filament that is sometimes remarkably twisted. "Vegetable growers attribute the disease to westerly winds and mild, rainy weather; by this we mean that these conditions favor the dissemination and germination of spores on nurse plants, as there can be no question of spontaneous generation. "When a lettuce seedling is suddenly invaded by Peronospora, where should we look for the cause? The cause must be attributed to weeds in the vicinity, to Seneçons, Laiterons or others, to Artichokes, to Chicories already carrying the parasite. Sometimes, however, none of these plants are to be found in the vicinity; in this case, the spots are produced by the germination of dormant spores or oospores, the parasite's second mode of reproduction. These oospores germinate after a long period of rest and can be preserved in the soil or on its surface, requiring only a little moisture and warmth to germinate. "These oospores develop in the tissue occupied by mycelium filaments and dried out by its action. They are common on melon, but very rare on lettuce, although their existence is highly probable. O09 "If we make a transverse section of the attacked leaf, we observe the mycelium crawling between the cells and emitting ovoid-oblong suckers; when the exhausted tissue dies, the mycelium disappears and is itself the cause of its death. This is the alteration that occurs in summer. "If the plant is more completely invaded, the conidiophore filaments are sparser on the leaf surface, which becomes paler; the leaf dies as a whole without drying out; it softens and turns brown. This change generally takes place away from the outer leaves, and is what occurs during winter. "In winter and spring, large quantities of lettuces are shipped abroad. Those affected by Meunier disease arrive at their destination altered as we have seen above, and this alteration is attributed to imperfect packaging or poor quality of the plant at the outset. The merchandise is rejected as a whole, causing considerable losses, and we don't know how to distinguish between lettuces that will spoil and those that will keep. "The parasite is formidable only for this reason; it is in order to remedy it that the market gardeners have proposed such a considerable price. "It is possible to find in the cultivation of plants and in the history of the parasite a means of protecting oneself from its attacks: I ask the Academy's permission to present to it at a later date some considerations on the general question of Peronospora. The study of this question has made little progress over the last twenty years, the committee that dealt with it having disbanded, some of its members having disappeared, and others no longer in business. However, Mr. Cornu's interesting report shows that scientists are well acquainted with the terrible parasite and its means of reproduction. O08 M. Max Cornu is: the neighborhood of weeds; market gardeners avoid it. Science must therefore focus all its efforts on the second, i.e. preventing the germination of oospores that can be stored for a long time in the soil or on its surface, and which germinate with a little heat and humidity. Science has long since shown that spontaneous generation is out of the question. Since it is only a question of destroying dormant spores, science, which has already rendered so many services to agriculture and viticulture, would render an eminent one to horticulture in general, and to Parisian market gardeners in particular, if it could find a way of combating the dreaded parasite. The scientist who wishes to attach his name to this discovery, Mr. Max Cornu (1) or any other, will always find an excellent auxiliary in the Board of Directors of the Union of Market Gardeners of the Paris region, whose headquarters are at the Hôtel de la Société d'Horticulture de France, rue de Grenelle-Saint-Germain, 84, Paris. Educated by experience, we believe that it is above all through a preventive remedy, in this disease as in all others, that we should try to operate. Indeed, once the miller's disease has set in, you have to work on plants that are so soft and tender that the slightest toxicity alters and tires them. Nevertheless, old market gardeners who have already dealt with this question could help the operator who takes this matter in hand from a scientific point of view. (1) Since the time we wrote this chapter, we have resumed experiments on this disease under the direction of M. Max Cornu. Max Cornu, and we publish the results in our more recent work entitled Traité de culture forcée et ordinaire des plantes potagères ainsi que des champignons. 908 D'HORTICULTURE DE FRANCE The revolution of 1789, in order to eliminate all the abuses that the organization of the old society had created, was forced to dissolve all existing associations, and thereby create individual freedom, the basis of our current legislation. But men soon recognized the need to associate again, basing their associations on more modern principles. The first horticultural association to be founded was the Horticultural Society, a scientific association bringing together horticultural practitioners, teachers, amateurs and artists alike. This important society was founded in 1826, with the aim of perfecting the art of gardening, improving cultivation methods, facilitating the study of new introductions, etc., etc., etc. As soon as it was founded, the society decided to publish a monthly compendium to publicize its work. The cost of this publication is covered by an annual membership fee of twenty francs. It also decided to create temporary exhibitions of horticultural products, as one of the best ways of publicizing and encouraging progress in this science. Since then, unless there have been very serious circumstances, hardly a year has gone by without one or two exhibitions. The society holds two meetings a month, on the second and fourth Thursdays, at its hotel, rue de Grenelle-Saint-Germain, 84, Paris. It is very rare that at each session, there are not a certain number of contributions made by horticulturists or amateurs, judged by competent committees on their novelty, beautiful cultivation, etc., and whose vote is ratified by the general assembly; and premiums varying from three to nine francs are awarded to the presenters, sometimes accompanied by a certificate of merit. This large association, which is active throughout France and has numerous corresponding members in all parts of the globe, encourages small local societies, professional associations, mutual aid societies, etc., by all the means at its disposal. It even lends the rooms of its hotel free of charge to the various societies, made up of horticulturists pursuing a goal tending to improve their trade or their well-being. Founded under the reign of Charles X, in 1826, it has been encouraged by every successive government since that time. When it was founded, it immediately won the king's good graces. He authorized it to inscribe his name as protector and founder; a precious support for a fledgling society to be so highly patronized. Under Louis-Philippe, it was also encouraged by the leading men of the day, notably the Comte de Rambuteau, the Duc Decazes, M. Héricourt de Thury, etc., through the association of patroness ladies; in addition to its scientific purpose, it became a relief and charity society. The Republic of 1848 also showered it with favors. Under the Empire, placed under the protectorate of the Emperor and headed by the most prominent men of that regime, such as the Duc de Morny and Marshal Vaillant, it could only make rapid progress, having been recognized as a public utility since the beginning of the Empire. During the terrible year, forced to suspend some of its work and publications, the Society did not remain 008 inactive. Horticulturalists in the Paris region, most of whom live on the outskirts of Paris, were particularly hard hit by the two sieges of the capital. How many establishments did we see ruined, first by the foreign war and then by the civil war? Houses often demolished or ransacked, equipment broken or burnt, plants devastated or wasted. I can still remember the efforts made by all of us, the more fortunate ones, who lived within the walls of Paris, to save the equipment and goods of either a relative or a friend. The Horticultural Society then stepped in, acting as a nucleus to concentrate the help offered by English and French horticulturists, who were fortunate enough not to suffer the horrors of the German invasion and the sadness of the civil war. With these sad days behind them, the horticulturists generally returned home to repair all the ruins; many, alas, had great difficulty in doing so, and some even succumbed. It's when you've been through these sad times that you feel, more than anyone else, the need to unite and join forces. The Third Republic, which devoted much of its efforts to encouraging agriculture, could not abandon horticulture, the elder sister of the first. Trade has flourished in all branches of horticulture. I remember that, immediately after these sad events, horticulturists started growing vegetables, saying that flowers could no longer be sold. Yet flowers have never been as expensive as they are today. So, little by little, everyone went back to their own speciality. In the nearly thirty years since these events took place, horticulture has taken on extraordinary importance in every respect. The exhibitions that the Society organizes in spring and autumn each year have become major O00 Parisian festivals; each one is visited by the President of the Republic, accompanied by a large entourage, and generally some outstanding horticulturists receive various decorations, even from the hands of the Republic's chief magistrate. And it couldn't be any other way, since horticulture at the end of this century has become one of the most active sources of our national production, and the powerful tools it brings with it have led other industries to develop and, by the same token, to increase their business. In recent years, exhibitions of horticultural products of all kinds and related tools have been joined by the fine arts. Artists come to exhibit paintings of landscapes, trees, flowers, fruit and vegetables. If you go down this road, there's hardly a painting you won't find at a horticultural exhibition. These are the products of artists of many different kinds, yet they live in perfect harmony under the aegis of these exhibitions. The number of visitors also increases from year to year, and certainly the space reserved for it in the Tuileries Gardens for the last few years will become too much by 1900. At each exhibition, the President of the Republic donates an objet d'art, which is awarded to the first prizewinner. Other major prizes are also donated, notably by the Minister of Agriculture, the General Council of the Seine, the City of Paris, the Society itself and various prominent personalities who are members of the Society. These donations are known as honorary prizes, and are in addition to the numerous gold, vermeil and silver medals awarded by the Society to exhibitors recognized as worthy by competent juries. 208 DES JARDINIERS HORTICULTEURS Du départernent de la Seine The great social movement of the 1848 generation was association on new bases, in keeping with the spirit of the times. A handful of intelligent, dedicated market gardeners and horticulturists set about creating a relief society, Société de secours-mutuels des jardiniers-horticulteurs du département de la Seine. It was founded on July 10, 1856. Its main aim was to build up capital to pay pensions to its members. Initially, a small contribution of twelve francs a year ensured a pension of three hundred francs at the age of sixty, or placement in an old people's home, putting an end to poverty. Here appears the highest, noblest idea that the human brain can conceive. Among the men who have contributed most to the organization of this society, we find the sons of those who began forced cultivation at the beginning of the century, notably the Laiziers, the Stainvilles, the Le Bouteuxs, a descendant of the president of the first society that the former gardeners set up; Marchand, Lefilleul, Garlin, Niolet, Dulac, and so on. Posterity owes a religious tribute to these courageous citizens, who guarantee and will continue to guarantee a large number of elderly people from destitution. These men have been succeeded by their sons and a number of newcomers, who bring the same dedication to the work. First as president, Stainville, son of the previous president; then, in various positions, Laurent, Hébrard, Hémard, Piver, Lecaplain and others. In order to inform our great-grandnephews about this work, we are publishing its articles of association in extenso, just as we did for the companies of the past: TITLE PREMIER Corporate purpose ARTICLE PREMIER - All horticulturists, gardeners, market gardeners and florists living in the Seine department may join the Society as participating or honorary members; however, the Society is limited to seven hundred and fifty participating members. Ministerial decree, October 26 1878, and the number may only be increased by virtue of an authorization from the Ministry of the Interior... . ART. 2 - The purpose of the Society is to provide mutual assistance in the event of old age. A special fund will be set up to assist members who have become unfortunate as a result of illness, injury or disability, leading to an inability to work; this fund will also be used to provide assistance to widows and orphans of members whose needy situation is known to the majority of the Society. This fund will be fed: 1° by a contribution of twenty-five centimes payable every three months, by each participating member, into the hands of the collector; 2° by a tenth of the annual subscriptions of honorary members. ART. 3 - The head office of the Society is provisionally established at Clichy-la-Garenne, rue des Bateliers, n° 12. 08 TITRE II Conditions d'admission et composition ART. 4 - Any person wishing to be accepted as a participating member must be introduced by two other members who vouch for the recipient's morality, that he or she is French and has been domiciled in the Seine department for at least six months, and that he or she enjoys his or her civil and civic rights. Ladies will be admitted to the Society under the same conditions as men; they may attend meetings, but will not be liable for any meeting fines; however, they will not be exempt from fines for failure to pay; in addition, they will be subject to the same charges and will consequently have the same rights as other members, but in no case may they take part in the administration or deliberations. ART. 5 - The Society is composed of participating members and honorary members, or free associates. ART. 6 - Participating members are those who have undertaken to comply with the present statutes and regulations, and are entitled to all the benefits of the Society in return for regular payment of their membership dues. ART. 7 - Honorary members are those who, through their care, advice and subscriptions, contribute to the prosperity of the association without sharing in the benefits. ART. 8 - Their names will be inscribed at the head of each directory, together with the amount of their subscription and the nature of their benefits. ART. 9 - To be admitted as a participating member, a person must be at least twenty-one years old, and less than fifty-five years old, and live in the Seine department. In future, participating members will not be admitted after the age of 40, or up to the age of 45, if they pay, in addition to the ordinary entrance fee, as many times 9 fr. as they have more than 40 years, and, then, they will have the right to retire at the age of 65, without this giving them any additional benefit to the right of the convoy fund. It was voted on October 10, 1880, that from January 1, 1882, no more participating members would be admitted after the age of 35. ART. 10 - Honorary members will be received by the President and the Executive Committee without distinction of age, residence or profession, and their number is unlimited. ART. 11 - Any judicial conviction tainting the honor or probity of a member shall entail forfeiture of membership and the impossibility of admission. Disqualification is pronounced by the General Meeting, on the proposal of the Executive Committee and without discussion: 1° for conviction of an offence; 2° for wilful prejudice to the interests of the company; 3° for any act contrary to honor; 4° for disorderly and notoriously scandalous conduct. Except in the case of an infamous conviction, the member whose exclusion is proposed is invited to appear before the Board to be heard on the facts imputed to him/her; if he/she fails to appear, his/her exclusion is pronounced at the General Meeting. ART. 12 - Resignation, striking off and expulsion do not entitle members to any reimbursement. However, life annuities constituted in accordance with the decree of April 26, 1856, in favor of resigning, struck-off or excluded members, remain their property. The admission 908 LES JARDINIERS DE PARIS of a member will be put to the vote and adopted or rejected by an absolute majority at the General Meeting. Exclusions will be decided in the same way. TITLE III Initial subscription payments and membership fees ART. 13 - All new members must pay a deposit of five francs, and four francs for the first quarter. ART. 14 - These sums will be returned to the recipient if he/she is not accepted by the General Meeting. ART. 15 - In the event of exclusion, striking off or resignation, any sum paid into the fund will never be returned to the depositor. ART. 16 - Each participating member, as from April 1, 1881, must pay three francs in dues, on the first of each of the quarters of April, July, October and January, from 10 a.m. to noon, at the home of a Society collector, appointed in each section of the suburbs and in each arrondissement of Paris, where this is deemed necessary. ART. 17 - Members who fail to pay their quarterly dues will be fined twenty-five centimes for each late payment. ART. 18 - Any member who is four quarters in arrears will be considered to have resigned and will be struck off the Society's rolls, if he fails to respond to the first appeal made to him by the Receiver General. ART. 19 - A member who resigns, or is declared to have resigned, and who wishes to be reinstated, can only be reinstated if he is not three years in arrears with his membership dues, and if he immediately pays double the amount he would have paid on joining the Society; and if he is represented by two participating members who certify that none of the cases mentioned in art. 1t have occurred to him since he left the Society. ART. 20 - Members may be represented by third parties to make their quarterly payments. TITLE IV Members' pensions ART. 21 - A retirement fund is created in accordance with the decree of April 26, 1856, and placed with the Caisse des dépôts et consignations. This fund is made up of: 1° deductions made by the Company from surplus revenues; 2° special subsidies granted by the State. 3° Gifts and bequests whose acceptance has been authorized by the competent authority. To be eligible for the pension, the participating member must have paid contributions for twenty-five years and must have reached the age of sixty, or sixty-five if he joined the Company after forty years of age, by paying nine francs as many times as he would have had more years than forty when he joined the Company; he is given until January 1, 1875 to pay this fee, after which time he will no longer be able to benefit from these advantages. Entitlement to a pension never cancels payment of membership dues, which remain payable until the member's death. ART. 22. - The pension rate is set at three hundred francs; however, if a member becomes infirm or reaches an age that does not allow him to live at home on this O sum, the Society, after recognizing the member's state of hardship and, consequently, the inadequacy of the pension, will, at its own expense, place the member in a government home most convenient to the Society: The President will send to the Minister of the Interior : 1° an extract of the deliberation concerning the vote and the amount of the pension, as well as the date of admission of the member, and an indication of the department in which the arrears are to be paid; the birth certificate, issued on plain paper and certified by the mayor: 2° After the death of the pensioner, the Chairman will forward to the Préfet an extract, on plain paper, of the death certificate, for reinstatement to the Society's retirement fund, in accordance with article 4 of the decree of April 26, 1856, of the funds used to set up the pension. ART. 23 - If a participating member becomes disabled before reaching sixty years of age, or twenty-five years of membership, and is recognized as needy by the majority of the. Society, each participating member must, each year, pay an extraordinary contribution to help him or her. However, in no case may this extraordinary assistance exceed two hundred francs. ART. 24 - The following are not covered by the provisions of the preceding article: 1° Members who are ill as a result of intemperance or syphilitic diseases; 2° Members who are ill as a result of voluntary brawls in which they have been aggressors or provocateurs, or who have received injuries in a riot in which they have voluntarily taken part, or in the event of unemployment. TITLE V ART. 25 - A special fund will be set up to pay for the funeral convoys of participating members who die; the convoys are set at one hundred francs, with a deduction made for municipal taxes, so that the Society pays only ninety francs to the families who pay the funeral expenses, but if this sum is not spent on the convoys, the Society will pay only on presentation of the funeral receipts, which will be the same for all participating members, regardless of the number of years they have been members of the Society. ART. 26 - A sum proportionate to years of membership in the Society will be deducted from this fund, to be used to pay a penny to widows and direct heirs. ART. 27 - From entry into the Society up to and including the fifth year of membership, the convoy only will be paid as explained above. ART. 28 - From the sixth to and including the tenth year of membership in the Society, the convoy will be paid and a denarius of fifty francs to the beneficiaries. ART. 29 - From the eleventh to and including the fifteenth year of membership in the Society, the convoy will be paid and one denarius of one hundred francs to the beneficiaries. ART. 30. - From the sixteenth year up to and including the twentieth year of membership in the Society, the convoy will be paid. and a denarius of one hundred and fifty francs to the beneficiaries. ART. 31 - From the twenty-first year and above, the convoy will be paid, and a denarius of two hundred francs to the beneficiaries. ART. 32 - All sums used for the deniers will be paid in the following order: !° to the widow or widower; 2° to the children, if the youngest is under fifteen years of age; 3° to the father and mother, if the youngest is over sixty years of age; it is understood that these sums will only be passed on to the children if there are no widows, and to the father and mother, if there are no children under fifteen years of age. O ART. 33 - If none of the aforementioned heirs remain in the order explained, the denier will remain by right in the convoy fund. ART. 34 - As all these sums are not the property of the members, no one may dispose of them by will, and no creditor shall have any right to them. ART. 35. - To form this "convoy" fund, participating members who are members of the Society shall pay, immediately upon approval, a sum of three francs into this fund, and shall continue to pay seventy-five centimes into it on the first of April, July, October and January of each year, and those joining in the future shall pay three francs into this fund on joining the Society, and shall continue to pay seventy-five centimes every quarter, as explained above; in the event of late payment of these sums, late payers will be fined as for all other payments due to the Society. TITLE VI Organization and Board ART. 36 - The Society is administered by a Board which meets every three months and whenever convened by the President. All Society meetings convened by the Chairman or by his order in the interests of the Society are compulsory. It is composed of. A Chairman. The President is elected by secret ballot by the Society for a five-year term, and no one is elected or proclaimed President unless he or she obtains an absolute majority of the votes cast. In the second ballot, election is by relative majority; in the event of candidates obtaining an equal number of votes, the eldest is proclaimed Chairman. The 99 "minutes of the election are immediately forwarded to the Minister of the Interior. No one may be elected as a member of the Board unless he or she is French and enjoys full civil and civic rights; all members of the Board, including the Chairman, are taken from among the participating or honorary members. The Chairman is the head of the Association, and in this capacity supervises all operations of the Executive Committee; he is the custodian of and responsible for the register of members and other printed documents belonging to the Association, and signs all such documents and acts relating thereto. He supervises and ensures the execution of the articles of association, and submits the annual report required by art. 20 of the decree on associations to the competent authority; 2° Two vice-presidents appointed for one year, and eligible for re-election by an absolute majority. They replace the Chairman in case of absence, or when required by him. They have the same powers and duties as the Chairman; 3° A Treasurer, appointed for one year, and eligible for re-election by an absolute majority. He is civilly responsible for all sums paid into his fund, as well as the books relating thereto. He may never, under any circumstances, have more than fifteen hundred francs on hand, the excess of which will be remitted by him, on behalf of the Company, to the Caisse des Dépôts et Consignations. He remains custodian and responsible for his cash books; 4° A secretary, appointed by an absolute majority, for one year, and eligible for re-election. He is specially entrusted with the Company's records; he calls the roll and notes absentees. He draws up the minutes of meetings and receipts, and signs them with the Chairman; 5° A Receiver General appointed by an absolute majority for one year, and eligible for re-election; he supervises the receivers of each section, receives from them the funds they have received from the members of their section, and remits them immediately to the Treasurer; 5° Three Auditors appointed by an absolute majority, p09 for one year, and eligible for re-election. They are specifically responsible for auditing the accounts, and must meet for this purpose, along with the Board of Directors, at all ordinary and extraordinary meetings. ART. 37. - Members absent from a meeting convened either by the Board or the General Meeting, or from any other meeting convened by order of the Chairman, shall pay a fine of one franc fifty centimes. ART. 38 - Only illness or national service will entitle the member to an exemption. ART. 39 - A delegated collector will be appointed in each suburban commune and in each arrondissement of Paris where this is deemed necessary. He will be specially charged with receiving, at his home, the dues and fines of the members of his district or commune, on the days and at the times indicated in art. 16. He will hand over the amount of his takings, at the latest on the day of the rendering of accounts, to the Receiver General, who will sign his register, or have it signed by the auditors; in the event of non-performance on the part of a collector, he will pay the fines of all those who have paid him. Should a section not agree to appoint one of its members as Receiver, all section members will be required to pay their dues to the Receiver General, under the same conditions as in art. 16. Should the workload of the officers become too great, the Society will appoint one or more deputies to assist them in their duties and replace them if necessary. TITLE VII General Meetings and their holding ART. 40 - General Meetings are held for the purpose of rendering accounts and receiving proposals, in the interests of the Company. 1C They may not deliberate unless they are composed of half plus one of the participating members who are members of the Company, and their deliberations, to be enforceable, must be voted by an absolute majority of the members present. All honorary members have the same rights as participants. ART. 41 - There will be two meetings a year, on the second Sunday in May and the second Sunday in October. The election of officers will take place at the second meeting, and members appointed to replace others will not take office until the second Thursday in January following, or those replaced will cease to hold office. No member of the executive committee may convene a section without the order of the president, except for a convoy of a member of his section, and of which he shall immediately give notice to the president, so that he may prevent it if the case so requires. ART. 42. - The May session will open at two o'clock sharp, and the October session at one o'clock sharp. Any member failing to respond to the roll call, which will take place a quarter of an hour after the opening, will pay a fine of one franc fifty centimes; and members present not wearing their badges will be fined twenty-five centimes. ART. 43 - When a member, honorary member or participant dies, all the members of his section must attend his funeral, on pain of a fine of one franc five centimes, and those present twenty-five centimes, if they are not wearing their badges. ART. 44 - No member will be admitted to assemblies unless in decent dress. ART. 45. - Any member who is present on behalf of an absent member will be fined three francs. 99 TITLE VIII Discussions ART. 46. - No member may speak unless he has obtained the right to do so from the Chairman; if he fails to comply with this article, he shall pay a fine of twenty-five centimes. Any member granted the floor must express himself with decency and moderation, failing which he will be deprived of the floor for the entire session. ART. 47 - Any political or religious discussion is strictly forbidden; any member who raises such a question will be immediately expelled from the meeting. ART. 48. - Any discussiòn or disgression foreign to the philanthropic aims of the association is prohibited. Any member who deviates from the discussion will be immediately called to order, and expelled from the meeting if he persists. ART. 49 - To prevent a member's expulsion from taking on the character of a brawl at a meeting, it will be put to the vote and decided by an absolute majority. If the member refuses to obey this decision, the meeting will be adjourned and he will be expelled from the Society. TITLE IX Police of the Society ART. 50. - Entry to the Society hall on the day of meetings is forbidden to any person not belonging to the Society, except to members of the authorities, when they can prove their status, or on presentation of their distinctive marks. ART. 51 - On the day of the general meeting, two heads of order will be appointed, charged with keeping out all persons not belonging to the association, and with supervising the internal police of the meeting. ART. 52 - Any member in a state of intoxication will be refused entry to the meeting room; when his name is called, he will be considered absent, and punished as such. The fine will be paid by the Heads of Order if they neglect their duties. ART. 53 - If a member takes the liberty of insulting one of the members, during a meeting, or one of the officers, in connection with their duties, he will pay a fine of fifty centimes and may, notwithstanding the fine, be expelled from the meeting. ART. 54. - Any member who assaults another member during a meeting will be struck off the Society's rolls by right, without ever being able to be reinstated, all without prejudice to legal proceedings. TITLEX Administrative provisions ART. 55. - All members of the Society have the right to check and compile their own accounts; accountants may not, under any pretext, refuse to carry out such checks. ART. 56 - Any member who, while enjoying his rights as a member, does not receive the assistance set out in the present regulations, has the right to bring a civil action against the Society to compel it to comply with the regulations, but before doing so, he may request an arbitration decision. ART. 57. - On entering the Society, each member shall be issued with a printed copy of the present regulations, which he shall sign in the minute of the register provided for this purpose; he shall pay fifty centimes for this printed copy. ART. 58. - Fines pronounced in execution of the regulations will be collected and appended to the general accounts. They will be deducted from any payment made by way of dues. ART. 59. - Receipts for amounts paid by members will be issued in a booklet issued for this purpose. If lost, it will be replaced at the member's expense. ART. 60 - The present regulations will be submitted for approval to the higher authority, in accordance with the decree of March 26, 1852. ART. 61. - The substance of these regulations may never be departed from; should it subsequently become necessary to add articles, they may only be adopted by a three-quarters majority, plus one member. ART. 62 - Any additions or modifications recognized as necessary, and voted by an absolute majority, will only be enforceable after having been submitted to the authorities for approval, and having received their sanction, in accordance with the decree of March 26, 1852. ART. 63. - The Company may only dissolve itself in the event of insufficient resources. Dissolution may only be pronounced at a general meeting specially convened for this purpose, and by a number of votes equal to two-thirds of the registered members. Such dissolution will be valid only after approval by the competent authority. In the event of dissolution, liquidation will be carried out in accordance with articles 6 and 17 of the decree of June 14, 1851, article 15 of the decree of March 26, 1852 and article 3 of the decree of April 26, 1856. - 1 = =- - TITLE XI General provisions ART. 64. - When a member changes his place of residence, he is required to notify the President as soon as he changes. Failure by the member to comply with this formality will result in a fine of one franc. ART. 65. - The costs of correspondence relating to the Society, printing, stamps, locality, office supplies and other outlays of this kind, shall be borne by the said Society. ART. 66. - The duties of office employees are free of charge. They may not, therefore, demand any remuneration for their employment. ART. 67. - The members of the Society are mutually and reciprocally bound to maintain, cause to be maintained and execute verbatim the present regulations. ART. 68 - The Society, having heard the reading of its by-laws and discussed them article by article, unanimously adopts their wording in all their form and content. These bylaws were approved by a decree dated August 25, 1856, and the officers appointed by the Society were approved by another decree dated September 8 of the same year. This society has been in existence for almost half a century, and we are a participating member. It remains for us to present its results, and then to critique them. Let us begin by praising the memory of its founding philanthropists, for at the end of this century, only a few of them remain. These men have rendered exceptional service to their guilds and, above all, to their contemporaries. Men who take the initiative to set up a charity of some kind are not always aware of the difficulties, even setbacks, they encounter from the outset. However, it should be borne in mind that the creation of the Society we are dealing with here came at a time when business had increased considerably, for the reasons we set out in an earlier chapter. Many families were already, if not wealthy, then very well off, and with the good spirit of confraternity that existed among gardeners, generous donors were immediately found, and the number of honorary members almost always exceeded that of full members. Donations of all kinds made to the Society up to December 31, 1898, amounted to two hundred and thirty-three thousand eight hundred and thirty-two francs (233,832 fr.). State subsidies amounted to seventy-seven thousand eight hundred and twenty-eight francs (77,828 fr.). Regular members' contributions: subscriptions, admission fees, fines of all kinds, badge purchases, etc., amounted to four hundred and forty thousand, four hundred and fifteen francs (440,415 fr.). Interest on invested funds amounted to one hundred and ninety-four thousand, three hundred and sixty-two francs (194,362 fr.). Grand total: nine hundred and forty-six thousand, four hundred and thirty-seven francs (946,437 fr.). In addition to this sum, a donation of eleven thousand, one hundred and twelve francs (11,112 fr.) was made by Mr. Moynet; this donation is not included in these figures and we will come back to it later. Up to the same period, the Society paid its pensioners a total of two hundred and twenty-three thousand eight hundred and twenty-two francs (223,822 fr.), divided between two hundred and twenty-four pensioners, of whom one hundred and eleven are still in existence. Many of the first members, as the reader can see from the articles of association, were not retired until they were sixty-five years old, which further reduces the proportional retirement figure for each member to date. In a few years' time, all members who reach the age of sixty OO0 will be entitled to a pension, since from 1881 onwards, no one joined the Company after thirty-five years. From its foundation to the end of 1899, the Society had one thousand seven hundred and seventy-five participating members, of whom two hundred and twenty-four were entitled to a pension. The average pension paid per retiree is six years, and 677 years of pension have been paid to !13 deceased retirees. These figures speak for themselves, without needing to comment on the services that this company has rendered, is rendering and will continue to render to the gardeners' guild of the Paris suburbs, without distinction of category, since all are entitled to membership. In addition to this fund, there is another known as the "convoy" fund, which is used to give the heirs or assigns of a deceased member a sum of one hundred francs, equal for all, to be spent on the funeral of the deceased, i.e., the Society makes it a point of honor that its members are not taken to their final resting place in a pauper's cart. In addition, depending on the number of years of membership, the Society allocates a sum ranging from fifty to two hundred francs, known as the "widow's or widower's denier". This donation is also a great help to families whose resources are too often depleted by long illnesses. For infirm members who cannot provide for all their needs, the company places them in a retirement home. Even those who become infirm before the age of sixty, if they are recognized as needy, are also helped. The Society's aim is therefore the extinction of pauperism in the corporation. It is regrettable that the Society does not include all those who may need it. Let us now examine the faults we can accuse it of. The first can be called a weakness. It consisted in allowing the religious spirit to enter a civil society by accepting a donation of 11,112 francs, the interest on which is used each year to issue savings books to the children of full members making their First Communion. This donation, accepted by the Society, removes the equality of rights that must always exist in a mutual society; all the more so as the recipient cannot be asked, on admission, to which denomination he or she belongs. It is quite certain that, in order to circumvent the law, we have given it another name, as otherwise the higher authority could not have authorized it. Let us hope that in the near future, this donation will go to the orphans' fund, where it should always have been. It will make an important contribution. It will make up for the mistake made. This donation, with the spirit of independence that animates men in these times, has given rise to bitter discussions within the Society, which has done much to exclude one of its most devoted and intelligent members, who is in a position to render useful services, precisely because of the spirit of independence that characterizes him. For it is always preferable to have a loyal adversary, who points out the faults committed, or those that could be committed, rather than a flattering friend who approves of everything, even when he knows perfectly well that you are in error. This member is Mr. Narcisse Laurent. It's quite certain that if he had wanted to, he would have forced the Society to reinstate him, but a sense of dignity prevented him from suing. The Society's biggest fault was that of not increasing its dues sufficiently when it began to pay its retirees. Up to that time, the contribution was twelve francs a year, an insufficient sum to guarantee a pension of three hundred francs. From then on, it had to be increased to twenty or twenty-four francs to have the necessary capital in hand to guarantee the commitments made by the Company. Today, the deficit stands at a minimum of one hundred thousand francs. The Board has been moved by this situation and has just 908 voted to raise the membership fee to twenty-five francs, a figure which will enable the Society to operate without making up much of its deficit. At some point, thirty francs will have to be paid to make up the deficit, unless substantial donations are made. By paying thirty francs a year, the young member will still find advantages in joining the Society. At the age of sixty, he will have three hundred francs assured for his retirement; plus, for his heirs, a sum of three hundred francs, both for his funeral and for the widow's penny. In reality, therefore, he will have paid four hundred and fifty francs to secure a three hundred franc pension. It has to be said that all mutual aid societies make mistakes in this respect: the first members pay less and have the same benefits as those who come after them. Almost all mutualists, seeing their capital increase, do not count the expenses that the commitments made entail. It is also necessary to take into account, to a certain extent, the difficulties the former have had to overcome to build up this capital. Despite these faults, which are not crucial to the Society's existence, young people will understand that it is in their interest to join. At the sine of this century, when establishments are tending to expand considerably, and when it will be necessary to be, so to speak, a small capitalist in order to work on one's own account, it is through mutuality that workers should think about protecting themselves from poverty in their old age. The deficit we are reporting may seem enormous to some timid minds. However, with a few francs more in contributions, it will be made up in fifteen or twenty years' time, and members will benefit from an institution that will continue to have generous donors for a long time to come, and which will give them, if not more, at least as much security as any other mutual aid society. O08 DE L'HORTICULTURE DE LA RÉGION PARISIENNE Against damage caused by hail and storms This association was created with the aim of insuring each other against damage caused by hail and storms, an idea no less generous than the one which presided over the foundation of the previous one, since one of these scourges can ruin this or that grower while sparing this or that other. Mutuality, insofar as possible, can save the victim from ruin. Alongside a few distinguished horticulturists, such as Messrs Boutreux, Landry, Simon, etc., we come across some new names among market gardeners: Duvillard, Poitou, Chemin, Pruniot, etc. This association can only include gardeners who are already members. This association can only include self-employed gardeners. Its aim is to protect them against the all-too-frequent ruin caused by hailstorms and storms to establishments with equipment as fragile as that of horticulturists and market gardeners. Its action is certainly not as broad as that of the previous society, which encompasses all gardeners, managers and workers of all categories. Nevertheless, its aim is also philanthropic; for it often happens that certain workers set up with little money in hand, and when one of these plagues destroys part of their crops and equipment, they find themselves unable to repair the losses incurred. Insurance, by immediately paying them a certain sum based on the estimated damage caused, enables them to continue working and gradually repair the disaster they have suffered. Despite all this, the association has not made rapid progress. It barely has a hundred policyholders, when it should have at least two thousand. When one of these disasters occurs in a given locality, a few people think of taking out insurance; but once the accident has passed, they believe that there will be no more; nevertheless, these events are very frequent, and we cannot insist too much on young people in particular, that they take out insurance. All these associations, like the guilds of the Middle Ages, will bring about new progress from which society in general will benefit. Mutual aid societies and pension funds, for example, will advance the idea of a pension fund for all elderly or infirm workers. Mutual insurance against the scourges of the sky, organized by canton and department, will one day force statesmen to take an ongoing interest in these most important of all issues, since they concern the feeding of the people. Each commune will make an average annual estimate of the production of each of its inhabitants, who will pay a premium in proportion to this estimate, and when a disaster occurs, experts appointed, as in these small associations, by the insured themselves, will allocate sums to the victims, according to the losses recognized and the total of the year's losses. The farmer, thus protected, will be less inclined to leave the countryside, where he will find sufficient guarantees for his existence, rather than move to the city, where he often suffers many setbacks. This crop insurance against hail and storm damage will also facilitate the creation of agricultural banks. Farmers, winegrowers and horticulturists will be able to borrow against their harvests, since they will be able to provide their lender with a serious guarantee. Don't we see Crédit Foncier requiring a fire insurance policy to lend on built-up property? In the same way, a farmer with an insurance policy against the most frequent elements that can destroy his crops, will be able to obtain a loan that will often enable him to produce more abundantly, thereby increasing our national wealth. Even in this century of progress, reforms are slow in coming. The issues, the ideas, have to be fully matured before we decide to take advantage of them. However, this idea is part of practical socialism, where the state only intervenes to form the unity of association between all land producers, and to unite all these small local associations into a single, immense bundle. ASSOCIATION STATUTES TITLE I Constitution and Purpose of the Association ARTICLE 1 - A mutual insurance association against damage caused by storms, hail and tempests is hereby constituted in the Paris region, the purpose of which is to indemnify, in the proportions hereinafter determined, the claimants who have adhered or will adhere to the present statutes. ART. 2 - The title of this association is "L'Horticulture Parisienne". Its head office is in Paris. It covers the Seine and Seine-et-Oise departments. Its duration is unlimited. ART. 3 - The association excludes any solidarity between its members, each of whom in any event only bears the premium to which the value he insures gives rise. ART. 4 - The association is made up of participating members and honorary members; ladies are admitted in both cases. ART. 5 - Premiums are variable, but may in no case exceed eight francs per thousand. The minimum premium is set at two francs per year and per thousand of the value declared by the member. This premium is payable in all cases, even if no claim has been made against the company during the year. It is payable half-yearly in advance, with a reserve of 5 0/0 deducted from the premium for the Provident Fund. It will be collected by post, at the expense of associates, from February 20 to 28, from August 20 to 31, and for additional premiums, in the fortnight following adoption by the General Meeting. ART. 6 - The maximum premium varies according to the number of claims incurred during the year. It is set at the September General Meeting and collected as stated in the above article. The amount of supplementary premiums is established in such a way that, after the settlement of claims, a sum at least equal to one-third of the annual minimum premium remains in hand. ART. 8 - A fixed contribution of fifty centimes is levied every six months for all participating members, and is intended to cover office expenses, printing, premises, etc. The contribution for honorary members is optional. Honorary member dues are optional, with a minimum of five francs per year. This contribution is added to the contingency fund. TITLE III Indemnities ART. 9 - Indemnities are variable, and will be calculated on the basis of maximum membership fees. In no case will compensation exceed fifty percent of the estimated damage. ART. 10 - The annual interest on the reserve fund will constitute an additional indemnity, distributed among all claimants, regardless of the rate of indemnity set. ART. 11 - As the aim of the Association is to come to the aid of disaster victims as quickly as possible, an initial indemnity determined by the Council will be paid within eight days of the adoption of the experts' report, which may not exceed ten percent of the estimated damage. The Council may order a further payment if necessary. ART. 12 - Every six months, the Treasurer will draw up a table of the damage suffered and the provisional indemnities already paid, but it is only at the September General Meeting that the final percentage to be allocated to all the victims of the same year will be determined. The balance of compensation due to each claimant will be paid to him or her during the following month, into the Treasurer's fund. The same meeting will close the financial year, the accounts of which will be approved by the next meeting. ART. 13 - The Association's financial year begins on September 1 and ends on the following August 31. TITLE IV Disasters ART. 14 - The disasters recognized by the Association are hail, cyclones or whirlwinds, waterspouts and storms. ART. 15 - Immediately following a claim, the insured shall notify the President, explaining the nature of the damage. There is no need to make a claim if the damage caused does not amount to 60me of the value of the insured products or equipment. ART. 16 - The insured must draw up a list with the approximate price of the equipment, crops and products damaged or destroyed, and hand it over to the experts, bearing his signature. ART. 17 - In the event of a second loss involving the same products, a new declaration and appraisal may be required.
