TITLEV Members of the Association ART. 18 - Any owner, horticulturist, market gardener or grower whose products and equipment, as described in article 1, are likely to be damaged or destroyed by the plagues referred to in article 14, may become a member of the Association. They must be presented to the President by two members of the Association; a form will be given to the applicant on which he will declare the value of the equipment, crops and products he wishes to insure; this declaration must be signed by the applicant and the presenters; admission will take place at the General or Council Meeting by a majority of the members present. An admission fee of five francs is payable. He or she will not be entitled to member benefits and new charges until twenty-four hours after presentation of his or her receipt and a duplicate of his or her declaration slip, which will mention the date and time of presentation to the interested party; this duplicate must be signed by the Chairman and the Treasurer. ART. 19 - Any person admitted to the Association during the course of a semester must pay the minimum premium and accept all charges. ART. 20 - Growers, nurserymen or other interested parties in the same type of cultivation must draw up a statement of the crops they insure in each territory or commune, indicating by number from one to the last, the rooms or gardens insured. This declaration must be renewed each year before May 1st. In the absence of the above declaration, the insured cannot claim compensation for crops or products that are not of the same nature as those listed for the previous year. A fee of one franc will be charged for each new declaration. ART. 21 - The minimum duration of membership is three years for all associates. Honorary members will also be presented to the Chairman by two members of the Association. TITLE VI Association funds ART. 22 - A reserve fund is established as follows: State subsidies, gifts and bequests made to the Association will constitute an inalienable reserve fund, the annual interest on which will be used to increase the rate of compensation paid to disaster victims. If these resources are not used during the financial year, they will be capitalized by operation of law, and these funds will be placed in a government fund. ART. 23 - A disposable provident fund is set up with the following resources: 1° The reserve deducted from the minimum premium (art. 5). 2° Annual dues paid by honorary members. 3° The surplus of the minimum premium remaining available after the close of each financial year. This contingency fund will be used to supplement or increase indemnities in the event that the maximum premium is insufficient in the event of a disaster. No more than two-thirds of this fund may be drawn down in any one year. The amount to be withdrawn will be determined at the Annual General Meeting. This fund will be invested in a state-guaranteed annuity. No associate, even one who leaves the Association voluntarily, may claim or exercise any right over the reserve or contingency funds, which may not be used for any purpose other than that indicated in the present statutes. TITLE VII Experts ART. 24 - Experts from each associate member speciality will be appointed annually by the General Meeting. Their duties will include on-site verification of the damage and detailed estimates of the losses suffered by the claimants. Following notification of a claim, the Chairman will appoint one or more commissions of three experts, depending on the extent of the loss. They will draw up a report on their work, which they will submit to the Chairman as soon as possible, and the Board of Directors will immediately decide on its adoption, after examination. TITLEVIII Administration ART. 25 - The Association is governed by a Board of Directors, made up of at least 18 and at most 21 members, elected at the General Meeting by an absolute majority of the members present, and renewable by thirds each year. ART. 26 - The members of the Board of Directors shall be chosen as far as possible to ensure that the localities and specialities making up the association are represented. ART. 27 - No member may be appointed to the Board of Directors who is deprived of his or her civil and civic rights. ART. 28 - The Executive Committee is appointed by the Board of Directors from among its members, and is renewable every year. It comprises a Chairman, two Vice-Chairmen, a Treasurer, two Assistant Treasurers, a Secretary and two Assistant Secretaries. Members of the Board of Directors and the Executive Committee are eligible for re-election to the same positions. In the event of a second ballot, election is by relative majority. Participating members who have become honorary members retain their rights to Association functions. All functions are free of charge. ART. 29 - The Treasurer is solely responsible for the general collection of all sums intended for or belonging to the Association, and for the investment and withdrawal of funds. He draws up the accounts for each insured party. He shall pay compensation to claimants. ART. 30. - An audit committee composed of five members drawn from outside the Board of Directors shall be appointed each year to audit the Treasurer's books and accounts, as well as the Association's financial situation, and shall draw up a detailed report to be submitted to the next General Meeting. TITLE IX General Meetings ART. 31 - The Association holds ordinary general meetings twice a year, on the second Thursday of March and September, and extraordinary general meetings when convened by the Board of Directors. Resolutions are passed by an absolute majority of votes cast, irrespective of the number of members present. Honorary members and ladies are not required to attend. Any discussion alien to the aims of the Association is strictly forbidden. ART. 32 - Meetings of the Board of Directors are compulsory, and take place fifteen days before each ordinary general meeting, or in an emergency, on notice from the Chairman. The Board meets after each claim to examine the experts' reports. The rapporteur is required to attend in order to provide explanations where necessary. TITLEX General Provisions ART. 33 - In the event of disputes or legal proceedings for the collection of premiums, the Treasurer is delegated by the Board of Directors to represent the Association. ART. 34 - Votes duly cast by the Ordinary General Meeting are binding on all members present, absent or dissenting. Postal votes are not permitted. ART. 35 - The present Articles of Association will come into force one month after they have been deposited with the Préfecture de la Seine. They cancel the previous Articles, and may only be modified at the proposal of the Board of Directors, or at the request of one tenth of the members of the Association. ART. 36 - In the event of dissolution of the Association, the available assets will be used in accordance with the Association's purpose. ART. 37. - The Board of Directors shall draw up internal regulations to implement the present Articles of Association, which shall be submitted to the General Meeting for approval. ART. 38 - Any subsequent case not provided for in the present articles of association shall be settled by the Board of Directors at an extraordinary meeting called for that purpose. By a return to the idea of common defense or struggle, a return which we have already explained, syndical associations were founded under the Third Republic, which recognized and regulated their existence by the law of March 21, 1884. Today, all guilds are unionized; so many people, as in every age, feel the need to get together to defend their corporate interests. To say that these new groupings will not cause abuses in future centuries is far from my intention. After the study I have made of human mores at different times, in writing this history, given man's domineering temperament, these institutions, like those of the Middle Ages, may perhaps turn to abuse. In the meantime, at the present time, they provide great services to their members. Each corporation draws up its own statutes to protect its corporate interests; these statutes are deposited with the competent authority, which is called upon to approve them. The Ministry of Commerce even has an Office du Travail, which deals specifically with professional unions, requesting monthly reports on their activities and operations, as well as a statement on the situation of their corporation. Armed with this information, the higher authority keeps a close eye on the world of work, and through this system, with our rapid means of communication, can procure work for workers who lack it in one locality, when another locality is short of manpower, and thus avoid a great deal of misery and conflict. For the 1900 Universal Exhibition, this same ministry is organizing a special exhibition of professional unions: their statutes, reports on their work and institutions of all kinds created by them, from every point of view. Economists, and indeed anyone interested in these matters, will be able to draw on this very useful information, which could later benefit this institution. Thousands of trade unions will be taking part in this exhibition, making it all the more interesting. Vegetable growers in the Paris region were the last to join a union, having already set up their own mutual aid society, the mutual insurance association against hail and storm damage. by the invasion of the "regrattiers", who were taking over their places at the Halles centrales, and seeing the time coming when their children would be obliged to sell their products on the floor, when places are specially allocated to them in the streets around the pavilions, that they were moved and decided to form a union, under the impetus given by a few of them, including Messrs. Duvillard, Laurent Narcisse, Laurent Joseph, Gagneau, Colmon, Cottereau Charles, Dumur Antoine, Becquerelle Eugène, Hérouart and yours truly. Pre-meetings were held to lay the foundations of the new association, statutes were drawn up by a committee appointed for the purpose, and these were voted on and adopted at a plenary general meeting convened by the committee. This meeting took place in a room in the hotel of the Société d'Horticulture de France, on October 908, 1894. The syndicate, now properly constituted, immediately appointed its Board of Directors, who were responsible for having the new syndicate recognized by higher authority. STATUTES OF THE UNION Foundation ARTICLE 1 - A union or professional association is hereby formed between the undersigned and those who will adhere to the present statutes, which will be governed by the law of March 21, 1884, and by the following provisions. Name ART. 2 - This Union shall be known as the "Syndicat des Maraichers de la Région Parisienne". Its duration is unlimited, as is the number of its members. Its existence will begin on the day its Articles of Association are legally registered. Registered office ART 3. - Its registered office is at 84, rue de Grenelle-Saint- Germain, Paris. Purpose of the Union ART. 4 - The general purpose of the Union is to study and defend the economic interests of our profession and to improve its living conditions. In particular, it proposes to: 1° Examine all practical and commercial questions concerning our crop; 2° Facilitate the means of sale and export of its products, by providing all necessary information to French and foreign merchants, in order to facilitate export to all countries; MARAICHERS' UNION 3° Defend the interests of all its members at markets, with land and sea transport companies, and with public authorities. 4° To propagate innovations and improvements of all kinds which may be of interest to its members. Arbitration and Expertise ART. 5 - The Syndicate appoints Expert Arbitrators at a General Meeting, to study and settle matters concerning the corporation. See the by-laws for terms of reference. Means of realization ART. 6 - The funds necessary for the realization of the program of the Syndicate consist of the proceeds of the admission fee, dues, donations and subsidies made to it. Obligations of Members ART. 7 - The admission fee for membership of the Syndicat is two francs per Member, once paid, for the first year of formation, and five francs for Members joining the Syndicat after that year. Each Member also undertakes to pay an annual subscription of one franc, payable in advance. Sundicat administration ART. 8 - The Syndicat is administered by a Council of twelve Members elected by secret ballot at a General Meeting. ART. 9 - The Council shall elect from among its members: a Chairman; a Vice-Chairman; a Secretary; an Assistant Secretary; an Assistant Treasurer. One-third of the Board is renewed each year, and outgoing members are eligible for re-election. ART. 10 - The Board has all necessary powers to manage and administer the affairs and property of the Association, to accept donations and legacies, to collect all funds belonging to the Association in any capacity whatsoever, and to supervise the use of such funds as the Association may direct on its proposal. It is also responsible for all administrative details and makes all internal regulations that may be required for the execution of the present Articles of Association. Revision of the Articles of Association ART. 11 - Amendments may be made to the present Articles of Association; the request for revision must be made and signed by half plus one of the Adhering Members and addressed to the President, who will make the proposal to the General Meeting convened for this purpose. Dissolution ART. 12 - The dissolution of the Association may only be pronounced at the request of three quarters of the Participating Members, who must take part in the vote, failing which the request will be null and void. In the event of dissolution, the Association's funds will be paid into the "Caisse de la Société d'Horticulture de France". RULES Admissions ARTICLE 1 - All persons wishing to become members of the Syndicat must be introduced by two Adherent Members, and will be admitted after a favourable vote by the General Meeting. SUBSCRIPTION ART. 2 - Article 8 of the Articles of Association sets the admission fee and the annual subscription. Any member who is one year in arrears with payment of dues will be informed by the Treasurer that he/she will be asked to leave the association if he/she fails to pay after this warning. General Meeting ART. 3 - The general meeting of the Association will be held once a year, on the first Thursday in September. In addition, when the Board of Directors deems it appropriate, extraordinary meetings may be held, but only by invitation. Board of Directors ART. 4 - The Board of Directors will meet at least twice a year, convened by the Secretary. Attendance is compulsory for all members of the Board. ART. 5 - Any member of the Board who has missed three meetings without a valid excuse shall be deemed to have resigned. ART. 6 - Any decision taken by the Board is valid regardless of the number of members present. Chairmanship ART. 7 - The Chairman is in charge of the Chamber; he directs the debates. He chairs the Board of Directors; he may attend all committees. Secretariat ART. 8 - The duties of the Secretary include: 1° keeping the Social Register, containing the names and addresses of Members and the date of their admission; 2° drafting the minutes; 3° reading the minutes, correspondence, etc. at the meeting. The Assistant Secretary assists him in the performance of his duties. TREASURY ART. 9 - The accounts and cash are kept by a Treasurer. The Treasurer collects, on his signature and by means of receipts drawn from a counterfoil book, the membership fees and collects all sums belonging to the Society; he pays all expenses ordered on the payment order, signed by the President. He may not possess a sum in excess of two hundred francs. Available funds are deposited in a Société Générale Cashier. The Assistant Treasurer shall deputise for him if necessary. Arbitrators and Experts ART. 10 - Expert arbitrators are appointed by the General Meeting, by secret ballot, for one year; they may be re-elected. All functions on the Board of Directors are compatible with those of expert arbitrator. Functions of Arbitrators and Experts ART. 11 - Expert arbitrators are entrusted by the Board of Directors, but under their personal responsibility, with: 1° The study and assessment of disputed cases entrusted to them. 2° To reconcile the parties involved as far as possible. 3° Report to the Board on the results of their operations. 4° to submit to the Board a statement of their fees, drawn up in accordance with article 12 of the Association's regulations. ART. 12 - The fees of expert arbitrators are set at twenty francs per session. ART. 13 - Expert arbitrators who, for any reason whatsoever, do not wish to deal with a dispute must immediately notify the Chairman, who will give immediate notice. ART. 14 - As soon as elected, the members of the Council shall proceed, by secret ballot, to the election of the Members of their Bureau, in the successive order of registration provided for in Article 10 of the Articles of Association. All functions are free of charge. ART. 15 - The present Statutes and Regulations, modiffés et votés par la Commission administrative nommée par la réunion préparatoire du 23 Août 1894, dans les séances des 6 et 20 Septembre 1894, ont été approuvés par le Syndicat en Assemblée générale dans sa séance du 12 Octobre 1894. The Syndicat des Maraichers, which was only five years old at the end of this century, nevertheless had three hundred members, and had already carried out important work. Since its foundation, it has led a campaign against the "regrattiers" who swarm the Ilalles Centrales in all shapes and sizes. Today, a federation of sixty agricultural and horticultural unions in the Seine and Seine-et-Oise departments, whose members are all growers who sell their produce directly at Ilalles Centrales, is continuing this campaign. A number of regrattiers have disappeared, and the time is not far off when those who remain will disappear in their turn, assimilating with the other merchants who sell on the Halles floor. A unanimous protest by interested parties against the regrattiers forced the Chambers to pass a law, dated June 11 1896, whose purpose was to set up a commission supérieure des Halles, charged with regulating the Halles Centrales trade. This commission immediately recognized the validity of the protest, and measures have already been taken against this illicit industry, which is detrimental to producers, pavilion merchants, city coffers and consumers in general. These measures are not yet strong enough to completely eliminate this industry. Indeed, what is regrat? If you open the Académie dictionary, you'll see that it's: a small business that consists of retailing certain commodities second-hand. Our ancient kings sold letters of regrat, which corresponded to today's patente. But at the Halles Centrales de Paris, those who engage in this trade, which has become a veritable industry, do not pay a patent, and find themselves assimilated to producers; and if we did not react against this state of affairs, they would soon be the masters of the Paris food trade. O8 As this state of affairs persisted, the President of the Republic issued a decree on August 5, 1898, which read as follows: "ARTICLE PREMIER - The carreau forain des Halles is reserved for growers who bring produce there to sell themselves, and for suppliers selling foodstuffs they own. Merchants selling products purchased outside Paris and shipped directly to them at Les Halles, or which they bring themselves, are considered to be "approvisionneurs". Merchants who contravene the above provisions will be excluded from the floor by decision of the Préfet de Police. "Farmers and suppliers must justify the origin of their goods by producing, on arrival at the carreau, either a declaration of introduction indicating their name, address, the number of cars brought in and the date stamp of the octroi post which controlled the entry of their goods, or a consignment note or railway receipt at their address. "Farmers established in Paris must provide proof of their status in the form of a certificate issued by the Police Commissioner for their district, stating their name, address and the extent of their cultivation. "ART. 2 - The President of the Council, Minister of the Interior, is responsible for the execution of the present decree, which will be inserted in the Bulletin des Lois. Done at Le Harre, July 27, 1898. FÉLX FAURE. For the President of the Republic, The President of the Council, Minister of the Interior, HIENRI BRISSOX Regrattiers are using every possible means to confuse legislators by trying to prove that they are suppliers. In this age of commercial freedom, the legislator's mind needs to be well enlightened if he is not to unfairly harm useful suppliers by wanting to destroy the parasites that everyone is calling for to be abolished. All the protesters are in absolute agreement, even with the Administration, not to harm traders bringing in food purchased at the place of origin. As far as market-gardeners in particular are concerned, the solution is easy to establish: 1° The Administration must only give fixed places in the streets specially allocated to them to those who can show proof that they really are producers. It may be objected that some may present titles today to obtain a space and tomorrow sell their business while keeping their space. To avoid these inconveniences, the Administration can ask the Syndicat for information from time to time, and the Syndicat is always well placed to provide reliable information. The lay reader may ask: why should this bother the producers? The answer is this: It goes without saying that the regrater buys only good-looking, in-demand produce, and sets up a nice fruit shop without paying a license fee, literally killing the space of the grower next door, who is obliged to sell all his produce, and not all of it is good-looking and in demand. As long as this type of regrater was only made up of former market gardeners, no longer able to do their trade, and doing this little business to help them live, the young people, in a spirit of good fellowship, didn't put up any obstacles, but over the last twenty years or so, real traders have come to set up in large numbers among them, and the number is increasing every day. This illicit competition has led some market gardeners to give up cultivation, in order to take up this trade themselves, which 00 is less risky and, above all, involves less trouble and more profit than the former. The regrattiers also create disastrous competition for the pavilion merchants, who are burdened with fees to pay for the places they occupy, and which they would eventually abandon to become regrattieres. This also results in a considerable loss for the city of Paris, as the pavilions are deserted and a considerable amount of trade is done without paying patents, square taxes, etc. The beautiful products are monopolized by the merchants, who are obliged to pay for the space they occupy. As the best produce is snapped up from the outset by all these merchants, fruit growers are already obliged to buy second-hand, and therefore at a premium, which inevitably leads to a price increase that consumers in general pay. Finally, the Syndicat des maraichers is making its influence felt on the economic conditions of the crop itself. In fact, it has set up cooperative centers in each region, where union members can buy their small tools at wholesale prices. Of course, as we've said elsewhere, this is only the beginning of this economic path. Later on, large tools will go through the same channel. It is absolutely essential that the younger generations seek every possible means of reducing the cost price of their products, if they are to stand up to the competition from products from the south of France, a competition that will grow by the day. These means consist of: 1° low-cost purchase of equipment; 2° increased yield through mechanical processes; 3° creation of new commercial outlets, by having products sold directly by commission agents on foreign markets. One of the Syndicat's most active members, Mr. Narcisse Laurent, has just exhibited his products, at his own expense, risk and peril, at the Saint Petersburg Exhibition. He won two silver medals for this small partial exhibition, which earned him the Cross of Agricultural Merit. The path is clear, it will be followed, and when the Syndicat takes this 1008 business in hand, it will achieve great results. Here, experience shows that no product of this kind can compete with the produce of market gardeners in the Paris regionnc. So, during the most difficult few years of the company's creation, this is what the Syndicat has been working on. When all growers understand the benefits of joining this nucleus, it will be able to undertake major reforms, create new commercial outlets, and make the industry it represents more flourishing than ever by increasing our national exports. The syndicate, currently in agreement with that of the French early fruit growers, is negotiating with the major fruit and early fruit commission houses and the President of the Compagnie Transatlantique, to ship French early fruit to North America. After the Exhibition, we hope to welcome the departure of the first French steamer to set sail for the New World, carrying French horticultural produce in refrigerated equipment. SYNDICAT DES MARAICHERS After the Revolution and the first Empire, as soon as the Bourbons returned, some of the brotherhoods we mentioned in the second part of this book were reconstituted. But very few of them occupied the same parishes, given that gardeners had moved away since the great social movement that had considerably increased the capital's surface area. However, we can cite the parish of Saint-Médard, which had had a brotherhood of gardeners since antiquity, and was one of the first to be reconstituted in this century. Others were formed in new parishes, such as Sainte-Marguerite and Vaugirard. In our century, these associations have lost the primordial motive that drives men: interest. Their existence could only be short-lived. All these brotherhoods were placed under the patronage of Saint-Fiacre, who had long been the only saint recognized as the patron saint of gardeners, whereas in the Middle Ages, some brotherhoods celebrated Saint-Jacques and Saint-Christophe, and others Saint-Fiacre and Sainte-Véronique. The former celebrated on July 25, and the latter, as today, on August 30. It's obvious that the timing of the feast day alone tipped the balance in favor of Saint-Fiacre, as this was roughly the beginning of the year for market gardening. When I was young, I remember seeing these constituted bodies fetch the image of the Saint from a confrere, bring it in procession to the mass said in his honor, and then take it to another confrere, where it remained until the following year. The feast was religious in nature, but it also gave rise to fraternal agape among confreres, which sometimes turned into drunkenness in certain quarters. In the evening, a grand banquet followed by a ball, which lasted until the following morning, brought together the entire brotherhood. Later, the religious festival was abolished, and only the banquet remained for some time. Today, it no longer exists anywhere in the guild, but the time is not far off when new associations will reconstitute it under another title. Already, the Chambre Syndicale des ouvriers jardiniers holds an annual festival, in which Saint-Fiacre plays no part, but which is nonetheless a corporate festival. This Syndicat will be followed by other societies, as men have always changed the titles of their associations without altering their morals or temperament. Our guild's feast day will always be celebrated within a fortnight, at the same time as our ancestors celebrated theirs: around Thermidor, which is generally the end of the gardener's year. Which once again vindicates our great ancestors of the Revolution in their desire to start the year at the time of sowing, i.e. when the harvest is almost complete and the seeds for the next crop are being entrusted to the earth. 1 In the chapter on the great gardens created by kings and the powerful, we said that a chapter would be devoted to the Jardin des Plantes in Paris, so much so has this establishment wiped out everything that had been done up to that time. The author does not pretend to provide a technical demonstration of the usefulness of this institution, nor a scientific picture of its current state. Such a work could only be produced by an authorized scholar. The author will therefore confine himself to outlining the history of its foundation, and quoting some of the most eminent men who have remained attached to it, devoting their science and their existence to the embellishment of this incomparable work. The King's Garden was founded under Louis XIII (1), by Hérouard, the King's first physician, and Gui-de-la-Brosse, his ordinary physician. These two doctors obtained authorization, by letters patent, to buy, in the king's name, a house plus twenty-four acres of land in the Faubourg Saint-Victor, in order to found a royal garden of medicinal herbs. This land was surrounded by marshes and crossed by the Bièvre, whose course was moved to its present location. Until the beginning of this century, rue Buffon, ruc Poliveau, etc. were still home to a number of market gardeners and, above all, horticulturists. In 1645, the following edict was issued and registered; (1) Bernard, etc. Le Jardin des Plantes. "Whereas medical schools are not taught to perform pharmacy operations. Sieur Bernard would have begged us that three doctors chosen by him from the Faculty of Medicine in Paris, be provided by us to demonstrate to schoolchildren the inner workings of plants and all medicines, and to work on the composition of all kinds of drugs, by simple and chemical means. "Voulons que dans un cabinet de ladite maison, il soit gardé un échantillon de toutes les drogues, tant simples que composées, ensemble toutes les choses rares, en la naturc qui s'y rencontreront; duquel cabinet ledit La Brosse, aura la clef et régie, pour en faire l'ouverture aux jours de démonstration". Gui-de-la-Brosse laid out the garden proper over an area of ten arpents, out of the twenty-four that comprised the property acquired by the King. Since then, the Jardin des Plantes has been considerably enlarged, forming a vast parallelogram between Rue Geoffroy- Saint-Hilaire, Rue Buffon, Place Valhubert, where it forms a cutaway, opposite the Pont d'Austerlitz, Quai Saint-Bernard and Rue Cuvicr. There is a monumental gate opposite the Pont d'Austerlitz; another at the corner of Rue Cuvier, facing the small square in front of the Hopital de la Pitié. A third at the corner of rue Geoffroy-Saint-Hilaire and rue Buffon, which must be the first entrance to the establishment, and several others giving access to the garden. The establishment is visited from every angle. Scientists who come to study this or that science; foreigners who don't want to cross Paris without visiting

this magnificent establishment. Parisians rich and poor alike bring their children here to visit the Swiss valley or menagerie. In summer, the locals come here to relax in the shade, as they do in the other squares of Paris. . 108 Rue Geoffroy-Saint-Hilaire is bordered by the Cabinet d'Histoire Naturelle, which contains specimens of all kinds of naturalized animals and birds; at the top of this street, the highest part of the garden, there is an immense labyrinth, from which you have a beautiful view over part of Paris and its suburbs. The beautiful cedar of Lebanon, planted by de Jussieu, can be found around the bend in this labyrinth. Rue Cuvier is bordered in part by the lecture hall, the anatomy cabinet and the Swiss valley. On the quay side is also the Swiss Valley, with a superb gate serving as a fence. Rue Buffon is bordered by the mineralogy cabinet and the garden; a new pavilion has been built in recent years along this street, almost at the corner of Place Valhubert. As you enter the garden from this square, you see before you the botanical garden surrounded by wide avenues, and next to the avenue on the right, the Swiss valley. Higher up, vast greenhouses where trees and shrubs of all climates can be cultivated. Next door, a garden created by Buffon for seedlings, which became a school, where we learn how to sow and raise plants, and where we follow the series of procedures necessary to raise plants in all climates. In its early days under Louis XIII, the king's garden covered twenty-four acres. Today, it covers thirty-one hectares, eight of which are occupied by the menagerie. It would take several volumes to list all the men who have served as directors and teachers of this establishment, and the services they have rendered to science and humanity. Among the best known is Joseph Pitton de Tournefort, who virtually created botany and enriched the establishment with a large number of plants. De Jussieu, following in the footsteps of Linné, who had taken botany a giant step forward, was the author of the so-called natural method, which still serves as the basis for the classifications that come from time to time, DO0 bringing modifications to the order and importance of plant families that are never more than secondary. Then came the illustrious Buffon, who deployed all his science to enrich the king's garden. Taking advantage of the resources offered by the famous establishment he directed, he set about tracing the picture of the whole of nature, and worked all his life on his Histoire naturelle. The author's style is considered one of the leading classics of the French language. In his acceptance speech to the Académie, he himself demonstrated that style is man himself. Buffon was joined by his compatriot Daubenton, who was also a great scientist. With men like these, the king's garden enjoyed great prestige throughout the world. At the time of the Revolution, Bernardin-de-Saint-Pierre was at the head of the establishment. By decree of June 23, 1793, the Jardin du Roi became the Muséum d'Histoire Naturelle, commonly known as the Jardin des Plantes. Since that time, every man who has held a chair in the establishment can be considered a great scientist, rendering immense services to his fellow citizens and to posterity. To name just one, let's take the centenarian Chevreul, who, in addition to the great body of work he produced, can serve as an example of sobriety to the men of today. It is said that this man never drank wine or other alcoholic beverages in his long life. In the chapter on the great French gardens, we mentioned this garden and the cultivation carried out by its creator, La Quintinie. But now that this kitchen garden has become our Ecole nationale d'horticulture, we're going to retrace its history, written by M. Nanot, its current director, in a fine book entitled: le Potager de Versailles. It would have been impossible to obtain such precise information anywhere else. Its creation dates back to the reign of Louis XIV. In 1678, Mansard and Le Nôtre had just completed the chateau and park of Versailles, and had destroyed the kitchen garden that had existed under Louis XIII and had been planted by Jacques Boyceau. This kitchen garden was around two hundred and sixty meters long and one hundred and twenty-six wide. In the middle, a long alley separated it in two, with each side divided into three small walled gardens with a circular driveway. This installation provided a very large surface area of walls suitable for espaliers. To the right of the entrance were the farm buildings and greenhouses. The garden having been destroyed by the construction of the château or its outbuildings, the king entrusted La Quintinie with the task of finding suitable land on which to reconstitute the kitchen garden. La Quintinie had already been managing the king's fruit orchards for several years. The new kitchen garden was built on ponds filled first with soil from the newly dug "pièce d'eau des Suisses", then with soil brought in from the Satory mountain, a free-flowing soil that becomes a mortar in wet weather, and petrifies in dry. By means of an aqueduct, La Quintinie diverted the water from the neighboring mountains into the filled-in ponds. To clean up the soil, he buried a lot of large-scale manure, and gave his squares a certain slope, in a word, he drained the garden. By this means, he says it didn't take him long to improve his crops. For watering, water was taken from a reservoir on the Satory hillock. With such means, one can judge what the installation of this garden must have cost. In those days, it was customary to prune trees with a billhook, giving them the shape of men, animals, ships and so on. Armand d'Andilly demonstrated the absurdity of these customs by making the first use of espaliers. He harvested beautiful fruit at Port-Royal, where he had created a model garden. La Quintinie adopted his principles and soon became a master in the art. He divided the twenty-five-acre kitchen garden into a large number of enclosures, separated by walls used for espalier cultivation, with different exposures; in the center, he laid out a vast square of ten acres surrounded by terraces; it's in this square that you can still see four large Catillac pear trees, said to have been planted by La Quintinie. The perimeter was divided into twenty-eight compartments, forming nine sunken gardens. Five sunken squares were dedicated to a few varieties of peach trees, the fig orchard and the melon orchard. To the east of the large square, three enclosures were devoted to the cultivation of asparagus for warming. Other squares were devoted to a variety of fruit and vegetable crops. Some wall exposures were used for espaliered peach trees, while other squares were used to grow strawberries. Before La Quintinie, Montreuil's growers cultivated peach trees with great skill, producing beautiful fruit. It's true that they have a special soil that suits this tree and makes it easier to grow than in other areas. 4009 It's by dint of study and expense, to make his soil his own, that the great gardener has succeeded in harvesting fruit as fine as that from special soils. Here we recognize the man of genius and the lead he had over his contemporaries in the same profession. La Quintinie had already studied the nature of the different soils required for different species of fruit trees, the varieties, the exposure, etc. He had not only studied the soil, but also the soil itself. What's more, he didn't need to count the cost of his studies. For the same reasons, taking advantage of his position as director of the king's vegetable and fruit gardens, he was one of the first to come up with the idea of forced cultivation of fruit and vegetables. Speaking of asparagus, he said that only the king could enjoy eating asparagus in winter. In January 1680, for the wedding of Mademoiselle de Blois, the king's daughter, to Prince Conty, the table was decorated with nineteen silver baskets filled with anemones, hyacinths, jasmine and orange blossom. The kitchen garden cost 1,170,983 livres to build, and its annual upkeep in 1685 cost between eighteen and twenty thousand livres. La Quintinie was so highly esteemed by the king that he ennobled him in 1687, his coat of arms being: "Argent, a chevron azure between two mullets of the same, and in base, a tree of vert, field of the same". After many transformations, the buildings he occupied became today's School of Horticulture. After La Quintinie, Nicolas Bénard took over the management of the gardens, followed by the Le Normand family, from father to son, until 1782. With these gardeners, the production of early vegetables increased considerably. The latter introduced the forced cultivation of grapes and pineapples, and it has been said how this D08 fruit was introduced to France, and the success Le Normand achieved in cultivating it. He also tried his hand at growing coffee trees, and succeeded in raising twelve of them, which grew to a height of four meters and produced up to ten pounds of coffee a year, so ripe and of such good quality that the finest gourmets at the Court mistook it for coffee from the tropical sun, which greatly amused the King. These gardeners also tried their hand at growing truffles, but without success. They were very good gardeners, and the kitchen garden was enlarged in good proportions under their management, which lasted for almost a century. The last Le Normand was succeeded by an Englishman, Alexandre Bronn, who was replaced by Gondouin, who was forced to resign by the Revolution. In a deliberation of February 1793, the district of Versailles declared that the kitchen garden could be rented, either in part or in its entirety. It was rented in part to several tenants, including J.-B. Forgeot and Nicolas Dufresne. On February 25, 1798, the Convention decided that a central school would be created in Versailles, and that the school's garden would be located in the kitchen garden. The management of this botanical garden was entrusted to Antoine Richard, a learned botanist, who was granted the right to establish a fruit tree nursery. This Convention law saved the kitchen garden. In 1801, the clos aux asperges and the eleven gardens were transformed into nurseries. In 1805, the property was returned to the Crown, and the garden returned to its original purpose. Under the First Empire, its management was given to Count Lelieur, who restored it to its former splendor. He was able to enlist the help of skilled garden managers such as Peradon, Moutroi, Delpierre, Massey and Souchet. An arboriculturist, Dumoutier, a pupil of Thouin and professor at the Jardin des Plantes, restored the kitchen garden's fruit trees. At the end of the Empire, the cultivation of early crops was entrusted to Barthélémy Edy, and open-ground cultivation to Modeste Lampérière. Edy made great strides in pineapple cultivation; his successor was Grison in 1824. In addition to the forced cultivation carried out by his predecessors, Grison planted a Chinese banana tree. All means of heating were experimented with by this skilful gardener, whose sous-chef was Gontier, who later settled in Montrouge. Count Lelieur's successor as manager was Montory, who was only an inspector, and was succeeded by Massey. Under Massey, the cultivation of early fruit and vegetables increased considerably, particularly pineapples, which underwent major expansion under Gontier père and fils. Massey introduced the cultivation of crambé cabbage to the garden. He cultivated peach trees, cherry trees, plum trees, fig trees and vines in pots. He remained in charge of the garden until 1819, when he returned to his native Tarbes. The list of sous-chefs includes Ecoffey, who succeeded Gontier. A man named Moreau joined the kitchen garden in 1832 as a laborer, and two years later became workshop head, becoming first boy in 1837, before leaving the garden in 1851. This list also includes Chouvet, a talented horticulturist. Grison père's sous-chef was his son, who replaced him on his death. Then came Gondouin, responsible for transporting the garden's produce to the National Palaces; he was replaced by Doré until 1877. In 1850, the vegetable garden changed its character, becoming a field of application, and was managed by Auguste Hardy, who retired in 1859. Under the Second Empire, the kitchen garden once again became what it had been before the Revolution. The agronomic institute was abolished, leaving the kitchen garden, which was entrusted to Hardy throughout the empire, assisted by skilled gardeners. For early produce, Pilon François succeeded Grison fils. Puteaux was succeeded by Louis Bernard. The Third Republic set out to organize a vast agricultural and horticultural education system in France. It was at the instigation of Picrre Joigneaux, deputy for the Côte-d'Or, that the present horticultural school was founded: Hardy remained its director until his death on November 24, 1891. LES JARDINIERS DE PARIS 009 The heads of cultivation were Pelou for early produce, Bernard and Rouland for fruit trees. Piétrot succeeded Pelou. In 1879, Lemaître Octave replaced Desvaux, whose successor was Lefèvre. Pavard was put in charge of the greenhouses in 1889; Lemaye replaced Dunur in 1892, with Delot as his sous-chef. On Hardy's death, interim management was entrusted to M. Henri Grosjean, inspector of agricultural education until April 12, 1892. On that date, Mr. Nanot took over as head of the school, with Mr. Piétrot as head gardener. THE GARDEN'S CURRENT CULTURE La Quintinie had divided the kitchen garden into twenty-nine enclosures, of which there are now only sixteen. Its surface area, including terraces, is three hectares and twenty-two ares, divided by alleys that cut the garden at right angles. Each of the enclosures is surrounded by flowerbeds, the middle of which is occupied by counter espaliers, planted in palmettes with horizontal branches, and along the alleys by apple trees in horizontal cords. In the past, access to the garden was by staircase, but in 1828, the east and west terraces were partly transformed. Under the terraces are vaults for storing vegetables and tools, one for growing mushrooms, and another for blanching, notably capuchin beard and endive. These different types of cultivation show that the school is keeping pace with all the progress made in vegetable growing in this century. Mushroom cultivation is also part of this specialty. In the north, the retaining walls of the terraces are lined with currant and raspberry bushes. To the south, back-to-back greenhouses are devoted to the forciculture of peach trees and vines. The total glazed surface area of the vegetable garden's tarpaulins, greenhouses and frames is 5,899 meters, almost all of which is heated with ther99 mosiphon, representing an annual expenditure of nine thousand five hundred francs. Following on from the winter garden is the bedded square, with a surface area of twenty-nine ares, divided into eight rows of thermosiphon-heated tarpaulins for growing melons and strawberries. On another section, white asparagus is grown on site. This crop has lost much of its importance due to competition from imported pineapples. From a commercial point of view, it will probably become impossible, but on large estates it will continue for a long time to come, both as an ornamental plant and for the flavor of its fruit. CULTURE OF MELONS One hundred and ninety-two thermosiphon-heated frames, and as many covering layers, with no other means of heating, produce Melons from late April to midsummer; these Melons belong to a variety derived from the Prescott Melon and selected by the School. At this point, we'd like to take the liberty of levelling a reproach at the administration of this school: this cultivation, as well as that of strawberry plants, resembles an industrial operation rather than an experimental garden, which should not be concerned with profit, but simply with teaching the cultivation of different varieties that can be grown in different climates, where students will be called upon to apply their art. What's more, since the School is national, i.e. maintained with taxpayers' money, it must not compete in the marketplace with specialists in these kinds of crops. Various petitions have been sent to the Minister of Agriculture to remedy this state of affairs, and those concerned hope that the competent authorities will recognize the validity of their protests. This criticism does not attack the culture of the school, which is C everywhere well done; it also proves that the teachers at the head of the establishment are more than equal to their mission. We will, however, criticize the poor maintenance of the frames, which are badly painted and glazed. However, these same men are well aware that one of the essential points in horticulture is to have equipment in good condition. There is no excuse for this neglect, when the equipment is clean in other schools, where the pupils are even younger, and therefore the instruction is much more elementary than at Versailles. A few frames are used for growing tomatoes, and a few for Concombi. Seven thousand strawberry plants are forced into pots. Around one hundred frames cover two thousand thermosiphon-heated plants in the open ground. Three hundred frames, without artificial heat, cover strawberry plants: around thirty large-fruit varieties and the main varieties of the four known seasons. Twenty-five frames are used for the forced cultivation of Asparagus. Four to five hundred pots of beans of various varieties are grown to demonstrate this forced cultivation, too often abandoned by practitioners. A layer of cloches for growing Romaine is made to demonstrate to students not only the forced cultivation of this plant, but also the counter-planting, the initiative for which belongs to the old Parisian market gardeners of the early 19th century. Frames and cloches are also used to raise plants such as: Eggplants, Tomatoes, Squash, Pumpkins, etc. Lastly, seedlings are sown to obtain new varieties, notably for remontant strawberries. Near the north terrace, the School has built a basin for sulphating mats, stakes, etc. Next to it, there is a shed for the production of strawberries. Next door, there is a shed used to make straw matting. Whereas, to be a true practicing gardener, one must, to this day, know how to make straw mats, glaze and paint frames, etc., the reader can see from the above that this is not the case. The reader can see from this broad outline (for more information, read M. Nanot's book: le Potager de Versailles), that our horticultural school spares no effort to keep up with modern progress. Lessons are given by knowledgeable and practical heads of cultivation, and the students who leave this school, if they wish to take advantage of all the lessons received, can in turn become artists in their trade. The services rendered to horticulture in general by the King's Garden in the last century can also be seen in the trials of forced cultivation carried out there by the scientists at the head of this establishment. To conclude this chapter, let us say that the men of the third republic were well inspired in creating a school of horticulture in the former kitchen garden of our kings; for the students who graduate from this school will be able to render useful services to horticulture in general. Let us therefore salute the memory of the initiator of the School's transformation, my honourable compatriot Pierre Joigneaux, who devoted his entire life to defending and encouraging agriculture and horticulture. PRIVATE FOUNDATIONS (FLEURY-MEUDON), Alongside horticultural schools founded by the State or the départements, there are others founded and maintained by philanthropic societies, or due to the generosity of wealthy private individuals. I'll list the main ones in the order of the visits I've made to them, starting with the Orphelinat de Saint-Philippe, in Fleury-Meudon. This school was founded by Madame Brignole, duchesse de Galliéra, around 1888. It's an important estate, of which we'll only talk about the part that concerns us: the garden, and especially market gardening. The garden covers an area of around five hectares, divided into three parts: vegetable growing, arboriculture, greenhouses and ornamental cultivation. The school has between fifty and fifty-five pupils aged between fourteen and seventeen, all of whom have lost one or both parents, and whose remaining family members want them to learn the gardening trade. For their education and upkeep, these children cost the organization around eight hundred francs a year. The services are divided into thirds: in the first year, the pupils work on market gardening, in the second on arboriculture, and in the third they move on to greenhouses and ornamental cultivation. The whole program is supervised by a director: currently Mr. Coffiniez, who seems to us to have great aptitude for all aspects of horticulture, judging on the spot, the products he gives in the specialty we treat. The market garden department has two sous-chefs, the arboriculture department has one, another is attached to the greenhouses, and a third is in charge of summer planting and city services. All theoretical courses are given by the director. MARAICH GROWING This is undoubtedly the part of horticulture that occupies the largest surface area. A large square of artichokes, asparagus, seedlings of peas, beans, etc., followed by seasonal salads, lettuces and Romaine, cabbages, onions, leeks, wild chicory, spinach, sorrel, all the condiments that this type of cultivation entails, etc. Strawberry plants are also grown on this square. Strawberry plants are also grown on a large scale and have a luxuriant vegetation in these grounds. I noticed the four-season Belle de Meaux variety, followed by large strawberries: Docteur Morère, Héricart and Général Chanzy, which grow less vigorously than the previous ones. All these crops are well understood and well-managed for a school whose aim is not specifically intensive cultivation, but demonstration to fourteen- and fifteen-year-olds. The school has a cellar where theoretical and practical demonstrations of mushroom cultivation are given, meaning that nothing is forgotten in this beautiful and vast establishment. CULTIVATION OF PRIMROSE CROPS It is here above all that we can judge the competence of modern market gardeners. The practitioner can examine 906 on the square assigned to this crop, a set very well understood to achieve the goal that the work proposes. Carrot beds sown in December, in which Radishes and Lettuce have been harvested. At the end of April, we are starting to harvest Carrots by thinning them out, and Cauliflowers, which are already strong, will replace them. A few panels of turnips are also being thinned for consumption. We also see Beans and Potatoes, also on layers, which are coming in very well. The frames used for the carrot and turnip beds have been transferred to strawberry plants in the ground, which will be harvested after the bedded ones. As and when a crop needs to be depanelled, new beds are prepared to plant Melons and receive the frames. Most of the frames at this time are used to grow melons and strawberries. Despite the school's lack of thermosiphon heating for this important crop (which is surprising, with such a rich installation), there are Prescott melons with grey bottoms, which are at full size and will ripen in the first ten days of May, followed closely by Prescott melons with white bottoms. A row of green cucumbers is also well treated. Several rows of Docteur Morère strawberries, which are nearing the end of their harvest, are a credit to the gardener who grows them. HORTICULTURE SCHOOLS EQUIPMENT The establishment has three hundred chassis for growing early fruit and vegetables. This equipment is in very good condition, with well-maintained paintwork and glazing; a compliment we were unable to pay to our national horticultural school in France. The school is set up for irrigation watering on all 908 points; nevertheless, barrels are placed on part of the garden, to teach students how to water with this system. Doormats are sewn in four rows and sulfated. In a word, everything is set up to train gardeners, and the underprivileged who are fortunate enough to enter this establishment can acquire the beginnings of a horticultural education, which will enable them later to adopt one or other of the specialties most suited to their tastes or aptitudes. The Director says that all this is taken into account when placing them on leaving the school; however, the majority adopt the speciality of managing middle-class gardens. In fact, the instruction they receive at school is highly suited to these kinds of gardeners, who need to know all the branches of horticulture. With practical experience, there's no doubt that many students will make excellent gardeners. As for the other two specialties, which are outside our subject, we won't talk about them here, as we don't have the necessary expertise to deal with them. A slightly smaller area than the one we have just examined is given over to fruit and ornamental arboriculture. Seedlings are planted to teach students the various grafting techniques, including grapevines. There is also a botany square, where students can learn the basics of botany. The garden has seven double and two adjoining greenhouses, each sixteen meters long, plus sixty-six frames of tarpaulin, all heated by thermosiphon. All plant species are grown here, from ferns to orchids. THE PRODUCT OF THE GARDEN Many of the plants grown in the greenhouses are used to plant the beds, which are so numerous in this vast establishment; 200 of the remainder are sold either to florists who trade in this way, or to large plants or cut flowers sold to boutique florists in Paris. Early fruit and vegetables are auctioned at the Ilalles Centrales de Paris. Everything common is consumed at home, where there are three or four hundred mouths to feed, or sold in the surrounding area. Proceeds from sales and food supplied to the house amount to fifteen or sixteen thousand francs a year. Deitised GO This establishment was founded some ten years ago by a philanthropic society of Israelites, in the outbuildings of the immense estates that the Duc du Maine owned in and around Sceaux in the last century (1). A small court that fought for a time against the Regency, during the minority of Louis XV. The aim of the organization is to take in, educate and moralize abandoned male children belonging to the Israelite faith. The pupils taken in are aged between ten and fifteen, all orphans, abandoned or morally abandoned. At the age of eighteen, they leave the shelter and are placed according to the trade they have learned. The school also has a small carpentry and joinery workshop, where frames, chests, etc. are made. But most work in horticulture or on the small farm adjoining the school. On leaving, the young man is not abandoned by the work, his primary education, even secondary for some, and vocational for all, is completed, and he receives as his boss, a member of the Board of Directors, responsible for supervising him. He continues to belong to the great family in which he was raised, and this family protects him against adversity when his conduct leaves nothing to be desired. A noble example of human solidarity! (1) Michelet, Histoire de France. As the school takes in children at the age of ten, their primary education must be perfected before they can be professionally trained. As the school has various workshops, as mentioned above, it is necessary to have an administrative director, who runs the school under the supervision of a Board of Directors appointed by the Society. The director is specifically in charge of teaching all theoretical courses. A schoolteacher is attached to the house to give primary and even secondary instruction to the pupils. Each workshop has a master to teach the trade to students who want to learn it. In the garden, a head gardener is in charge of all cultivation; he is assisted by sous-chefs in charge of greenhouses, fruit trees and vegetables. The work supports fifty-five children, at least two-thirds of whom will become gardeners. In the few unannounced visits I've had the honor of making to the establishment, I've always found the crops in good condition. The espaliered trees, in rows of various species, represent a length of several kilometers. New plantings are made every year, as well as seedlings for grafting. In this area, the school's teaching leaves nothing to be desired. It is the dominant part of the establishment. However, over the last three years, the company has made rapid progress in other branches of horticulture. In fact, in 1896, the director asked the Société nationale d'horticulture for a joint commission of arboriculturists and market gardeners to visit the farm. I was part of this commission and, appointed rapporteur for market gardening, I was in a better position than anyone else to observe, during my last visit, the progress made in general, and especially in market gardening. 908 Horticultural equipment has been substantially increased. A twenty-five-meter long, sixteen-meter wide and five-meter high, lean-to vineyard greenhouse has been installed with every possible comfort. Vines are planted in different varieties. Fruit trees are also foraged here. A second, temperate greenhouse is used to grow plants for the beds and flowerbeds in the ornamental area, while a third, hot greenhouse, the same size as the other two, is used to grow the most delicate ornamental plants. The potting room is five meters above the boiler room. All the plants are well labeled, so that children can remember the names of the different varieties. The same applies to the botanical garden, where new collections are added every year. Market gardening has also made rapid progress, and the equipment has been increased; the school now has one hundred and fifty trays used for this purpose, under which we have seen strawberry plants and melons of good quality, though not as advanced as those at the school we described earlier. The cultivation is well done and this can only contribute to good results from a professional point of view. There are also Carrots, early Potatoes, well cared for, depanelled on time and the frames busy with other crops. We'd like to take this opportunity to point out the weak point of non-specialist gardeners: since forced cultivation is always very costly, it's absolutely essential that the plants and seeds used are selected with the utmost perfection; this is the most important quality, otherwise the costs and care are the same, and the result is mediocre. Consequently, gardeners (of whom there are many in middle-class homes today) who engage in this type of cultivation should only use plants and seeds of which they are certain that the selection is the most perfect. 908 Apart from this criticism of an old practitioner, which is addressed not only to this school, but to almost all gardeners who are not specialists, the cultivation is well established, with order and good taste, and irreproachable cleanliness, an essential quality for a school, given that when children, from an early age, are accustomed to working cleanly, they continue to do so later, and this is the basis of all cultivation at all times and in all places. The weak point of this establishment - you wouldn't believe it on a visit - is the lack of water. The Board of Directors, which is not made up of practitioners, and which takes such pride in beautifying this magnificent estate, will one day get to grips with this idea, and when its resources permit, it will do its utmost to fill this gap. With this result, the Plessis-Piquet horticultural school, already so well established, will rank among the first of its kind in our beautiful country of France. Having the advantages that many others don't: proximity to the big city, for the sale of products it has in excess for its food, the ease of obtaining fertilizers, the exceptional quality of the land it cultivates, etc. All sorts of cases to its advantage over others. All sorts of advantages over other, less well-situated schools. This vast establishment, covering eighteen hectares and so picturesquely located, can establish many types of cultivation and requires a great deal of fertilizer. The men of progress at its head, notably the current director, Mr. Coudry, are not neglecting any modern ideas; they are experimenting with chemical fertilizers, variously constituted, taking into account the dominant characteristics of the plant they are cultivating. Perhaps they will achieve satisfactory results, and thereby render a valuable service to horticulture in general. In this respect, we can only applaud them and encourage them to persevere with their experiments, by enrolling us among their students. 108 Between 1881 and 1883, the General Council of the Seine, ever eager to improve the lot of the underprivileged, founded a number of vocational schools, notably those at Monténain and Villepreux. The aim of the former is to train industrial and construction workers. It is beyond the scope of this article. The second, designed to produce gardeners, is of interest to us alone. The initiative for this institution was taken by the third commission of the General Council, chaired at the time by Mr. Doeteur Bourneville, with Mr. Rousselle as secretary. The members of the commission were: Messrs Cattiaux, Curé, Dubois, Forest, Hamel, Loiseau, Mamès, Aristide Rey, Rayé and Thulié. Mr. Michel Moring, then Director of Public Assistance, began implementing the project voted by the General Council, on the proposal of its third Commission. His successor, Mr. Quentin, who took a keen interest in the project, had the honor of creating it, and gave it the name "Service des enfants moralement abandonnés" (Service for morally abandoned children). Through his activity and his contacts with the press, he was able to involve the public in this act of human solidarity, which received considerable donations, so that the department of the Seine had virtually nothing to spend on the purchase and organization of the Villepreux school. Mr. Quentin had enthusiastic collaborators in Mr. Bruyère, Head of the Division for Assisted Children, and above all Mr. Guillaume, a former pupil of Grignon, to whom the direction of the school was entrusted; Mr. Guillaume kept it until 1898, to take a well-deserved rest, although he remained, in spite of everything, honorary director, so much this man of heart made this work his own. For the competent men who saw the school's beginnings, who have followed its development and progress to date, it is to this democrat, this tireless worker, who was never put off by anything, who devoted his youth, his intelligence and part of his fortune to this institution, that we owe them in large part. Men of this temperament are so rare, even in our century of democracy, that it would be an ingratitude on the part of the historian not to communicate their names to posterity. At the beginning of 1882, the school opened with three pupils; by the end of the year, it had some forty. A bourgeois house in Villepreux and three hectares of adjoining land were acquired by the Département de la Seine; the land was cleared by the pupils and converted first to the cultivation of large vegetables. A donation from M. Joseph Reinach was then used to build a hot-house, the first of the major facilities the school now has. At this time, a member of the French Horticultural Society, Mr. Laisné, well known as a philanthropist in the society, founded an annual prize of one hundred francs, consisting of a savings bank book, to be awarded each year as an incentive to the most deserving pupil. Since this initial donation, the sum has been doubled by the generous benefactor, and three prizes are awarded: one for one hundred francs and two for fifty francs. The school and its equipment continued to grow; a fifty-five-acre plot of land not adjoining the establishment, surrounded by walls, was purchased and devoted specifically to fruit growing; new DO0 greenhouses for different crops were built, including one for forcing vines and another for peaches. Chassis and bells are acquired for market gardening. The estate itself was considerably enlarged, from its original three hectares to five. Various workshops were set up to maintain equipment and even manufacture new items: one for carpentry and locksmithing, and another for basketry, where the students make all the objects used in horticulture in bad weather. They also bake their own bread, an excellent household bread that makes all these young boys look great. All the building alterations required for these various services are carried out by the students, who are thus initiated into masonry work. A very important apiary is established at the top of the estate, where students learn how to care for honey flies. A cow barn, a pigsty and a farmyard containing numerous poultry have also been added to the estate, giving the pupils the opportunity to cultivate crops outside the school. The highest part of the estate was cleared and transformed into an arboretum with flowerbeds and lawns, enabling students to maintain a pleasure garden, and the school to earn its title of "Le Nôtre" school. A large nursery was created, containing all the shrubs that gardeners use to plant beds in squares, promenades, public and private establishments. The School began to supply the hospices and hospitals of the Seine department with the various plants, including annuals, which adorned the gardens that had become so fashionable in these establishments, and for which it would soon become the main supplier. What a wonderful innovation in modern times! For nothing is more comforting for the unfortunate who have suffered, when they begin to take the air, to find themselves 1000 ver in the midst of shrubs and flowers that other destitute people have provided for them, while learning a trade that can later put them out of misery. This account of the school's set-up gives the reader an idea of the various courses it offers the pupils. In addition to general subjects such as farm cultivation, cow farming, pig farming, barnyard work, apiary work, carpentry, locksmithing, masonry, basket-making, baking, etc., the most important teaching is the "art of farming", The main subject is horticulture in general: ornamental plants, annuals and perennials, trees and shrubs for bedding, landscape architecture, fruit growing from sowing, grafting, pruning and all aspects of fruit tree care, including forced cultivation and vines. As the house is located far from a town, we only grow vegetables for our own needs: a few panels of forced strawberry plants, melons, carrots, turnips, lettuces and romaine, etc. All of this is well cultivated, with a great deal of care and attention. Everything is well cultivated, tidy and clean, and the plants fairly well selected. This cultivation is very useful for teaching students who will not become specialists, and a good apprenticeship for those who will engage in this art. The table of placements given to students, which we have before us, shows us the professions they adopt on leaving school. Most remain gardeners, while a few have already made a name for themselves in horticulture. The school's current head gardener is the first winner of the Prix Laisné. A former student of the school is director of the trial garden in Libreville (French Congo), officer of agricultural merit, academy officer. Another is director of the Tunis test garden, reporting to him are four former colleagues, like him wards of the Seine department. A number of them are gardeners in public assistance homes; others manage bourgeois gardens of some importance; many are currently in the military, since the oldest pupils are barely thirty-five years old; some have re-enlisted at the age of 500 to make their military career, and are already supplying some non-commissioned officers to the army. Some of them have also taken up the trades they began at school: locksmith, carpenter, basket-maker, baker and so on. These young people will be of real service in the countryside, giving gardening lessons to their former childhood friends; for this is the weak point of rural dwellers, who could obtain great pleasures that would cost them almost nothing, if they had a few notions of horticulture. ORGANIZATION OF THE SCHOOL It can support and house fifty to fifty-five pupils, who enter at the age of twelve or thirteen and leave at sixteen; in other words, at the age when one can earn a living in horticulture. PRIMARY SCHOOL Classes are held every day from half past four to half past six. One section includes pupils without a primary school certificate. The majority of pupils who do have the certificate are taught in another section, which deals with surveying, levelling, accounting and writing. These courses are taught by the local schoolteacher. PROFESSIONAL INSTRUCTION Theoretical and practical professional instruction is given concurrently by the school director and the heads of the farms or workshops, in lectures by the former, and on the building sites by the latter. Lectures are reserved exclusively for second- and third-year students, and take place three times a week, covering botany, applied chemistry, fertilizers, cultivation methods and so on. At the end of their studies, they are awarded a certificate by a jury appointed by the Société nationale d'horticulture de France. WOODWORKING AND IRONWORKING Two workshops, equipped with sufficient tools to carry out work related to cultivation equipment, operate under the direction of two technical foremen who are also supervisors. Each student learns the essentials of the trade. During the off-season, a gardener's boy, or a former student placed with a basket-maker, gives apprentices lessons, making baskets, baskets, etc., as used by horticulturists. LIBRARY AND MUSEUM The school has a reading room, collections of molded fruit, seeds and herbariums. An annual subsidy of five hundred francs is allocated for upkeep. In this model establishment, no effort is spared to complete the education of the children entrusted to its care. Pleasurable arts are not forgotten and form part of the program. Music, gymnastics and games. Two and a half hours are devoted to music, twice a week. Lessons are given by a musician from the Republican Guard. There is also a brass band, whose players are suitably trained. GYMNASTICS The same time as for music is devoted to this exercise, so useful for young people. Cane and boxing lessons are given by a non-commissioned officer from the Saint-Cyr military academy, located a few kilometers from Villepreux. A covered gymnasium, equipped with all the regulation apparatus, is at the students' disposal. GAMES All kinds of games are set up in the house to entertain students during breaks: bowling, barrel games, ball games, etc., even a billiard table for older students. Each pupil receives a salary, part of which is left at his or her disposal, while the rest is used either to buy small items or to build up a nest-egg. The salary consists of : 1° A weekly cash bonus, to be used at the child's discretion. 2° Quarterly allowance to be spent in kind. 3° Reserves placed in his name at the savings bank by the administration. First-year students receive: 1° A weekly allowance of forty-five francs per year. 2° Twenty-five francs set aside for personal purchases. 3° Payment into the savings bank by the administration, fifty francs. Total: one hundred and twenty francs. Second-year students receive one hundred francs for the weekly bonus, and twenty-five and seventy-five francs for the other two. Third-year students receive one hundred and twenty-five, one hundred and fifty and one hundred and seventy-five francs. Not only does it teach them a trade that will enable them to earn an honorable living, but it also leaves them with a small nest egg that many others would not have, at the age when they leave school, when they leave their parents' home. Each year, we organize professional training trips, either in France or abroad. The principal accompanies students chosen from among the most deserving, and provides them with explanations on site to help them make the most of these trips. In addition, good subjects are authorized to visit their home agency each year, at their own expense. Their ties with their foster parents are not severed, and for one month they help them with the work in the fields. This last fact sufficiently explains why a certain number return to their adoptive families, to become farmers in the country where they were raised. As I said earlier, they will be able to render great service to their adopted brothers. May all these young people, now citizens, never forget to return to society all the kindness and consideration it has shown them, and may the education that their situation has enabled them to acquire become a national benefit by themselves educating the inhabitants of our countryside. PUNISHMENTS Punishments consist of the total or partial withdrawal of gratuities, and additional drudgery. A register of punishments lists the reasons. The student always receives a weekly report. THE ESTATE This beautiful estate of more than five hectares, with its various facilities: accommodation for the director, students, library, museum, schools, various workshops, farm, pigsty, cow barn, playrooms, gymnasium, various services. Horticultural equipment and plants. The establishment has a surface area of twelve hundred meters of glass greenhouses, equipped with thermosiphon heating and containing plants of considerable value. Frames and cloches are used for other crops. Two thousand five hundred meters of trees of various species and espaliered and counter-espaliered vines, all well trained and in full ratio. The nurseries, arboretum, livestock, poultry, tools, etc., represent an enormous capital, largely due to the generosity of numerous donors, the main ones being : Baron Joseph de Reinach, Simon Emden, Kahn, the Iycée Charlemagne, and the Ministry of Agriculture. But one of the most important, the most disinterested, the most modest, whose name appears nowhere, and yet well known to practitioners who have followed the progress of the work, is without doubt the honorable director: M. Guillaume. This good man with a certain fortune, which has always enabled him to remain independent, even from the administration, has devoted most of his income to improving the physical, moral and above all professional well-being of his pupils. C This new Vincent de Paul will have his place in the republican calendar, if ever there was one, as the benefactor of morally abandoned children. In the meantime, let us cite him as one of the men who have rendered the most services to humanity in our century. WATER SUPPLY When creating a horticultural establishment as important as the one we have just described, the first thing to consider is how to obtain abundant water. In this respect, the school is in an exceptional position, thanks to the intelligence and energy of its director. The rû de Gally runs alongside the garden at the bottom of the property; it's worth noting that the terrain in this area is very uneven. Not far from the building, the commune owns a fountain fed by springs from the neighboring hillsides. The water is clear, colorless and odorless, with a slightly pasty taste. Initially, a connection was made to the schoolyard, for drinking water and all domestic needs. This installation has now been removed. Today, the water used in all departments comes from springs tapped at the top of the estate. With the slope of the land, and after studying the various layers of which it was composed, he had the students dig a gallery one hundred and fifty meters long, reaching the level of the spring.
