By this means, he was able to capture the entire spring, which yields a flow of two hundred and fifty liters of pure, healthy water per minute, used for watering the entire garden and feeding a stream that runs through immense lawns adorned with plant beds; rustic bridges are built over this stream, to give students an insight into landscape architecture; a beautiful aspect of horticulture, which can be of great service to them when they have to design a public or private park; a situation which some students already occupy, as we have seen above. All these trays, made by the students in their own time, were done at an unbelievably low price. Visiting these works with a friend and accompanied by the principal, we suggested to him the idea of supplementing the school's teaching by taking advantage of these galleries to establish a mushroom farm. The school's budget will not be increased; a few cubic meters of good horse manure will be the only expense, and as we shall see in the Traité de la culture du champignon (1), this manure will then be used as fertilizer for the crops. With the main notions of this cultivation, given by a few friends who are interested in the work, to the intelligent head gardener we knew as a pupil, this teaching will soon have reached the level of the others given at the school. In this respect, everyone knows the danger of consuming wild mushrooms. As we said in the chapter devoted to these plants, unless you're a serious connoisseur, you can make a mistake picking them, and risk poisoning an entire family. Student gardeners should therefore be encouraged, when they later take charge of a garden, to cultivate these plants whenever possible. Let us add, to conclude this chapter, that in addition to all these generous men who have taken care of the School, there have been certain general councillors of the Seine, who have taken it under their protection. Among so many others, we can mention Messrs Bourneville, Thulié, Rousselle, Curé, etc. Since Mr. Guillaume's retirement, the school's management has been entrusted to a young man, hard-working and intel(1) Work that the author will be publishing shortly. OQ ligent: M. Potier, like his predecessor, a former student of Grignon. We have every reason to hope that he will follow in Grignon's footsteps, helped by his advice, and that the Villepreux school will flourish even more than before. We also hope that the generous souls who have already done so much for this philanthropic work will not soon forget him. Deused CO D'IGNY Various other schools exist in the department of Seine-et-Oise, which can be considered part of the Paris suburbs. These more or less fee-paying schools are generally run by clerics, and the teaching they provide is more a reflection of the large-scale cultivation carried out in this suburb than of horticulture per se. We visited the Saint-Nicolas Brothers' school in Igny. This establishment specializes in fruit growing. The garden is laid out in very large squares, where we grow large vegetables such as potatoes, peas, beans, artichokes, etc., framed by three rows of fruit trees, apple and pear trees in cordons and pyramids, well trained and in full ratio. There are two small greenhouses, however, where students can learn about ornamental plants and vine forcing. A small bedding area is used for cuttings of annual plants and for forcing a few vegetables. A few panels of strawberry plants, varieties : Docteur Morère and Marquerite that don't look bad, the rest are insignificant for the season (beginning of May). It's only in autumn that this school is at its best, and its products are noticed every year at the exhibition held by the French Horticultural Society at this time of year. We feel that this is not enough for such a well-designed establishment, given that the students who graduate from it are specially destined to manage bourgeois gardens. As we know, owners who take on a student from a school like to have vegetables in all seasons, especially early ones. Osteed CO AND PUBLIC COURSES The statutes of the community of master gardeners under the old regime contained an article on apprenticeship that provided a valuable advantage for young apprentices and a guarantee for the employer. At the beginning of the century, having retained some of the customs of the past, young people were hired as apprentices and paid as such. Once they had spent some time with the same employer, they became skilled workers, capable of doing their trade well. When, for one reason or another, they left their apprentice boss to work for another, we knew where they'd come from and what they knew how to do. These circumstances helped a great deal in securing contracts, whereas nowadays, a worker comes to work for us declaring that he knows how to do the job, when sometimes he has never set foot in a swamp, or knows very little about the work he has to do. The boss, often believing he's dealing with a good worker, has agreed to pay him as such, but seeing himself deceived, he dismisses him immediately and the worker starts again elsewhere. It also happens that, when the work is urgent, we keep him on for a while, employing him on the big jobs, which are the easiest to do. The worker, for his part, knowing that he does not possess the special aptitudes to fulfill the commitment he has entered into, is convinced in advance that he will be dismissed as soon as the heavy work is finished; he becomes more demanding when he does not leave the job of his own accord. This state of affairs gives rise to unease and discontent on both sides, often extending to the good workers themselves, who then become the victims. Labor is becoming so hard to come by these days, that we have to anticipate the moment when almost everything that is possible will be done on the job. Will this way of working reconcile all interests? Only time will tell. From this brief account, we can see that almost all the evil stems from the lack of training of workers. Without going back to the practices of the Middle Ages, couldn't we require a certificate of apprenticeship from the worker, which would justify the degree of professional instruction of the applicant? This certificate would inform the employer, at the time of hiring, about the work that the worker would be capable of performing. This procedure would make it easier to sign up to new contracts, and would often help them to last longer. In the century of freedom in which we live, it applies to many corporations. In Paris especially, where everything is specialized, there is a huge difference between a worker accustomed to working in one specialty, and another working in another. The method we suggest would not be detrimental to anyone's freedom, and would be of great benefit to both employers and workers. Frequent job changes benefit no one, and on the contrary, often cause a significant loss in the industry where they occur, and always to the workers in their wages. Only one free vocational course exists in the Seine department, given by an experienced practitioner: Mr. Duvillard. This course, paternally given in his beautiful 908 marsh in Arcueil-Cachan, is both theoretical and practical, but it can hardly be applied to apprentices, who above all need to learn manual labor, before knowing when to sow seeds, how to care for plants, how to rotate crops; in a word, all the profit that can be made from the land we cultivate. This course is more useful for those who intend to run private gardens, where horticulture in general is practiced. It is the intellectual and practical side of the profession, not the manual. In the latter case, it is essential that the apprentice knows how to use the tools placed in his hand, before learning how to manage the crop, which he learns by practicing. In spite of everything, we would strongly urge workers to follow this course, which can only strengthen them in their art. It would encourage them to take notes on the various crops they are studying; the results obtained, and so on. Make comparisons with what they see being done elsewhere, and draw their own conclusions. In horticulture in general, and in market gardening in particular, where you have to continually help or fight nature, you never know your job inside out; a fact that vindicates your methods one year vindicates them the next. The horticulturist cannot be compared to the artist; the latter, once he has perfected his art, does so forever. In horticulture, on the other hand, there are always new things to come along that you don't expect, and which you therefore don't know without studying them. It is therefore only through the study of these facts and through experience that the horticulturist can overcome all the difficulties that arise in his cultivation. Until the middle of the 19th century, the trade in horticultural products remained purely local. As a result, gardeners did not feel the need to display their products. But since this trade has become almost international, like that which results from all industries, they have felt the need to make their products known to merchants in all countries. For over thirty years, market gardeners have played a major role in the annual spring exhibitions organized by the French National Horticultural Society. Their products are always much admired by the many visitors to these exhibitions, and win them a large number of prizes for the beauty and, above all, the perfect selection of their plants. Market gardeners don't take part in autumn shows, believing that at this time of year everyone can grow vegetables when there's plenty of water to water them. Despite this, we feel that they could still organize an exhibition at this time of year, where the hand of these skilled practitioners would be recognized, especially in the many varieties of plants they have so artfully selected. These include cauliflowers, tomatoes, celery and other seasonal salads and vegetables. At the 1867 World's Fair, market gardeners wanted to grow crops on the spot, but unless they had a special plot of land suitable for the purpose and considerable resources at their disposal, success was unlikely. In 1878, there were temporary competitions, i.e. the display of products capable of being delivered to the trade for the duration of the Exhibition. In our opinion, this was the only practical way to showcase Parisian market garden production. In 1889, this was the only way to do so, and connoisseurs from all over the world stopped to admire this unique production. This exhibition was the subject of an important report by Mr. Duvillard, secretary and rapporteur of this class. REPORT BY M. DUVILLARD Vegetable plants At the 1889 Universal Exhibition, class 80 centralized all products supplied by market gardening, vegetable growing and related agricultural activities. As with the other classes in Group IX, eleven competitions, representing the various successive periods of cultivation, were organized to enable visitors to follow more easily the different phases and the many advances in market garden production. In this way, everyone was able to get an accurate picture of all the improvements made by our horticulturists, both to increase and to enrich the products delivered for consumption, from the most sought-after early crops to the vegetables that form the staple diet. In the Class 80 competition, for example, we saw a succession of market garden varieties, the most prized of which were strawberries, pineapples, asparagus and melons, grown in the Paris region and available to a privileged few at the start of each season, Thanks to the special care that Parisian growers give to this type of cultivation, without neglecting the products sought after by popular consumers, such as potatoes, cabbages, radishes and salads whose families are so varied. The harvests exhibited in the Class 80 competitions were highly commended for the quality and beauty of their produce, as well as for the diversity of their species. It is indisputable that, for several years now, the horticultural industry has been undergoing the greatest development in Paris and neighbouring regions; workers have never neglected to use all the means at their disposal to facilitate the use and supply of the rich gifts of the earth and nature. The Universal Exhibition showed us that agricultural exhibitors had kept pace with progress, and, through the improvements they had achieved, that they fully understood the importance of the place they occupy in our century of civilization. In the galleries of class 80, all the vegetable products supplied by the Parisian countryside were exhibited in turn, over a relatively short period of time, to supply the Halles and the capital's markets throughout the year. The results obtained by the grain specialists were entirely in line with the very encouraging progress made by the market gardeners; the exhibitors of leguminous species had been able to broaden the scope of this specialty, and not limit it to species grown exclusively in our country; they were thus able to offer visitors specimens of all the varieties harvested throughout the world, which was scarcely possible for vegetables for which freshness and earliness are the primary qualities. From the point of view of education and experience, the exhibition of products grown outside our homeland gave the greatest benefits. We must also point out that all the efforts of our *I workers in the field were not wasted: for the duration of the Exhibition, the products they exhibited continued to be the object of special favor from the public and amateurs, who thus testified to their interest in our agricultural industry. On the other hand, our exhibitors had taken meticulous care to facilitate the study of leguminous plants, presenting them to visitors in a methodical order and layout that enabled them to follow, almost step by step, the progress of vegetable cultivation. The excellent impression made on the public mind was certainly not the result of satisfied curiosity: among our compatriots, as well as foreigners, it was with legitimate pride that we admired the brilliant results due, in large part, to the initiative of the growers who contribute so largely to the life of the capital and the civilized world. This popularity of the public, the great popular jury, did not slow down for a moment: every day we could see a large number of people, belonging to all classes of society, following with keen interest all the work of class 80, and paying great attention to the explanations and information provided by the exhibitors to the visitors; many of them collected important notes, intended as much to fix their memories, as to report to those who were not able, alas! to come and admire the great festival of modern progress, to which France had invited all the peoples of the globe! Farmers in other regions also appreciated the remarkable advantages obtained by Parisian market gardeners and grain growers; they were able to remember a few names or improvements of types, neglected or insufficiently known in their own regions. Thanks to the information they have gathered during their visits to the Exhibition, they will in turn be able to give new impetus to hortiO06 culture and make a powerful contribution to the development of field work, both in the provinces and abroad. From this new point of view, the Class 80 competitions have demonstrated that with intelligent care, sustained hard work and an equitable distribution of agricultural aids, the most advantageous services can be obtained from cultivation in and around Paris, as well as in more clement and hospitable climates. Now that we've talked about agricultural aids, without encroaching on the realm of agronomy, let us briefly mention the most favorable causes that come to the aid of the Parisian or suburban farmer: the main ones in particular are inherent to current progress, and it would perhaps be ingratitude on our part not to pay them the just tribute they deserve. First of all, we find a whole system of perfected agricultural tools and machines. The Universal Exhibition, among all its marvels, showed that science had achieved a great deal, and that it had not yet said its last word: it used to take a great deal of effort and toil to obtain, from rudimentary equipment, results which today would certainly be inadequate for general supply; productive forces were incomplete, cultivation faltered, and land often remained uncultivated, even in our beautiful country of France. Today, we are able to provide for almost all consumer needs. Secondly, we have found powerful collaborators in the fertilizers used in recent years. As the learned agronomist Schwertz said, "Whoever wants to get a lot out of the soil, must also give it a lot". In Paris, more than anywhere else, it was necessary to give a great deal to the fertility of the soil, and if we have just made a quotation from a foreign worker in our country, we 009 also note, to the glory of our national self-esteem, that it is still in France, especially in the vicinity of the City of Light, that the most brilliant results have been obtained in this respect. From all the products exhibited in the Class 80 competitions, it is clear that market gardening, better improved and perhaps better understood, has developed far beyond our expectations. The initiative and dedication of the workers have kept pace with the prodigious activity of consumption, while ensuring the satisfaction of all the requirements of our times. Perhaps the future will bring further improvements, but we are convinced that these will be inspired by the studies that the Exhibition's competitions will have led to. This will be to the credit of our farmers, who will have proved that they have never ceased to keep up with the times and with progress. And it is also with real satisfaction that they will have contributed, within the limits of their attributions, to ensuring the greatness and triumph of the Universal Exhibition which crowned the great centenary of 1789". At the 1900 Exposition, vegetable production will be widely represented in all the competitions taking place, and it is to be hoped that this industry will play a leading role in the great peaceful struggle that will take place on the threshold of the twentieth century. The admission committee for this class (class 44; group VIII) has made every effort to persuade the Exhibition's senior management to include mushroom cultivation. Mushroom cultivation had previously been included in group IX, class 54; harvesting equipment, instruments and products. As we have already said, this cultivation was begun by the gardeners of Paris, at least two centuries ago, and has since been continued by their descendants, a number of whom have become absolute specialists. In the temporary horticultural competitions, visitors and especially enthusiasts will be able to examine this culture as it is grown in the immense quarries surrounding Paris; they will also see the seedling blanks and the products of these blanks. A discovery made by modern scientists. The exhibition will also be retrospective, to show the progress made in each industry. This will not be its least curious aspect, to mark in a clear and well-fixed way the stage humanity has reached in this century of progress. The author, having been asked by his class to prepare this work for our specialty, has produced an abridged version of this history, which has been unanimously accepted both by the board of directors of the Paris region market gardeners' union, and by the committee of the aforementioned class. PLANTES POTAGÈRES gitired by Groupe vin (horticulture), Classe 44 (culture potagère) CULTURE MARAICHÈRE PARISIENNE Fistorique sorrrnaire de l'industrie Market gardening has only really made progress in this century; our ancestors were content to cultivate the plants they had, in the season when nature made them grow. The forced cultivation of the late 19th century, which became the basis of Parisian market garden production, is where most of the science of the modern market gardener resides. It is due in particular to the initiative taken in the 18th century by the illustrious La Quintinie. This art of making plants grow out of season is therefore only two hundred years old, and throughout the last century it has served only to satisfy the culinary tastes of kings and the powerful. At the beginning of the 19th century, a few bold and enterprising market gardeners tried to imitate the great gardener of Louis XIV and his successors, by forcing some plants. These men are the masters of modern gardeners, and we respectfully salute their memory in passing. However, for more than a century (1), market gardeners have been making a few layers in the air to advance the Raves, (1) Delamare, Traité de Police. Radishes, lettuces, etc. The great market gardener mentioned above acknowledged that he had learned a great deal from the Parisian "mareschais" (1). The latter, in turn, as well as their posterity, owe him a tribute of gratitude for the progress in cultivation he made in his royal kitchen garden. At the end of the 18th century, a small number of market gardeners had a few hundred bells and very few frames. Asparagus was first cultivated "à cloches", as indicated by La Quintinie in his Traité de culture potagère; then came other growers who cultivated it "à châssis", a method still used today. Other crops were also tried, by the sons of the former masters of the gardening community. It was not until the Restoration, when tranquility returned and trade took off again in a new and unprecedented way, that these crops became widespread. At the same time, a large number of young men and women left the countryside to set up families in Paris. These less well-off families tried to amass a small nest egg to work for themselves. Establishments were of little value; a spade or two, a rake, a pair of watering cans, a few barrels, a pulley with its rope and buckets, a few hoods, mannes, etc. constituted all the equipment for these arrivals. Consequently, water was drawn by hand, and produce was carried to Les Halles on hoods, as in (1) La Quintinie, Traité de culture potagère. O0 the old days, even though settlements were already moving away from them. At that time, they were located around Louis XVI's enclosure walls (intra and extra-muros), especially for the latter, while the sons of the former masters still lived in the suburbs around Charles V's destroyed enclosure. As the latter had a few frames and a number of bells, they installed cranks (1). This is a very old tool, but it requires the use of a horse to operate, and until then, settlements were not large enough to cover these costs. The arrival of the hand crank was therefore a revolution in market gardening, requiring the use of a horse and carriage, as well as the distribution of water in the marsh; with the bells, frames and accessories that this equipment entailed, this already formed a market gardening establishment, where production could take place all year round. Around 1835, piston and merry-go-round pumps replaced the cranks, thus solving the problem of drawing water from great depths, enabling market gardeners to tackle the plateaux around Paris, since water is the most important issue for market gardeners at all times and in all places. As the population of Paris grew rapidly during the 19th century, and the number of middle-income earners became much greater, early crops were in demand, and market gardeners gradually increased their equipment for these crops. In 1845, Messrs Moreau and Daverne, who wrote the history of their corporation, reveal that in their time it employed three hundred and fifty thousand frames and around two million bells. But since these authors wrote, the greatest economic, industrial and commercial revolution of modern times has taken place. The creation of the railways (1) Malpeyre, Bixio et Bailly, Maison Rustique. 900. Trade has grown steadily since then, and has gone from being local to our corporation to being international, and market gardeners' equipment has increased considerably today. Around 1860, a new advance was made in the guild: the installation of irrigation. This watering system, initially resisted by the older members of the guild, took little more than a decade to become widespread, and helped to increase market-garden production in great proportions, enabling growers to brave the most severe droughts. After 1870, a new crop was added to those already grown by market gardeners: a few young people began to grow the early turnip known as Navet marteau. This crop was a great success, almost doubling the number of market gardeners' frames. The cultivation of other early vegetables followed the same progression, in particular: forced Romaine on bell beds, one of the most marvelous and lucrative crops bequeathed to the guild by the old market gardeners of this century. At the 1889 Universal Exhibition, mechanical engineers presented gas and oil engines; a large number of market gardeners were quick to install these tools to operate the pump, replacing the horse which, given the size of the marshes and their remoteness from the capital, has enough work to do transporting produce to Les Halles and bringing in the manure needed for the farm. These small gas or oil engines worked very well, and young market gardeners quickly learned how to use them. At the end of the century, mechanical power was put to work in market gardening. It's a far cry from the way the first gardeners of the century drew water by hand. A number of gardeners have installed rotating "C" cities in their marshes, with portable rails to enter the squares; this is an innovation that will make its way and replace back-pack transport of vegetables, manure, potting soil, etc., in the century that is about to begin. In the very near future, we can expect to see small motor-driven ploughs replacing the spade. At this point, I realize that I'm encroaching on the future and that I'm leaving the questionnaire that's been imposed on me. MAIN PRODUCTION CENTERS At the end of this century, the main production centers for market gardening proper are on the right bank of the Seine: Maisons-Alfort, Créteil, Champigny Bobigny, Aubervilliers, La Courneuve, Stains, Asnières and Gennevilliers. The localities on the left bank with the largest groups are : Issy-les-Moulineaux, Malakoff, Montrouge, Bagneux, Arcueil-Cachan, Gentilly, Ivry and Vitry. TRADE IN PRODUCTS DURING THE CENTURY (exports and main consumption centers). Our ancestors left no written records to give us an idea of the turnover they were able to achieve, but based on the work so wisely done by Messrs Moreau and Daverne, in 1845, we can, by deduction, establish it approximately. These authors estimated that market gardeners in their day occupied three hundred and fifty thousand frames, about two million bells and that production could amount to thirteen or fourteen million francs annually, including mushroom cultivation, which became a specialty at the end of the century. They also cultivated beautiful1- 61- "1e produit n'appartient plus au domaine "n taking all these facts into account: for forced cultivation in the 208 middle of the century, deducting the cultivation of mushrooms and flowers, which belong to other specialties, we can estimate, without fear of deviating much, market garden production at the beginning of the century, at three or four million francs a year. By the end of the century, market gardeners occupied approximately one million frames and five to six million bells, cultivating around one thousand hectares of land, with an average yield of thirty thousand francs per hectare: in other words, this industry had grown almost tenfold in terms of business and much more in terms of production: the price of early vegetables being lower than at the beginning of the century, and only twenty or twenty-five years ago. So it's consumers in general who are benefiting from this increase in production. It is estimated that more than a third of this produce is exported abroad, notably to England, the largest consumer center for Parisian market garden produce, Belgium, Germany and, to a lesser extent, Russia. Les primeuristes have just presented their products directly at the inter-national horticultural exhibition in Saint Petersburg, where they won almost all the first prizes. This augurs well for opening up new commercial outlets for the market-gardening industry, which fears no competition in terms of product selection and beauty. The national horticultural exhibitions held every year in Paris, and international ones at intervals, are proof of this. The vegetable displays put on by the market gardeners' guild always win top prizes, and are much admired by the many French and foreign visitors. LAND VALUES 2 LEUIS : -- . -. In the mid-19th century, Y tells us that Louis XVI terraces and Louis-Philippe's fortified enclosure were worth between twenty-eight and thirty thousand francs per hectare. Those with no easy access were worth between sixteen and twenty thousand francs, including walls and housing. The sites occupied by market gardeners at the beginning of the century, in the lower suburbs of Charles V's enclosure, were already worth from eighty to one hundred thousand francs per hectare; today they are worth from five hundred to eight hundred francs per meter, and the latter, from fifty to two hundred francs depending on their location. The same authors estimated the rental of well-placed plots at twelve or thirteen hundred francs per hectare, and the second quality at eight to nine hundred francs, all including housing. These prices had roughly doubled since the beginning of the century. Between the time these authors were writing and the end of the century, market gardeners had moved away from Paris, and the same proportion existed for the increase in rents; they had roughly doubled, which meant that they had quadrupled during the course of the century. Today, land suitable for market gardening in the Paris suburbs sells for an average of three to four francs a meter, in field soil of course. To set up a farm: house, outbuildings, fencing, well, etc., you need to spend around twenty thousand francs. Consequently, a hectare of marshland is currently worth between fifty and sixty thousand francs. EXPLOITATION Market gardening, with the enormous equipment it requires, consists in producing rimeurs in autumn, winter and spring, along with 1- C1 11s, the finest commercially available. As for the market gardeners themselves, today they should simply be called "primeuristes": their method of forcing plants has not yet changed much; it is still done by layers of manure, as in La Quintinie's day. But with modern progress in replacing animal traction with mechanical traction, we can expect the price of this raw material to rise, as another industry, that of mushroom growers, employs more and more of it. At some point, therefore, manure production will not keep pace with the needs of this industry. Some market gardeners have already added thermosiphon technology to their crops. When the time comes, they too will be forced to use chemical fertilizers on their other crops. Moreover, the intelligent market gardener who loves his trade is like a traveller climbing a mountain whose sites are unknown to him. With each step he takes forward, new horizons open up before him, increasing the scope of his research and, later, often that of his knowledge. These are the reasons why Class 44 of Group VIII decided to present these questions for discussion at the 1900 Horticultural Congress. It's a sort of preparation for l'Avenir. MARAICH GROWING SOCIETIES AND COURSES The market gardening guild has a mutual aid society, or rather a pension fund. Founded in 1855, the fund has around a thousand members, and has provided, is providing and will continue to provide useful services to its members. In agreement with horticulturists, it has set up a mutual insurance company against damage caused by hail and storms. It also has a professional association, which was set up only a few years ago, and which is also destined to be of great service to the company, both economically and commercially. 900 A free public course in vegetable growing is given once a month in Arcueil-Cachan by Mr. Duvillard, also a teacher with the Chambre syndicale des ouvriers-jardiniers. EXISTENCE IMPROVEMENT (Wages, number of bosses and workers) Although this first question is not included in the questionnaire, it is difficult to talk about workers' wages without saying a word about improving their existence. At the end of this century, market gardeners in Paris are the only ones still feeding their workers; with the spirit of independence that drives the men of our time, the time is not far off when this state of affairs will cease; for, on both sides, people aspire to this moment, which will come with the enlargement and remoteness of establishments, and with other methods of selling produce. The patriarchal mores that existed among the ancients are tending to disappear by the day. There is no longer the mutual esteem that once existed between bosses and workers, and the latter no longer stay with the same boss for many years, as they once did. At the beginning of the century, our ancestors had a Spartan existence, which no worker would be content with today. We might even add that very few workers live as well as market gardeners; they enjoy all the benefits that civilization has given to mankind, they have the fortifying food of city dwellers, and the hygiene and fresh air of country dwellers. A distinguished economist who has established the mortality of men by corporation, estimates that it is one of those where they live the longest. The wages of both male and female workers have quadrupled in the course of the century. Most are paid by the month: from seventy-five to eighty francs in summer, and from fifty-five to sixty in winter. As they are housed and fed by their bosses, they are unaware of the rising cost of living and rents; the women workers who go to Les Halles earn as much as the men. It's quite obvious that the day they're no longer fed and housed, they'll have less profit, or else they'll live worse; however, generosity aspires after that day, which is hardly surprising, as men want to enjoy as much freedom as possible. The market-gardening guild we have just described comprises around one thousand two hundred establishments, making a total of two thousand four hundred male and female bosses, because it's worth saying here that, in this profession, women have, if not more, then as much work and responsibility as men. It's in her department that products are prepared for sale, and until then it's she who goes to sell them at the central Ilalles. These 1,200 establishments employ around 5,000 workers of both sexes, not counting children, who today start work at a younger age than in the past. Since the Third Republic passed a law on primary education, they hardly start work until they are thirteen or fourteen. In a word, and to conclude, market gardening is one of the hardest jobs men can do, but it's also one of the healthiest. In this respect, didn't one of our great classics, Boileau, write the following two superb lines? Work, to men necessary, Makes their felicity rather than their misery.
